\chapter*{Abstract}
\label{sec:abstract}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Abstract}

\justify

\begin{fullwidth}
	As of 2026, 549 satellites weighing less than \SI{1}{\kilogram} have been launched into Earth orbit.
	Compared to their larger counterparts, CubeSats, these ``\acf{SCS}'' provide benefits in terms of size, cost and mass, at the expense of reduced functionality and performance.
	%
	%\acs{SCS} teams are characterised by small sizes, limited budgets.
	%
	%The reduced technical size and complexity of \acs{SCS} have made them suitable for shorter development cycles and increased understanding.
	%However, no established systems engineering methodology currently exists for these spacecraft.
	%
	%As there is currently no established systems engineering methodology for these miniaturised spacecraft, most teams adopt the development approaches of CubeSat, or follow ad-hoc processes.
	However, despite their reduced complexity compared to CubeSats, \acl{SCS} still experience a high failure rate in orbit, and a median development time of \qty{2}{years}, which is higher than the typical \qtyrange[range-units = single]{1}{1.5}{year} turnaround for \acs{SCS} launch opportunities.
	Scholars have linked these observations to inadequate systems engineering practices and insufficient project planning.
	Currently, the majority of \acs{SCS} developers either use ad-hoc processes or adapt existing methodologies from larger spacecraft.
	These methodologies are deemed unsuitable for the different technical and environmental characteristics of \acs{SCS} projects, either because of the significant overhead they introduce, or because of their incapability to integrate past lessons learned from similarly-sized missions.

	\vspace{-1cm}
	\newthought{This thesis presents} a tailored systems engineering methodology for the development of \acl{SCS}, which covers the aspects of Technical Management, System Description, and System Realisation for these systems. The goal of this methodology is to reduce the development duration between development start and qualification, with an aim of consistently achieving \qtyrange[range-units = single]{1}{1.5}{year}-missions, without an adverse effect on technical risk.

	The proposed methodology incorporates the practices of Agile software engineering, aided by the considerably low manufacturing difficulty and cost of an \acs{SCS} system. The methodology is founded on an iterative lifecycle process model, which revolves around rapid prototyping, short iterations, and continuous system-level verification. Based on this model, more methods suited for \acs{SCS} are proposed, targeting requirements management, technical planning and engineering data management.

	Additionally, the methodology covers technical aspects of the development process, by providing a set of guidelines for defining a design solution and for verification. It is found that the concepts of modularity and reuse have more limited benefits compared to larger spacecraft, while verification via reduced, automated testing instead of analysis can decrease development duration without reducing confidence in the system design. Furthermore, the use of different software development practices is evaluated, showing that an appropriate selection of programming language can generally increase software reliability, based on a case study comparison between different On-Board Computer frameworks.
	
	The methodology is validated through its implementation on four internal case studies, which returned successful results from testing, qualification or flight: \begin{enumerate*}
		\item a hosted ChipSat to demonstrate \acl{VLC},
		\item a hosted \acs{PCB} payload to demonstrate \acs{AI} edge computing,
		\item a 1p PocketQube with 2 technology demonstration payloads, and
		\item an independent ChipSat with a lens-less camera.
	\end{enumerate*} %Three of these missions were successfully qualified, and one was launched into orbit.
	
	By performing counter-factual analysis on the programmatic data generated in these missions, it is estimated that the proposed methodology can reduce development duration by an average of \qty{40}{\percent} compared to waterfall-style approaches. The biggest influences towards a short development time are found to be the \emph{reduced analysis/testing} approach (average 29\% reduction) and the \emph{multi-step iterative process} (average 25\% reduction), with other factors showing a less significant correlation. Perceived technical risk and cost are found to be correlated, either negatively or positively, with some of the individual aspects of the methodology.

	From the presented analysis, it can be concluded that the methodology can be applied to Sub-CubeSat systems to reduce development duration, but requires specific tailoring to reach a desired optimal point, or to individually control for cost and risk.
	A goal of approximately 1 year-long development time was reached for the participating case studies, which can be generalised across similar missions.
	As future work, the proposed methodology can be expanded with more \acs{SCS}-specific mission data to aid tailoring decisions, or extended to apply to larger spacecraft domains.

\end{fullwidth}

\section{Motivation}

\begin{marginfigure}[-1cm]
	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{analyses/launch_mass_plot_no_starlink.pdf}
	\caption[Wet mass distribution of satellites flying as of 2023, excluding Starlink]{Wet mass distribution of satellites flying as of 2023, excluding Starlink \cite{unionofconcernedscientistsSatelliteDatabase2023}}
	\label{fig:i_mass_distribution}
\end{marginfigure}

Out of more than \num{23000} satellites that have been launched into Earth orbit \autocite{esaspacedebrisofficeESAsAnnualSpace2025}, more than \num{3200} belong in the \emph{nano-satellite} class \autocite{kuluNanosatsDatabase2023}, weighing less than \SI{10}{\kilogram}.
Nano-satellites are mostly represented by the CubeSat form factor, a concept introduced in 1999 by the \citeauthor{CDS14} \autocite{CDS14}.

CubeSats are designed in a modular format consisting of \qtyproduct{10 x 10 x 10}{\centi\meter} cubes (termed ``U(nits)''). Subsystems typically follow the ``PC/104'' mechanical standard for subsystems, along with a shared electrical specification \cite{BoardSpecificationLibreCube}.

The advances of miniaturisation in electronics since the 1980s have gradually increased the capabilities and utility of nano-satellites \cite{sweetingModernSmallSatellitesChanging2018}. Services such as Earth Observation, Telecommunications and Technology Demonstration are very frequently covered by missions comprising one or more CubeSats.\footnote{21 constellations of 5 or more nano-satellites have been launched so far. Additionally, CubeSats represent over \qty{90}{\percent} of all nano-satellites launched  \autocite{kuluNanosatsDatabase2023}} Most CubeSats are orbiting in \acs{LEO}, with a few missions also planned and launched for deep space \autocite{walkerDeepspaceCubeSatsThinking2018}.

The low cost of CubeSat launches (typically \(\sim\)\SI{100000}{USD/\kilogram} \autocite{crispSmallSatelliteLaunch2014}) has significantly reduced the entry barrier to space
\autocite{poghosyanCubeSatEvolutionAnalyzing2017,sweetingModernSmallSatellitesChanging2018}.
24\% of launched CubeSat missions are academic in nature.
94 countries in total have launched CubeSats or smaller satellites \cite{kuluNanosatsDatabase2023}, with some serving as their countries' first launched spacecraft \cite{europeanspaceagencyEIRSAT1OneSmall2025}.
This has been aided by an evolving CubeSat industry, which has created a market for subsystems and platforms that are, to some degree, interoperable with each other and with common deployers. As of 2023, roughly 20\% of spacecraft in orbit belong in the nano-satellite class \cite{unionofconcernedscientistsSatelliteDatabase2023} (\Cref{fig:i_mass_distribution})\footnote{Excluding the Starlink constellation, which represents \qty{53}{\percent} \cite{unionofconcernedscientistsSatelliteDatabase2023}.}, with 25 CubeSats being launched per year on average since 2020 \cite{kuluNanosatsDatabase2023}, and a more than \num{500} million USD market in 2025 \autocite{CubeSatMarketSize}.

Despite the significantly fewer resources needed for CubeSat development and production, there is still a significant cost connected with CubeSat missions. Launch and development costs can range up to millions USD, and development times can range between 1--5 or more years \autocite{berthoudHowSetCubeSat2016}. Despite their reduced complexity, CubeSats also experience a \qty{39}{\percent} failure rate in orbit, often attributed to ad-hoc procedures, insufficient testing to uncover mistakes, and workmanship flaws \autocite{swartwoutCubeSatMissionSuccess2019}.

The need from industry and academia for a smaller and less complex standardised platform has given rise to concepts for so-called \textbf{\acf{SCS}}. These spacecraft take advantage of the further miniaturisation of electronic and telecommunication components to push the envelope of size. Classes of planned or launched \acs{SCS} include:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-1ex]
	\item \textbf{Fractional-Unit} CubeSats: 1/2U, 1/3U or 1/4U CubeSats, which do not comply with the CubeSat standard \cite{CDS14}, but are mechanically compatible with standard deployers \autocite{kuluNanosatsDatabase2023,krebsThinSat1A1L2021};
	\item \textbf{PocketQubes}: Modular spacecraft based on the ``1p'' unit (a \qtyproduct{5 x 5 x 5}{\centi\meter}) \autocite{PQ1};
	\item \textbf{SunCubes}: Modular spacecraft based on \qtyproduct{3 x 3 x 3}{\centi\meter} units \autocite{martinezSunCubeFemtoSatDesign2018};
	\item \textbf{Chipsats} or \textbf{\acs{PCB}sats}: Flat spacecraft that consist entirely of a single circuit board, usually less than \qtyproduct{5 x 5}{\centi\meter} in size.
\end{itemize}

These satellites are also classified as pico-, femto- and atto-satellites.\footnote{See \Cref{sec:LITERATURE_classes} for exact terminology.}

\acl{SCS} have enjoyed increased popularity especially since 2018, with 439 pico-satellites and 110 smaller satellites having been launched (\Cref{fig:i_scs_launched}). 99 of these follow the \textbf{PocketQube} standard.
The PocketQube concept was introduced in 2009 by Bob Twiggs \autocite{twiggsMakingItSmall2009} and standardised in \citeyear{PQ1} \autocite{PQ1}. Thanks to the availability of deployers targeting different sizes of PocketQubes, this form factor has enjoyed an average rate of \num{18} satellites launched per year since 2021 \autocite{wikipediacontributorsListLaunchedPocketQubes2025}.

On the sub-PocketQube scale, one of the most notable cases were Zac Manchester's ``Sprite'' ChipSats. Measuring \qtyproduct{3.5 x 3.5}{\centi\metre} (\Cref{subfig:i_manchester_chipsat}), more than 100 ChipSats were deployed by Manchester in 2019, with the goal of allowing members of the public to have ``their personal spacecraft in space'', and marking the first known occasion where atto-satellites were launched into orbit \autocite{manchesterCentimeterScaleSpacecraftDesign2015,thenationalassociationforamateurradioKickSat2AliveBeing2019}.

The distinct characteristics of PocketQubes, ChipSats and similar spacecraft, mainly related to size and cost, have been explored in the literature as they motivate capabilities which cannot normally be attained by larger spacecraft \autocite{perezSurveyCurrentFemtosatellite2016,heinAttoSatsChipSatsOther2019}. One notable example is the concept of Breakthrough Starshot, which exploits the small mass of sail-equipped ChipSats to propel them beyond the Solar System using terrestrial lasers \autocite{finkbeinerNearLightSpeedMissionAlpha2017}. Other proposed concepts for \acs{SCS} include distributed data collection, mother-ship based missions, and rapid testing \cite{heinAttoSatsChipSatsOther2019}.

\begin{marginfigure}
	\includegraphics{media/pictures/starshot.jpg}
	\caption[Artist's depiction of a \emph{Breakthrough Starshot} lightsail]{Artist's depiction of a \emph{Breakthrough Starshot} lightsail \cite{spacerefBreakthroughStarshotProject2016}}
\end{marginfigure}

Due to the smaller size and component count of \acs{SCS}, these systems are generally simpler and easier to develop than their larger counterparts. While the capabilities of a single system are reduced due to these constraints, these form factors make spacecraft development accessible to engineers and communities that would otherwise not be able to follow the complete \ac{MAIV} process for a CubeSat, with an extreme example being Manchester's \qty{300}{USD} ChipSat Kickstarter cost \autocite{manchesterKickSatYourPersonal2016}. PocketQubes have been described as suitable for education and training in academic environments, especially due to the decreased manufacturing and launch costs \autocite{bouwmeesterSurveyWorldwidePico2010}. \citeauthor{heinAttoSatsChipSatsOther2019} \autocite{heinAttoSatsChipSatsOther2019} also note that the development of flight hardware for ChipSats can be finished within 6 months, costing on the order of dozens to hundreds of dollars for manufacturing, and allowing fast build-and-launch cycles.

However, an investigation of available data for \acs{SCS} missions reveals a different story; even though their complexity and scale is low, their development time follows a median of 26 months, going up to 62 months (\Cref{tab:i_otherdevtime}). Yet, outliers that develop systems with similar constraints and form factors at or below a 1 year limit are present. 

At the same time, literature from Sub-CubeSat and CubeSat systems points to \textbf{inadequate \acl{SYE} practices and knowledge} as a main reason for reduced mission success \cite{swartwoutUniversityClassSpacecraft2023,sanchoLessonsLearnedFYS2021,kinnairdSelectionLessonsLearned2022,doyleMissionTestingImproved2021,hermanExperimentalResultsModular2025}, and links tailored \acs{SYE} approaches with improved understanding, error identification, shortened integration times and lower costs \autocite{honore-livermoreIntegratingAgileSystems2022}.
Authors have emphasised the need for reduced development times \autocite{pradhanShorteningDeliveryTime2020,uludagDelfiPQInOrbitPerformance2025} and improved reliability \autocite{swartwoutCubeSatMissionSuccess2019,villelaThousandthCubeSatStatistical2019}.
This observation is combined with the fact that, in contrast to CubeSats and larger spacecraft, there is \textbf{no widely established \acl{SYE} methodology} associated with PocketQubes, ChipSats, and other \acs{SCS}, as evidenced by available literature \autocite{heinAttoSatsChipSatsOther2019,bouwmeesterUtilityConstraintsPocketQubes2020} and semi-formal interviews that have been conducted within the scope of this project (\Cref{sec:LITERATURE_interviews}). As a result, developers need to either adopt existing approaches from larger systems, or develop their own ad-hoc methodologies.


However, the combination of \emph{small scale} with \emph{mission criticality} is one that is not commonly found in other engineering fields. Yet, discussions with \acs{SCS} developers show that they operate in environments and under time/risk constraints similar to those of CubeSat systems (\Cref{sec:LITERATURE_interviews}). \acs{SCS} systems can therefore benefit from a \textbf{tailored methodology} that can take advantage of their unique characteristics. Using a methodology originally intended for \emph{larger spacecraft} or systems can introduce significant overhead in terms of cost and time, which may be unnecessary for these systems \autocite{clementsTailoredSystemsEngineering2020}. On the other hand, the use of an \emph{ad-hoc approach} increases programmatic and technical risk, since it provides no guarantees on the timely results of a project and no observability on factors such as progress, quality, or the critical path \autocite{honourTechnicalReportValue2004}. Nevertheless, due to the mission-critical nature of \acs{SCS} and their integration with larger systems\footnote{Such as launchers, deployers}, developers need to achieve a correct balance between the effort and time spent across design, realisation and verification \autocite{sebok_value,boehmROISystemsEngineering2008,dillonFasterBetterCheaperProjectsToo2015}.

\newthought{Thus,} the \textbf{main hypothesis} of this research project is that a \textbf{tailored \acl{SYE} methodology} specifically targeting \acf{SCS} can take advantage of their unique characteristics to reduce time-to-orbit and cost, while maintaining an acceptable level of reliability and functionality. This methodology can also serve as a guideline for new missions, allowing first-time developers to establish a starting point for new projects, drawing on lessons learned from previous missions. The methodology should cover the aspects of \acl{SYE} that affect high-level planning and design, as well as daily work, being concrete enough to not require ``reinventing the wheel'', but flexible enough to be tailored for different pocket-sized projects. In practical terms, it should cover the entire development lifecycle of a spacecraft, from design and technical management to implementation and validation.

In \Cref{sec:INTRODUCTION_defining_systems_engineering}, I will further explore the concept of \acl{SYE}, showing how it can be partitioned into different fields, and investigating its utility and current approaches. \Cref{sec:INTRODUCTION_research_gaps} will explore the gaps in \acl{SYE} research in the field of \acf{SCS}, and analyse the shortcomings of current approaches. Finally, \Cref{sec:INTRODUCTION_research_questions,sec:INTRODUCTION_structure} will explain how I plan to address these shortcomings, by gathering data to synthesise and verify a bespoke \acs{SYE} methodology for pocket-sized systems.

\section{The Scope and Impact of Systems Engineering}
\label{sec:INTRODUCTION_defining_systems_engineering}

\NextCitationOffset{1cm}%
Systems Engineering is defined as a process of synthesis, development and operation of a real-world system that satisfies the requirements for that system \autocite{sebok}.
Systems Engineering processes are especially useful for complex and/or critical systems, such as those used in aerospace engineering \autocite{SMAD,sheaNASASystemsEngineering2020}.
The \acs{NASA} Systems Engineering Handbook partitions Systems Engineering into further specific sub-disciplines, showing its connection with Project Management (\Cref{fig:i_nasa_sye_decomposition}).


%<span class="orange">Can add more criticism towards heavyweight methods here, esp. for systems **why do we need something new?**</span> 

\begin{figure}[h]
	\includegraphics{media/graphics/NASASYE}
	\caption[Decomposition of Systems Engineering in connection with Project Management]{Decomposition of Systems Engineering in connection with Project Management \autocite{sheaNASASystemsEngineering2020}}
	\label{fig:i_nasa_sye_decomposition}
\end{figure}

The space engineering community has used several prominent frameworks for the realisation of complex projects, which may cover, for example, \emph{needs definitions}, \emph{lifecycle approaches}, \emph{engineering/data management} or \emph{system architecture definition} \autocite{sebok}. Some of the most popular examples are:
\begin{itemize}
	\item 
 Separation of a project into separate stages, from conception to end-of-life, following a ``waterfall'' model
 \autocite{ECSS-M-ST-10C,sheaNASASystemsEngineering2020}
 \item 
Requirements Engineering, where specific technical specifications are set for the systems and its components \autocite{ECSS-E-ST-10-06C} 
\item The V-model, which defines a sequential, stage-gated process to implement and verify a system with its components
\autocite{bundesrepublikdeutschlandVModellXT2006,sebok}
\item Iterative lifecycle models, where iteration or evolution cycles are added to allow easy implementation of lessons learned
\autocite{larmanIterativeIncrementalDevelopment2003,sebok_incremental}
\item Concurrent Engineering, where a design is progressed synchronously by multiple engineers \autocite{bandecchiConcurrentEngineeringApplied1999}
\item Model-based Systems Engineering, where formalised models are used to support Systems Engineering tasks \autocite{sebok,hendersonValueBenefitsModelbased2021}
\end{itemize}

\begin{marginfigure}
	\includegraphics{external/nasa_sye_benefit.pdf}
	\caption[It is harder to reverse design decisions, the more effort has been dedicated to a project]{It is harder to reverse design decisions, the more effort has been dedicated to a project. Taken from \cite{sheaNASASystemsEngineering2020}}
\end{marginfigure}
The value and benefits of Systems Engineering are asserted by the ``\acs{SYE} hypothesis'': that additional effort spent in Systems Engineering, especially early in the project, will increase the overall \emph{value} of it, especially in terms of cost, schedule and objectives \autocite{honourUnderstandingValueSystems2004}. A 1992 survey of 32 \acs{NASA} programs, for example, shows a correlation between time spent in the design phases of a project, and reduced final cost overruns \cite{honourUnderstandingValueSystems2004,stutzkeEstimatingSoftwareintensiveSystems2005}. 
Various other surveys have shown similar correlations, demonstrating the value of Systems Engineering 
\autocite{sheaNASASystemsEngineering2020,francoisSystemicsCyberneticsHistorical1999,hendersonValueBenefitsModelbased2021}.

The specific constraints of a project which measure its success and resource utilisation are elaborated by \citeauthor{akbarStatisticalAnalysisEffects2018} \autocite{akbarStatisticalAnalysisEffects2018}, who follow the \emph{six-pointed star model}\footnote{Originally inspired by the cost-time-scope project management triangle} enumerating the following constraints that need to be balanced in a project: \textbf{schedule, scope, budget, risk, resources and quality}. An elaboration of these factors is shown in \Cref{tab:i_success_factors}.

\begin{table}[h]
	\caption[Success factors for systems engineering projects]{Success factors for systems engineering projects. Adapted from \cite{akbarStatisticalAnalysisEffects2018}}
	\label{tab:i_success_factors}
	\setfloatalignment{b}
	\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}lX@{}}
		\toprule
		\textbf{Constraint} & \textbf{Aspect} \\ \midrule
		\multirow{2}{*}{\textbf{Scheduling}}
		& Delivered on time \\
		& Agreed workflow followed \\ \midrule
		\multirow{2}{*}{\textbf{Scope}}
		& Scope is well-defined \\
		& Requirements completed \\  \midrule
		\multirow{2}{*}{\textbf{Cost Budget}} & Project completed within budget \\
		& Good Return On Investment \\ \midrule
		\multirow{1}{*}{\textbf{Risk}} & Project risk is managed \\ \midrule
		\multirow{2}{*}{\textbf{Resources}} & Human and material resources are available \\
		& Maximum utilisation of available resources \\ \midrule
		\multirow{3}{*}{\textbf{Quality}} & Quality requirements met \\
		& Client satisfaction \\
		& Project is successful \\ \bottomrule
	\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

Specifically for university spacecraft, \citeauthor{swartwoutUniversityClassSpacecraft2023} notes that projects with fixed delivery times often fail to plan and prepare for the design and complexity of their systems and to devote enough resources to systems-level testing, and cites this as a main reason for a high mission failure rate \autocite{swartwoutUniversityClassSpacecraft2023}. Interviews conducted by \citeauthor{campoModelbasedSystemsEngineering2023,honore-livermoreAcademicsPerceptionSystems2022} also note the benefits of \acs{SYE} and \acs{SYE} approaches in improving communication, project understanding and verification capabilities \autocite{campoModelbasedSystemsEngineering2023,honore-livermoreDigitalEngineeringDevelopment2022}.

%\citeauthor{stutzkeEstimatingSoftwareintensiveSystems2005} found a correlation between more time spent on Systems Engineering for \acs{NASA} projects before 1995 and reduced cost overruns \autocite{stutzkeEstimatingSoftwareintensiveSystems2005}.

However, a considerable overhead and a number of inefficiencies are added when following highly detailed or bureaucratic processes. \citeauthor{royceManagingDevelopmentLarge1970} \autocite{royceManagingDevelopmentLarge1970} famously criticised the ``waterfall'' model mainly because of its inflexibility towards design and requirements changes, and incorrect implementations of the lifecycle model. During the 1990s, these so-called ``heavyweight'' methods started receiving more criticism, and being seen as an impediment for development of lower-scale products \autocite{fowlerNewMethodology2005}. 
%\citeauthor{whitneyWhyMechanicalDesign1996} \autocite{whitneyWhyMechanicalDesign1996} draws parallels between the design of a complex mechanical system, and very efficient and modular VLSI design process.

\citeauthor{boehmROISystemsEngineering2008} \autocite{boehmROISystemsEngineering2008}
analysed the 161 software projects from the \acs{COCOMO} II database to identify the ideal \textbf{trade-off between time spent architecting and total time spent} for a project.
They identified a ``\textbf{sweet spot}'' between too little and too much time spent in software architecting and risk management\footnote{Which, for the purposes of this work, loosely corresponds to effort spent for Systems Engineering}.
For example, on a \SI{10}{\kilo\acs{SLOC}} project, any additional time spent for Systems Engineering above 5\% of the total project time leads to an increase to overall project duration.
This is in contrast to a larger \SI{1000}{\kilo\acs{SLOC}} project, where this sweet spot is found at 33\%, implying that larger projects need disproportionately higher effort for Systems Engineering (\Cref{fig:i_software_roi}).
Similarly, \autocite{honourTechnicalReportValue2004} concluded, after a survey of 44 projects, that an optimum level of \acs{SYE} effort is between \qtyrange{15}{20}{\percent}, above which there seem to be no \acs{SYE}-related cost/schedule improvements.

\begin{marginfigure}
	\includegraphics{external/boehm_roi.png}
	\caption[Comparison between time spent in architecting versus time spent for reworks in software projects]{Comparison between time spent in architecting versus time spent for reworks in software projects \cite{boehmROISystemsEngineering2008}}
	\label{fig:i_software_roi}
\end{marginfigure}

The above results show that an adequate balance needs to be found when adopting a \acl{SYE} methodology for an engineering project. \citeauthor{akbarStatisticalAnalysisEffects2018} classified \acs{SYE} methodologies into \emph{heavyweight} ones, which are typically process and tool-oriented, comprehensive and predictive, and \emph{lightweight} ones, which are more empirical, collaborative, and decentralised \autocite{akbarStatisticalAnalysisEffects2018}. Their survey of 20 software-developing organisations matches the expectation that the ``scale'' of a methodology should be adapted to the size of a project to reach the best possible benefits.

\FloatBarrier
\section{Research Gaps}
\label{sec:INTRODUCTION_research_gaps}

The impact and benefit of Systems Engineering is widely understood in the fields of space systems, and more specifically, \textbf{CubeSat engineering}. Apart from individual missions that explore tailor-made \acl{SYE} approaches \cite{cappaertBuildingDeployingOperating2018,chaiebOpenOrbiterCubeSatSystemofsystems2015,dallmannAgileSpaceParadigm2015,lillAgileMissionOperations2018,lubian-arenillasNanosatelliteDevelopmentMethodology2019,segretPavingStonesInitial2014}, research has been made on more generic \acs{SYE} methodologies that can be universally applied to nano-satellite projects \autocite{alanaziEngineeringMethodologyStudentDriven2019,andersonEnterpriseModelingCubeSats2014,deckerSystemsengineeringAssessmentMultiple2016}. These methodologies generally use a foundation based on traditional phase-based lifecycle processes \cite{sheaNASASystemsEngineering2020}, augmented with iterative or incremental elements. Many CubeSat methodologies also implement Agile practices, which represent a philosophy focused on emergent and interpersonal methods \autocite{beckAgileManifesto2001}. Conversely, another set of CubeSat projects does not use a well-defined methodology, instead implementing original ad-hoc approaches applicable to specific missions \autocite{cheongRobustFrameworkLowCost2020}.

The development approaches for current \acf{SCS}, mainly PocketQubes and ChipSats, largely follow the aforementioned approaches for CubeSats, with examples following hybrid \autocite{cappellettiHandsSpaceEducational2020}, Agile-based \autocite{sperettaCubeSatsPocketQubesOpportunities2016} and ad-hoc \autocite{manchesterCentimeterScaleSpacecraftDesign2015,daradimosQUBIK1pPocketQube2024} methodologies.

Indeed, both nano-satellites and smaller classes can be classified as \emph{(very) small spacecraft}, and usually have similar high-level requirements. These requirements revolve both around the function of the devices (to reliably and autonomously operate one or few payloads in \acl{LEO}) and the programmatic constraints (such as launch deadline, limited budget, small team size).\footnote{Non-examples that may not fit some of these criteria include: human spaceflight or massive constellations.} These similarities can also extend over non-spacecraft projects, such as hosted payloads, scientific balloon/sounding rocket missions or \acl{VLEO} projects.\footnote{An example of a sub-orbital initiative is \acs{ESA}'s REXUS/BEXUS programme, where teams are given 8--16 months to develop a scientific payload \cite{REXUSBEXUSSchedule}.}

Based on these similarities and the number of successes of launched \acs{SCS} missions so far, one can claim that these methodologies maintain their validity in terms of project quality and scope. Do they, however, maintain their validity in terms of the rest of \Cref{tab:i_success_factors}'s factors, such as schedule and budget? Or, expressed differently, are these methodologies still \emph{effective} and \emph{optimal} for \acs{SCS} projects?

\citeauthor{heinAttoSatsChipSatsOther2019} posited, for example, that atto-satellites offer characteristics that make them possible to develop (from conceptualisation to flight hardware) within 6 months, enabling major accessibility combined with novel or distributed mission concepts. The authors note how rapid low-cost development can motivate capabilities such as rapid technology testing or missions requiring a quickly-produced spacecraft constellation \autocite{heinAttoSatsChipSatsOther2019}.\footnote{Examples include distributed earth observation, data collection, communication and impactor missions, as well as concepts that involve attachment or transportation via larger spacecraft.}

The specific characteristics of \acs{SCS} that differentiate them from CubeSats to justify the usage of a different methodology are as follows:
\begin{itemize}
	\item \textbf{Size and mass}: The mass of a single pocket-sized spacecraft can be \( 1 /2 \)\textsuperscript{nd} to \( 1/200 \)\textsuperscript{th} of a CubeSat, with the volume following a similar trend. The first implication of this constraint is a significantly \textbf{reduced launch cost}, which can affect \begin{enumerate*}
		\item whether an organisation can launch any spacecraft at all;
		\item how often an organisation can launch spacecraft;
		\item how many spacecraft can be launched in a single time period.
	\end{enumerate*}

	Another implication of a smaller footprint is less available real-estate for subsystems and components. In turn, this means that \begin{enumerate*}
		\item some components have to be further miniaturised, increasing difficulty or complexity;
		\item space for fewer components and therefore reduced functionality compared to larger systems;
		\item more difficulty applying concepts that require space, such as modularity or redundancy.
	\end{enumerate*}

	Finally, the physical constraints related to reduced space can limit the performance of the system and its subsystems. Most notably, a system's energy generation, energy storage and communication capabilities are directly tied to the solar panel area, battery volume and effective antenna areas respectively \autocite{uludagAttoFemtoScale2026}. Other effects include thermal management, attitude control or propulsion.

	Combined with these direct implications, low size and mass can also affect other environmental and technical aspects of \acs{SCS} development, as described below.
	\item \textbf{Facility requirements}: Some of the most expensive, hard-to-maintain and critical equipment for integration and verification of CubeSat projects typically needs to support the sizes of 6U or larger spacecraft. This can include clean rooms, \ac{TVAC} chambers, or shakers for vibration testing. A smaller physical size of a device under manufacturing or test corresponds to reduced requirements for this equipment as well.
	
	\item \textbf{Low mass / area ratio}: Specifically for flat spacecraft, such as ChipSats, one dimension can be much smaller than the other two. This more asymmetric shape increases the average drag coefficient of the system, as well as the relative dissipation area for thermal radiation.
	
	This latter characteristic speeds up thermal equilibrium in the hot and cold cases. Combined with the potentially increased difficulty of thermal control due to size, this characteristic discourages the use of thermally-sensitive payloads. From a \acl{SYE} point of view, it makes the use of conventional batteries difficult, as they cannot be charged or discharged below a specific temperature. \citeauthor{manchesterCentimeterScaleSpacecraftDesign2015} performed a thermal analysis of of the Sprite ChipSats, finding that they can reach temperatures down to \qty{-110}{\degreeCelsius} in eclipse \autocite{manchesterCentimeterScaleSpacecraftDesign2015}. These low temperatures also prevent the operation of most commercial electronics.

	A higher drag coefficient will also lead to a faster decay of the spacecraft, reducing its mission life. \citeauthor{manchesterOrbitalDebrisAssessment2013} calculated that a \qty{325}{\kilo\meter} Sprite launch has an expected lifetime of \qty{6}{days}, significantly shorter than the few years calculated for CubeSats.\footnote{Even though the re-entry time is also a factor of launch altitude, this parameter cannot be usually controlled by customers, and lies in the range between \qtyrange{500}{550}{\kilo\meter} for \acsp{SSO}.}
	\item \textbf{Low technical size}: Intuitively, a smaller number of components and subsystems should lead to a smaller number of functions that can be performed by the system,\footnote{While a non-scalable definition of the minimum required spacecraft functionality exists (e.g. \autocite{ECSS-E-ST-70-11C}), low spacecraft scale allows many of these requirements to be disregarded. For example, some ChipSat missions may not need telecommanding capabilities, which significantly reduces complexity.} as well as fewer interfaces that need to be managed.\footnote{See \Cref{sec:LITERATURE_metrics} for the definition the technical size and complexity metrics.} In turn, the reduced number of physical components, functions and interfaces reduces the amount of effort required in design, development and integration. This renders \acs{SCS} more ``lightweight'' spacecraft compared to their larger counterparts, and also reduces the amount of resources required for these projects, making them potentially ``small-scale''.
	
	At the same time, however, the required miniaturisation of components can increase complexity for specific applications, such as deployment mechanisms and thrusters, because of technical manufacturing and fabrication challenges related to the need for increased precision \autocite{ouDesignPerformanceMicropulsed2024}.
	Creating extremely miniaturised hardware may require components that are not available off-the-shelf, as well as specialised machinery and expertise that may not be available in-house.
	
	\item \textbf{\acs{COTS} availability}: Due to both the small size and the lower popularity of \acs{SCS} compared to CubeSat and larger spacecraft, the number of suitable \acf{COTS} subsystems tailored for use in space is limited, especially when considering high-\acs{TRL} parts. Teams will therefore be more likely to design or develop subsystems in-house. This characteristic can also extend to support equipment, such as FlatSat supports, test jigs, or analysis software.
	
	Similarly, the novelty of PocketQubes, and more-so smaller femto-satellites and atto-satellites, also restricts the amount of standardisation and established conventions in this field. In practical terms, fewer deployer and interface designs may be available as specifications or physical implementations. Especially for ChipSat-scale spacecraft, the different proposed applications have been associated with a multitude of different form factors, making future standardisation potentially harder than on their larger counterparts. However, the overall physical structure of many \acs{SCS} projects can be described in simple terms: either as a stack of \acsp{PCB} supported by a small frame (PocketQubes), or as one or two flat \acsp{PCB} with minimal support (ChipSats).
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure*}
	\includegraphics{graphics/Methodoconcept.pdf}
	\caption[Mapping between novel SCS characteristics and capabilities for a SYE methodology]{Mapping between novel \acs{SCS} characteristics (compared to CubeSats) and capabilities for a \acs{SYE} methodology}
	\label{fig:i_important_mapping}
\end{figure*}
The above characteristics explain differences between CubeSats and \acs{SCS} which inform some of the key decisions taken during \acl{SYE} activities. Taking this argument one step further,\footnote{Roughly following \citeauthor{heinHeritageTechnologiesSpace2016}'s characteristics --- capabilities method \autocite{heinHeritageTechnologiesSpace2016}} they can directly motivate different shifts in established CubeSat development methodologies and approaches as follows:
\begin{itemize}
	\item Once a design has been elaborated, the development, manufacturing, assembly and integration of either a subsystem, or the complete spacecraft, can be performed in a much shorter time. In the extreme case of ChipSats, this production chain involves the procurement of a \acs{PCB} and component assembly, which can last 2 weeks or less. Likewise, the material cost for an entire satellite model can drop down to a few hundred euros (\Cref{chap:CASESTUDIES}). This observation encourages mass iteration cycles on a subsystem or even system level.
	
	\item An already constrained architecture might prevent the implementation of concepts and design approaches that would further constrain it, such as Separation of Concerns, modularity, or reusability.

	\item There is a natural tendency to shift both manufacturing and verification in-house, due to the reduction in the requirements for expertise and tooling.
	Instead of a using cleanroom requirement, teams can also comfortably work on a flow bench. If thermal chambers, electrodynamic shakers, Helmholtz cages or other testing equipment is needed, its scale can be reduced. This makes it possible for developers to own and operate manufacturing and testing equipment very close to the
	normal working location, limiting costs, time spent, administrative burden, and facilitating very short manufacturing runs and test campaigns.
	
	\item While \acs{SCS} projects still retain mission-criticality, since irreparable failures can propagate and lead to a loss of mission, the consequences of failure are usually less severe in terms of lost value.
	Additionally, shorter mission durations indicate that accumulating effects (such as those related to radiation or aging) will be limited.\footnote{Note, however, that most CubeSat mission failures happen at the beginning of a mission, as a result of ``infant mortality'' \autocite{langerReliabilityEstimationTool2017}} As a result, developers need to take fewer derating and technical risk mitigation measures, such as the use of radiation-tolerant components or redundancy. Administrative measures for technical risk reduction (such as \acs{FMEA}) can also be performed with reduced fidelity.
	
	\item While CubeSat projects have a fixed set of launch requirements, originating from the launcher, the deployer and the CubeSat specification, non-standardised projects may require the implementation of proprietary interfaces, potentially specific to each company that can offer a flight slot for \acs{SCS} spacecraft. In extreme cases, these interface specifications may not have been devised at all. Then, \acs{SCS} developers will have to collaborate with their provider to agree on shared interfaces, or design their own\footnote{This was, for example, the approach taken by Manchester, who designed a 3U CubeSat capable of hosting and deploying multiple ChipSats \cite{manchesterCentimeterScaleSpacecraftDesign2015}.}. These uncertainties increase the possibility of external requirements changing during a mission.
	
	\item The reduced launch costs, sizes, and number of components, combined with a trend towards swarm-based or distributed \acs{SCS} concepts, greatly favour the development and launch of multiple similar spacecraft together, sometimes in the form of constellations. This approach adds a form of ``operational reliability'', since a launch of multiple spacecraft can be considered as a redundancy strategy.
	
	\item The constrained power generation and storage capabilities of \acs{SCS}, combined with more limiting earth communication capabilities\footnote{Influenced not only by the available energy, but also by antenna size.}, enforce different approaches for \acf{CONOPS}. While data transmission is expensive, Moore's law has successfully predicted significant processing capabilities with reduced power consumption and real estate \autocite{mackFiftyYearsMoores2011}, encouraging the processing of data ``on-the-edge''\footnote{that is, on the spacecraft instead of the ground station} \autocite{moreiraEdgeComputingSpace2025}.
\end{itemize}

The above opportunities and possible decisions demostrate that a distinct, tailored methodology for \acs{SCS} can be followed. What would be, however, the potential effects of such a methodology? In \Cref{tab:i_success_factors} I listed the ``success factors'' of Systems Engineering for a project, which include schedule, cost, risk, scope, resources and quality. For a proposed methodology to be considered useful, it must therefore:
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-1ex]
	\item Demonstrate \textbf{internal and external validity} \autocite[34]{yinCaseStudyResearch2014}, showing that it can indeed be used to bring an \acs{SCS} project from conceptualisation to qualification/launch.
	\item Demonstrate that its effects on one or more of the aforementioned success criteria are \textbf{significant} compared to other environmental, programmatic or technical factors.
	\item Demonstrate that it has \textbf{improved performance} \autocite{pedersenVALIDATINGDESIGNMETHODS} compared to current State-of-the-Art approaches for pocket-sized spacecraft, in terms of one or more of these factors.
\end{enumerate}



\FloatBarrier

\section{Research Questions and Thesis Objectives}
\label{sec:INTRODUCTION_research_questions}

\begin{figure}[p]
	\includegraphics{graphics/decomp.pdf}
	\caption[ARC diagram for ``Systems Engineering for sub-CubeSat Spacecraft'']{\acf{ARC} diagram for ``Systems Engineering for sub-CubeSat Spacecraft''}
	\label{fig:i_thesis_decomposition}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[p]
	\includegraphics{graphics/DecompSYE.pdf}
	\caption{Focus on ``Systems Engineering'' from \Cref{fig:i_thesis_decomposition}}
	\label{fig:i_sye_decomposition}
\end{figure}

In order to establish the area of interest for this thesis, I will use the \acf{ARC} diagram concept of \citeauthor{blessingDRMDesignResearch2009} \autocite{blessingDRMDesignResearch2009}. \Cref{fig:i_thesis_decomposition} shows the decomposition of this work into the areas that are related to Systems Engineering for \acs{SCS}, and the areas where my contribution will be. Due to the broad scope of ``Systems Engineering'' as a topic, I present a further partition of it into 13 areas (\Cref{fig:i_sye_decomposition}). This partition is based on the original decomposition by \acs{NASA} \autocite{sheaNASASystemsEngineering2020}, shown in \Cref{fig:i_nasa_sye_decomposition}, and tailored for \acl{SCS}.

\begin{figure}[h]
	\includegraphics{graphics/UoA1.pdf}
	\caption[Systems Engineering for SCS: Unit of Analysis]{Systems Engineering for \acs{SCS}: Unit of Analysis}
	\label{fig:i_uoa}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[h]
	\includegraphics{graphics/UoA2.pdf}
	\caption[Systems Engineering for SCS: Unit of Analysis (including neighbouring areas)]{Systems Engineering for \acs{SCS}: Unit of Analysis (including neighbouring areas).}
	\label{fig:i_uoa_all}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure*}[h]
	\includegraphics{graphics/MindMap.pdf}
	\caption[Mind-map between the methods and goals of the SCS systems engineering methodology][-2cm]{Mind-map between the methods and goals of the \acs{SCS} systems engineering methodology}
\end{figure*}

From the perspective of \acs{SCS} projects and \acs{SCS} developers, the scope of this thesis is shown in \Cref{fig:i_uoa,fig:i_uoa_all}.

\begin{figure*}[h]
	\includegraphics{graphics/Research.Questions.pdf}
	\caption{Mapping of Research Questions to Research Objectives}
	\label{fig:i_research_questions}
\end{figure*}

The main objective of this thesis is to develop a new Systems Engineering methodology, tailored for \acl{SCS}, that covers the technical management, system description and system realisation aspects of these projects. The goal of this methodology is to offer an improvement over current approaches, with a focus on pico-satellite, femto-satellite and atto-satellite developers in academia and industry. The \textbf{main hypothesis} is therefore:
\begin{quote}
	Novel systems engineering approaches can drastically decrease the development duration of miniaturised, sub-CubeSat-size spacecraft.
\end{quote}


In order to develop a sound and valid methodology, I will address the following research questions:

\begin{fullwidth}
\begin{tikzpicture}
		\node [rectangle,fill=MaterialAmber100,draw=MaterialAmber800,minimum height=1.5cm,text width=\linewidth,align=left,inner sep=1.5em]
		at (0,0)
		{
Research Question 1 (RQ1):
\textbf{What are the current \ac{SYE} approaches used by \ac{SCS} developers?}\\[2ex]

Research Question 2 (RQ2):
\textbf{What are the shortcomings of current \ac{SYE} approaches used by \ac{SCS} developers?}\\[2ex]

Research Question 3 (RQ3):
\textbf{Can a new, tailored \ac{SYE} methodology address a selection of the shortcomings identified in \texttt{RQ2} for \ac{SCS}?}\\[2ex]

Research Question 4 (RQ4):
\textbf{Is the \ac{SYE} methodology developed in \texttt{RQ3} effective in practical scenarios?}\\[2ex]
		};
\end{tikzpicture}%
\end{fullwidth}


The objectives of this thesis are therefore as follows:
\begin{enumerate}
	\item Investigate the current \ac{SYE} approaches used by \ac{SCS} developers and gather data about the environment of typical \ac{SCS} missions.
	\item Identify quantifiable parameters that can describe shortcomings in current \ac{SYE} approaches.
	\item Synthesise a set of methods for technical management and system realisation that describe a complete \ac{SYE} methodology for \ac{SCS}.
	\item Validate the new methodology on at least one space mission that will succeed in environmental qualification.
\end{enumerate}

\FloatBarrier
\section{Thesis Structure}
\label{sec:INTRODUCTION_structure}

The chapters of this thesis are organised as follows:

\Cref{chap:INTRODUCTION} (\nameref{chap:INTRODUCTION}) provided a brief introduction into the research topic and its alignment within space and systems engineering.

\Cref{chap:SURVEY} (\nameref{chap:SURVEY}) presents the terminology and definitions that will be used in the rest of this work for methodological and technical aspects. It also introduces the topics and results from the literature survey and initial data gathering that are used in \Cref{chap:METHODOLOGY,chap:CASESTUDIES}.

\Cref{chap:RESEARCH_APPROACH} (\nameref{chap:RESEARCH_APPROACH}) introduces the \acs{DRM} Design Research Methodology, and how it is adapted as a research approach for the rest of this work.

\Cref{chap:METHODOLOGY} (\nameref{chap:METHODOLOGY}) presents the justification, conceptualisation and elaboration of the proposed methodology that supports \acs{SCS} design and development, which is separated into three overarching areas: technical management, hardware, and software.

\Cref{chap:CASESTUDIES} (\nameref{chap:CASESTUDIES}) presents the development of four space systems where I participated as a systems engineer. These products serve as case studies for the generation of data and experimental evaluation of the methodology presented in \Cref{chap:METHODOLOGY}.

\Cref{chap:DISCUSSION} (\nameref{chap:DISCUSSION}) analyses the results of \Cref*{chap:CASESTUDIES}'s case studies as part of a cross-case counter-factual analysis, in order to evaluate the validity of the proposed methodology. Additionally, answers to the Research Questions are given, and the limitations of the study are examined.

\Cref{chap:CONCLUSION} (\nameref{chap:CONCLUSION}) summarises the contributions of this work as well as the evaluation of the proposed methodology in combination with the case studies, and proposes potential directions for future research.

\begin{figure*}[h]
	\includegraphics{graphics/Research.Structure.pdf}
	\caption{Thesis Chapter Structure}
	\label{fig:i_thesis_structure}
\end{figure*}

A mapping between each section and the objectives of the thesis is shown in \Cref{fig:i_thesis_structure}. The data gathering, synthesis and validation process is explained in higher detail in \Cref{chap:RESEARCH_APPROACH}, and is based on the \acs{DRM} Design Research Methodology by \citeauthor{blessingDRMDesignResearch2009} \autocite{blessingDRMDesignResearch2009}.


\chapter{Literature Survey}
\label{chap:SURVEY}

\begin{fullwidth}
\epigraph{\emph{%
		``Agile'' doesn't mean "last-minute".}}{Alexander F.\@ B\"uhler}
\end{fullwidth}

This chapter will introduce the background information necessary to develop the \ac{SCS} methodology.
Parts of it serve as the DS-I phase of the \acs{DRM} research methodology\footnote{as seen in \Cref{chap:RESEARCH_APPROACH}}. \Cref{sec:LITERATURE_terminology} introduces the definitions of common terms used in the thesis.\footnote{A full list of definitions can be found in \Cpageref{sec:definitions}. A full list of acronyms can be found in \Cpageref{sec:acronyms}.} \Cref{sec:LITERATURE_cubesat} introduces the CubeSat ecosystem, and relevant information that can be applied or tailored for \acs{SCS}. \Cref{sec:LITERATURE_subcubesat} introduces the background, environment and current state-of-the-art for Sub-CubeSat Systems Engineering. Finally, \Cref{sec:LITERATURE_systems_engineering} presents more generic Systems Engineering topics that are not strictly connected with space systems, but can inform the proposed \acs{SYE} methodology.

\section{Terminology}
\label{sec:LITERATURE_terminology}

\subsection{Spacecraft Classes}
\label{sec:LITERATURE_classes}

%The idea of a \textbf{Sub-CubeSat Spacecraft} (\acs{SCS}) depends on the definition of a CubeSat

The CubeSat Design Specification \autocite{CDS14} defines a CubeSat as a class of satellite composed of 1 to 12 cube units with a size of \SI{10}{\centi\meter}  (``U''). As such, I define \textbf{\acf{SCS}} to include all spacecraft classes smaller than \SI{10}{\centi\meter} on all dimensions. Depending on launch requirements, this definition will usually restrict an \acs{SCS} mass to less than \SI{2}{\kg}, the maximum mass for a 1U CubeSat \cite{CDS14}.

Various \acl{SCS} classes have been defined in the literature. Most prominently, \textbf{PocketQubes} are standardised in the PocketQube Standard \autocite{raduDelfiPQFirstPocketqube2018} as combinations of \SI{5}{\centi\meter} cube units (``p''). Smaller sizes are represented by classes such as \textbf{\acs{PCB}sats}\footnote{Also referred to as ``\textbf{PlanarSats}'', not to be confused with \emph{FlatSat}, a type of partition of a satellite system.} \autocite{barnhartLowcostFemtosatelliteEnable2009}, which are typically flat in one direction, and \textbf{SpaceChips} or satellites-on-a-chip \autocite{barnhartSatelliteonaChipFeasibilityStudy2006}, which would be complete spacecraft implemented on an \acf{IC} smaller than \qtyproduct{2 x 2}{\centi\meter}. The notion of a \textbf{ChipSat} can define a flat-sized spacecraft with an envelope smaller than \qtyproduct{5 x 5}{\centi\meter} \autocite{manchesterCentimeterScaleSpacecraftDesign2015}.

``Fractional-unit CubeSats'' have been also developed and launched (e.g.~1/2U, 1/3U, 1/4U) \autocite{kuluNanosatsDatabase2023}. While these conform to many standard CubeSat requirements and are designed to be hosted in CubeSat containers, they are not part of the official CubeSat Design Specification \cite{CDS14}, and due to their size, are considered \acl{SCS} and therefore within the scope of this work.

\begin{margintable}
	\caption[Spacecraft mass classes as defined in this work]{Spacecraft mass classes as defined in this work \autocite{sweetingModernSmallSatellitesChanging2018,heinAttoSatsChipSatsOther2019}}
	\label{tab:satellite_sizes}

	\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
		\toprule
		Class & Mass \\ \midrule
		large satellite & \( > \) \qtyrange{1000}{}{\kilo\gram} \\
		small satellite & \qtyrange{500}{1000}{\kg} \\
		mini-satellite & \qtyrange{100}{500}{\kg} \\
		micro-satellite & \qtyrange{10}{100}{\kg} \\
		nano-satellite & \qtyrange{1}{10}{\kg} \\
		pico-satellite & \qtyrange{100}{1000}{\gram} \\
		femto-satellite & \qtyrange{10}{100}{\gram} \\
		atto-satellite & \(<\) \qtyrange{}{10}{\gram} \\ \bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
\end{margintable}

Apart from the size-based definitions, spacecraft can also be classified based on their mass through the use of metric-like prefixes.
Note that the widely accepted classification, perhaps misleadingly, does not follow the \acs{SI} definition of unit prefixes \cite{InternationalSystemUnits2019}. The convention used in this paper is shown in \Cref{tab:satellite_sizes}.

\begin{definition}[Sub-CubeSat Spacecraft]
Based on \Cref{tab:satellite_sizes}, the classification of \acf{SCS} roughly corresponds to the classes of pico-, femto-, and atto-satellites.
To reduce verbosity, I also introduce the notions of \textbf{pocket-sized spacecraft} and \textbf{centimetre-scale spacecraft} to be the equivalent of \ac{SCS}.
\end{definition}


\subsection{Method and Methodology}

As the primary objective of this work is to synthesise a \emph{methodology} for Sub-CubeSat spacecraft, it is important to define the scope of a design \emph{methodology} compared to a \emph{method}, \emph{process} or \emph{approach}.

\citeauthor{gerickeWhatWeNeed2017}'s works \autocite{gerickeWhatWeNeed2017,gerickeElementsDesignMethod2022} investigate the differences between different definitions of the above terms. I adopt the following definitions:

\vspace*{2ex} %vadOK
\begin{definition}[Design Method]
	A method is specification of how a specified result is to be achieved. This may include specifications of how information is to be shown, what information is to be used as inputs to the method, what tools are to be used, what actions are to be performed and how, and how the task should be decomposed and how actions should be sequenced. \cite{gerickeWhatWeNeed2017}
\end{definition}

A complete description of a design method must include its core idea, its representation of the information the method produces, a procedure (set of actions or activities to be followed), and its intended use \autocite{gerickeElementsDesignMethod2022}. As tools are given specific attention in my research, I further reproduce their definition here:
\begin{definition}[Tool]
	A tool is an object or artefact that is used to perform some action [...]. For design methods, [tools are] documents or software that direct and structure designers' thoughts and actions, or facilitate constructing representations, or perform computations. \cite{gerickeElementsDesignMethod2022}
\end{definition}

The term \emph{methodology} itself lacks a universally agreed-upon definition, often being used interchangeably with ``method''. For this work, I will adopt part of the definition provided by \autocite{pahlIntroduction2007}:

\begin{definition}[Design Methodology]
	A design methodology is a concrete course of action for the design of technical systems that derives its knowledge from design science and cognitive psychology, and from practical experience in different domains. It includes plans of action that link working steps and design phases according to content and organisation. These plans must be adapted in a flexible manner to the specific task at hand. It also includes strategies, rules and principles to achieve general and specific goals as well as methods to solve individual design problems or partial tasks.
\end{definition}

In the general sense, a methodology can be then seen as a collection of methods used for the design and development of a system.

\begin{figure}[h]
	\includegraphics{graphics/Methodology.pdf}
	\caption[Representation of a Design Methodology]{Representation of a Design Methodology, based on \autocite{gerickeWhatWeNeed2017,gerickeElementsDesignMethod2022}}
\end{figure}


It is also important to separate my proposed \emph{Design Methodology}, presented in \Cref{chap:METHODOLOGY} ``\nameref{chap:METHODOLOGY}'', from the \emph{Research Methodology} used to synthesise and validate the former, presented in \Cref{chap:RESEARCH_APPROACH} ``\nameref{chap:RESEARCH_APPROACH}''.

A new Design Methodology is often the result of Design Research. \citeauthor{blessingDRMDesignResearch2009} \autocite{blessingDRMDesignResearch2009} define Design as follows:
\begin{definition}[Design]
	Design refers to those activities that actually generate and develop a product from a need, product idea or technology to the full documentation needed to realise the product and to fulfil the perceived needs of the user and other stakeholders. \cite{blessingDRMDesignResearch2009}
	\label{def:design}
\end{definition}

\citeauthor{blessingDRMDesignResearch2009} \cite{blessingDRMDesignResearch2009} also define the relevant concept of Design Research:
\begin{definition}[Design Research]
	Design research has two objectives:
	\begin{enumerate}
		\item The formulation and validation of models and theories about the phenomenon of design with all its facets [\textellipsis]; and 
		\item The development and validation of support founded on these models and theories, in order to improve design practice, including education, and its outcomes. \cite{blessingDRMDesignResearch2009}
	\end{enumerate}
	\label{def:design_research}
\end{definition}

On the other hand, a \emph{Research Methodology} or \emph{Research Approach} is understood within the context of \emph{Research Design}, which can be defined as:
\begin{definition}[Research Design]
	The logical sequence that connects the empirical data to a study's initial research questions and, ultimately, to its conclusions. \cite[20]{yinCaseStudyResearch2014}
\end{definition}


\subsection{Systems Engineering}

The \acf{SEBOK} defines Systems Engineering as the:
% https://sebokwiki.org/wiki/Systems_Engineering_(glossary)
\begin{definition}[Systems Engineering]
	Interdisciplinary approach governing the total technical and managerial effort required to transform a set of customer needs, expectations, and constraints into a solution and to support that solution throughout its life. \cite{sebok_book_213}
\end{definition}

Systems Engineering is also inherently connected with Project Management, which itself is however a separate discipline defined as:
\begin{definition}[Project Management]
	%https://sebokwiki.org/wiki/Project_Management_(glossary)
	The application of knowledge, skills, tools, and techniques to project activities to meet the project requirements. \cite{projectmanagementinstituteGuideProjectManagement2021}
\end{definition}

Both concepts can be decomposed into a set of more specific activities, which sometimes overlap,
as shown in \Cref{fig:i_nasa_sye_decomposition}. 

\FloatBarrier
\section{CubeSat Ecosystem}
\label{sec:LITERATURE_cubesat}

As CubeSats present similarities with \acl{SCS}\footnote{This is true for nano-satellites in general (and not just CubeSats). However, since the overwhelming majority of nano-satellites follows the CubeSat form factor \cite{kuluNanosatsDatabase2023}, and most nano-satellite research is performed on CubeSats, I will use the concepts here interchangeably.}, and because the former's development style has been typically adopted by \acs{SCS} (\Cref{sec:INTRODUCTION_research_gaps}), they serve as a valuable data source before analysing their smaller counterparts.

%A \citeyear{bouwmeesterSurveyWorldwidePico2010} survey of nanosatellite technology
%...augmented by \citeyear{villelaThousandthCubeSatStatistical2019}
%Actual information about cubesat environment: \autocite{berthoudHowSetCubeSat2016}



\subsection{CubeSat Bus}

\begin{figure}
	\includegraphics{external/cubesat_iss.jpg}
	\caption[A 6U CubeSat being deployed from the ISS]{A 6U CubeSat being deployed from the \acs{ISS}. Image credit: NASA \cite{johnsonCubeSatEjectedEarth2024}}
\end{figure}

From a technical point of view, the shared requirements of CubeSats are defined by the CubeSat Design Specification \autocite{CDS14}. The main constraint for CubeSat design is the dimensional envelope defined by the specification, which dictates the mechanical interfaces of the spacecraft. With regards to the internals, CubeSats are typically separated mechanically, electrically and functionally into subsystems, with an example partition shown in \Cref{tab:l_subsystem_partition}.

While no universally accepted formal standard exists for CubeSat subsystems and internal components, most CubeSats are based on a PC/104-style stack of subsystem boards \autocite{choCubeSatElectricalInterface2020}. This approach has enabled a limited interoperability between commercially available subsystems. Developers therefore often mix-and-match in-house and \acs{COTS} subsystems.\footnote{\acs{COTS} subsystems from different manufacturers can also be combined, but interoperability is not guaranteed.} The LibreCube specification \autocite{BoardSpecificationLibreCube} defines a reference architecture, standard board design and an electrical specification for such PC/104 subsystems. 

\begin{marginfigure}
	\includegraphics{external/board_stacking.png}
	\caption[CubeSat board stacking]{CubeSat board stacking. Taken from \cite{BoardSpecificationLibreCube}}
\end{marginfigure}

Nevertheless, this theoretically modular design often experiences compatibility or compliance issues, as subsystem interfaces and dimensions can be incompatible. Developers tend to face administrative difficulties and delays, because \acs{COTS} subsystem specifications are not publicly available and are subject to change after new revisions. This is further exacerbated by an overall shift of the CubeSat industry focus towards entire CubeSat platforms, instead of individual subsystems.\footnote{Based on personal experience. Compare also, for example, past with current versions of \cite{CubesatNanosatProducts2020}.}

\begin{table}[h]
\begin{threeparttable}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ccc@{}}
\toprule

Cross-cutting Disciplines & Systems Design &    \\
Payload & Payload & Payload   \\
Propulsion & Propulsion & ---\tnote{1} \\

\acs{OBDH} or \acs{CDH} & \acs{OBDH} & \acs{OBC}   \\

\end{tabular}

	\item[1] As of 2025, no propulsion systems for PocketQubes have been demonstrated in orbit \cite{franzesePocketQubePicosatellitesSurvey2025}.
	\item[2] No documented use of an active \acs{TCS} system for PocketQubes exists \cite{franzesePocketQubePicosatellitesSurvey2025}.
\end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}
\caption{Example partitions of large spacecraft, CubeSats, and PocketQubes into subsystems}
\label{tab:l_subsystem_partition}
\end{table}
\acempty{TTC}

CubeSat payloads, which normally define platform requirements, can serve technology demonstration, imaging, science, communications, or military missions \autocite{berthoudHowSetCubeSat2016}. Most CubeSats are launched in \ac{LEO}, therefore showing significant similarities regarding the basic principles of operation for every subsystem.


Apart from the structure\footnote{According to \cite{CDS14}, a CubeSat structure should be based on aluminium. Alternative materials such as 3D-printed plastics \cite{grimMakerSatCubeSatDesigned2016} or wood \cite{guentherWoodWoodBasedMaterials2024} are being explored.}, a simple CubeSat architecture can involve mainly electrical components \autocite{equisat}. Power generation is made through solar-panels\footnote{CubeSat dimensions limit power generation to an average of \qtyrange{0.5}{4}{\watt/unit}, depending on design, whether the panels are deployable, and attitude control \cite{SMAD}.}, while power storage is predominantly based on secondary (rechargeable) batteries. Developers usually set up their own ground station, communicating with CubeSat on \acs{UHF}, S-Band, or higher frequencies. A large number of nano-satellites also make use of the radio-amateur satellite service for communications \autocite{nasacubesatlaunchinitiativeCubeSat101Basic2017}. In terms of data handling, most architectures are based on \acs{COTS} microcontrollers and basic communication protocols.

When needed, CubeSats include an \acl{ADCS}, which can position the satellite passively or actively, typically through magnetorquers and reaction wheels \autocite{bouwmeesterSurveyWorldwidePico2010}. When orbit changes are required\footnote{For example, to increase mission duration, or to enforce collision/disposal regulations}, CubeSats can also integrate propulsion systems \autocite{CDS14}. Finally, buses can be supported by active thermal control for components sensitive to temperature, and include mechanisms or deployable structures, which can be solar cells, antennas, sensors, or solar sails \autocite{cappellettiCubeSatHandbookMission2021}.

\paragraph{CubeSat Reliability}
\label{sec:LITERATURE_cubesat_reliability}

%\newthought{Extensive research has been performed} on CubeSat reliability.
Extensive research has been performed on CubeSat reliability and mission success rates, with a focus on high infant mortality experienced by these missions, especially when compared to larger spacecraft.

A 2018 database of 855 launched CubeSat reveals a 61\% success rate, with a 21\% rate of CubeSat failure, and a 14\% launch failure rate \autocite{villelaThousandthCubeSatStatistical2019}. %The authors also noted a general decrease in CubeSat infant mortality over time, from 2002 until 2018.
\citeauthor{langerReliabilityAssessmentReliability2018} has composed a CubeSat Failure Database of 70 CubeSat failures based on publically available data and privately provided information, finding that 77\% of CubeSat failures occur in the first month after deployment \autocite{langerReliabilityAssessmentReliability2018}. \citeauthor{swartwoutCubeSatMissionSuccess2019} has also performed a CubeSat reliability analysis, showing a 43\% failure rate across 347 missions until 2018 \autocite{swartwoutCubeSatMissionSuccess2019}.
\citeauthor{swartwoutCubeSatMissionSuccess2019} further classifies the failures by their criticality\footnote{i.e.\@ partial or complete} and by the developer type\footnote{``hobbyists'' vs.\@ ``smallsat developers'', see \Cref{sec:LITERATURE_cubesat_environment}}, shown in \Cref{fig:l_cubesat_swartwout_2019_failures}.


\begin{figure*}[h]
	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{analyses/mission_outcomes_by_developer_2018}
	\caption[CubeSat failure rates by developer type and criticality][-0.5cm]{CubeSat failure rates by developer type and criticality (data up to 2018) \cite{swartwoutCubeSatMissionSuccess2019}}
	\setfloatalignment{t}
	\label{fig:l_cubesat_swartwout_2019_failures}
\end{figure*}

The cause of CubeSat failures and their high infant mortality is not always known. CubeSats that are ``\acl{DOA}'' provide no information on their status\footnote{Although a \acs{DOA} status usually indicates an issue in power or communications.}, while the usual lack of redundant and critical-\acs{TM} transmitters means that even later failure causes are not always observable. Nevertheless, some research has also been done to investigate failure causes and mitigation measures.

\citeauthor{langerReliabilityAssessmentReliability2018} has classified space mission failure causes into \textbf{design errors\footnote{By definition, design errors also include software bugs.}, manufacturing errors, part failures} and \textbf{operational errors}.
%Most CubeSat failures are caused by design errors \autocite{langerReliabilityAssessmentReliability2018}.
Based on developer knowledge, \citeauthor{langerReliabilityAssessmentReliability2018} has identified the \acs{EPS}, \acs{OBC} and \acs{COMMS} subsystems as the most common contributors to CubeSat failures \cite{langerReliabilityAssessmentReliability2018}. The author provides deeper detail in a \citeyear{langerReliabilityCubeSatsStatistical2016} survey of 114 participants, where the expected fault cause is further analysed: \textbf{Design or manufacturing faults in electronics and software design errors are more frequently believed to be failure causes}, compared to degradation, thermal balance, radiation or loss of structural integrity \autocite{langerReliabilityCubeSatsStatistical2016}.
\marginnote{Interestingly, Langer also noted that developers overestimate the chances of success of their own mission compared to the average success rate, indicating a possible lack of self-perception that leads to less effort dedicated to risk reduction \cite{langerReliabilityAssessmentReliability2018}.}
Finally, \citeauthor{swartwoutCubeSatMissionSuccess2019}'s data also indicate design errors as causes, with the main sources being software bugs, mechanism binding, inadequate power budgets, and non-robust startup sequences \cite{swartwoutCubeSatMissionSuccess2019}. \citeauthor{venturiniImprovingMissionSuccess2017}'s similar survey of 27 ``anomalies'' similarly noted communications, power, deployables and software as common failure loci, with developers noting that many failures could have been avoided with more end-to-end functional testing \autocite{venturiniImprovingMissionSuccess2017}.
\marginnote{\citeauthor{venturiniImprovingMissionSuccess2017} also note mismatches between commercial part datasheets and actual behaviour as another common cause of errors, and recommend risk assessment to be made at the beginning of a mission \cite{venturiniImprovingMissionSuccess2017}.}

Motivated by the high prevalence of electrical faults in CubeSats, \citeauthor{bouwmeesterSurveyImplementationReliability2017} also focused on electrical bus interfaces, being able to identify the \acs{I2C} data bus as a cause for ``catastrophic satellite failures'' and ``bus lockups'', noting that despite its known shortcomings, \acs{I2C} is implemented on 75\% of CubeSats in their \(N=92\) dataset. Their questionnaire results indicated that 60\% of the \acs{I2C}-using satellites in orbit experienced an issue the protocol, compared to 5\% and 6\% for the \emph{RS-232} and \emph{SPI} protocols respectively \autocite{bouwmeesterSurveyImplementationReliability2017}.
The frequent use of an ``unreliable'' protocol amongst missions demonstrates a need for better awareness of mission ``lessons learned'', but it also indicates the most popular protocols used by \acs{COTS} subsystems.
The authors also performed a similar analysis for the \acs{EPS} subsystem, albeit with 71\% of CubeSats not experiencing any power-related issues. 
%((0.71 × 56) + (0.81 × 36))/(36 + 56) ≈ 0.749 130 434 8

\citeauthor{swartwoutCubeSatMissionSuccess2019} speculates on the underlying causes of design and manufacturing errors, noting that they are higher for ``hobbyist'' developers. He more specifically cites ``ad hoc procedures for design, integration and test'' and a ``lack of time spent on integration \& test'' as the main motivators behind design errors \cite{swartwoutCubeSatMissionSuccess2019}. 

Despite the high general failure rates, CubeSat mission success seems to be following an upwards trend \cite{villelaThousandthCubeSatStatistical2019,swartwoutCubeSatMissionSuccess2019}\footnote{\citeauthor{swartwoutUniversityClassSpacecraft2023}, however, in a \citeyear{swartwoutUniversityClassSpacecraft2023} article, still notes a downwards trend for university-class missions \cite{swartwoutUniversityClassSpacecraft2023}.}. This is probably influenced by the increased understanding of the reasons for failure, an increased availability of past mission documentation, lessons learned and recommendations for developers, as well as the various CubeSat-focused educational opportunities provided by universities and space agencies \autocite{swartwoutUniversityClassSpacecraft2023}.
\citeauthor{villelaThousandthCubeSatStatistical2019} justify the increase of CubeSat reliability through the use of products with flight heritage, and the availability of CubeSat international standards\footnote{As an aside, I note that the application of standards can work as a double-edge sword; even though a standard can describe a universally-acceptable a priori correct solution, it can add considerable overhead to teams that need to implement it. See, for example, \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_software_features} for a discussion of software features that CubeSats need to implement based on \acs{ECSS} standards. Even narrowing down to the CubeSat ecosystem only, the differences in requirements between projects might be too large to define a one-size-fits-all solution. This implementation overhead is further exacerbated when the documents are not freely available, e.g.\@ with \acs{ISO} standards. Nevertheless, simple standards, or ready-to-use software/hardware implementation can still present enormous value to the CubeSat community.} \cite{villelaThousandthCubeSatStatistical2019}.

Some of the most common \emph{lessons learned} or recommendations for CubeSat developers to increase reliability include\footnote{Note that due to the academic nature of the published data, these tips are mostly skewed towards usefulness for academic missions, rather than industrial ones.}:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-1ex]
	\item Adequate time dedicated to \textbf{system-level testing}, with a baseline of 30--50\% of the overall project schedule to be dedicated to integration and verification
	\cite{swartwoutUniversityClassSpacecraft2023,venturiniImprovingMissionSuccess2017,langerReliabilityAssessmentReliability2018}\footnote{\citeauthor{venturiniImprovingMissionSuccess2017} further provides a list of system-level tests that are recommended to be performed for CubeSats: a command execution test, a day-in-the-life test, an end-to-end communications test, and a complete power system charge cycle \cite{venturiniImprovingMissionSuccess2017}.}.
	\item Early evaluation of \textbf{project risk} and \textbf{potentially problematic components} \cite{venturiniImprovingMissionSuccess2017,sanchoLessonsLearnedFYS2021}.\footnote{This might involve, for example, performing a system-level \acs{FMEA} analysis in the very early design phases of a project.}
	\item Dedicating appropriate attention to \textbf{firmware and software development} \cite{venturiniImprovingMissionSuccess2017,sanchoLessonsLearnedFYS2021}.
	\item Especially for university projects, management of \textbf{developer turnover and knowledge transfer} \cite{venturiniImprovingMissionSuccess2017,sanchoLessonsLearnedFYS2021,swartwoutUniversityClassSpacecraft2023}.
\end{itemize}


\subsection{CubeSat Development Environment}
\label{sec:LITERATURE_cubesat_environment}

\citeauthor{swartwoutYouSayPicosat2018} has classified CubeSat builders by their \textbf{organisation type} into University, Government, Commercial, and Military developers.
He noted that certain similar patterns within these organisations can further classify them into four further categories\footnote{\citeauthor{swartwoutYouSayPicosat2018} performed a manual evaluation of CubeSat launch databases and private data to measure the number of each developer category, shown in \Cref{tab:l_cubesat_mission_types}.}: \cite{swartwoutYouSayPicosat2018}
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item \textbf{Hobbyists}, who approach missions as a side activity and lack standard practices;
	\item \textbf{Traditional Contractors}, who have an established history and set of practices;
	\item \textbf{SmallSat Developers}, who fall between the two previous categories; and
	\item \textbf{Constellation Developers}.
\end{itemize}
