Traditional & \smallbarplot{32}{150} & 32 \\
SmallSat & \smallbarplot{125}{150} & 125 \\
Constellation & \smallbarplot{147}{150} & 147
\\ \bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{margintable}
This classification has an impact on the environmental parameters of CubeSat missions, such as the available resources, developer experience, and degree of risk acceptance, and can inform the desired scope of this work. \Cref{tab:l_cubesat_mission_types} shows that all developer types cover a roughly equal amount of the development spectrum, with the exception of traditional contractors, who represent only 7\% of CubeSat missions flown.

\citeauthor{berthoudHowSetCubeSat2016} performed a survey of 40 CubeSat groups (37 from universities) to generate numerical data about CubeSat environments \autocite{berthoudHowSetCubeSat2016}, which are summarised in \Cref{tab:l_cubesat_team_characteristics}. Various CubeSat projects in the design, manufacturing or operations stages have also published extracts from their Project Management plans, providing information about the main drivers behind costs, phases, development effort, and their critical path.

\begin{table*}
	\caption[CubeSat team characteristics][3ex]{CubeSat team characteristics (\(N = 40\)) \cite{berthoudHowSetCubeSat2016}}
	\label{tab:l_cubesat_team_characteristics}
	\def\arraystretch{1.1}
	\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}lX@{}}
		\toprule
		Prior experience &
		50\% without prior launches, 7.5\% launched balloon or equivalent, 22.5\% have launched 1 CubeSat already, 20\% have launched more than 1 \\
		Programme duration &
		12.5\% one year or less, 37.5\% between 2--4 years,
		50\% more than 4 years\\
		Estimated project cost & 39\% less than \qty{50000}{EUR}, 48\% between \qty{50000}{EUR} and \qty{500000}{EUR}, 13\% more than \qty{500000}{EUR} \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabularx}
\end{table*}



In terms of cost, results can vary significantly, depending on the environment and objectives of the project.
\citeauthor{berthoudHowSetCubeSat2016}'s data display a wide range of estimated project costs, from below \qty{5000}{EUR} to above \qty{500000}{EUR} \cite{berthoudHowSetCubeSat2016}. As an example, two extremes can be found in the EQUiSat project, which is a 1U CubeSat bus costing \SI{3800}{USD} \autocite{equisat}, and the Hermes proposal, which has estimated \SI{35300000}{USD} for a 9U deep-space CubeSat \autocite{pujolgasullaHermesCubesatOnsite2021}. The accounting for CubeSat projects highly depends on whether labour, facility and launch costs are considered. These services may often not be accounted in project budgets, or be provided for free as part of an external agreement \autocite{gambleMetricsSpacecraftDesign2012}.\footnote{For example, space agency projects may cover test campaigns and launch opportunities, or laboratory facilities may already available. Labour costs are sometimes not considered in academic projects in the case of voluntary participation.} In case labour costs are considered, the project cost will be also very closely tied to development time.

Due to this high variance between project costs, and the lack of concrete data on team environment and cost budgets, I have not performed a comparative analysis of CubeSat project costs.

\newthought{Apart from} reliability, \textbf{development time} has also been identified as a \textbf{target metric for reduction} by CubeSat teams \autocite{honore-livermoreIntegratingAgileSystems2022}. \citeauthor{swartwoutUniversityClassSpacecraft2023} notes that the lack of time is often the reason for incomplete systems-level testing, especially since missions have to work around fixed launch schedules \cite{swartwoutUniversityClassSpacecraft2023}.  \citeauthor{pradhanShorteningDeliveryTime2020} further focus on improving development time, citing the following reasons for their focus \autocite{pradhanShorteningDeliveryTime2020}:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item Ability to incorporate newer technological developments;
	\item Lower cost, affordability, wider market availability; and
	\item Better knowledge retainment for university teams;
\end{itemize}
Individual organisations and missions have also noted their desire to restrict development time within a specific limit \cite{sousaCubeSatDevelopmentFramework2021,cheongRobustFrameworkLowCost2020,cappaertBuildingDeployingOperating2018}.

A comparison between CubeSat unit sizes and programme duration does not reveal an obvious correlation (\Cref{fig:l_cubesat_size_duration}). Initially, one would expect that programme duration would scale with the technical size of the mission, which itself should be linked with the physical CubeSat size. However, the data shows that smaller CubeSat projects last disproportionately longer. This can be explained by a weaker link between physical and technical size, as well as by a preference of smaller, amateur teams with less funding, linked with increased delays, for smaller satellites.

Available CubeSat project planning literature allows comparing between different CubeSat schedules. A sample of projects based on available project planning literature is shown in \Cref{tab:l_cubesat_time_drivers}. The comparison between different time allocation strategies reveals that:
\begin{itemize}
	\item Some projects allocate work packages to ``preparatory'' activities, such as feasibility assessment and training for the application of methodologies and tools. These activities are not strictly part of a project's engineering efforts, but can be necessary before work can continue on certain engineering tasks.
	\item ``Procurement'' as an activity is planned and often part of the critical path. While it usually involves little to no work from the developers, this downtime is significant and long enough to be part of high-level plans.
	\item The estimated duration of the same activity can vary significantly between projects, often crossing one order of magnitude. This is affected by different project-specific and environment-specific parameters.
	\item Some engineering tasks have only been considered as part of the project plan only by a single project, such as \emph{software development} or \emph{reviewing}.
\end{itemize}

\newthought{Finally,} to the author's knowledge, there is no comprehensive survey on the number of members in CubeSat teams. However, typical team sizes can range between 5 to over 100 members, depending on the project and its environment.

\subsection{CubeSat Development Approaches}

There is \textbf{no single and universally followed development approach}, methodology or process for CubeSats.
Even though the satellites present technical similarities, the development environments of different teams can vary significantly. 

A comprehensive analysis and comparison of different CubeSat development lifecycles was made by \citeauthor{sousaCubeSatDevelopmentFramework2021}, as part of an effort to define a complete CubeSat development framework \autocite{sousaCubeSatDevelopmentFramework2021}. 
In his work, \citeauthor{sousaCubeSatDevelopmentFramework2021} compared the lifecycle models of different national, military and academic organisations, some of which have been directly applied to CubeSat projects. The identified lifecycles are shown in \Cref{fig:l_cubesat_lifecycles_sousa}.

\begin{figure*}[p]
	\includegraphics{external/Sousa_Lifecycles.png}
	\caption[Example CubeSat development lifecycles]{Example CubeSat development lifecycles. Taken from \cite{sousaCubeSatDevelopmentFramework2021}}
	\label{fig:l_cubesat_lifecycles_sousa}
\end{figure*}

These examples from the CubeSat sector seem to cover many lifecycle models defined in the \acl{SYE} literature\footnote{Also see \Cref{sec:LITERATURE_life_cycle_models}.}, and based on the general approach followed, can be categorised into:
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-1ex]
	\item \textbf{Single-step }\textbf{sequential} approaches \cite{nieto-peroyCubeSatMissionDesign2019,MAIVP,rodigerOSIRIS4CubeSatSystemEngineering2020}, where a typical progression of phases is followed (e.g.\@ from design to assembly to verification). Many missions follow a phased approach based on \acs{NASA} \autocite{sheaNASASystemsEngineering2020} or \acs{ESA} \autocite{ECSS-M-ST-10C} project phases, sometimes combining phases 0, A and B for simplicity.
	\marginnote{%
		The 7 phases of typical space projects, according to ECSS-M-ST-10C \cite{ECSS-M-ST-10C}:
		\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
			\item Phase 0: Mission analysis/needs identification
			\item Phase A: Feasibility
			\item Phase B: Preliminary Definition
			\item Phase C: Detailed Definition 
			\item Phase D: Qualification and Production  
			\item Phase E: Utilisation
			\item Phase F: Disposal
		\end{itemize}
	}

	Typical approaches for CubeSat integration also follow an \emph{progressive fidelity increase}, starting with with the \textbf{electrical integration of subsystems} in what is called a ``Flat Sat'' format, followed by an \textbf{engineering model}, before assembling the final \textbf{qualification and/or flight models.}

	The sequential nature of these processes motivates the use of ``stage gates'': Each phase is associated with a review, usually by the relevant stakeholders, which aims to confirm whether the project can progress to the following stage \autocite{sousaCubeSatDevelopmentFramework2021}.

	\item \textbf{Multi-step }\textbf{sequential} approaches, where one or more phases of the project's lifecycle are \textbf{repeated}. An example is shown in the work of \citeauthor{deckerSystemsengineeringAssessmentMultiple2016}, who recommends planning for and building two \emph{flight-model equivalents} of one CubeSat, so that lessons learned from the first iteration can be integrated into the second \autocite{deckerSystemsengineeringAssessmentMultiple2016}.
	
	Nevertheless, the ``sequential'' specifier for this approaches implies that no two phases are performed in parallel.
	\item \textbf{Concurrent} approaches, where two or more of the traditional space project phases are performed in parallel. The selection, order and duration of parallel phases may be pre-specified or emergent. This also includes processes termed as ``Agile'' \autocite{honore-livermoreAgileSystemsEngineering2021}. Projects following concurrent approaches can be found in \cite{coyleEECSatCubeSatDevelopment2020,honore-livermoreIntegratingAgileSystems2022,schummerApproachSystemAnalysis2022}.

	\item \textbf{Ad-hoc} approaches, where no specific lifecycle process is followed, and developers do not rely on a formal methodology to develop a system.
\end{enumerate}

In \citeauthor{sousaCubeSatDevelopmentFramework2021}'s comparison (\Cref{fig:l_cubesat_lifecycles_sousa}), it is interesting to note some more key differences between lifecycle processes, which bear similarities to the differences shown in \Cref{tab:l_cubesat_time_drivers}\footnote{Not including the difference in expected project duration, which was already discussed in \Cref{sec:LITERATURE_cubesat_environment}.}. Most notably, the phase of the initial \textbf{Conceptual Analysis} is considered only by a few institutions. This can reflect both that the initial needs, feasibility and mission analysis do not need to be performed for CubeSats, or that these activities are performed in parallel to other activities. Secondly, the \emph{development / implementation / fabrication} phase, where a design is converted into a physical product, is given different names and gravity depending on the project format, and especially on the degree of \acs{COTS} reuse.

The above describe the system-level approach; when subsystems or components need to be developed in addition to the system, these follow similar approaches to the above. However, the lifecycle process of subsystems can differ from the lifecycle process selected for the spacecraft itself. Nevertheless, many projects follow the \text{V-Model} to link between high-level (system-level) and low-level (component-level) design. The V-Model specifies an order of specification, manufacturing and verification, where the design requirements flow from the system to the subsystems and components, while the opposite order is followed for verification \autocite{bundesrepublikdeutschlandVModellXT2006}.

Based on the above, one should note that the line between different types of CubeSat lifecycle processes is not always clear. Especially when different process models are used for system, subsystem, and software development, a CubeSat project may claim to follow a \textbf{hybrid} approach.


The benefits and drawbacks of different lifecycle processes are also a topic of research.
\citeauthor{honore-livermoreAcademicsPerceptionSystems2022} interviewed 18 academics participating in CubeSat research projects, and supports the more generic findings of \Cref{sec:INTRODUCTION_defining_systems_engineering}, claiming that a balance between \acs{SYE} benefit and overhead has to be identified, with smaller teams being less able to afford ``expensive'' processes \autocite{honore-livermoreAcademicsPerceptionSystems2022}. 
Different works explore the benefits of ``sequential-like'' \autocite{shiotaniEndtoEndDesignDevelopment2014,sanchoLessonsLearnedFYS2021,deckerSystemsengineeringAssessmentMultiple2016} and ``concurrent-like'' \autocite{labargeCubeSatAgileSystem2014,garzanitiEffectivenessScrumMethodology2019,segretPavingStonesInitial2014} approaches. Conversely, \citeauthor{honore-livermoreIntegratingAgileSystems2022} notes that Agile methods are suited to university projects \autocite{honore-livermoreIntegratingAgileSystems2022}, however no similar research covers industrial environments, to the author's knowledge.
%segretPavingStonesInitial2014

\newthought{Similarly,} existing literature covers some of the remaining aspects of \acl{SYE} for CubeSats\footnote{See \Cref{fig:i_sye_decomposition}.}, albeit again without consensus. \textbf{\acl{MBSE}} is a common theme, with a number of proposals for formal models \autocite{kaslowMissionEngineeringCubeSat2021,gateauOpensourceFrameworkConcurrent2021}, along with \textbf{risk management} \cite{honore-livermoreIntegratingAgileSystems2022}\footnote{Various levels of \acs{FMEA} are typically used for failure management}, \textbf{documentation} \autocite{nieto-peroyCubeSatMissionDesign2019} and \textbf{software-hardware co-validation} \autocite{honore-livermoreIntegratingAgileSystems2022,kiesbyeHardwareInTheLoopSoftwareInTheLoopTesting2019}\footnote{usually achieved through some form of \acf{HIL} platform.}. \citeauthor{honore-livermoreManagingProductDevelopment2021} place particular emphasis on the use and effectiveness of \textbf{software tools} in the course of CubeSat projects, noting that the latter are \emph{socio-technical systems}, and that the tools used need to be both accessible, and supported by the project's management \autocite{honore-livermoreManagingProductDevelopment2021}.

\FloatBarrier
\section{Sub-CubeSat Ecosystem}
\label{sec:LITERATURE_subcubesat}



\subsection{Pico-satellites}

The success of the CubeSat platform combined with increasing trends of miniaturisation encouraged the \textbf{inception of the PocketQube platform}.
With the practical assumption that electronics can be infinitely miniaturised, the limiting factor for spacecraft size and mass become physics restrictions, and most importantly communications power/antenna size, solar panel area, and battery size.
Inspired by the success of pocketable microelectronics, the PocketQube idea was proposed by Bob Twiggs on the 2009 Cal Poly Developers' Workshop \autocite{twiggsMakingItSmall2009}. The \qtyproduct{5 x 5 x 5}{\centi\meter} footprint was selected to provide dimensional compatibility with standard CubeSat deployers.

\begin{figure*}
	\includegraphics[height=6cm]{media/pictures/poquito_coin.jpg}%
	\hfill%
	\includegraphics[height=6cm]{media/pictures/qubik_bmps.jpg}%
	\caption[Left: Comparison of a 1p PocketQube with a coin. Right: QUBIK EPS board.]{Left: Size comparison of a 1p PocketQube with a 0.50€ coin. Right: QUBIK \acs{EPS} board.\quad The 9-pin PQ9 connector is visible on the right.}
	\setfloatalignment{t}
\end{figure*}

The first PocketQubes were launched in 2013 \autocite{franzesePocketQubePicosatellitesSurvey2025,wikipediacontributorsListLaunchedPocketQubes2025}.
After the execution of different community workshops, the first version of the PocketQube standard was published in \citeyear{PQ1} \autocite{PQ1}.
Since then, companies and organisations such as \emph{FOSSA Systems}, \emph{Alba Orbital} and the \emph{Libre Space Foundation} have developed commercial buses, subsystems and deployers to support the PocketQube ecosystem. The PQ60 \autocite{PQ60}, PQ9 and CS14 \autocite{PQ91} standards provided shared electrical interfaces that could be used for a modular ecosystem, similar to the CubeSat PC/104 subsystem form-factor.

\NextCitationOffset{3cm}
Lists of launched PocketQubes are maintained by \citeauthor{kuluNanosatsDatabase2023} \autocite{kuluNanosatsDatabase2023} and on Wikipedia \autocite{wikipediacontributorsListLaunchedPocketQubes2025}.
\citeauthor{franzesePocketQubePicosatellitesSurvey2025} also released a review of PocketQube missions launched until 2023 \autocite{franzesePocketQubePicosatellitesSurvey2025}.
As of 2026, 93 PocketQubes have been launched \autocite{kuluNanosatsDatabase2023}.


\begin{figure}
	\includegraphics{external/picobus.jpg}
	\setfloatalignment{b} %vadOK
	\caption[The open-source PICOBUS PocketQube deployer, developed by the Libre Space Foundation]{The open-source PICOBUS PocketQube deployer, developed by the Libre Space Foundation. Image taken from \autocite{librespacefoundationPICOBUS}.}
\end{figure}

In \citeyear{bouwmeesterUtilityConstraintsPocketQubes2020}, \citeauthor{bouwmeesterUtilityConstraintsPocketQubes2020} analysed the general constraints and applications of PocketQubes \autocite{bouwmeesterUtilityConstraintsPocketQubes2020}. They identified space debris regulations and considerations, technology limitations, and financial constraints as the main limitations encountered by PocketQube developers. They also proposed applications in the fields of education, \acs{IOD}, earth/space weather observation, communications, and space exploration.

\begin{marginfigure}
	\centering
	\includegraphics[width=.8\marginparwidth]{external/delfi_pq.jpg}
	\caption[Delfi-PQ PocketQube]{Delfi-PQ PocketQube. Taken from \cite{uludagDelfiPQInOrbitPerformance2025}}
	\label{fig:l_delfi_pq}
\end{marginfigure}

\newthought{While} no comprehensive survey exists regarding the systems engineering methodology or constraints used by PocketQube and pico-satellite developers, a number of individual publications offer insight on the approaches followed by different teams.

\citeauthor{uludagDelfiPQInOrbitPerformance2025} have presented the development of Delfi-PQ, a 3P PocketQube developed at the Delft University of Technology, and launched in 2022 \autocite{uludagDelfiPQInOrbitPerformance2025}. The authors reported \textbf{cost reduction} as one of the main design drivers, and noted the use of an \textbf{iteration-heavy} development schedule, with \textbf{iterations lasting between 3 and 10 weeks} across a 4-year schedule. Their satellite bus consisted of \acs{EPS}, \acs{OBC}, \acs{COMMS}\footnote{including a deployable antenna} and \acs{ADCS}\footnote{based on an active 3-axis magnetorquer system} subsystems.

The authors have also provided a detailed breakdown of the cost budget of the mission,
% and we are very grateful!
replicated here in \Cref{tab:l_delfi_pq_price}. Given that the developers performed 39 manufacturing runs, the reported cost accounts to \qty{2}{\kilo EUR} per iteration, and the original paper calculates a \qty{27}{\kilo EUR} cost for the manufacturing of a single engineering and a flight model from scratch.

\phantomsection
\label{sec:LITERATURE_interviews}

Within the scope of this thesis, I have also conducted informal interviews and facility visits with PocketQube developers, with the aim of generating data about the State-Of-The-Art in terms of needs and approaches followed by PocketQube projects (\Cref{tab:l_pq_interview_list}).

\begin{table}[h]
	\caption[Data-gathering sources]{Data-gathering sources. 2 interviewees were from the University of Luxembourg, but are \emph{not} considered as inputs here, since they were already associated with the case studies presented and analysed in \Cref{chap:CASESTUDIES}.}
	\label{tab:l_pq_interview_list}
	\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{clX}
		& Type & Organisation \\
		\toprule
		N/A & University & University of Luxembourg \\
		N/A & University & University of Luxembourg \\
		1 & University & Delft University of Technology \\
		2 & University & Budapest University of Technology and Economics \\
		3 & University & Carnegie Mellon University \\
		4 & Non-Profit & Libre Space Foundation \\
		5 & Industrial & PocketQube manufacturer \\
		6 & Industrial & PocketQube manufacturer \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

Through these discussions, I noted the following information:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-1ex]
	\item All respondents have had at least one \acs{SCS} satellite launched and successfully operated in \acs{LEO}.
	\item 3 respondents noted \emph{development time} as their main constraint, one respondent noted \emph{cost}, one responded noted \emph{reliability}, and one \emph{production capability}.
	\item All respondents use some form of \textbf{iterative development} for their satellite, although the duration and scope of each iteration varies between a small amount of hardware models, and system-level rapid prototyping. No respondent follows a \emph{named} or strictly documented methodology\footnote{Such as Agile, Lean, Waterfall, \acs{ECSS}-based etc.}. 3 respondents use an \emph{undocumented} form of methodology, where satellite revisions and processes are not formally kept track of. The 3 remaining respondents use some form of formal process or tool to document the system's development lifecycle and status, such as GitLab, Odoo (an \acs{ERP} tool), or Figma (a visual design tool) for revision representation.
	\item 4 respondents have used elements from \acs{SYE} approaches similar to \textbf{CubeSat} approaches used in the literature or based on their previous experience. % this includes the high production capacity industry which is applicable to cubesats in general but I haven't found any literature specifically pointing to this
	2 respondents are using highly iterative system-based approaches, which are generally considered impractical for CubeSat projects\footnote{Based on the understanding that it is impractical to perform more than 3 iterations on an entire CubeSat system}.
	\item 3 respondents mentioned software issues as one of the main sources of issues and non-conformances, many of which were discovered shortly before launch and caused by inadequate prior testing.
	\item All respondents have developed the payloads and PocketQube bus in-house, without the use of \acs{COTS} subsystems.
	\item 4 respondents generally perform soldering in-house.
	\item All respondents had already acquired or were in the process of acquiring \textbf{in-house testing facilities}, ranging from a plain vacuum chamber to a \acs{TVAC} and a shaker table.
	\item 2 respondents have deployed 2 or more identical PocketQubes in a single launch, noting the increased redundancy and/or capability at a relatively low cost.
	\item Team sizes range between 2--15 members.
	\item All respondents noted an intermediary use of software support tools for Project Management and Systems Engineering, with notable examples being GitHub, GitLab, Slack, and Odoo.
\end{itemize}

\newthought{Based on} the available literature and data, I have noted the typical, expected environmental characteristics of PocketQube developers in \Cref{tab:mission-characteristics}, based the classification of \textbf{selection criteria} by \citeauthor{kalusCriteriaSoftwareProcess2013} for software process tailoring \autocite{kalusCriteriaSoftwareProcess2013}, and the classification of \textbf{developer classes} by \citeauthor{swartwoutYouSayPicosat2018} \autocite{swartwoutYouSayPicosat2018}\footnote{Also see \Cref{sec:LITERATURE_cubesat_environment}}. For this table, note that:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-1ex]
	\item The characteristics of the role of \emph{traditional contractors} are extrapolated and based on the CubeSat equivalent, since this role is not represented in the pico-satellite ecosystem yet.
	\item \emph{Constellation developers} are not enumerated in the table, since their characteristics can align with \emph{smallsat developers} or \emph{traditional contractors}, depending on the organisation style.
	\item Due to a much lower number of available organisations, this table will not be repeated for smaller satellites, although I expect the characteristics to be similar.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Femto-satellites and atto-satellites}

The earliest ideas about spacecraft miniaturisation to a mass below \SI{100}{\gram} started from attempts to miniaturise satellite avionics, as shown by \citeauthor{alkalaiIntegratedMicrospacecraftAvionics1996}'s \autocite{alkalaiIntegratedMicrospacecraftAvionics1996} 3D \ac{MCM} proposal.
One of the first mentions of pocket-sized satellites was made by \citeauthor{jansonMassproducibleSiliconSpacecraft1999} in \citeyear{jansonMassproducibleSiliconSpacecraft1999} \autocite{jansonMassproducibleSiliconSpacecraft1999}, who proposed using Silicon as the structural material for a microchip-based \SI{10}{\centi\meter} earth observation satellite.
%\citeauthor{jacksonReconfigurableMultifunctionalArchitecture2000} \autocite{jacksonReconfigurableMultifunctionalArchitecture2000} presented a design based on ``Multifunctional Structural Units'' 
% (no dimensions proposed, concept more akin to nano/pico-satellite?)
A few years later, \citeauthor{dasilvacurielSpacecraftonachip2003} \autocite{dasilvacurielSpacecraftonachip2003} proposed a \qtyproduct{90 x 55}{\milli\meter} ``Spacecraft-on-a-Chip'' design called ``PalmSat'' \autocite{underwoodSCIENCEMISSIONSCENARIOS}.
The proposed PalmSat design included solar cells generating \SI{2}{\watt} of power, a PIC microcontroller, a magnetorquer and reaction-wheel-based \acs{ADCS}, and space for a camera-based remote-sensing payload.

\citeauthor{barnhartSatelliteonaChipFeasibilityStudy2006} \autocite{barnhartSatelliteonaChipFeasibilityStudy2006} proposed another conceptual design for a ``satellite-on-a-chip''. The authors drew from the concept of ``Smart Dust'' computers \autocite{warnekeSmartDustCommunicating2001} to propose a self-powered\footnote{Using miniaturised capacitors and solar cells generating an average of \SI{16}{\milli\watt} based on an area of \SI{7}{\centi\meter\squared}} femto-satellite. Communications would be made using a \SI{3.4}{\centi\meter} antenna and a low \SI{300}{\bit/\second} data rate, and the system would include a passive \acs{ADCS} and a small camera as payload.

\begin{figure}[h]
	\centering
	\includegraphics[width=.7\textwidth]{media/external/space-on-a-chip}
	\caption[Proposed SpaceChip design]{Proposed SpaceChip design. Taken from \autocite{barnhartVerySmallSatelliteDesignDistributed2007}}
	\label{fig:l_proposed_spacechip}
\end{figure}

\citeauthor{barnhartVerySmallSatelliteDesignDistributed2007} further evolved the ``Satellite-on-a-Chip'' design by proposing a \qtyproduct{18 x 20}{\milli\meter} single-die \ac{ASIC} which would contain all spacecraft subsystems, an external antenna, and no other structural elements (\Cref{fig:l_proposed_spacechip}) \autocite{barnhartVerySmallSatelliteDesignDistributed2007}. The authors also designed and manufactured a \qtyproduct{9 x 9.5}{\centi\meter} and \SI{70}{\gram} ``PCBSat'' hosted on a \acl{PCB}, intended to be producible solely with low-cost \acs{COTS} components.

A number of developments followed the introduction of the \emph{N-Prize}, a competition launched in 2008 by Cambridge biologist Paul H.~Dear. The N-Prize was a £9,999.99 prize fund that would be offered to teams that could sent a femto-satellite to space, or more specifically:
\begin{quote}
The prizes will be awarded to the first persons or groups to put into orbit around the Earth a satellite with a mass of between 9.99 and 19.99 grams, and to prove that it has completed at least 9 orbits. [...]

The cost of the launch, but not ground facilities, must fall within a budget of £999.99.
\autocite{OfficialSiteNPrize2022}
\end{quote}

\begin{marginfigure}
	\includegraphics{external/WikiSat-v1-preliminary-design.png}
	\caption[WikiSat preliminary design]{WikiSat preliminary design. Image taken from \cite{tristanchoProbeConceptFemtosatellites2011}}
\end{marginfigure}
While the prize was never awarded, at least 11 teams expressed interest in the competition. 
WikiSat was one such example, where a prototype of a \qtyproduct{14 x 3 x 0.7}{\centi\metre} femto-satellite was qualified and tested on a few balloon missions between 2009--2014 \autocite{tristanchoImplementationFemtosatelliteMinilauncher2010,WikisatWiki}. Another femto-satellite prototype, RyeFemSat, was built by \citeauthor{stuurmanRyeFemSatRyersonUniversity2010} \autocite{stuurmanRyeFemSatRyersonUniversity2010}, measuring \qtyproduct{9 x 9 x 1}{\centi\meter}.

In \citeyear{atchisonPassiveSunpointingMillimeterscale2010}, \citeauthor{atchisonPassiveSunpointingMillimeterscale2010} \autocite{atchisonPassiveSunpointingMillimeterscale2010} proposed a \SI{1}{\centi\meter} square silicon chip called ``Sprite'', modelling it as a solar sail thanks to its estimated mass of \SI{7.5}{\milli\gram}. They envisioned a thin dipole antenna connected to the chip, transmitting a simple beacon with \SI{10}{\milli\watt} of power. This initial Sprite was the precursor of the ``KickSat'' project.

Perhaps the first launch and operation of centimetre-scale spacecraft was achieved by Zac Manchester, as a part of his work in Cornell University.
Manchester designed ``KickSat'', a crowd-funded mission to design and launch multiple ChipSat-class spacecraft \autocite{manchesterKickSatCrowdFundedMission2013,manchesterCentimeterScaleSpacecraftDesign2015}. ``KickSat'' was also the name given to the 3U CubeSat that would launch the ChipSats, which themselves retained the ``Sprite'' name.

\begin{figure}
	\includegraphics{external/kicksat.jpg}
	\caption[``Sprites'' being deployed from the KickSat CubeSat, concept art.]{``Sprites'' being deployed from the KickSat CubeSat, concept art. Taken from \cite{manchesterKickSatYourPersonal2016}}
\end{figure}

The Sprites were atto-satellites based on a single-\acs{PCB} design, measuring \qtyproduct{3.5 x 3.5}{\centi\meter} and weighing \qtyproduct{5}{\gram}. They included solar cells, a transmitter, and a V-dipole antenna. KickSat had a capacity of 104 Sprites.
A Sprite's development cost, as shown on the crowdfunding campaign, was close to \SI{300}{USD},\footnote{The crowdfunding campaign mentioned that launch costs were not covered by the raised funds.} allowing supporters to purchase ``their own Sprite in space with [their] initials in its radio transmissions'' \autocite{manchesterKickSatYourPersonal2016}. 

The Kickstarter crowdfunding campaign was successful, raising \SI{74586}{USD}.
The first ChipSats were exposed on the ISS in May 2011 \autocite[Sec.~2.5]{manchesterCentimeterScaleSpacecraftDesign2015}. Although the location at which they were mounted prevented their signals from reaching Earth, testing after their return showed that the Sprites remained in working condition.
The actual launch of the KickSat-1 spacecraft took place in April 2014, although, due to anomalies in the CubeSat, the Sprite deployment failed.
The Sprite design was tested once again in space, as a secondary payload of the Venta-1 mission in 2017, with a successful demonstration \autocite{adamsTheoryApplicationsGramScale2020}.
A second attempt to launch the Sprites took place with the KickSat-2 project, which followed a different CubeSat bus design based on pycubed \autocite{hollidayKickSat2DesignArchitecture}, and could include 105 ChipSats.
KickSat-2 was deployed from Cygnus NG-10 in 2018-11 \autocite{thenationalassociationforamateurradioKickSat2AliveBeing2019}.
% Note: nwekeKickSat2DismissalPrejudice2016 seems like an appropriate source, but it is not related to this restriction, instead referring to an earlier rejection.
The Sprites were originally not allowed to be deployed due to FCC constraints. However, they were eventually deployed on 18 March 2019, and re-entered the atmosphere on 21 March 2019 \autocite{abateInexpensiveChipsizeSatellites2019}.
Their signals were detected on Earth, demonstrating a successful deployment \autocite{estevezDetectingSpritesKickSat22019}.

% According to brashearsSpinorSatStep502018 (introduction): seven
KickSat-2 also included seven ChipSats designed by a separate team from UC Berkeley \autocite{brashearsSpinorSatStep502018}, referred to as \emph{PCBSats} or \emph{SpinorSats}, measuring \qtyproduct{3.5 x 3.5 x 0.4}{\milli\metre}.
The authors also provide cost information for their satellites, measuring \SI{5400}{USD} for a single spacecraft, or \SI{3000}{USD} at scale.

\begin{figure}[t]
	\includegraphics{external/cornell}
	\caption[View of an early Sprite iteration, a Sprite, and a Monarch ChipSat]{View of an early Sprite iteration, a Sprite, and a Monarch ChipSat. Image taken from \cite{cornelluniversityAlphaCubesatHomePage2025}}
\end{figure}

The popularity and success of the KickSat project sparked follow-ups in both research and projects. For example:
\begin{itemize}
\item \citeauthor{brashearsaBuildingFutureWafersat2016} \autocite{brashearsaBuildingFutureWafersat2016} envisioned a \SI{1}{\gram} ``WaferSat'', which would use components integrated on a single silicon wafer.
An early \SI{10}{\gram} \qtyproduct{3.5 x 3.5 x 0.6}{\centi\metre} \acs{PCB}-based prototype was tested on a balloon flight \autocite{WaferScaleSpacecraft}. The authors also provide development details for their designs, citing a 1--2 month iteration cycle and an assembly time of less than 24 hours.
%The spacecraft was tested in the stratosphere ([[@WaferScaleSpacecraft]]), and was also carried by a solo-piloted aircraft on a pole-to-pole expedition (https://www.deepspace.ucsb.edu/wafer-scale-spacecraft-pole-to-pole-flight-nov-2019-to-aug-2020).
 \item At Cornell University, \citeauthor{adamsTheoryApplicationsGramScale2020} designed the ``Monarch'' ChipSats as an evolution of the Sprite design \autocite{adamsRSelectedSpacecraft2019,adamsTheoryApplicationsGramScale2020}. A number of them have been deployed on earth for use in agriculture and stockraising \autocite{adamsTheoryApplicationsGramScale2020,nuttMonarchSatellitesOpen2019}. On December 2025, Cornell launched a CubeSat lightsail mission which had 4 ChipSats mounted on the edges of the sail, in order to demonstrate the capability of a free-flying sail \autocite{cornelluniversityAlphaCubesatHomePage2025}.
\item The {ThumbSat} initiative was a commercial project allowing customers to launch a payload on a \qtyproduct{4.8 x 4.8 x 3}{\centi\meter} platform \autocite{singhDoItYourselfSatellitesApplications2019}.
While ThumbSat was active between 2015--2017, it seems that regulatory obstacles due to the \acs{FCC} led to a halt of operations \autocite{vanlandinghamThumbSatOrbitalDebris2017,vanlandinghamExhibitSubmissionThumbSat2017}.
\item \emph{AmbaSat} is another UK-based company offering a \qtyproduct{3.5 x 3.5}{\centi\meter} ChipSat launch for a cost of \SI{1200}{GDB}, based on a 3U Kicksat-like deployer \autocite{3AmbaSat1Satellite}.
%They also list the price of their individual spacecraft at \SI{140}{GDB}, without a launch opportunity.
\item Project Pinpoint, managed by the Initiative for Interstellar Studies, designed a \qtyproduct{2.5 x 2.5}{\centi\metre} atto-satellite as a precursor to a laser-sail equipped ChipSat \autocite{broekerProjectPinpointPushing2021}.

\item Four \SI{28}{\gram} \qtyproduct{6.5 x 6.5}{\centi\meter} \emph{Stardust} femto-satellites were developed by the National University of Defense Technology in China, launched as companion satellites participating in the \emph{Tiantuo-3} cluster \autocite{yangDesignExperimentStardust2020}. The 4 satellites were tethered to their mothercraft using deployable booms, but communicated wirelessly. 
\end{itemize}
\begin{marginfigure}
	\includegraphics{external/monarch_new.jpg}
	\caption[The 2025 iteration of the ``Monarch'' ChipSat]{The 2025 iteration of the ``Monarch'' ChipSat. Image taken from \cite{cornelluniversityAlphaCubesatHomePage2025}}
\end{marginfigure}



%The Sprite, SpinorSat, Monarch and AmbaSat 
A comprehensive list of femto-satellites and other ``PlanarSats'' is also provided by \citeauthor{uludagAttoFemtoScale2026} \autocite[p.\ 24]{uludagAttoFemtoScale2026}. Many of these designs follow the open-source and open-hardware philosophies, with their source code, hardware design and documentation available online.


%\footnote{\url{https://github.com/space-technologies-at-california/PCBSat} \
%In \citeyear{perezSurveyCurrentFemtosatellite2016}, \citeauthor{perezSurveyCurrentFemtosatellite2016} investigated the current femto-satellite state of the art. \autocite{perezSurveyCurrentFemtosatellite2016}

\begin{figure}[h]
	\includegraphics{external/hein_capabilities}
	\caption[AttoSat characteristics and capabilities]{AttoSat characteristics and capabilities. Taken from \citeauthor{heinAttoSatsChipSatsOther2019} \cite{heinAttoSatsChipSatsOther2019}}
	\setfloatalignment{b}
	\label{fig:l_hein_capabilities}
\end{figure}

\newthought{\citeauthor{heinAttoSatsChipSatsOther2019} \autocite{heinAttoSatsChipSatsOther2019} particularly investigated} the \qtyrange{1}{10}{\gram} atto-satellite class. The authors mapped the specific characteristics of atto-satellites (such as the small size and low mass/area ratio) into capabilities that may not be easily achievable by other spacecraft classes (\Cref{fig:l_hein_capabilities}).
Example applications include distributed communications, Synthetic Aperture Radar, spacecraft health monitoring\footnote{As secondary spacecraft, tethered or following a parent spacecraft}, Very-Low Orbit science missions, rapid/statistical technology testing, ballistic coefficient determination, and position data beacons.

Authors have also explored different specific applications of atto-satellites, either in an individual or constellation configuration.
\citeauthor{umansky-castroChipSatsPlanetaryExploration2021} explored the use of ChipSats for atmospheric and magnetic field measurements of planetary bodies, finding an optimal ballistic coefficient range for measurements in Mars, Titan, and the Moon, and noting that mass-scale measurements would ``bring us a step closer to an ideal dataset'' for the aforementioned measurements \autocite{umansky-castroChipSatsPlanetaryExploration2021,timmonsMarsAtmosphericCharacterization2021}.
\citeauthor{streetmanDualExplorationArchitectures2016} considered ChipSats as an enabling technology for \textbf{dual exploration} missions, providing an example case study of measuring the gravity field of a planetary body using interferometry \autocite{streetmanDualExplorationArchitectures2016}. Finally, \citeauthor{sundaramoorthySystematicIdentificationApplications2010} also mapped the capabilities of a femto-satellite swarm to meteorological applications on Earth \autocite{sundaramoorthySystematicIdentificationApplications2010}.


One of the most famous examples of an application of atto-satellites is the Breakthrough Starshot project.
\citeauthor{marxInterstellarVehiclePropelled1966} initially proposed the idea of using terrestrial lasers to propel interstellar spacecraft in \citeyear{marxInterstellarVehiclePropelled1966} \autocite{marxInterstellarVehiclePropelled1966}, while
%
\citeauthor{forwardRoundtripInterstellarTravel1984} in \citeyear{forwardRoundtripInterstellarTravel1984} proposed the use of lightsail\footnote{equivalently, laser-sail} spacecraft, calculating different mission designs that can reach Alpha Centauri after \(\sim\)40 years of travel \autocite{forwardRoundtripInterstellarTravel1984}.\footnote{\citeauthor{forwardStarwispUltralightInterstellar1985} also proposed an alternative interstellar probe concept based on beamed \acs{RF} microwaves \cite{forwardStarwispUltralightInterstellar1985}}
\citeauthor{manchesterStochasticSpaceExploration2011}, on the other hand, proposed using the smaller ChipSat spacecraft for space exploration \autocite{manchesterStochasticSpaceExploration2011}.
%https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starwisp

The Breakthrough Starshot project was announced in 2016 by Breakthrough Initiatives \autocite{finkbeinerNearLightSpeedMissionAlpha2017}. It envisioned the use of a laser array that would propel \SI{15}{\milli\meter}-wide ChipSats to Proxima Centauri to speeds between \(0.15c\) and \(0.9c\) \autocite{parkinBreakthroughStarshotSystem2018}. At the core of Breakthrough Starshot was the \emph{StarChip}, a \qty{15}{\milli\meter}-long carrier for a metre-scale lightsail, fitted with experimental technologies, such as atomic batteries \autocite{wordenBreakthroughPrizes2017}. As of 2026, while scientific progress has been made towards the Proxima Centauri goal, the Starshot project seems to be on hold \autocite{scoles$100MillionMissionAnother2025}.




Femto- and atto-satellite---specific technologies have also been developed, with examples in the fields of attitude control \cite{liuDesignMagneticField2019,streetmanLimitationsScalingMomentum2017,weisChipscaleSpacecraftSwarms2017,stamatLaboratoryscaleTestPlatforms2019,vidal-valladaresFemtoSatelliteLocalizationMethod2022}, %electronics  \autocite{wangFeasibilityStudyHighly2016}
sails \cite{atchisonPassiveMicroscaleSolar2008,umansky-castroDesignAlphaCubeSat,atchisonPassiveSunpointingMillimeterscale2010,renNovelDesignThermal2021}, lens-less imaging \cite{kuoDiffuserCamDiffuserBasedLensless2017,boominathanPhlatCamDesignedPhaseMask2020,boominathanRecentAdvancesLensless2022,wuRealtimeDeeplearningAided2023,liLenslessCameraUnraveling2024}, distributed beamforming \cite{hadaeghDevelopment100GramClassSpacecraft2016}, station-keeping \cite{luckingElectrochromicOrbitControl2012,weisChipscaleSpacecraftSwarms2017,niccolaiReviewSmartDust2019,gondarDecentralizedControlElectromagnetic2021,timmonsExperimentalTestingRangebased2022}, and propulsion \cite{siegelSiliconWaferIntegration2020}.
%\autocite{bruce}

The technology and applications of femto-scale and smaller satellites can also be linked to the development of ``smart dust'' or ``smartdust'' networks, which represent the corresponding swarm concept for terrestrial applications \autocite{ucberkeleyroboticsandintelligentmachineslabSMARTDUST2008}. Smart dust research has focused on energy harvesting, communication and instrumentation \cite{agoginoSensorValidationFusion,warnekeSmartDustCommunicating2001,romerTrackingRealWorldPhenomena2004,romerTrackingRealWorldPhenomena2004}.

\newthought{Recent launches}\label{sec:LITERATURE_debris} of femto-satellites and smaller objects in space have given rise to space debris and tracking concerns. Manchester's KickSat-2 mission was initially rejected by the \ac{FCC} on the grounds of increased risk due to lack of traceability \cite{FCCBigProblem2018,thenationalassociationforamateurradioKickSat2AliveBeing2019,nwekeKickSat2DismissalPrejudice2016}. KickSat-2 then sought a \acs{NASA} launch which shifted the responsible regulatory body.
Another notable case was the one of \emph{Swarm Technologies}, a USA-based startup which attempted to launch four 1/4U spacecraft called \emph{SpaceBEEs}. Despite their attempts for improved tracking\footnote{Including a passive Ku-band radar reflector on each face and a planned GPS beacon}, their application was rejected again due to their small size \autocite{serafiniSWARMDismissalPrejudice2017}. Despite the rejection, Swarm did launch their spacecraft. This led to a halt in their next filing and a fine \autocite{harrisFCCAccusesStealthy2018}.

Nevertheless, \autocite{FCCBigProblem2018,albaorbitalTrackingPocketQubes2020} claim that, as of 2020, at least all PocketQubes that had been launched so far, have been shown to be identifiable, trackable, and posing no higher danger than other CubeSats with no propulsion. \autocite{sperettaCubeSatsPocketQubesOpportunities2016} also analysed a combined PocketQube-CubeSat launch, and found that \acsp{TLE} were acquired for all launched spacecraft of different sizes, noting that PocketQubes and CubeSat experienced similar acquisition times, with the exception of a 1p PocketQube, for which data was acquired after 4 days instead of the average 1.

\FloatBarrier
\section{Topics from Systems Engineering}
\label{sec:LITERATURE_systems_engineering}

\subsection{Metrics}
\label{sec:LITERATURE_metrics}

\citeauthor{honourUnderstandingValueSystems2004} \autocite{honourUnderstandingValueSystems2004} provides a list of quantifiable\footnote{Compare with the more \emph{qualitative} parameters defined in \Cref{sec:INTRODUCTION_defining_systems_engineering}.} parameters that can gauge the effect of Systems Engineering:
\begin{itemize}
	\item \textbf{Project Duration}: The overall time between the initial conceptualisation until the validation of a product. In aerospace, ``validation'' can be taken to mean environmental testing and qualification, or an actual flight of a project.
	\item \textbf{Project Cost}: In the context of aerospace systems, this can include launch costs, personnel costs, material costs, and further development costs. One should note that academic projects often include volunteer engineers, resulting in lower or zero personnel costs.
	\item \textbf{Risk}: Risk can be understood in a technical (e.g.~a hazard or probability of system failure) or programmatic (e.g.~risk of cost/schedule overruns) sense.
	\acs{ECSS} defines risk as an ``undesirable situation or circumstance that has both a likelihood of occurring and a potential negative consequence on a project'' \autocite{ECSS-S-ST-00-01C}.
	\citeauthor{honourUnderstandingValueSystems2004} \cite{honourUnderstandingValueSystems2004} provides a mathematical definition for risk \(r\): \[
	r = c_{90\%} - \bar{c}
	\]
	where \( \bar{c} \) is the average expected cost of the project, and \( c_{90\%} \) the maximum cost that 90\% of identical projects would experience.
	\item \textbf{Technical Quality}: A comparison between the actual resulting system and the intended objective.
	A simple mathematical definition of quality can be constructed as: \[
		q = \frac{\text{\# Project objectives met by actual system}}{\text{\# Original project objectives}}
	\]
\end{itemize}

It is also important to define the \textbf{attributes} of a project, that is the various parameters that describe its environment and goals. These attributes can determine what kind of \acs{SYE} frameworks, approaches and decisions can be followed.

\citeauthor{honourUnderstandingValueSystems2004} \autocite{honourUnderstandingValueSystems2004} defines two main quantifiable attributes:
\begin{definition}[Technical Size]%
	\textbf{Technical Size} (or \emph{Scale}), representing the overall size of the development effort.
	Some measures can be the number of requirements, number of components, or number of functions.%
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Technical Complexity]%
	\textbf{Technical Complexity}, representing the required degree of interaction between components, independent from size.\label{def:complexity}%
\end{definition}

On the other hand, \citeauthor{kalusCriteriaSoftwareProcess2013} \autocite{kalusCriteriaSoftwareProcess2013} have identified 127 different criteria for choosing a software process, which can be extrapolated for use in aerospace \acs{SYE}. These criteria are classified into the following categories:
\begin{itemize}
	\item \textbf{Team}: Personal and collective characteristics of the people involved in a project, including experience, size and distribution;
	\item \textbf{Internal Environment}: The available tools, decisions, and current situation of a project, including budget, duration, and management philosophy;
	\item \textbf{External Environment}: The project effects of external factors, such as stakeholders, clients and users;
	\item \textbf{Objectives}: System design imperatives and decisions that affect the project, including the domain, complexity and required innovations.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Lifecycle Models}
\label{sec:LITERATURE_life_cycle_models}
\label{sec:LITERATURE_lifecycle_models}
The selection of a lifecycle model for the development phases is a key decision in systems engineering.
The \ac{SEBOK} wiki defines a lifecycle as ``a series of stages [\textellipsis] which confirm that the system is mature enough to leave one stage and enter another'' \autocite{sebok}.
\marginnote{For this work, when using the term ``lifecycle'', I refer to the lifecycle of the \emph{project}, and not the \emph{system} or an instance of the system. The former includes all project stages, from initial ideation to operations, while the latter refers to the stages in the life of a physical system, such as commissioning, main mission operations, and disposal.} %This is also where terms like "infant mortality" come into place, see Bathtub curve.
A lifecycle model is individual and specific to each project, but usually follows a pre-specified lifecycle \emph{process model}. 
\acs{SEBOK} categorises these models into three categories:
\begin{enumerate}
	\item Pre-specified and sequential processes (such as the waterfall model)
	\item Evolutionary and concurrent processes (such as the V-model and spiral model)
	\item Interpersonal and emergent processes (such as Agile development and scrum)
\end{enumerate}
\acs{SEBOK} also provides a ``generic lifecycle model'', which serves as a starting point for lifecycle processes, and includes the main stages of
system definition, conceptualisation, realisation, production and utilisation. 

\begin{figure}[h]
	\includegraphics{graphics/ProcessModelExamples.pdf}
	\caption[Typical lifecycle process models]{Typical lifecycle process models \autocite{sebok_drivers}}
	\label{fig:l_sebok_models}
\end{figure}

\acs{SEBOK} defines an additional partition into \emph{Single-Step} and \emph{Multi-Step} processes.
Single-Step process models expect no development iterations, while Multi-Step models split the development, usually by repeating some stages of the process in an iterative or incremental fashion.

\citeauthor{HEEAGER201822} \autocite{HEEAGER201822} explain the differences between ``incremental'' and ``iterative'' lifecycles:
Incremental development defines an approach where complete versions (\emph{increments}) of a system are produced as part of repeated lifecycle stages,
while iterative development refers to an approach where different \emph{pieces} of the system are reworked one at a time.
Hybrid approaches that combine incremental and iterative development are also observed \autocite{garzanitiHybridAgileProduct2020,carpenterAgileTooFragile2014}.

\begin{figure}[h]
	\includegraphics{graphics/ProcessModelGroups.pdf}
	\caption[Different categorisations of lifecycle process models]{Different categorisations of lifecycle process models \cite{sebok_book_211,sebok_book_212,HEEAGER201822}}
	\setfloatalignment{b}
\end{figure}


Traditional space systems engineering has traditionally followed a V or waterfall-type process \autocite{ECSS-M-ST-10C,sheaNASASystemsEngineering2020}, with well-defined stage-gates between different project stages.
Project phases are typically separated into: \cite{ECSS-M-ST-10C,sheaNASASystemsEngineering2020}
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item Phase 0 (concept studies/mission analysis/needs identification),
	\item Phase A (feasibility/concept development/technology development),
	\item Phase B (preliminary definition),
	\item Phase C (detailed definition/fabrication),
	\item Phase D (assembly/qualification),
	\item Phase E (operations), and
	\item Phase F (close-out/disposal).
\end{itemize}
This approach arises from the high costs associated with early space systems engineering, and the risk-averse attitude of developers, especially in the case of human spaceflight.
Indeed, more time spent during the early definition phases combined with a rigorous review process has been shown to reduce later mission cost, risk, and delays. \autocite{sebok_value}.
However, this paradigm has often come under challenge, with different missions taking incremental or iterative approaches to system design \autocite{carpenterAgileTooFragile2014}, the most notable example being SpaceX's ``move fast and break things'' approach \autocite{chrisimpeySpaceXScienceFailure2023}.
\acs{SEBOK} claims that the integrated and interactive nature of modern hardware-software systems has rendered pre-specified processes obsolete, as system interfaces and behaviours can emerge better with their use, rather than extensive and bureaucratic processes.


On the other hand, it is possible for engineering projects to be associated with no predetermined lifecycle process.
These projects can be said to follow a ``by-default'' or ``ad-hoc'' model \autocite{cowper941SystemsEngineering2004}.
This is often the case for small, volunteer-based or ``\ac{DIY}'' projects \autocite{bohmerOpenInnovationEcosystem}. However, even if no formal description is provided for the lifecycle used, the models described earlier in this section can still describe the actual work process followed. Very simple projects follow a single-step process, which can potentially evolve into a multi-step process with an undetermined number of steps, with the benefits of systems engineering and a predetermined methodology becoming clear as the project scale grows (\Cref{sec:INTRODUCTION_defining_systems_engineering}).

\subsection{Agile Systems Engineering}

Pre-specified lifecycle processes, as described in the above section, usually prescribe a predetermined stage or product at the end of each development phase \autocite{sebok_incremental}.
However, this requires prior knowledge about the system, and often involves the expectation of no errors or external influences during design or development.

In \citeyear{beckAgileManifesto2001}, the Agile Manifesto \autocite{beckAgileManifesto2001}, signed by 17 prominent software engineers, sought to challenge this model by essentially proposing ``evolutionary'' processes, where the end state of each phase of development is unknown.

\begin{marginfigure}
	\begin{tcolorbox}[colback=white, colframe=black, sharp corners, boxrule=0.5pt, width=\linewidth]
	\small Individuals and interactions over processes and tools\\%
	Working software over comprehensive documentation\\%
Customer collaboration over contract negotiation\\%
Responding to change over following a plan%
	\end{tcolorbox}
	\caption[The Agile Manifesto]{The Agile Manifesto \autocite{beckAgileManifesto2001}}
\end{marginfigure}

The Agile Manifesto, originating from the field of Software Engineering, proposed a set of \emph{4 values and 12 principles} for evolutionary systems engineering and product management. The principles, aimed at high adaptability to changing requirements and rapid application of lessons learned during development \autocite{sebok_drivers}, are shown in \cite{beckAgileManifesto2001}. The Agile Manifesto however does not propose a lifecycle process model or a design methodology on its own; different Agile methodologies have been proposed and used, such as: \autocite{boehmBalancingAgilityDiscipline2004}
\begin{fullwidth}
\begin{multicols}{2}
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item Adaptive Software Development
	\item Agile Unified Process
	\item Crystal
	\item Extreme Programming \cite{beckExtremeProgrammingExplained2008}
	\item Feature-Driven Development
	\item Lean Development
	%\item Lean software development (???)
	\item Kanban
	\item Rapid Application Development
	\item Scrum \cite{schwaverDefinitiveGuideScrum2020}
	\item Scaled Agile Framework
\end{itemize}
\end{multicols}
\end{fullwidth}

These methodologies cover different parts of the lifecycle of a system, and are suited for different types of teams and sources of constraints \autocite{boehmBalancingAgilityDiscipline2004,abrahamssonAgileSoftwareDevelopment2017}.
While their core is usually the lifecycle process model, some of them also branch into other system engineering fields. \citeauthor{boehmBalancingAgilityDiscipline2004} \cite{boehmBalancingAgilityDiscipline2004} provides a comprehensive comparison of the above methodologies.
I note that often synonymous terminology is used in methodology definitions and description, creating potential confusion in comparisons; especially words such as ``agile'', ``lean'', ``rapid'', and more are sometimes used interchangeably or with conflicting definitions. 

Incremental development finds itself at the core of most Agile methodologies. For example, Scrum \autocite{schwaverDefinitiveGuideScrum2020} requires the preparation of a complete product increment every \qtyrange{1}{4}{weeks}. The incremental process is initiated with a \ac{MVP} which may not satisfy all customer requirements, and is repeated on every cycle based on a conversation between customers and developers.

The use of Agile methodologies in software engineering has become industry-standard since the introduction of the concept \autocite{15thAnnualState,StatusQuoScaled}. Meta-analyses performed against case studies, surveys and experiments, have found that the correct application of Agile methodologies can reportedly reduce project management inertia and increase developer and customer satisfaction \autocite{leeAgileIntegratedAnalysis2010,dybaEmpiricalStudiesAgile2008}.
Criticism against Agile is often targeted towards incorrect implementations.
Scrum, one of the most popular Agile frameworks, has faced criticism both for its foundation, and due to its potential for misuse \autocite{tsalicoglouHowTransitionAgile2017,bjornDarkAgilePerceiving2019,grayCriticismScrum2015}.\marginnote{\citeauthor{kustersScreamGuide2020,jeffriesDarkScrum2016} \autocite{kustersScreamGuide2020,jeffriesDarkScrum2016} have notably produced tongue-in-cheek documentation demonstrating common incorrect applications of the Scrum approach.}

The success of Agile in software engineering has encouraged its adoption in the engineering and development of physical products. The main obstacle that has to be overcome are what are referred to as ``\acp{COP}''  \autocite{schmidtAgileDevelopmentConstraints2017,petersonWhenWorldsCollide2021}.
These constraints are related to the difficulties of creating shippable increments for each iteration, higher complexity, or increased external requirements. In the context of \ac{SCS}, f these constraints are impactful because of a reliance on physical components that may not be manufactured in-house, such as \acsp{IC}, \acsp{PCB}, or machined parts, the impracticality of automated testing, especially in the case of environmental qualification, and the expensive, non-repeatable launch process.

For this reason, different approaches and methodologies have been investigated for the use of Agile in Systems Engineering \autocite{carsonCanSystemsEngineering2013,AgileComplexSystems,mcdanielGoodBadAgile2003,darrinAgileManifestoDesign2017,bottAnalysisTheoriesSupporting2019,petersonWhenWorldsCollide2021}.
The \acf{SEBOK} \autocite{sebok_agile} and \citeauthor{douglassAgileSystemsEngineering2015} \autocite{douglassAgileSystemsEngineering2015} have provided comprehensive tailorings of Agile for Systems Engineering. \citeauthor{douglassAgileSystemsEngineering2015} notably adapted the four Agile principles as follows \autocite{douglassAgileSystemsEngineering2015}:
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item \emph{Individuals and interactions over processes and tools}
	\item \emph{Verifiably correct specifications over comprehensive documentation}
	\item \emph{Customer collaboration over contract negotiation}
	\item \emph{Responding to change over following a plan}
\end{enumerate}

Agile approaches have also been documented for use in critical systems and space systems. One of the earliest applications of practices similar to Agile can be observed in NASA's \emph{Faster-Better-Cheaper} approach in the 1990's, which is considered by some to be a success. \autocite{dimarioSystemicsNASAFasterBetterCheaper2005} provides a comprehensive review of the approach, its results, and successes and failures. \autocite{dillonFasterBetterCheaperProjectsToo2015} also analyses the controversy on the results of the FBC paradigm by comparing its strong and weak outcomes.

There are various examples in literature investigating the adoption of Agile practices in entire, or at least parts of, space projects, such as for the Multi Mission Bus Demonstrator \autocite{huangAgileHardwareSoftware2012}. %
 %have not yet found a strong footing on safety-critical and highly mission-critical systems:
 \autocite{kasauliSafetyCriticalSystemsAgile2018} is a meta-analysis of only software development for such systems, while \autocite{carpenterAgileTooFragile2014} tries to investigate how Agile could be tailored for such systems. Fully Agile approaches, where increments involve versions of the entire system for large-scale spacecraft, are not yet documented in the literature.
 
\paragraph{Agile Frameworks}
\label{par:LITERATURE_agile_frameworks}

While a number of different Agile frameworks have been proposed, only a small number of them are in effective use in the industry as of 2026.
Scrum and the \acf{SAFE} are the two most popular formal frameworks in use, with developers also creating ad-hoc methods based on Agile principles \autocite{digital.aiStateAgileReport2025}.
A \citeyear{atzbergerAgileDevelopmentPhysical2020} survey by \citeauthor{atzbergerAgileDevelopmentPhysical2020} \autocite{atzbergerAgileDevelopmentPhysical2020} of hardware developers found that \qty{90}{\percent} of the respondents used a form of Scrum as a lifecycle process model, while other methods, such as \emph{Kanban} and \emph{Design Thinking}, also enjoy some popularity.

In this section, I will analyse the most popular Agile frameworks and evaluate their applicability to hardware applications, and more specifically the \ac{SCS} methodology.

\subparagraph{Scrum}
\label{sec:LITERATURE_scrum}
Scrum, one of the most popular Agile frameworks in use, was developed by Ken Schwaber and Jeff Sutherland and covers management practices during the entire lifecycle of a software project \autocite{boehmBalancingAgilityDiscipline2004}.
\marginnote{Scrum's authors claim it was derived from complex systems theory, and influenced by best practices and research results from Japanese and USA-based companies in the end of the 20\textsuperscript{th} century. They describe it as a ``pattern language for hyperproductive software development'' \autocite{sutherlandNUTBOLTSORIGINS}. The name ``Scrum'' originated from rugby football terminology, where the term signifies players packing closely together to gain control of the ball \autocite{sutherlandNUTBOLTSORIGINS}.}
Scrum is primarily defined by ``The Definitive Guide to Scrum'' by \citeauthor{schwaverDefinitiveGuideScrum2020} \autocite{schwaverDefinitiveGuideScrum2020}, which defines the theory and processes behind the framework.

The framework is built around fixed-time iterations called \emph{Sprints}, which follow a repeated pattern of \emph{planning-\-standup-\-review-\-retrospective} with the aim of presenting a complete product at the end of each iteration. It also defines the different terminology and roles within small development team.

With its main description being no more than 14 pages long, Scrum is considered a lightweight framework, and can therefore be easily applied or tailored for different organisations. Popular generic tailorings include \acf{LESS} and \emph{Scrum@Scale}, variants designed for organisations with multiple Scrum-based teams that may be building one or more products \autocite{larmanLargescaleScrumMore2017,ReadScrumScaleGuide}.

\subparagraph{Extreme Programming (XP)}
Extreme Programming is a software development methodology, similar to Scrum, which combines different methods and guidelines that particularly apply to software development.
Typical aspects of XP include having a customer representative continuously present on-site, iterations lasting three weeks or less, pair programming, code reviews, frequent refactoring, test-driven development, and continuous integration. Despite its initial popularity, it received criticism due to its environment-specific assumptions, and difficulties in scaling \autocite{boehmBalancingAgilityDiscipline2004}. \citeauthor{atzbergerAgileDevelopmentPhysical2020}'s survey shows that it was used by \qty{7}{\percent} of industrial developers \autocite{atzbergerAgileDevelopmentPhysical2020}.

\subparagraph[SAFe (Scaled Agile Framework)]{\acf{SAFE}}

The \acl{SAFE} is another popular set of organisational practices, designed to scale Agile across organisations consisting of multiple teams. It includes concepts such as \emph{Lean Portfolio Management} to guide an organisation's strategy, it guides the product integration and delivery flow based on Scrum-like iterations, and it provides guidance for leadership and organisational structure. Its primary concept is the \emph{``Release Train''}, a team-of-teams with more than 50 individuals that share a common goal. As a scaled framework, it mainly targets organisations with more than 250--1000 developers \autocite{heusserIntroducingScaledAgile2015}. Common criticisms against \acl{SAFE} address its rigidity and strict hierarchical structure, as well as its dependence on predictability, which are said to only create an illusion of implementing Agile \autocite{schwaberUnsafeAnySpeed2013}.

\subparagraph{Miscellaneous Frameworks}
Most other documented Agile frameworks are not reported to be as popular, but can still represent useful inputs for the proposed methodology.

The Rational Unified Process (RUP), introduced in 1998, was an iterative framework created by a division of IBM, designed to be adaptable and tailorable by different software organisations. While it is not officially supported as of 2025, and has received considerable criticism, it is reported to be a precursor of \acs{SAFE} \autocite{schwaberUnsafeAnySpeed2013}. DAD (Disciplined Agile Delivery), introduced in 2013, is a set of guidelines for Agile software organisations that aims to encapsulate other, more fundamental Agile frameworks \autocite{ChooseYourWoW}. The \acf{DSDM}, introduced in 1994, is a project-specific framework that covers planning, requirements, development and project management \autocite{agilebusinessconsortiumAgilePMHandbookV22014}.
Crystal, introduced in the 1990s, is a family of frameworks that aim to cover different sizes and criticalities\footnote{The 4 ``criticalities'' in Crystal are \emph{comfort, discretionary money, essential money, and life.}} of projects, although only some of them have been formally described \autocite{boehmBalancingAgilityDiscipline2004}.
Finally, Feature-Driven Development (FDD), introduced in 1997, is a lightweight process that resembles an incremental waterfall approach. A key concept of FDD is assigning the ownership of code classes to individuals \cite{boehmBalancingAgilityDiscipline2004}.
% For sources on the dates, check the relevant references on Wikipedia articles

\subparagraph{Miscellaneous Methods}
Apart from frameworks that cover the entire structure of a team, project, or organisation, there also exist specific methods and tools which can be categorised as Agile but cover only specific parts of project management or software development. 

\begin{marginfigure}
	\includegraphics{external/Kanban Board.pdf}
	\caption[Mockup of a Kanban board]{Mockup of a Kanban board. Taken from \autocite{falcoKanbanBoardRepresentation2023}}
\end{marginfigure}
Kanban is a visual approach to manage work in human systems, which aims to create a single view of tasks at various stages by modelling them as cards that are moved across different columns on a board \autocite{ChooseYourWoW}. \citeauthor{atzbergerAgileDevelopmentPhysical2020}'s survey measures a \qty{75}{\percent} usage of Kanban in the physical product industry \cite{atzbergerAgileDevelopmentPhysical2020}.

Test-Driven Development (TDD) is a software development method which prioritises writing working, automated unit tests before the actual code, and is natively supported by different programming languages and frameworks. In TDD, the tests are considered equivalent to the specification of the software (or, correspondingly, the requirements of a space system) \autocite{beckTestDrivenDevelopment2002}.

\paragraph{Hardware-specific Frameworks}
Because of the introduction of Agile methods into hardware development, a number of hardware-specific recommendations or frameworks have also been proposed for different scales of physical systems. However, there is no single hardware-specific framework that enjoys considerably widespread adoption \autocite{atzbergerAgileDevelopmentPhysical2020}, with the field being supported instead by various proprietary approaches. Nevertheless, most developers tailor software-based Agile ideas for their use \autocite{cooperAgileDevelopmentManufacturing2023}.

\subparagraph{Agile-Stage-Gate}
\citeauthor{cooperAgileDevelopmentManufacturing2023} \autocite{cooperAgileDevelopmentManufacturing2023} observed an emergent hybrid model that is used by physical product developers, which replaces traditional project management methods with Scrum, but maintains traditional stages and gates \emph{``for strategic and directional reasons''}. In contrast to Scrum, iterations can last between two weeks and three months, stand up meetings are only held two--three times per week, and each iteration has the goal of presenting a ``demo'' to management or customer.

\subparagraph{Slice-Based Integration}
\citeauthor{radekaWhenAgileGets2022} \autocite{radekaWhenAgileGets2022,iberleAgileGetsPhysical2022} also proposed a Scrum-based framework, based on the concept of a \textbf{slice}, which is an independently verifiable feature or set of features of a product\footnote{Also see \Cref{def:slice}.}. Then, instead of working on the entire product, teams have a clear set of functionality that needs to be achieved at the end of every timebox. This purportedly helps coordinating hardware and software development. Likewise for verification, different test sets are defined and distributed across iterations.

\begin{figure}[h]
	\includegraphics{external/iberle_slices.jpg}
	\caption[Example of an Agile slice-based integration plan for a physical product]{Example of an Agile slice-based integration plan for a physical product. Image taken from \cite{iberleAgileGetsPhysical2022}}
\end{figure}

\chapter{Research Approach}
\label{chap:RESEARCH_APPROACH}

Research Design calls for a selection between a qualitative and quantitative approach \autocite{creswellResearchDesignQualitative2023}. A common issue in engineering research is the difficulty of applying and investigating a study's results in the real world, preventing the generation of statistical data based on experimentation. This lack of numeric data calls for an application of a mixed or qualitative research approach.

Qualitative research typically follows approaches such as narrative, phenomenological, grounded theory, action or case study research \autocite{creswellQualitativeInquiryResearch2025}. 
For this work, I will follow
the DRM Design Research Methodology \autocite{blessingDRMDesignResearch2009} by \citeauthor{blessingDRMDesignResearch2009}.

In engineering research, DRM is a widely adopted research approach that proposes a plan to create and verify \emph{support}, i.e.~``the possible means, aids and measures that can be used to improve design''. DRM divides the research process into the following four stages:
\begin{enumerate*}
	\item \textbf{Research Clarification (RC)}, where researchers try to find evidence to support their assumptions and research goal, based on a literature review,
	\item \textbf{Descriptive Study I (DS-I)}, where researchers improve their understanding of the research gap and influencing factors through more detailed data gathering,
	\item \textbf{Prescriptive Study (PS)}, where researchers develop and evaluate the new support (such as a methodology, process, method or tool), and
	\item \textbf{Descriptive Study II (DS-II)}, where researchers evaluate the \emph{applicability} and \emph{usefulness} of their new support by further data gathering.
\end{enumerate*}

DRM acknowledges that the comprehensive implementation of all stages can be impractical for a single research project, and proposes different partial plans to cover them. It also allows for parallel execution \emph{iterations} as part of research planning to enable feedback between different stages.

The specific research plan for this work is shown in \Cref{fig:r_activity,fig:r_methodology} and is elaborated as follows:
\begin{enumerate}
	\item \textbf{Research Clarification (RC)} (Review-based):
	I have performed a literature survey to identify the shortcomings of 
	current Systems Engineering approaches for \ac{SCS}, and an engineering gap that can be resolved through a novel methodology. My survey has focused on available resources (such as time, cost, personnel and technology), goals (such as functionality and reliability), and previous projects.
	
	This is elaborated in \Cref{chap:INTRODUCTION,chap:SURVEY}.
	\item \textbf{Descriptive Study I (DS-I)} (Comprehensive):
	As a first step, I have identified the organisational and technical requirements of typical \acs{SCS} missions, and compared them with the ecosystem of larger space missions. I also analysed the concept of Systems Engineering and identified its components that have both a higher impact on \acs{SCS} mission success, and can be improved through a Systems Engineering methodology. Finally, I conducted informal interviews to validate my findings and the usefulness of my proposed approach.

	The findings for \acs{SCS} missions are complemented with corresponding findings for \textbf{CubeSat} missions and mission environments, since:
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-2ex]
		\item The architecture of CubeSats is similar enough to \acs{SCS} to allow reusing and applying methods, processes and data from the former to the latter;
		\item Many \acs{SCS} developers directly follow CubeSat-based approaches;
		\item \acs{SCS} literature is significantly limited compared to the more established CubeSat field.
	\end{enumerate}

	This literature survey is performed in \Cref{chap:SURVEY}.
	
	As a second step, I will participate in the \textbf{design and development of \acs{SCS} space missions} with the objective of \textbf{qualification or flight} as case studies. These space missions will be in the form of payloads, subsystems or satellites that can reach a flight-ready stage. They will be used either as a direct source of information to develop the required support, or as experimental applications of the new proposed methodology, in an iterative fashion.
	
	For this work, four different internal space missions were investigated, as shown in \Cref{chap:CASESTUDIES}. More details about the result acquisition and evaluation process are detailed in the lead section of \Cref*{chap:CASESTUDIES}.
	
	\item
	\textbf{Prescriptive Study (PS)} (Comprehensive):
	Based on the gathered data, I will describe a set of design methods applicable to different components of systems engineering, which combined define a methodology for \acs{SCS}.
	
	The PS phase is further separated by \acs{DRM} into Task Clarification, Conceptualisation, Elaboration, Realisation, and Support Evaluation. These aspects are elaborated in \Cref{chap:METHODOLOGY}. The \textbf{Task Clarification} step of PS, explaining the goals and corresponding metrics of the proposed methodology, is presented in the lead section of \Cref*{chap:METHODOLOGY}.
	
	\item 
	\textbf{Descriptive Study II (DS-II)} (Initial):

	The DS-II stage includes the validation of the application and impact of the proposed design support, based on the execution of empirical studies.
	
	Validation is usually separated into \emph{internal validity}, which establishes a causal relationship between the premises and results of the proposed support, and \emph{external validity}, which shows whether these results can be generalised \autocite{yinCaseStudyResearch2014}.
	\marginnote{%
		An alternative definition of validity is given by \citeauthor{pedersenVALIDATINGDESIGNMETHODS} \cite{pedersenVALIDATINGDESIGNMETHODS}. In this work, \citeauthor{yinCaseStudyResearch2014}'s \emph{internal} and \emph{external} validation are analogous to \textbf{performance validation}, which tests whether:
		\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-1ex]
			\item ``the outcome of the method is useful with respect to the initial purpose for some chosen example problem(s)'';
			\item ``the achieved usefulness is linked to applying the method''; and
			\item ``the usefulness of the method is beyond the case studies''.
		\end{enumerate}
	}
	
	In an ideal scenario, I could perform a statistical evaluation of the support, for example by comparing the application (or non-application) of our methodology over a large number of projects, or through complete A/B experiments. However,  Space Systems Engineering projects are lengthy, taking multiple years and resources to complete. Systems design is also highly contextual and non-repetitive, making the generation of statistical data even more difficult. I have therefore concluded that only an initial part of the DS-II stage is within the scope of this study.
	
	Nevertheless, the intended goal of short \( < \qty{1}{year}\) projects allows implementing and qualitatively validating the proposed \acs{SCS} methodology, as part of specific \textbf{case studies} that are developed in parallel with this thesis.
	
	More specifically, during this work I participated in four miniaturised space system design missions, most of them targeting environmental qualification\footnote{equivalent to a \acs{TRL} of 8}.
	These case studies were used to iteratively generate data, test, and validate the proposed methodology.
	
	
	The description and results of the case studies are elaborated in \Cref{chap:CASESTUDIES}.	
	
\end{enumerate}

%\label{sec:ra_scope}

%\section{Case Study Approach}
