\chapter{A Methodology for Sub-CubeSat Spacecraft}
\label{chap:METHODOLOGY}

\begin{fullwidth}
\epigraph{\emph{%Law of Douglass \#30:
		The best way not to have any defects in your system is not to put defects in your system.}}{Bruce Douglass}
\end{fullwidth}

This chapter will present the proposed ``\acs{SCS} Methodology''. Following the \acs{DRM} methodology described in \Cref{chap:RESEARCH_APPROACH}, this chapter will cover the \emph{Prescriptive Study} phase.
\Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_technicalmanagement,sec:METHODOLOGY_hardwaredevelopment,sec:METHODOLOGY_softwaredevelopment} will present the conceptualisation, elaboration and realisation of the methodology, separated into methods for different aspects of Systems Engineering, as decomposed in \Cref{fig:i_sye_decomposition}. \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_tailoring} will finally present a tailoring questionnaire to help developers with tailoring the proposed methods to their specific project, as well as recommend the overarching tools that can support the methodology.

Before identifying and realising the functions of the intended methodology, the \emph{Task Clarification} step has to be performed, by identifying the goals and success criteria of the methodology. The requirements I identified are listed in \Cref{tab:m_methodology_general_rqeuirements} and will be justified below.

\begin{table*}[h]
	\def\arraystretch{1.1}
	\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}lX@{}}
		\toprule
		\textbf{No.} & \textbf{Requirement}                                                   \\ \midrule
		RM1            & The SCS Methodology shall enable developing a flight-capable SCS project in 1 year or less, starting from preliminary design to acceptance.  \\
		RM2            & The SCS Methodology shall cover all aspects of Systems Engineering of SCS, as identified in \Cref{sec:INTRODUCTION_defining_systems_engineering}.                 \\
		RM3            & The SCS Methodology shall enable faster development of SCS, compared to corresponding ad-hoc or CubeSat-based methodologies.    \\
		RM4            & The SCS Methodology shall enable equally or less risky development of SCS, compared to corresponding ad-hoc or CubeSat-based methodologies.  \\
		RM5            & The SCS Methodology shall be applicable to both industrial and academic developers of single spacecraft and small constellations. \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabularx}
	\caption[SCS Methodology Requirements]{\acs{SCS} Methodology Requirements}
	\label{tab:m_methodology_general_rqeuirements}
\end{table*}

The coverage of the methodology starts from preliminary design, and not conceptualisation or concept evaluation, which is not considered within the scope of this thesis. The intended end of application of the methodology is the end of environmental testing for the spacecraft. I consider a successful acceptance campaign to signify the end of a spacecraft's development, as launch regulations usually prevent modifications after this point, and the spacecraft should be considered completed. Conversely, the high variability of integration and launch dates could add a significant overhead in the measured time.

Out of the metrics identified in \Cref{sec:LITERATURE_metrics}, I have chosen two as the relevant success criteria: \textbf{development time} and \textbf{risk}, as the available literature and data on the development on CubeSats, PocketQubes, and smaller \acs{SCS} has demonstrated that they are \begin{enumerate*}
	\item common key targets for optimisation in academic and industrial environments, and
	\item causally linked to the selection and application of a \acl{SYE} methodology or process. \todo{[cite]}
\end{enumerate*}
I have not selected cost as a target criterion, as its accounting will vary by different orders of magnitude between different projects, and as it is also often positively correlated with development time.\footnote{Nevertheless, I will evaluate any potential cost savings or cost increases of different methods/approaches, but I will not consider cost as a success criterion or requirement}

Finally, the ``target audience'' of the methodology includes the groups of ``hobbyists'' and ``smallsat developers'', as classified by the \citeauthor{swartwoutYouSayPicosat2018} taxonomy \autocite{swartwoutYouSayPicosat2018}.\footnote{Also see \Cref{sec:LITERATURE_cubesat_environment} for an explanation of the Swartwout taxonomy.} This scope excludes ``traditional contractors'' and large constellation developers. This selection is inspired by the common characteristics of the two former groups (\Cref{tab:mission-characteristics}), which also indicate that they are more receptive of a new, Agile-centred approach. The lack of larger scale ``traditional contractors'' within the \acs{SCS} ecosystem as of this moment also prevents data gathering and experimentation for these types of organisations.

\section{Technical Management Methodology}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_technicalmanagement}

This section will cover the \emph{Technical Management} aspect of \acf{SCS} Systems Engineering (as shown in \Cref{fig:i_sye_decomposition}), which relates to how both the high-level plan and day-to-day work of a project and a development team are organised.

\Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_requirements} will present how project goals and requirements should be managed. Then, \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_satscrum,sec:METHODOLOGY_slicebased} will present two lifecycle process models that guide most decisions behind \acs{SCS} development. In \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_workpackage}, I will present recommendations for work package and task tracking, while \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_datamanagement} will compare different approaches to documentation, modelling, and \acf{EDM}.
%Finally, \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_tailoring} is orthogonal to the previous sections, presenting the requirements and needs for software tools that support the recommended methods, as well as a tailoring guideline.


\FloatBarrier
\subsection{Requirements: Slices and the MoSCoW method}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_requirements}

The concept of Mission and System Requirements is often a topic of discussion, specifically for CubeSat missions.
In traditional space systems engineering, requirements are often elaborated in significant detail, covering test specifications and guiding all the required features for development. This approach is summarised as one of Akin's laws \autocite{akinAkinLawsSpacecraft}:
\begin{quote}
	Design is based on requirements. There's no justification for designing something one bit "better" than the requirements dictate.
\end{quote}
In these cases, requirements elicitation takes place in the beginning of the project, with little room for changes and modifications at the later phases. Requirements have to use specific wording and a ``cadence'', so that they can be used during design, early verification and testing as guides on whether a product is successful or not \autocite{ECSS-E-ST-10-06C}. In order to enforce this objectivity, \acs{ECSS} standards for example forbid the use of words such as \emph{optimise}, \emph{minimise/maximise}, \emph{typical}, \emph{rapid} etc. The standards also specify key concepts that must be followed by all technical requirements, including \emph{traceability}, \emph{no ambiguity}, \emph{uniqueness}, \emph{identifiability}, \emph{singularity}, \emph{completeness}, \emph{verifiability} and \emph{tolerance} \cite{ECSS-E-ST-10-06C}. In this ideal case, requirements can be made machine-readable and compatible with formal modelling for automated verification \autocite{katisCaptureAnalyzeDiagnose2022}.

However, in the CubeSat world, some projects follow less strict approaches, where requirements do not cover the entire system, and are usually elaborated in a more subjective manner. An example of differences between two CubeSat projects is shown in \Cref{tab:m_example_requirement_different}. The decision to follow a less well-defined specification approach can be a result of the following factors:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-1ex]
	\item For complex and emergent systems, some parameters may not be known in the design phase, but may require prototyping or manufacturing. This can be true especially for novel technologies, or when a team lacks experience to predict technical values.
	\item Some critical parameters may be unknowable or unmeasurable, with teams relying on their intuition or a ``best-effort'' approach to manage.\footnote{The main examples supporting this factor are reliability and risk-related parameters. As the probabilities of part failure and design errors are not known/modelled, or would require prohibitively expensive tests, reliability-related parameters can rarely be quantified, even for CubeSats.}
	\item The time and effort required to estimate a parameter in early development phases may not justify the added benefits of an early precise specification, especially when there is an intuitive understanding of the requirement both by developers and stakeholders, and in short-duration projects, where knowledge transfer is less formal.
\end{itemize}

\begin{table*}
	\def\arraystretch{1.4}
	\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}lXX@{}}
		\toprule
		& AUTh AcubeSAT \cite{retselisAcubeSATTSVCD2020} & University of Hawai'i Artemis CubeSat Kit \cite{zhu23Requirements} \\ \midrule
		& The spacecraft shall use an attitude determination and control system to achieve a pointing accuracy within 20 degrees. & The CubeSat kit shall be low-cost and accessible to universities and private individuals \\
		& The spacecraft shall store at least 5 Ah (\acs{TBC}) in batteries. & The CubeSat kit shall include software that is intuitive for undergraduate students \\
		& The unibody's cross section on the X-Y plane shall have maximum external dimensions of 70x70 mm. & The CubeSat kit shall be tested to meet environmental requirements set forth in NASA GEVS for spaceflight. The end-user will be responsible for doing final flight environmental testing set forth by their launch provider. \\ \bottomrule
	\end{tabularx}
	\caption{Example requirements from different space projects}
	\label{tab:m_example_requirement_different}
\end{table*}

Especially for \acs{SCS} projects, I have also made the following observations:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-1ex]
	\item \acs{SCS} projects \emph{need} to conform to external requirements to some degree, provided by their host or launch provider.
	\item Due to the lack of standard launch interfaces, specifications and standards, these requirements may change by the relevant stakeholders during the project.
	\item The planned feature-set of a project may change during its course (usually via the removal of functionality) due to incorrect planning, external requirement change or une
	Projects with fixed deadlines will prefer to remove features, instead of adding additional iterations or reworking the product, since the latter may lead to a launch cancellation.
	\item Each project evolves around a central strategic goal that expresses a simple objective (such as \emph{receive at least some data from orbit} or \emph{demonstrate a specific technology}). Additional objectives, when completed, increase the perceived \emph{value} of the project.
	\item The definition of strictly specified requirements itself as a process can last up to a month, and requires some prior knowledge of the system's principles, feasibility and available technology. In the case of inexperienced teams, external reviews may be necessary.
	\item Well-specified requirements can be the guide for functional and environmental testing campaigns. However, requirements-based testing campaigns cannot offer complete coverage of the executed design, as they do not cover design-specific activity flows and edge cases.
\end{itemize}

It is therefore beneficial to define an \acs{SCS}-tailored method for requirements capture that can still be part of the proposed \emph{project lifecycle approach}, while reducing the \ac{SYE} overhead.

An inspiration for such an approach can come from the \acs{DSDM} Agile framework, which advocates the \textbf{prioritisation} of different project requirements using the \acf{MOSCOW} classification \autocite{agilebusinessconsortiumAgilePMHandbookV22014}, which itself categorises requirements as follows:
\begin{itemize}
	\item
	\textbf{Must Have} requirements: The Minimum Usable subset of requirements that a project should comply to in order to be legal, safe, compliant with external requests, and functional.
	
	Not complying to these requirements represents a cancellation or failure of the project.
	\item
	\textbf{Should Have} requirements: These represent features or requests that significantly increase project value, but are not project-breaking in case they are not implemented.
	\item \textbf{Could Have} requirements: These represent features that are desirable but less important than \emph{Should Have} ones. They may refer to requests by external entities, features that slightly increase reliability, mission duration or configurability, and generally slightly increase mission value.
	\item \textbf{Won't Have this time} requirements: These requirements represent those that the team has agreed to not implement in a specific iteration. This classification usually refers to a reassignment of requirement status.
\end{itemize}

While \emph{Must Have} requirements are a necessity for the flown project, they are not required to demonstrate a complete \emph{functional} model of the product. One can therefore present a further classification of ``must-have'' requirements, as ones that stem from the functionality of the project and have to be present for every \ac{MVP}, and the ones that stem from environmental constraints and need not be implemented for strictly functional models.

%vadOK
\acs{MOSCOW} requirements are editable during the development lifecycle of a system. This can take place, for example, during the sprint planning phase of each iteration (\Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_satscrum}).\sidenote[][-2cm]{Changes to requirements are not forbidden in ``traditional'' space systems engineering. \acs{NASA}'s Systems Engineering handbook expects requirements to change, and defines a Requirements Management Process to approve and perform requirements changes \cite{sheaNASASystemsEngineering2020}. Similarly, \acs{ECSS} defines a Request for Deviation and Non-Conformance control system. However, it would be safe to say that the addition of these processes add an additional overhead when the requirements need to be inevitably updated, which is often disproportionate to the corresponding overhead for the initial requirement definition, and discourage requirements modifications. Instead, the \acs{SCS} methodology defines a unified approach for requirements elucidation and sets the expectation that requirements will change at any iteration of the project.  }

\paragraph{Requirement Scope}

\citeauthor{radekaWhenAgileGets2022} \autocite{radekaWhenAgileGets2022,iberleAgileGetsPhysical2022} observed a mismatch between the ``large batches'' that are often being iterated on by hardware models, compared to the smaller batches of work that take place before automated software verification, creating a disconnect between the delivery of hardware and software. To resolve this, they introduced the concept of a \textbf{``slice''}, which is defined as follows:
\begin{definition}[Slice]
	A \textbf{slice} is a set of hardware and software changes, as well as test suites, that describe a single set of testable behaviour \cite{radekaWhenAgileGets2022}.
	\label{def:slice}
\end{definition}
The authors provide the example of a printer feature that allows the device to print from a mobile app as an example of a slice.\marginnote{Based on \citeauthor{radekaWhenAgileGets2022}'s examples, in \acs{SCS}, example slices could be: \begin{itemize}[itemsep=-0.5ex]
	\item \emph{The satellite can point its solar panels towards the sun}.
	\item \emph{The satellite can operate during eclipse}.
	\item \emph{The satellite can send data to the ground station}.
\end{itemize}} In that sense, a slice can be seen as a less well-specified requirement that describes one of the general goals of a system.

The concept of slices can provide a useful starting point for early system designs of a system, which are then elaborated in more detail and with additions of more specific features and constraints. They represent the concept of \textbf{inheritance} or \textbf{traceability} defined in ECSS-E-ST-10-06C, which states that each requirement should be traceable back to a source requirements from which it derives \autocite{ECSS-E-ST-10-06C}. While slices ``pull the disparate development efforts together'' \cite{radekaWhenAgileGets2022}, they can still be broken down into more specific requirements that are applicable to specific parts of the component under development.

The above conversation already defines a two-tiered requirement hierarchy. However, most \acs{SCS} systems are considered \textbf{``systems-of-systems''}, consisting of multiple \textbf{parts} that interact with each other, and which can and may be developed independently. In the case of PocketQubes, these parts are subsystems that are designed to fit in the same structure together. In the case of ChipSats, the different parts of the satellite cannot be separated physically, but can still be \textbf{separated functionally}. It would be beneficial, therefore, to define a unit of analysis that represents the result of a functional and potentially physical decomposition of a system.

I will define this unit to be a \textbf{``module''}, defined as follows, based on the dictionary definition of the noun \autocite{merriam-websterDefinitionMODULE2026}:
\begin{definition}[Module]
	A \textbf{module} is an independently functional part of the total function or structure of a spacecraft that contains no other modules. \\%
	\emph{(Note that this definition is not directly related to the concept of modularity.)}
	\label{def:module}
\end{definition}

With this definition, one can finally define a three-tiered system requirement specification system, which starts from high-level modules, linked to slices, linked to requirements. This requirement specification method does not follow a strict ``one-to-many'' relationship, meaning that complex structures can be created as needed, but still in a restricted structure that can be supported by e.g.\@ spreadsheet tools. Even though traditionally the decomposition of a system into modules or subsystems would happen after the requirements have been specified, in the context of \acs{SCS} \begin{enumerate*}
	\item the decomposition into a few overarching modules is usually obvious from the offset, and 
	\item an emergent, iterative process can lead to future modifications.
\end{enumerate*}

Finally, when a project uses an automated functional testing process or framework, the specific testing objectives and goals have to be coded as part of a formally specified testing suite. This means that the \textbf{test suite definition becomes the technical specification itself} (\Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_automated_testing}). This capability even further reduces the need for a formal requirements specification, and reduces time by combining the requirements specification process with the test preparation actions.

\FloatBarrier
\subsection{Project Lifecycle Approach: The Sat-Scrum Model}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_satscrum}

	%What makes Agile interesting?

	%It is that it is a sequential yet evolutionary process. The number of iterations must not be predetermined but what happens inside an iteration is. This is the conicept of the sat-scrum model.

The previous section explained how the main goals and objectives of a space engineering project can be defined, typically in the first days of development. Before the rest of the development can continue, it is important to define the \textbf{lifecycle process model} that an \acs{SCS} project will use. The specific model will guide the high-level and daily actions of the team, specifying the trajectory from specification to qualification, and will inform the rest of the methods attached to the \acs{SCS} methodology.

A first version of a lifecycle process model can be inspired by the characteristics and processes of Agile methodologies. More specifically, \citeauthor{douglassAgileSystemsEngineering2015} \autocite{douglassAgileSystemsEngineering2015} lists six principles for Agile systems engineering% 
\marginnote{\citeauthor{douglassAgileSystemsEngineering2015}'s 6 principles of Agile Systems Engineering:\begin{enumerate}
		\item Work Incrementally
		\item Plan Dynamically
		\item Actively Reduce Project Risk
		\item Verify Constantly
		\item Integrate Continuously
		\item Validate Frequently
\end{enumerate}} which involve dynamic, iterative/incremental work, with verification/validation cycles at the end of every iteration.
When comparing existing frameworks (\Cref{par:LITERATURE_agile_frameworks}), a disparity becomes quickly apparent: Agile Frameworks built for systems and large-scale deployments carry with them significant overhead and would not fit within the benefits of a proposed methodology; while lower-overhead Agile frameworks are built around software development only, and prescribe day-to-day individual operations or enterprise structures that are not within the scope of this work.

As such, it is beneficial to synthesise a lifecycle process model for \acs{SCS}. The \textbf{\emph{Scrum}} framework, in particular, has already gathered attention in the hardware engineering community \autocite{bottAnalysisTheoriesSupporting2019,doveFundamentalsAgileSystems2014} and has been implemented in previous CubeSat systems and subsystems \autocite{honore-livermoreDigitalEngineeringDevelopment2022,garzanitiEffectivenessScrumMethodology2019}. \citeauthor{boehmBalancingAgilityDiscipline2004} \autocite{boehmBalancingAgilityDiscipline2004} describes Scrum as covering the management, risk handling, and customer interfacing processes of the entire lifecycle of a project, which can range from a system built by an individual to a business system.\marginnote{Note that the, while scaled tailorings of Scrum described in \Cref{sec:LITERATURE_scrum} might seem suitable for systems more complex than software, such as \acs{SCS}, they instead cover the more administrative aspects of larger organisations, and are therefore out of scope for this work.} Finally, the suitability of Scrum for the \acs{SCS} context, particularly \textbf{``hobbyists''} and \textbf{``smallsat developers''}, is supported by evaluating the typical environmental characteristics of these projects, which are generally compatible with Scrum's principles (\Cref{tab:mission-characteristics}).

For the remainder of this section, I will tailor the Scrum process for use in \ac{SCS} systems. This tailoring will roughly follow the steps proposed by ECSS-S-ST-00-02C \autocite{ECSS-S-ST-00-02C}, which include the identification of project characteristics, and selection/modification of applicable requirements, after going through each aspect of the method. Apart from the official definition of Scrum \cite{schwaverDefinitiveGuideScrum2020}, I will rely on external elaborations \cite{boehmBalancingAgilityDiscipline2004} and criticisms \autocite{kustersScreamGuide2020,jeffriesDarkScrum2016} of incorrect applications of it.

\paragraph{Tailoring of The Scrum Process}

\subparagraph{Scrum Team}
Scrum calls for small teams of ``10 or fewer'' people, responsible for all product activities (from conceptualisation to verification) without any additional hierarchies. This matches the typical environment present in \acs{SCS} projects.

While most participants in the team will be developers, responsible for technically advancing each iteration, Scrum specifies the role of a \emph{Product Owner}, responsible for managing and communicating work packages.

The final role defined by the framework is that of the \emph{Scrum Master}, who is responsible for maintaining the Agile processes and coaches the team towards self-management. While it is seen as a critical role by the Scrum guide, it has been by far the most controversial role in practical settings, especially when its responsibilities are not handled correctly, and is usually limited by the team's needs and competencies \autocite{kustersScreamGuide2020,doshiDownfallScrumMaster2025}. As such, I will not include the role of Scrum Master in the tailoring of this methodology -- however, the tasks performed are still essential, especially for younger teams that are not experienced in adherence to specific methodologies.

\begin{table*}[h]
	\caption{Sat-Scrum team responsibilities}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}XX@{}}
	\toprule
 \textbf{Product Owner}                                            & \textbf{Developers}                                                                          \\ \midrule
	 Creating and communicating Product Goal and Product Backlog Items & Creating a plan for each Sprint                                                              \\
	 Interacting with customers and stakeholders                       & Advancing the development of the product                                                     \\
	 Delegating responsibilities to others, if needed                  & Participating in design, development, manufacturing, integration and verification operations \\
	 Removing impediments to the Scrum process                         & Holding each other accountable                                                               \\
	 Organising Scrum events                                           & Participating in Scrum events                                                                \\
	 Setting up interactions with stakeholders                         & Participating in interactions with stakeholders                            \\
	\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table*}

\subparagraph{The Sprint}
Scrum works around the primary concept of a \emph{Sprint}, a  ``fixed event of one month or less'' that has the goal of producing a new iteration of a working product when it ends. In Scrum, each Sprint starts with a planning phase\footnote{The planning phase aims to answer questions such as why is the Sprint valuable, what can be done during it, and how this work will be done.},  continues with daily timeboxed meetings, and finishes with a review and retrospective\footnote{The purpose of the Sprint Review is to assess technical completion and future goals, while the Retrospective is a programmatic assessment of processes and interactions \cite{schwaverDefinitiveGuideScrum2020}.}.

The original Scrum describes a predefined sprint duration. However, in hardware-heavy projects, the duration of each iteration is heavily dependent on external and unplannable factors, such as procurement and manufacturing lead times, or component and equipment failures. \citeauthor{douglassAgileSystemsEngineering2015} \autocite{douglassAgileSystemsEngineering2015} advocates varying the iteration length by \( \pm 25\% \) to make sure that a coherent amount of work can be performed. The duration of each Sprint will also be generally larger in hardware-based projects, as it is physically limited by the tasks of procurement and manufacturing, which can take 1 week or more, each. These tasks are also often on the critical path of development, as they prevent the verification and validation of the produced system until they are completed.
In industrial environments, iterations can last between 2 to 8 weeks, or even longer \autocite{cooperAgileDevelopmentManufacturing2023}. However, within the context of Scrum combined with \acs{SCS}, 8 weeks will represent a reasonable upper bound for iteration length.

These \ac{COP}-related constraints add time uncertainty to each iteration, and make the direct application of Scrum difficult, as they have the potential of adding ``waiting time'', when developers cannot verify a product before a certain external process is completed \cite{cooperAgileDevelopmentManufacturing2023}. The context of \acs{SCS} development makes it possible to control these so-called ``deadtime periods'', by proactively reducing external lead time.\marginnote[-1cm]{Reduction of external lead time can be done by:\begin{itemize} %vadOK
		\item Moving manufacturing in-house, to the degree possible
		\item Collaborating with entities also following an Agile methodology
		\item Preparing ready-to-use alternatives in case an external product is not ready on time
\end{itemize}}
Mitigating this difficulty by separating an iteration into predetermined, sequential steps, runs the risk of propagating the drawbacks of waterfall-based methods. It is therefore important to maintain some degree of flexibility and allow parallel work during each Scrum iteration.
%However, past attempts to mitigate this difficulty by separating an iteration into pre-determined, sequential steps have received criticism, due to propagating the drawbacks of waterfall-based methods. The original Scrum itself avoids prescribing the distribution and allocation of work, as that is team-specific and dependent on the self-organisation between developers.It is therefore important to maintain some degree of flexibility and allow parallel work during each Scrum iteration.

\begin{figure*}[h]
	\begin{adjustbox}{width=1.1\textwidth,center}
	\begin{tikzpicture}[

	\caption{Activity diagram of one Sat-Scrum iteration}
	\label{fig:m_sat_scrum_iteration}
\end{figure*}

\Cref{fig:m_sat_scrum_iteration} presents a proposed activity diagram for a Sat-Scrum Sprint, expanded with activities that are typical in an \acs{SCS} project and that can be performed in parallel. These activities are described as follows:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-1ex]
	\item \textbf{Design} activities describe the ``architecting'' work that may be necessary before producing a hardware product or a software function. They can involve:
	\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
		\item High-level architecting
		\item Component selection
		\item \acs{PCB} schematic and layout design
		\item Structural component design
	\end{itemize}
	
	The design phase is defined to last \qtyrange[range-units = single]{1}{2}{weeks}, but can take longer during the early stages of a project, where iterations are expected to last longer. However, this design time is significantly shorter than traditional, often year-long design phases in space projects. In Scrum, instead of a ``\textbf{\acf{BDUF}}'', the design definition process is split into smaller chunks \autocite{watermanHowMuchUpFront2015}.
	\item \textbf{Procurement} and \textbf{manufacturing} activities involve waiting for the preparation of physical components by external providers or internal process completion, and essentially prevent the verification of the design made in the previous step, acting as blockers in the critical path. After procurement and manufacturing is completed, \textbf{integration} involves ``putting those components together'', which can include breadboard prototyping, soldering, cabling, and assembly.
	\item \textbf{Software Development} is defined as a separate activity, as the act of writing, and sometimes testing (see \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_softwaredevelopment}) software can often be performed independent of the designed hardware in the previous steps. \acs{SCS} are also software-heavy systems that require appropriate time dedicated to software completion, which has been demonstrated to be often neglected in CubeSat projects \autocite{sanchoLessonsLearnedFYS2021}.
	
	% Talk about software testing, link to HIL
	\item \textbf{Auxiliary Activities} define those activities that are essential to support a hardware project, can be completed in parallel to work towards the Product Goal, and do not fit in any other category. They are usually team and environment-dependent and can include:
	\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
		\item Preparing and managing testing infrastructure and equipment
		\item Performing domain-specific analyses
		\item Finalising design, development and verification tasks that were not completed in the previous sprint
		\item Designing and developing support tools (e.g.~test jigs, adapters, cables)
		\item Conducting \acf{DEVOPS}
		\item Negotiating with external shareholders
		\item External design reviews
	\end{itemize}
	
	While there is no single definition for these activities as they are heavily project-specific, experience from case studies has shown that they are key to reducing the development and integration time and reducing risk.
	\item \textbf{Verification, Validation \& Improvement} activities represent \textbf{inspection and testing} of the, now manufactured, product's hardware and software. Testing may include functional verification only, or also involve environmental verification which may be done in external facilities.
	
	In software-only Scrum and Agile engineering, verification \& validation is not considered as a separate phase, as it is made in tandem with development by means of \ac{CI}/\ac{CD}. In Sat-Scrum, this is represented by the ``improvement'' keyword, which requires this almost-instant feedback loop, to the degree possible.
	
	The improvement step prescribes the correction of faults and non-conformances. In the case of software, this can be done swiftly. In the case of hardware, reworks of layout errors and component replacement can sometimes be done.
	
	The duration of this phase can be long, depending on the complexity of testing, the need for external 
	facilities, and the amount of non-conformances discovered. A common guideline is to allocate 50\% of a project's development time into testing activities \autocite{venturiniImprovingMissionSuccess2017}.
	Experience from past projects has shown that testing time is often underestimated. It is therefore important for developers to account the duration of this phase, adding appropriate margins.\marginnote{For example, when external testing is involved, this phase should last at least 3 weeks, including: \begin{itemize}
			\item Preparatory activities (such as test fixture design) performed before the verification phase, as part of the \emph{auxiliary} activities
			\item 1 week for functional testing of the \ac{DUT}
			\item 1 week for environmental testing
			\item 1 week for correction of errors
		\end{itemize}}

	\item \textbf{Sprint Planning, Review \& Retrospective} activities represent the administrative tasks that need to take place at the beginning and the end of each Sprint, in order to clarify the goals of the iteration, the work that can and needs to be done, the results of the previous processes, and the status of the project.
	
	The Scrum guide separates reviews and retrospectives, with the review addressing the technical aspects and \textbf{changes in the environment and external requirements}, often with external stakeholders present, and the retrospective focusing on interactions within the team. The guide also recommends timeboxing these events to reduce lost time.

	Finally, the Scrum guide also defines a 15-minute ``Daily Scrum'' meeting, in order to allow developers to create an actionable plan for a single day of work. In practice, it might be difficult to guarantee a daily meeting time for student teams, teams working asynchronously, or teams with members who have a limited duty cycle of contribution to the project. Daily meetings also have a generally negative reputation and perception in the software engineering community \autocite{singhExploringLivedExperiences2023,cooperAgileDevelopmentManufacturing2023}. As daily meetings are therefore more closely related to Project Management, I consider their evaluation as out-of-scope for this work.
\end{itemize}

Each Sat-Scrum Sprint can therefore last between \qtyrange{4}{8}{} working weeks, with the exact time being often unpredictable.

\subparagraph{Definition of Done}

After the activities of the Sprint have been defined, the next question is \emph{what should a Sprint achieve} and \emph{what should the goal of a Sprint be}?

The Scrum Guide \cite{schwaverDefinitiveGuideScrum2020} defines a \emph{Definition of Done} as a shared understanding of the \emph{quality measures that a product should meet at the end of a Sprint}. In practical terms, the Definition of Done may be a list of requirements or features different for each Sprint\footnote{Original Scrum links the Definition of Done to a single increment, nothing that a Sprint may contain one or more increments. However, for simplicity, I will consider here that a Sprint produces a single increment.}, or a common checklist of actions that need to have been completed.

The Definition of Done is linked to the \emph{Sprint Goal}, which is a \textbf{single objective} that the team needs to achieve during the Sprint. The singularity of the goal implies that it can be described in one single sentence. 

The Definition of Done and Sprint Goal are agreed upon at the \emph{sprint planning} phase of each iteration, and usually refer to characteristics of the entire product. The more specific work packages that need to be completed during the Sprint are defined as items of \textbf{backlogs}, which are ordered lists of ``what is needed to improve the product''. The set of all work packages related to a product are listed in the Product Backlog, and then copied to the Sprint Backlog during the planning phase. It is then the responsibility of the developers to advance the development of the product by implementing specific Sprint Backlog items. In the case of \acs{SCS} engineering, the Definition of Done and Sprint Goal can be typically linked to the success of the performed functional and environmental verification campaigns, which in turn verify that specific requirements are complied to.

It is not always possible to complete Sprint Backlog items within a single iteration. In this case, these items can be postponed and pushed to a future Sprint.

\newthought{The Scrum guide does not describe a \textbf{review}} process as part of the design and development activities. This expectation of self-organisation conflicts with the strict review hierarchy that is expected in traditional space systems.
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_reviews_general}

The importance of reviews can be ascertained from the software development field, where various studies have been performed on the efficacy of \emph{code reviews}. Meta-analyses of expert opinion surveys show that code reviews increase perceived code quality, understanding and defect minimisation \autocite{badampudiModernCodeReviews2023,yangRoadmapModernCode2026}. While this is supported by data-backed studies \autocite{kononenkoInvestigatingCodeReview2015,bavotaFourEyesAre2015,krutauzCodeReviewMeasures2020,kemererImpactDesignCode2009}, these findings are not consistent, especially depending on reviewer workload and experience.

Given these positive indications, \acs{SCS} can potentially also benefit from a design review scheme, when experienced developers are involved, as an auxiliary activity. Alternatively, automated \acs{LLM}-based reviews have also been proposed and are supported by typical engineering artifact platforms, triggered when a change is pushed to a repository \autocite{rasheedAIpoweredCodeReview2024}. For non-text artifacts\footnote{For example, screenshots of engineering drawings or \acs{PCB} layouts}, multi-modal \acs{AI} models can be used, and since real-time responses are not required, the environmental impact of \acsp{LLM} can be reduced.


\paragraph{High-level planning}

\marginnote{Another feature of Sat-Scrum's definition is the fixed nature of iteration durations: If a defect is discovered 15 days after a 30-day iteration starts, the prescribed Scrum process requires waiting 15 more days until a new, non-defective product is iterated on.}
\acs{SEBOK} describes Agile lifecycle process models as \emph{evolutionary sequential} models, in the sense that, the operational capabilities and performance of a system at each stage of the project lifecycle are not predetermined, but the sequence of activities within each iteration (in this case, sprint) is well defined
\autocite{sebok_drivers}.
Scrum and Sat-Scrum do not prescribe a certain number of iterations, and, therefore, do not create by default a prediction on the development time until a ``launch-ready'' version of a project is prepared. However, this can create a \textbf{conflict with projects that have a fixed deadline}, such as projects with \textbf{launch commitments}.

An adverse example for this situation would be if, during the last iterations, the product is shown to not fulfil all ``must'' requirements (\Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_requirements}), or contains critical defects. If a launch opportunity has been procured in this case, this situation would risk a cancellation or mission failure.

Possible work-arounds for this situation are as follows:
\begin{itemize}
	\item \textbf{Do not establish} a launch agreement until a ``Minimum Launchable Product'' has been produced, or there is high confidence that one will be produced soon.
	
	This option adds an additional delay to the time until launch, which however can be allocated to a potential development of the next mission, or the implementation of optional features (should/can requirements).
	\item \textbf{Reduce the scope} of the mission, by downgrading some requirements from \emph{must} to \emph{won't} classifications.
	
	The possibility of this option depends on an agreement between stakeholders and developers, and the requirement structure of the project, and might result in it changing its scope completely.
	\item \textbf{Accept the risk} that the product may not be ready for launch, in which case the agreement can be cancelled.
	
	This option adds a significant ``wasted'' financial, administrative and time cost, since launch preparations can take up a large part of a team's schedule.
	\item Allocate a \textbf{flexible} launch slot, if available, which can be postponed to a future date. This will require the possibility of a flexible option from the launch provider, or the cooperation between rideshare customers to possibly rearrange launch slots.
	
	In the ideal case, an Agile developer will cooperate with partners who also follow Agile workflows; however, in the case of space system launches, dates can be agreed multiple years before each launch, rendering this option impossible.
	\item Prepare a \textbf{fallback} satellite. 
	This is possible in the case of experienced teams that already have a product developed, and can quickly adapt it to an available launch slot as a back-up for the actual project.
\end{itemize}


\paragraph{Manufacturing}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_manufacturing}

As \acl{SCS} rely on hardware, a considerable effort has to be dedicated to assembly, manufacturing and integration of physical components. Compared to a waterfall lifecycle process model, Sat-Scrum requires this hardware to be available at the end of every iteration, increasing the load for these activities. In the context of \acs{SCS}, these activities can include, for example:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item Soldering and coating of \acsp{PCB}
	\item Additive (e.g.\@3D printing) or subtractive (e.g.\@ machining) of structural elements
	\item Assembly/crimping of cable harnesses
	\item Securing of fasteners
	\item Application of adhesive
\end{itemize}
These activities represent both the \emph{manufacturing} of new, custom-made components, or the \emph{rework} of already procured such components, because of design, manufacturing, or handling errors.

Traditionally, many of these activities have been performed by external manufacturers and suppliers. However, in \acs{SCS}, these activities are usually limited in number and complexity, and can be reliably performed in-house. This raises the \textbf{make-or-buy} question, on whether these activities should be outsourced, or performed in-house. The answer to this question involves an optimisation problem against non-recurring costs, quality, and experience \autocite{medina-serranoStrategicSourcingDeveloping2020}, noting however that both ``outsourcing'' and ``vertical integration'' have traditionally been associated with risks and hidden costs \autocite{hilmerStrategicOutsourcing1994,barthelemyHiddenCostsIT2001}.

In the context of Agile, developer co-location and rapid response to changes is encouraged, which often clashes with typical outsourcing expectations \autocite{durmicAdoptingAgileScrum2024}. A \citeyear{lemmaSystematicLiteratureReview2025} meta-analysis on the implementation challenges of Scrum identified multi-team environments as one of the main mentioned themes, citing ``inter-team coordination challenges [...] as the most pertinent large-scale Agile challenges demanding immediate research attention'' \autocite{lemmaSystematicLiteratureReview2025,moeTrendsLargeScaleAgile2016}.

Within the context of \acs{SCS}, the typically limited resources available generally prevent the collaboration with manufacturers who can support direct communication and instant delivery. Instead, based on the performed case studies, the procurement of assembled boards and manufactured components has generally involved a 1-month long lead time, which cannot be easily supported by short Sat-Scrum iterations. This duration is also influenced by internal organisational limits, such as long procurement processes.

Therefore, the recommendation for Sat-Scrum is to perform \text{manufacturing in-house}, to the degree that is possible, by acquiring the necessary equipment and expertise to complete these hardware-based activities.
The most common in-house activity in \acs{SCS} will typically be \textbf{assembly of unpopulated \aclp{PCB}} through component soldering. Teams are therefore encouraged to invest in equipment such as reflow ovens to be able to test and validate \acsp{PCB} shortly after design.\footnote{In university or large industry contexts, inspection, manufacturing and testing facilities (e.g. ``fab labs'') are sometimes available in close proximity of the designers, and can be used.} These assets can further be used when rework or corrections are required, saving on the administrative overhead of returns.

Alternatively, when the \acl{FM} requires adherence to certain workmanship standards, a hybrid approach can be used: early iterations can involve use in-house manufacturing, while flight-ready models can involve external suppliers.

\begin{figure*}[h]
	\includegraphics{graphics/SatScrum.pdf}
	\caption{Example of a Sat-Scrum based project lifecycle}
	\label{fig:m_scrum_example}
\end{figure*}

\paragraph{Representation}

The selection of a lifecycle process model is one of the most critical decisions that have to be taken early in a project, as it is also the driver for the rest of the management decisions and stakeholder communication.
A question that can then come up is how the proposed lifecycle process should be visualised and communicated internally, and to external stakeholders. Teams can chose an ad-hoc representation (as in \Cref{fig:m_scrum_example}), a representation created by their used tools, or a standardised one.

Gantt charts are a common tool used in project scheduling, showing the durations and dependencies between different parallel activities \autocite{honore-livermoreIntegratingAgileSystems2022}. They also provide utility by allowing developers to predict the durations of tasks and the critical path of a project. \citeauthor{douglassAgileSystemsEngineering2015} \autocite[62]{douglassAgileSystemsEngineering2015} provides a statistical estimation method for task predictions. Gantt charts can be augmented with resource allocation or simplified by showing monthly planning only, as required. In any case, they need to be updated at the end of each iteration, to account for the updated project status and knowledge.

% vadOK
\marginnote{%
	The statistical estimation method by \citeauthor{douglassAgileSystemsEngineering2015} \cite{douglassAgileSystemsEngineering2015} calculates the estimate of a task duration as:
	\[
	E = \frac{E_{20\%} + 4 E_{50\%} + E_{80\%}}{6} E_c
	\]
	where \(E_{x\%}\) is an estimate of a specific confidence (i.e.~that the task will be completed within that duration at least \(x\%\) of the time), and \(E_c\) a subjective confidence factor.

	He proposes a different ``customer schedule'' and an internal ``goal schedule'', based on the pessimistic \(E_{80\%}\) and optimistic \(E_{20\%}\) estimates respectively.
}

Another idea, based on \citeauthor{ruedePatternLanguageComplexmode2023}'s approach \autocite{ruedePatternLanguageComplexmode2023}, is to use a visual pattern-based vocabulary to represent goals, processes and outputs of each iteration.
An example vocabulary is shown in \Cref{fig:m_visual_vocabulary}, where the intended use of an assembled product is represented with squares, tests are represented by diamonds, and processes by circles.
These symbols can then be arranged vertically, when happening in parallel, or horizontally, to show a sequence of activities. This representation then allows exploring how a spacecraft project differs from a typical software Scrum iterations due to the number of various activities that need to be performed on the system, but can usually not be combined in a single iteration.

\FloatBarrier
\subsection{Project Lifecycle Approach: The Partition-Based Sat-Scrum Model}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_slicebased}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_partitions}

\todo{Add the missing figure, add some explanatory images as examples perhaps}

\citeauthor{iberleAgileGetsPhysical2022} noticed that industrial Agile environments incorporating software and hardware have to deal with confusion and conflicts when the work of different engineering teams has to be integrated \autocite{iberleAgileGetsPhysical2022}. In traditional Scrum, a single work package that can be completed by one person within a Sprint may require additional interfacing functionality to translate to a product feature or function. \citeauthor{iberleAgileGetsPhysical2022} provide an example of three teams (hardware, firmware, cloud software) collaborating on the same product, who however implement different iteration durations and have to deal with an iteration lag when errors need to be fixed in another part of the product. This is exacerbated by the fact that software teams can affort to perform short iterations, while hardware teams have to spend multiples of that time to produce and verify a physical product \autocite{cooperAgileDevelopmentManufacturing2023}. 

This criticism of Scrum for systems engineering essentially targets the ``Definition of Done'' and ``Sprint Goal'' aspects, which by default are either expressed in a simple form, or refer to a requirement against the product as a whole. Instead, \citeauthor{iberleAgileGetsPhysical2022} proposes the concept of a \textbf{slice} (\Cref{def:slice}), which describes a single batch of integration/testing that defines a single, typically user-visible, feature. Apart from the features only, a slice also defines a rough testing plan that will be followed after integration is completed. Then, \textbf{every iteration/Sprint is defined to cover only a single or more slices}. Developers from the hardware and software disciplines can therefore select the work packages that work towards this common goal.

% Partition-based is different from slice-based since we are actually making only a subset of the system. But there is no need to mention this because there is no need to claim difference against slice-based.

In Sat-Scrum, a complete iteration de facto involves the entire product. However, building upon the concept of slices, usually it is only parts of the product that need to be iterated on, especially for more complex ``systems of systems''. A slice will usually match only one physical (i.e.\@ structural) or functional part of the entire system. These parts were defined above as \textbf{modules} (\Cref{def:module}). When an iteration is tied to one or more slices, these slices will be, by definition, assigned to one or more physical or functional modules. It can therefore be inferred that each iteration is tied to one or more modules, or that \textbf{during each iteration, only a subset of system modules are altered}.

A final layer of abstraction has to be added, since, based on my definition, \textbf{a module won't always represent a physically ``buildable'' or ``interfaceable'' part that can be manufactured and verified independently of other modules}. For example, in the case of a ChipSat, the power system can represent a single module, however, as it is part of a \acl{PCB}, it may not be practically manufactured and interfaced independently to the rest of the system. When the iteration is tied to such a module, \textbf{the developers will have to iterate on this module only, but construct and verify the rest of the circuit board as well}.

\begin{figure}
	\begin{subfigure}{.3\textwidth}
		\centering
		\includegraphics{renders/pocketqube_subsystem}
		\caption{\label{subfig:m_slice_subsystem}}
	\end{subfigure}\hfill
	\begin{subfigure}{.3\textwidth}
		\centering
		\includegraphics{renders/pocketqube_slice}
		\caption{\label{subfig:m_slice_slice}}
	\end{subfigure}\hfill
	\begin{subfigure}{.3\textwidth}
		\includegraphics{renders/pocketqube_system}
		\caption{}
		\label{subfig:m_slice_system}
	\end{subfigure}%
	\caption[Example of (a) module, (b) partition, (c) system]{Example of (a) module, (b) partition, (c) system. Renders based on QUBIK/POQUITO \cite{daradimosQUBIK1pPocketQube2024}
	}
	\label{fig:m_partition_example}
\end{figure}

As a next logical step therefore, one can define a \textbf{partition} as a set of modules that not only outline a \emph{functional} boundary of the system, but also a unit which can be physically manufactured and tested on its own.
\begin{definition}[Partition]
	A partition is a set of \hyperlink{def:module}{modules}, whose combination can be manufactured and verified, and represents a useful proportion of the system that can potentially be an iteration target.
	\label{def:partition}
\end{definition}

A synonym of partition is a ``configuration'' of subsystems.

An example of the difference between a \emph{module}, \emph{partition} and \emph{system} is shown in \Cref{fig:m_partition_example}, where a module is a single subsystem\footnote{In this case, the subsystem is also a distinct physical unit that can be manufactured and tested independently. However, this might not be the case in a ChipSat project, where a subsystem may be part of a \acl{PCB}.}, the system is an entire PocketQube, and the partition is \textbf{a stack of some subsystems}, without side/top walls or mechanisms. In this example, the developers might have decided to work on a partition because:
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-1ex]
	\item Working on a single subsystem prevents the generation of knowledge about interface compatibility, running fit checks, and practicing the assembly of the spacecraft.
	\item They can perform rudimentary procurement and design activities for the required interfacing elements, in order to generate minimal prior knowledge about these components.
	\item Changes or design decisions on the rest of the design depend on the results gained from elements of this partition only.
	\item They do not want to spend additional time within a single iteration to work on the entire system.
\end{enumerate}

In CubeSat systems, an example of a well-known commonly defined partition is that of a ``\textbf{FlatSat}''\footnote{Sometimes referred to as an Engineering Test Unit} \autocite{desouzaFlightSoftwareDevelopment2022,nasacubesatlaunchinitiativeCubeSat101Basic2017}, where all subsystems of a satellite are laid out, connected and tested in the same level, before their integration-ready versions are combined in the CubeSat stack.
The FlatSat method is often supported by harnesses, boards or test jigs that facilitate the interconnection of all subsystems, provide ready-to-use voltage rails, and expose test points for observability. These benefits can often be also attained in an \acs{SCS} environment \autocite{raduDelfiPQFirstPocketqube2018}, especially for larger form factors. However, developers need to consider whether the added effort to create a configuration not present in the flight design is worthwhile; especially for simple designs with few interfaces, the results of a FlatSat might be also obtainable from a stacked configuration, or an integrated system build.

In an extreme interpretation of \Cref{def:partition}, a subsystem's \textbf{software} can be considered as a partition, if there is adequate infrastructure to ``manufacture'', execute and test it without the linked hardware (e.g.\@ using an emulator\footnote{Similarly to a FlatSat, care has to be taken when creating support for a configuration not present in the final flight model. The development of mocks and emulation support can potentially be a particularly large undertaking if no reusable libraries are integrated.}). Indeed, some projects may want to perform system-wide iterations on software only if necessary, for example for software-heavy projects. 

\newthought{Compared to} system-level Sat-Scrum as presented in \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_satscrum}, \emph{partition-level Sat-Scrum} also addresses an inherent problem with Agile methods when applied to complex physical systems: During the first iterations in a project, only a few weeks are allocated towards design efforts. However, in hardware engineering, it is possible that many activities with a minimum baseline of effort need to take place in every iteration, such as design elaboration, analysis/optimisation, \acs{PCB} design/routing, \acs{BOM} consolidation, prototype building, and more. For a system with multiple in-house boards that require these processes, it may be impractical or impossible to complete this design effort within a single iteration, thus requiring either lengthening the iteration, or the use of partition-based Sat-Scrum.

Partition-based iterations are also similar to the V-style or incremental approaches favoured by CubeSat projects, where distinct subsystems are manufactured first, often in parallel, before being integrated into the entire system.
The main differentiating element of
a partition-based approach are the recommendation to always iterate on \textbf{a combination of more than one module at a time} in order to encourage interface definition through verification instead of specification. Partition Sat-Scrum also encourages the flexible definition and usage of partitions, which can be done between system-wide iterations when project needs dictate.

The main disadvantage of partition-based Sat-Scrum, compared to iterating strictly system-wide, is that it defers iterating on some parts of the system (and therefore also interfaces) to  later time points, therefore either reducing the total knowledge gained within a fixed duration, or increasing the total development time of a project.
In extreme cases, developers may be enticed to delay working on parts of the system until the later stages of a project, thus risking that neither enough design nor iteration effort has been dedicated to them. For this reason, it is important to define and maintain a robust work management and planning approach.
\FloatBarrier
\subsection{Technical Planning Approaches}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_workpackage}

Almost all spacecraft development teams, even those following ad-hoc methodologies, have to decide \textbf{how to divide, organise and assign} the technical (but also administrative) activities that need to be performed in the project. These ``\textbf{work packages}''\footnote{The ``work package'' term may have different meanings in different contexts, for example referring to a large, months-long group of activities, but here I will use the term interchangeably with ``activity''.} are often the subject of questions such as:\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item How should a large activity be separated into a smaller activity?
	\item How can we estimate the duration and resources needed to complete an activity?
	\item Which team member(s) should be assigned to an activity?
	\item How can we verify successful completion of an activity?
	\item What data model should we use to link an activity to a requirement, slice or module?
	\item Which tasks are blocking other tasks, and what is the critical path of the project?
	\item How do we record and store the answers to the above questions?
\end{itemize}

In traditional Scrum, these activities are part of a highly visible backlog that is continuously updated by developers themselves in real-time \autocite{schwaverDefinitiveGuideScrum2020}. This is usually supported by the popularised \textbf{Kanban} methods, whose original concept involved posting features on a physical board where developers could interact with post-it notes, but is now supported by specialised software tools \autocite{kanbanGuide}. To assign estimated effort and priorities to these tasks, \textbf{story point} calculation methods or heuristic scoring models such as RICE (Reach, Impact, Confidence, Effort) are used \autocite{mcbrideRICESimplePrioritization2018}.

In theory, technical planning supported by software tools can easily answer all of the above posited questions. However, in practice, developers have expressed frustration both in task management processes \autocite{singhExploringLivedExperiences2023} and tools \autocite{hadiCriticalSuccessFactors2021a,johanssonHowCanAtlassian2017,correiadasilvaOptimizingUserExperience2025}. Given that technical planning is an activity that developers need to be informed about and participate in daily, it makes sense intuitively that the overhead of any task management process and tool needs to be minimal and the benefit significant, in order to both improve the productivity and comfort of team members.

I have classified most technical planning tools that are presently in either of two categories:\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item \textbf{Project management-centric} approaches, involving high-level planning approaches (such as Gantt charts) and supported by tools such as Microsoft Project; and
	\item \textbf{Software development-centric} approaches, which are tailored to the software engineering discipline, involve low-level task planning, and are linked with software-specific features, such as code reviews and Continuous Integration.
\end{enumerate}
When a team chooses to use an approach not covered by available (and easy-to-use) tools, they may decide to use a \textbf{spreadsheet} or \textbf{\acf{ERP}} solution, after customising it to the specific data model that has been agreed on. Alternatively, practical paper-based approaches can also be used, such as traditional Kanban, provided that the team is co-located\footnote{Complete co-location of teams is highly unlikely given the increasing popularity and benefits of home-working \cite{honore-livermoreManagingProductDevelopment2021}. Even if a team needs to physically work on hardware devices and be present on location, usually different rooms are used for meetings, design, manufacturing, integration and testing, making a post-it-based approach impractical.\label{txt:colocation}}.

\newthought{Within the context of \acs{SCS}}, the following questions then need to be answered:\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item Who should be responsible for responding to the questions in the beginning of this section, and how should they be answered?
	\item What is a reference data model that can represent work packages in the \acs{SCS} methodology?
	\item What are the characteristics of a software tool that can effectively support the \acs{SCS} technical planning process?
\end{enumerate}

The Scrum method attempts to answer the first question of \emph{Who} and \emph{How} to manage work packages, by employing the concept of \textbf{self-organising teams} \autocite{schwaverDefinitiveGuideScrum2020}. Tasks are elaborated in meetings which occur daily and before each iteration. Work packages are elaborated through a collaboration between the developers and the product owner. Scrum also defines task management aspects that permeate the previous processes, including the fact that developers need to hold each other accountable.

A potential data model for task management would have to support the typical attributes of work packages defined typically in Agile environments, such as:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-3ex]
	\item Story points (or estimated time)
	\item Priority
	\item Current status
	\item Target iteration
	\item Assigned team member(s)\footnote{Potentially more than 1 for strictly collaborative tasks}
\end{itemize}
with attributes typically found in project management models, such as:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-3ex]
	\item Task dependencies/blocking tasks
	\item Resource dependencies (facilities, manufacturing, external suppliers)
	\item Linked requirement
\end{itemize}
along with attributes specific to the \acs{SCS} methodology:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-3ex]
	\item Linked slice
	\item Linked module
	\item Linked partition
\end{itemize}

Some of these attributes are already implemented in some form across available task tracking software. For example, \emph{Jira} defines the concepts of an \textbf{\emph{Epic}} and a \textbf{\emph{Component}}, which can be linked to \emph{slices} and \emph{modules} respectively. Alternatively, I provide a sample data model for technical planning in \Cref{fig:m_uml_planning_draft}.

\begin{figure}[p]
	\includegraphics{plantuml/WPDataModel.pdf}
	\caption{\acs{UML} data model for a representation of technical planning in the \acs{SCS} methodology.}
	\label{fig:m_uml_planning_draft}
\end{figure}

A procedural trade-off that has to be kept in mind  is that the more attributes are added to a task/work tracking model, the more the cognitive load of its maintainers increases, leading to either an undesirable increase in overhead, or more distractions from actual development. In most cases, modern software tools should be able to minimise this administrative overhead by offering convenience or automation functions to allow mass inspection, creation and modification of these artifacts.

\FloatBarrier
\subsection{Data Management: A Model-Based Approach}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_datamanagement}

\textbf{\ac{EDM}}, \textbf{Technical Data Management} or \textbf{Configuration Management}
is the process of acquiring, accessing, managing and using technical data that describe a system's specifications\footnote{``Specifications'' taken here to describe ``characteristics'', not ``requirements''.} \autocite{sheaNASASystemsEngineering2020,ECSS-M-ST-40C,ECSS-E-TM-10-25A}. 
The importance of this discipline is shown in the \acs{NASA} Systems Engineering Handbook, explaining that ``[Configuration Management] enables all stakeholders in the technical effort, at any given time in the life of a product, to use identical data for development activities and decision-making'' \cite{sheaNASASystemsEngineering2020}.

In the context of \ac{SCS}, ``Engineering Data'' can specifically mean:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item Requirements specifications and acceptance thresholds
	\item Product trees of the system and subsystem
	\item Interface definitions and specifications between components
	\item System performance parameters (e.g.~mission/phase duration, trajectory)
	\item Subsystem and component physical characteristics (e.g.~mass, dimensions, quantity, location)
	\item Subsystem and component performance characteristics (e.g.~efficiency, power consumption, capacity)
	\item Lists of associated objects (e.g.~operational modes, risk matrices, failure modes)
	\item Analysis and test results
	\item Domain-specific models (e.g.~\acs{CAD} models)\footnote{For the purpose of this work, I will not consider domain-specific models, as they are usually managed and accessed through different discipline-specific tools, in contrast to the rest of the data, which can be essentially represented in plain human-readable formats.}
\end{itemize}

It is also important to make a distinction between \ac{EDM} and the term ``documentation'', as ``documentation'' can be considered to additionally include explanations and instructions for a system in different formats.

As Engineering Data is most likely stored and handled in a digital format\footnote{While the use of paper or other physical methods is possible and has been proposed for parts of system design, such as task management, I consider here that the typical scope of an \acs{SCS} project includes remote participants and external stakeholders who need access to all engineering data, making their digital representation essentially a requirement. Also see \Cpageref{txt:colocation}.}, \ac{EDM} is essentially a discipline shaped and limited by the available tools.

In general, the success of an \ac{EDM} method does not only depend on the short and long-term correctness of the available data, but should also be focused on minimising the additional \ac{SYE} overhead.
A quantitative model for the evaluation of documentation effectiveness was proposed by \citeauthor{amblerStrategiesAgileSoftware2008} \autocite{amblerStrategiesAgileSoftware2008} as follows:
\[
	\text{Potential value} = \text{Produced value} \cdot C \cdot R \cdot U \cdot F \cdot T
\]%

\begin{table}[h]
	\colorlet{No}{MaterialRed600}
	\colorlet{Maybe}{MaterialOrange600}
	\colorlet{Yes}{MaterialTeal600}
	\def\yes{\textcolor{Yes}{Yes}}
	\def\partially{\textcolor{Maybe}{Partially}}
	\def\no{\textcolor{No}{No}}
	\def\highbad{\textcolor{No}{High}}
	\def\highgood{\textcolor{Yes}{High}}
	\def\lowbad{\textcolor{No}{Low}}
	\def\lowgood{\textcolor{Yes}{Low}}
	\def\depends{\textcolor{Maybe}{Varies}}
	\centering
	\caption{Comparison of documentation methods}
	\label{tab:documentation}
	\begin{tabular}{@{}lllll@{}}
		\toprule
		& \multicolumn{2}{c}{Document-based} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Model-based} \\ \cmidrule(r){2-3} \cmidrule(r){4-5}
		& \textbf{Informal} & \textbf{Formal} & \textbf{Informal} & \textbf{Formal} \\ \midrule
		\textbf{Completeness} & \lowbad  & \highgood  & \depends  & \highgood  \\
		\textbf{Efficiency} & \highgood & \lowbad  & \highgood & \lowbad \\
		\textbf{Flexibility} & \depends & \highgood  & \lowbad  & \highgood  \\
		\textbf{Accessibility} & \depends & \lowbad  & \depends  & \lowbad  \\
		\textbf{Maintainability} &  \highgood & \lowbad  & \depends & \lowbad \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
\end{table}


\marginnote{where:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-0.5ex]
	\item Produced value is guesstimated by the producers of the artifact.
	\item \(C\) is the percentage of the artifact that is currently “correct”.
	\item \(R\) = The chance that the artifact will be read by the intended audience.
	\item \(U\) = The chance that the document that is actually understood by the intended audience.
	\item \(F\) = The chance that the advice contained in artifact will be followed.
	\item \(T\) = The chance that the advice will be trusted.
\end{itemize}
}
As I lack the statistical data to quantify \ac{EDM} approaches based on this equation, I instead define the following \textbf{criteria for \acl{EDM} processes}:
\begin{enumerate}
	\item \textbf{Correctness}: The System Description should contain factual information at any point in time.
	\item \textbf{Completeness}: The System Description should cover all aspects of the system to the degree of detail required for normal engineering work. This includes information about lower-level components and internal/external interfaces.
	\item \textbf{Efficiency}: The System Description should require a minimal amount of effort and time to produce and maintain.
	\item \textbf{Flexibility}: The System Description should be adaptable to different types of system structures, interfaces, and project hierarchies.
	\item \textbf{Accessibility}: The System Description should be easily readable, and require a minimal amount of time and actions to access the desired specification.
	\item \textbf{Maintainability}: The System Description should provide a way of quickly updating and propagating specification updates, without harming correctness.
\end{enumerate}


Traditionally, the description and documentation of system specifications have taken place via text documents.
The combination of human language with rich text capabilities (e.g.~images, tables, equations, references) has made it possible to represent most ordinary engineering data. In practical terms, text documents {follow a specific format such as \acs{OOXML} or \acs{MD}}. However, the use of prose can quickly become cumbersome as the complexity of a system increases, as it is difficult to represent modular and repeated structures, and the representation of data is not consistent.
\marginnote{In practical terms, document-based approaches lack \begin{enumerate*}
		\item \emph{maintainability}, as a change in one parameter does not update all of its dependents;
		\item \emph{accessibility}, as there is no well-defined structure that allows quick retrieval of information;
		\item \emph{efficiency}, as additions and modifications require tampering with entire documents.
\end{enumerate*}}

The shortcomings of document-based specification approaches (\Cref{tab:documentation}) have led to the adoption of model-based approaches, such as SysML. While \ac{MBSE} is not always used to represent the same data as text-based documentation, there is a significant overlap between the two, in terms of the definition of the system, its functions, and its interfaces.\footnote{This is also where most \acs{MBSE} documentation focuses, along with performance information, decision justifications, and test results.}

\citeauthor{hendersonValueBenefitsModelbased2021} have performed a literature review of 360 published works citing benefits of \ac{MBSE}, but concluded that there is insufficient evidence to definitively support these claims \autocite{hendersonValueBenefitsModelbased2021}. The authors also found that there is no consensus on the predominant benefits of \ac{MBSE}, if any.
\citeauthor{campoModelbasedSystemsEngineering2023} also investigated the perceived value of \ac{MBSE} from 60 academic sources, and found the counter-intuitive result that \textbf{different authors perceive the same project metrics as having been both worsened and improved by \acs{MBSE}}, with \emph{time, cost, consistency} and \emph{manage-complexity} listed as the overlapping benefits and drawbacks amongst the top 10 \autocite{campoModelbasedSystemsEngineering2023}.

While there is no systematic evidence linking the benefits of \acs{MBSE} to specific project or team characteristics, \citeauthor{campoModelbasedSystemsEngineering2023} found that the drawbacks of \acs{MBSE} can be linked to a lack of training or familiarity with the used tools. Regarding development time, they note that ``due to the significant time needed for building  a model of the system, implementation of MBSE on short-term projects  may not yield much, if any, return on investment.'' \cite{campoModelbasedSystemsEngineering2023}
This matches the expectation of a ``Systems Engineering sweet spot'', as explored in \Cref{sec:INTRODUCTION_defining_systems_engineering}.

While the use of a formal model might prove prohibitive for systems such as \acl{SCS}, an informal model-based approach has the potential to provide some of the perceived benefits of \acs{MBSE}, while reducing the overhead required to maintain such a model. As an example, I provide an opinionated list of requirements (\Cref{tab:m_edm_requirements}) and a graphical mockup (\Cref{fig:m_edm_mockup}) for such a tool. Alternatively, \citeauthor{uludagTailoringModelBasedSystems} have also performed a discipline-specific tailoring of tool-backed \acs{MBSE} for pocket-sized spacecraft, based on a power-first sizing approach \autocite{uludagTailoringModelBasedSystems}.

		A ``product tree'' structure is commonly used to represent different abstraction levels within a product, and is often seen in most \ac{EDM} tools. A single level of abstraction is equivalent to the model of each entity taking up one row in a spreadsheet.
		
		Compared to a Block Definition Diagram or an Internal Block Diagram, a tree itself cannot represent interfaces or relations between components, apart from hierarchy, which should however be enough for low-complexity projects.

While this is not a strict requirement, it can still function as a suggestion towards simplifying user interactions. \emph{Progressive Disclosure} has been cited as another User Interface pattern that can increase perceived simplicity without sacrificing functionality \cite{springerProgressiveDisclosureWhen2020,ProgressiveDisclosure}.

This expects users to implement the structure of their model themselves, and format it as they see fit.

Especially when users are asked to switch between different platforms, it may be difficult to maintain awareness of changes at the right time.
