\FloatBarrier
\section{Hardware Development Methodology}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_hardwaredevelopment}
In this section, I will go into more detail on the decisions behind selecting and designing an \acs{SCS} system and its components. \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_defining_design_solution} will explain the process to define a design solution, while \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_design_guidelines} will present concrete guidelines to aid decision-making during the design process. In \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_verification}, I will explain how to tailor the verification process that takes place at the end of every \acs{SCS} iteration, including recommendations about analysis and test frequency and fidelity. Finally, \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_operations} will provide an overview of the work done after a system has been designed, built, and launched.

\subsection{Defining a Design Solution}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_defining_design_solution}

The \acs{NASA} Systems Engineering Handbook \autocite{sheaNASASystemsEngineering2020}
defines a ``design solution definition process [that translates] high-level requirements [...] and the outputs of the Logical Decomposition Process into a design solution''. In Design Research (\Cref{def:design_research}), a ``design solution'' can be defined as a ``description or set of instructions to make something''\footnote{Also see \Cref{def:design}.} \autocite{loveDefinitionDesign2015}.
From a formal point of view, a Design Space can be defined as the set of all possible design solutions that satisfy a set of requirements; then, the process of Design Space Exploration selects a specific design solution that seems the most ``optimal'' based on project-specific criteria (such as cost, performance, or value). Different methods have been proposed since the mid-20\textsuperscript{th} century both to evaluate different design solutions, and to achieve the highest possible coverage of the design space during the exploration process  \autocite{griesMethodsEvaluatingCovering2004}.

CubeSat projects have traditionally followed less formal approaches to design \autocite{sousaCubeSatDevelopmentFramework2021}. The \acs{NASA} \acs{SYE} handbook defines the following steps to defining a design solution \autocite[66]{sheaNASASystemsEngineering2020}:\footnote{Presented here simplified}
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-3ex]
	\item Define alternative design solutions
	\item Analyse each design solution
	\item Select the best alternative
	\item Verify the solution
	\item If needed, repeat this process for lower-level products
\end{enumerate}

Based on the above, the two main questions that need to be answered when defining a design solution are:
\begin{itemize}
	\item How can one discover possible design solutions?
	\item How and under what criteria are design solutions evaluated?\footnote{Evaluating a design solution is a different process compared to the verification of a built system (\Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_verification}). Evaluation needs to predict the performance, feasibility and correctness of a design solution \emph{before} the solution is built.}
\end{itemize}

\newthought{Within the context of \acs{SCS}}, the design solution definition is one of the most critical aspects of a project's life cycle, as it is the process that converts the product's requirements to the actual product. It depends on the available technology, capabilities/preferences of the developers involved, and the available institutional support.
Usually, it involves the selection and arrangement of hardware components at a system or subsystem level.
\marginnote{Examples of design decisions that can be taken include:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0ex]
	\item Should the satellite be built around a modular subsystem stack or a monolithic structure?
	\item Should we use active or passive \ac{ADCS}?
	\item From which manufacturer should we procure solar cells?
	\item What type of surface treatment should we use for each part, if any?
\end{itemize}
Examples of design solution evaluation criteria can include:\begin{itemize}
	\item Number of features (``should have/could have'' requirements satisfied, see \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_requirements})
	\item Added cost
	\item Technical risk (due to technology maturity, redundancy, heritage etc.)
	\item Organisational risk (due to technology complexity, familiarity, \acs{COTS} availability etc.)
	\item Compatibility
	\item Any combination of the above
	\end{itemize}
}
Design decisions can often be binary in nature,\footnote{i.e.~an answer to a yes/no question} splitting the design space into two. Evaluation criteria for design solutions can be quantitative (such as analyses, measurements, experiments, heuristic scoring, or data reuse) or qualitative (such as consultations, reviews, comparisons, or \acs{SWOT} analyses) in nature. While \acs{SCS} are relatively simple systems, there is still high interdependence between different subsystems and parts, requiring a holistic approach to design, even between hardware and software.

\paragraph[Concurrent Design]{\acf{CDE}}
Traditionally, the initial design phases of a space system are performed using a Concurrent Engineering or \emph{\acf{CDE}} method, where all subsystem experts are gathered in a single location to iterate over the design elements of the system. These iterations are aided by automated simplified analyses, usually performed with simple spreadsheet software and mission analysis tools \autocite{bandecchiConcurrentEngineeringApplied1999}.
The \acs{CDE} method is supported by specialised tools that allow almost instant exchange of parameters and data between experts,\footnote{This software typically has features that assist with the design solution defininition. For example, different \emph{options} of parameter sets can be created, and then \emph{evaluated} based on algorithmically calculated metrics, answering the two main questions of design definition.} along with technical support in a physical location that facilitates screen sharing and online collaboration \autocite{ranaVisionNextGenConcurrent2021}. This exchange can also take place through online meetings \autocite{retselisAdaptationAcubeSATNanosatellite2022}, although \citeauthor{bandecchiConcurrentEngineeringApplied1999} have noted the importance of organic interactions to influence the design rather than cooperation during strictly scheduled meetings only \cite{bandecchiConcurrentEngineeringApplied1999}. The \acs{CDE} method is often viewed as an alternative to a centralised \acs{SYE} authority, which decides on the high-level design and subsystem requirements, which then flow down to individual vendors.

Within the context of the \acs{SCS} methodology, the design solution definition process has to be repeated in the beginning of every iteration (\Cref{fig:m_sat_scrum_iteration}). On the other hand, the small teams dedicated to \acs{SCS} are usually co-located or are able to communicate on a frequent basis. These characteristics make it possible to \textbf{implement \acs{CDE} during every iteration of the project}. This idea makes it possible to evaluate the different dependencies between disciplines and provide instant feedback on design changes.

\begin{margindefinition}[Timebox]
	Timeboxing refers to the practice of strictly limiting the duration of an activity (e.g.~a meeting, an iteration, or a process) to a predefined length of time. It is especially useful when diminishing returns are expected as the activity becomes longer.
\end{margindefinition}
Depending on the project, the \acs{CDE} sessions can follow a formal, tool-based structure, or an informal approach, where information is stored in documents or directly inserted into domain-specific models, where available. An important aspect is to keep these sessions timeboxed, as the primary strategy of generating information in Agile methodologies is through experimentation, and not up-front design. The resolution of the \acs{CDE} result can be better than a high-level subsystem specification, but needs not answer low-level questions that can be handled by engineers as part of their daily work.

\paragraph[On Big Design Up-Front]{On \acf{BDUF}}
Since common practices and the \acs{SCS} methodology separate design into more than one iteration, a question that often comes up is \emph{``how much time to spend on initial design''}.
The \acs{NASA} \acs{SYE} handbook notes how changing the design at a later point becomes harder or more costly, making the case for specifying an initial detailed design early in the project \cite{sheaNASASystemsEngineering2020}.
On the other hand, \citeauthor{watermanHowMuchUpFront2015} \cite{watermanHowMuchUpFront2015} claim that a trade-off between time spent in \acs{BDUF} needs to be made:
\begin{quote}
If an Agile software team spends too little time designing architecture up-front then the team faces increased risk and higher chance of failure; if the team spends too much time the delivery of value to the customer is delayed, and responding to change can become extremely difficult \autocite{watermanHowMuchUpFront2015}.
\end{quote}
The authors note the following factors as reducing the need or effectiveness of a \acl{BDUF}:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-3ex]
	\item Requirements instability
	\item Early value
	\item Team culture
	\item Customer agility
	\item Team experience
\end{itemize}
Conversely, they note that the existence of high technical risk and a need to reduce that risk reduce the effectiveness of iterative design. Therefore, the ideal decision on time spent for early design will vary based on the environment of each project. However, typical \acs{SCS} environments, coupled with the emergent features of such systems, can generally afford a reduced early design time.

\subparagraph{Mathematical Model for design effort}


The drawbacks of incorrect allocation of design time early or late in the project can be described in terms of time and cost overruns \begin{enumerate*}
	\item due to unnecessary design effort, or
	\item due to reworks because of errors uncovered late.
\end{enumerate*}
As the results of this trade-off are not selected beforehand, but are probabilistic in nature, a stochastic model can be created to model the design--verification--rework cycle.
This stochastic process for a single product iteration can be modelled as an absorbing Markov model (\Cref{fig:m_rework_markov}) \autocite{grinsteadChapter11Markov1998}.

\begin{figure}[h]
	\centering
	\begin{tikzpicture}[shorten >=1pt,node distance=2.1cm,]
		\node (q_0)  [state,initial] {Design};
		\node (q_1)  [state,right=of q_0]   {Rework};
		\node (q_2)  [state,right=of q_1]   {End};
		
		\path[->] (q_0) edge[bend right=10] node[above,yshift=1mm] {$p_{1}$} (q_1)
		edge[bend left=35] node[above] {$1-p_{1}$} (q_2)
		(q_1) edge [loop below] node        {$p_{2}$} ()
		edge [bend right=20] node[above,yshift=2mm]       {$1-p_{2}$} (q_2)
		(q_2)
		edge [loop below] node        {1} ();
	\end{tikzpicture}
	\caption{Representation of a Markov process describing the design--rework cycle}
	\label{fig:m_rework_markov}
\end{figure}

% simplified mathematical model can be created to describe the design--verification--rework cycle, based on the possibility of defects unidentified during the design.
For this model, I am making the following assumptions:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-1ex]
	\item A first transient state, \textbf{\emph{``Design''}}, represents the completion of the initial engineering effort to produce the iteration's result.
	
	A second transient state, \textbf{\emph{``Rework''}}, represents the completion of an added iteration with additional engineering effort to design and produce an extra product, in case of defects or unfulfilled needs found during the previous iteration.
	
	The final absorbing state, \textbf{\emph{``End''}}, represents a product which has completed its iteration's goals.
	\item The \emph{``Design''} state implies that verification of the current iteration (through analysis or testing) has been completed. There is a probability \( p_1 \) that the iteration has not succeeded in its goals, and the model starts transitioning to the \textbf{\emph{``Rework''}} state.
	
	After the \emph{``Rework''} state, there is a probability that the open issues have still not been resolved, or that new ones appeared during the rework process. In this case, there is a probability \( p_2 \) that the system transitions again to the \emph{``Rework''} state. In the case where an iteration was added only to address the issues identified in the previous iteration, I expect \( p_2 < p_1 \).
	\item States and transitions are associated with a mathematical \emph{cost}. This cost can represent time spent, monetary cost, or an objective function that combines different resources as needed. Transitioning to a state incurs the following costs:
	\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
		\item For the \textbf{\emph{``Design''}} state, the cost of the \emph{initial} design process \( C_{d0} \), the recurring manufacturing costs \( C_m \), and the cost of verification \( C_v \).
		\item For the \textbf{\emph{``Rework''}} state, the cost of the additional design \( C_d \), manufacturing \( C_m \) and \( C_v \).
	\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

Given the above, the problem of measuring the expected cost of the Markov process is reduced to calculating \emph{the expected number of visits to the \textbf{\emph{``Rework''}}} state.

\marginnote{The canonical representation of a transition matrix of an absorbing Markov chain is given by \cite{grinsteadChapter11Markov1998} as:
\[
\mathbf P = \left[
	\begin{matrix}
		\mathbf{Q} & \mathbf{R} \\
		\mathbf{0} & \mathbf{I}_r
	\end{matrix}
\right]
\]
where each row represents a source state, each column a destination state, \( \mathbf{Q} \) is a \( t \times t \) transition matrix and \( \mathbf{R} \) is a \( t \times r \) transition matrix.

For an absorbing Markov chain, the \(i,j\) entry of the \emph{fundamental matrix} \( \mathbf{N} \) is the expected number of times the chain is in state \( s_j \) if it starts in state \( s_i \) \cite{grinsteadChapter11Markov1998}, where:
\[
\mathbf{N} = \left( \mathbf{I}_t - \mathbf{Q} \right)^{-1}
\]

For a \( 2 \times 2 \) matrix, the inverse can be simply calculated as:
\[
\mathbf{N}^{-1} = \frac{1}{N_{11}N_{22} - N_{12}N_{21}} \left[
	\begin{matrix}
		N_{22} & -N_{12} \\
		-N_{21} & N_{11}
	\end{matrix}
\right]
\]
}
The Markov chain of \Cref{fig:m_rework_markov} has \( t=2 \) transient states and \( r=1 \) absorbing state.
Then the transition matrix is:
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{P} = \left[
	\begin{matrix}
		0 & p_1 & 1 - p_1 \\
		0 & p_2 & 1 - p_2 \\
		0 & 0   & 1
	\end{matrix}
	\right]
\end{equation}
The fundamental matrix is calculated as:
\begin{equation}
	%\nonumber
	\mathbf{N} = \left( \mathbf{I}_t - \mathbf{Q} \right)^{-1}
	%\nonumber
	= \left[
		\begin{matrix}
			1 & - p_1 \\
			0 & 1 - p_2
		\end{matrix}
	\right]^{-1}
	= \frac{1}{1 - p_2} \left[
		\begin{matrix}
			1 - p_2 & p_1 \\
			0       & 1
		\end{matrix}
		\right]
\end{equation}
Therefore, the expected number of visits to the \emph{``Rework''} state, starting from the \emph{``Design''} state, is:
\begin{equation}
	E_{\mathrm{rework}} = N_{12} = \frac{p_1}{1 - p_2}
\end{equation}

Considering the above assumptions, the total cost of the design--verification--rework cycle is:
\begin{equation}
	C = C_{d_0} + E_{\mathrm{rework}} C_d + \left( E_{\mathrm{rework}} + 1 \right) \left( C_m + C_v \right)
\end{equation}
and the systems engineering ``sweet spot'' can be found by tuning a project's engineering parameters to find the minimum of \( C \).

To provide an example, I will consider that the manufacturing costs \( C_m \) and verification costs \( C_v \) of a sample project are fixed. The initial design cost can be considered as a multiple of the redesign cost, i.e.\@ \( C_{d_0} = k_0 C_d \).
The probabilities of rework \( p_1,p_2 \) should be decreasing functions of the linked design costs \( C_{d_0}, C_d \) respectively. This function is generally not known. Different models have been proposed to link between the two and external confounding factors \autocite{leImpactsConcurrencyIteration2012,tanRelativeImpactEarly2017}. Example heuristic estimations of rework probabilities can be made with an exponential function: \cite{leImpactsConcurrencyIteration2012}: \begin{equation}
p(C) = e^{-kC} \text{ for some } k
\end{equation} or a sigmoid function \cite{kucharavyApplicationSshapedCurves2011}: 
\marginnote{\(k_\infty\) represents the asymptotic probability when cost goes to infinity. \( k_S \) represents the steepness of the curve.}%
\begin{equation}
p(C) = \frac{2 - 2\frac{k_\infty}{2} }{1+e^{k_S C}} + k_\infty.
\end{equation}

	ymax = 1.1,
    samples = 100,
    restrict y to domain = 0:1,

]

\end{figure}

\FloatBarrier
\subsection[SCS Design Guidelines]{\acs{SCS} Design Guidelines}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_design_guidelines}

This section will describe guidelines for various choices that have to be taken during the \acs{SCS} design process, starting from the overarching concepts of \textbf{Modularity} and \textbf{Reuse}, and continuing with more specific guidelines based on lessons-learned for interfaces and miscellaneous spacecraft design aspects.

\paragraph{Modularity}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_modularity}

A defining characteristic of CubeSats and, to a lesser extent, PocketQubes, is their \textbf{modular structure}.

\begin{definition}[Modularity]
	Modularity can be understood as the use of interfaces to connect different subsystems/components of a system, in a way that allows them to be separated and recombined in different ways.
\end{definition}
Modularity can be described in terms of:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item \textbf{Structural Modularity}, where the interfaces are physical, and
	\item \textbf{Electrical Modularity}, where the interfaces are electrical, and used to transfer power and/or data.
\end{itemize}

In CubeSats, modularity can be achieved through the use of PC/104-sized boards, along with a roughly-defined electrical interconnect specification \autocite{BoardSpecificationLibreCube}. Commercial CubeSat subsystems can then be placed inside a shared subsystem ``stack'', and often rearranged as needed. A list of protocols, such as \acs{I2C} or \acs{CAN}, allow the communication between subsystems of different manufacturers.
However, in practice, different subsystems may show incompatibilities with each other. There is also no shared specification for low-level interfaces, meaning that additional work may be needed to allow different subsystems to communicate.

For \acs{SCS}, modularity presents more challenges, as it introduces constraints to a system's design. For example:
\begin{itemize}
	\item In a PC/104 board, the 104-pin connector covers \qtyproduct{6 x 0.8}{cm} of the \qtyproduct{9.6 x 9.0}{cm} board, taking up \qty{5.6}{\percent} of the available area, even though all 104 pins are rarely used \autocite{BoardSpecificationLibreCube}.
	\item The \acs{CAN} bus used by some CubeSat subsystems requires two \qty{120}{\ohm} terminating resistors, which will consume a maximum of \qty{33.3}{\milli\watt}\footnote{assuming a constant \(\Delta V = \qty{2}{\volt} \)} during operation.\footnote{This is generally negligible for CubeSats, but a significant amount for ChipSats with an \acs{OAP} of, for example, \( \sim \qty{100}{\milli\watt} \).}
	\item Most standard CubeSat packages separate the \emph{\acf{COMMS}} and \emph{\ac{OBDH}} functions into different boards, in order to accommodate the required flexibility of supporting different communication schemes and different subsystem vendors respectively. However, recent single-purpose missions have combined \ac{COMMS} and \ac{OBDH} into a single board, as \ac{OBDH} functionality is not usually resource-intensive.
\end{itemize}
While the above anecdotal examples do not need to translate to \acs{SCS} as-is, they show that the concept of modularity has to add additional constraints to a system. As \acs{SCS} designs often try to optimise available real estate, mass, power consumption and general performance, \textbf{a modular design may not be optimal in every situation.} This is especially valid in the case of femto- and atto-satellites, whose simple \acs{PCB} shape does not favour additional effort spent to make subsystems modular. I also note that modularity is not a binary decision, but can involve implementing different levels of modularity across different system modules.

\begin{figure*}[h]
	\includegraphics{graphics/Monolithic.pdf}
	\caption{Concept map comparing the advantages of monolithic and modular \acs{SCS} architectures}
	\label{fig:m_monolithic}
\end{figure*}

In practical terms, if the developers decide to implement a modular architecture, this might involve:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item Separating the spacecraft into different subsystems following a common interface.
	\item Drafting a fixed specification (using a common or proprietary protocol) for the communication between subsystems.
	\item Developing software that can does not make assumptions about the existence or position of different components.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{On Component Reuse}
Another decision that developers are faced with involves whether they reuse existing \textbf{parts} and \textbf{subsystems}\footnote{Also known as \acf{COTS}. This might also involve customised build-to-order components that rely on a \acs{COTS} design.}, or design components and subsystems in-house.

	\label{fig:m_reuse}
\end{figure*}

		2 & System & \href{https://github.com/PyCubed-Mini}{PyCubed-Mini} & Carnegie Mellon University & 2021 & 1P PocketQube designed to be programmable in Python, based on the PyCubed CubeSats. Reused by \href{https://github.com/AFS-pt/PROMETHEUS-1}{PROMETHEUS-1}. \\

		% https://www.albaorbital.com/open-source-pocketqube#:~:text=%2450SAT%20%281%2E5P%29%2C%20Morehead%20State%20university

\paragraph{Interface Management}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_interface_management}

\marginnote[1cm]{%
	External interfaces can be categorised into:
	\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-0.5ex]
		\item Mechanical (e.g.\@ allowable envelope, fasteners, load limits)
		\item Electrical (e.g.\@ interconnects, voltage/power limits, switches)
		\item Software (e.g.\@ protocols, data rates, packet throughput)
	\end{itemize}
}
External interfaces, usually defined between the spacecraft and its launch provider, represent one of the fixed functional requirements of an \acs{SCS} mission. These interfaces can be standard and well-defined (e.g.\@ the PocketQube standard \cite{PQ1}), or proprietary and, sometimes, ambiguous. Teams should focus on verifying these interfaces as early as possible, for example through the use of simulators or test jigs, ideally provided by the interfacing entities.

In the case of high-severity interfaces (such as the ones informing the satellite when it is safe to operate), formal documentation must be provided and agreed upon between stakeholders, as stress-testing of the actual interface is rarely possible, and launch providers want to ensure that these requirements are maintained under different environmental conditions. Typical interface documentation will be provided in a document-based format (to ease interoperability between different entities) and include:\footnote{Based on \cite{gerstenmaierInternationalDockingSystem2016} and experience from past missions.}
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item The intention and justification behind the interface.
	\item Acceptable environmental conditions for operation and storage (temperature, humidity).
	\item Types of protocols used, and if/how they are tailored. For public protocols, a link to the reference implementation\footnote{This is important in the case of protocols like \acs{I2C} which are not supported by a universal standard.}.
	\item The performance envelope of inputs and outputs (e.g.\@ acceptable voltages, maximum torque, static envelope).
	\item The performance envelope of undesired inputs and outputs (e.g.\@ maximum ripple, dynamic envelope).
	\item Timing or repetition limitations (e.g.\@ maximum number of mates, maximum data rate, maximum packet rate).
	\item Cleanliness, \acs{EMC}, outgassing and documentation requirements.
	\item What assumptions can be made about the reliability of either end (e.g.\@ guaranteed correct operation at all times / no guarantee of operation but guarantee that the line will be kept in Hi-Z).
	\item For physical interfaces, a \acs{CAD} model of the interface or its exterior, along with a specification of the coordinate system used.
	\item For electrical connectors, if there are any mechanical restrictions (e.g.\@ material, compliance, maximum force).
	\item For conductive materials, what the grounding requirements are.
	\item Where newer versions or errata of the interface specification can be found.
\end{enumerate}

This more ``strict'' approach to interface definition represents a deviation to the ``lighter'' Agile approach described in the rest of this chapter, since the potential impact of an interface device (or interface requirement) failure could be considered catastrophic (e.g.\@ loss of launcher, major injury). 

%Finally, interface specifications should be kept up-to-date

% In some cases, the criticality of such interfaces might be high enough to require proof that no system external to the satellite might be negatively impacted. Metrics such as the ECSS severity scale [86] can be used to classify this impact, ranging from negligible to catastrophic (such as loss of launcher or major injury) effects. For example, an International Space Station (ISS) launch may have specific limits on electrical system configuration, deployment switches and battery cell heritage. Interfaces with catastrophic failure modes should be verified formally and independently from the rest of the system, as risk cannot be tolerated in this case.}

\paragraph{Miscellaneous}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_design_miscellaneous}
This paragraph will enumerate further low-level design guidelines based on lessons learned that do not fit into any of the above categories:
\begin{itemize}
	\item Traditional satellite manufacturers focus heavily on the \textbf{reduction of in-orbit technical risk} through administrative measures (e.g.\@ \acl{FMEA}), system-level solutions (e.g.\@ redundancy) or high-reliability components \autocite{kanavourasDesignFaultDetection2021}.
	This includes the use of radiation-tolerant parts and radiation effect mitigation measures.\footnote{For \acs{LEO} \acs{SCS} in particular, due to the low mission durations, \acs{TID} effects are typically less severe than random \acs{SEE} effects.}
	Due to the general lack of statistics to evaluate the criticality and reliability effects of different choices, it is not always possible to confidently assert whether an amount of risk aversion is ``enough'' or not\footnote{Although it is generally possible to assert whether risk decreases after the implementation of a reliability measure.}.

	When choosing how many risk mitigation measures to implement, \acs{SCS} developers should consider the expected lifetime of their spacecraft, their degree of risk acceptance, and the practicality of launching more than one spacecraft. Software-based measures (such as watchdog timers, error-correcting codes, resilient operating systems) can cover a large part of the large part of the failure surface area, but cannot address some hardware faults. Expensive measures, such as the use of redundant subsystems, may require larger form factors that cannot be accommodated by the system's requirements. Finally, measures such as the use of radiation-tested or radiation-tolerant parts may provide diminishing returns, when compared to other risk-reduction measurements.

	One proposed risk mitigation solution is the execution of a system-level \acs{FMEA} at the beginning of a project, and its revision at the beginning of every iteration. System-level \acsp{FMEA} start from evaluating the top-level subsystems and critical components of the product tree\footnote{It is important to follow a top-down approach when executing a \acs{FMEA} analysis, by starting with an overview of the design, instead of trying to enumerate possible failures based on experience. Subsystems and components usually have limited failure modes (e.g.\@ unacceptable performance, temporarily inoperable, permanently inoperable, ``babbling idiot''-type faults) which can be enumerated for each and are listed in pre-made databases}, but do not go into detail on each component.
	Within the context of \acs{SCS}, they can be performed in an informal fashion\footnote{An informal \acs{FMEA} can be converted to an informal \acs{FMECA} by integrating failure probability rates of subsystems based on lessons learned from past missions, or available CubeSat reliability surveys.}.
	Teams can use these analyses to make a more informed decision on whether to accept or 
	mitigate risks, as well as to implement an \acs{FDIR} plan.
	\item Prototyping is heavily encouraged in \acs{SCS} projects, through the creation of low-fidelity parts that can be used as Development or Engineering, but not Flight Models. These parts can aid in the creation of partitions, to avoid testing subsystems in isolation (\Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_partitions}), and can include structural elements (spacers, struts, frames), expensive components (e.g.\@ batteries, reaction wheels) or entire subsystems.
	It is common to use additive manufacturing (e.g.\@ 3D printing) for relatively rapid prototyping of parts in-house, with some custom solutions also available for more specialised parts (e.g.\@ \acs{PCB} printers).
	\item Apart from software, which is analysed in \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_softwaredevelopment}, satellite failures are often manifested in \textbf{energy storage} and \textbf{deployable mechanisms}.
	Batteries, in particular, are sensitive to temperatures below \qty{0}{\degreeCelsius}, where they may refuse to provide adequate current, or might get damaged if charged \autocite{uludagDelfiPQInOrbitPerformance2025}. Hardware state control, heaters or other passive thermal management solutions are recommended to mitigate these issues. On the other hand, deployable antenna mechanisms represent a common source of errors, especially during ground testing, due to the sensitivity of common solutions (e.g.\@ melt lines) to the external environment (especially during cold deployment temperatures) \autocite{sanchoLessonsLearnedFYS2021}. For this reason, \citeauthor{sanchoLessonsLearnedFYS2021} recommend early definition, implementation and testing of mechanisms, especially in a \acs{TVAC}.
	\item \marginnote{Nevertheless, I note that the existence of a separated ``clean'' environment can motivate developers to adopt a more ``cautious'' approach when working with flight equipment, compared to the latter being co-located with normal developmennt activities in the same physical space.} Access to cleanroom facilities is a significant burden, especially for academic spacecraft developers. If the launch provider's cleanliness requirements are relaxed, developers can consider a laminar flow bench, a ``glove box'', or a restricted-access room \cite{sanchoLessonsLearnedFYS2021}. These solutions can potentially reduce the burden of interaction between team members who are located in different rooms.
	\item On low-performance systems, there is no need to have a central orchestrator and/or router for subsystem functions and spacecraft state. In practical terms, there is no need to have a separate board or microcontroller for an \acs{SCS} \acf{OBC}, with this functionality being transferable to e.g.\@ \acs{COMMS} or \acs{ADCS}. This choice also reduces the number of interfaces, and therefore satellite complexity.
	\item With the goal of miniaturisation, developers are encouraged to make use of the most real estate possible, through the use of dense designs, multi-purpose \acsp{IC}, multi-layer \acsp{PCB}, and packages without exposed pins. The disadvantages of this approach mainly constitute the difficulty of testing and inspection, as well as potential \acs{EMC} issues\footnote{Note, however, that the low performance and power of these systems tends to counteract the small distances between components, in terms of \acs{EMC}.}.
	\item Easy-to-assemble board-to-board \textbf{connectors} should be preferred in favour of cables, as routing cables on a limited space increases volume, time spent and risk during assembly \autocite{uludagDelfiPQInOrbitPerformance2025}. Alternative ideas can include spring-loaded ``pogo'' pins, magnetic connectors, or traditional stackable board-to-board connections\footnote{With the appropriate attention given to correct alignment with adequate margins.}.
	\item New, experimental technologies that have not been demonstrated in-space or commercialised can prove useful for \acs{SCS}, as the risks associated with low \acsp{TRL} can be more easily accepted. Specifically, mobile device technologies can strike a valuable balance between size and manufacturability. Examples include lens-less cameras \autocite{liLenslessCameraUnraveling2024}, high-density batteries \autocite{guptaDesigningAdvancedLithiumBased2020}, low-power radios \autocite{gouvalasSmallestInterSatelliteTerminal2024,liangCognitiveRadioNetworking2011}, energy harvesting \autocite{amirtharajahMicropowerProgrammableDSP2000}, low-power \acsp{FPGA} or \acfp{NPU} for edge processing \autocite{moreiraEdgeComputingSpace2025}, or low-volume \acp{ASIC}.
	\item Hardware--software co-design is highly desirable, as it allows early verification of the interactions between the two, without requiring detailed formal specification. In the ideal case, all software will be written and tested on representative hardware from the onset. However, this is impractical due to the numerous \acl{COP}. Developers can consider the use of \acf{HIL} systems, \acf{RPA} or emulators to ensure software--hardware compatibility.
	\item As the reduced \acs{BDUF} may fail to accurately predict in-orbit conditions, \acs{SCS} should support a minimum amount of \textbf{reconfigurability} from the ground that can persist through software restarts. This is, for example, supported by implementations of the \acs{ECSS} \acl{PUS} standard \autocite{ECSS-E-ST-70-41C}. Accordingly, less attention should be dedicated to designing a fully-autonomous spacecraft, in favour of a manually operated approach, potentially based on scheduled \acs{TC}.
	\item Operators should investigate the trackability of their spacecraft before the start of the design to ensure compliance with local regulations. Passive measures, such as retro-reflectors, are preferred to make \acs{SCS} systems more trackable, as active solutions may not be able to guarantee constant and correct operation. 
\end{itemize}


\subsection{Verification \& Validation Method}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_verification}

Verification and validation are an integral part of a sprint cycle, and describe the stage that generates insight about the developed systems that can be reused as lessons learned and applied to the next cycle.

%The distinction between the ``verification'' and ``validation'' terms is provided 

\begin{margindefinition}[Validation]
	\textbf{Validation} is the process which demonstrates that the product is able to accomplish its intended use in the intended operational environment \cite{ECSS-S-ST-00-01C}.
\end{margindefinition}

In traditional space systems engineering, \acs{ECSS} defines verification as the process demonstrating that the built system satisfies all requirements and is free of defects \cite{ECSS-S-ST-00-01C}. However, in the \ac{SCS} methodology, the system is not strictly defined by its requirements specification, but also by an agreement between the developers and stakeholders on its capability and performance. Therefore, based on the \acs{ECSS} definition \cite{ECSS-S-ST-00-01C}, I propose the following alternative definition:

\begin{definition}[Verification]
	\textbf{Verification} is the process which generates objective evidence to evaluate a product artifact, with the goals of:
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-2ex]
		\item Assessing the number of requirements that the product is compliant with, based on the current requirements specification (see \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_requirements})
		\item Demonstrating that the product is free of defects
		\item Measuring the product's performance
		\item Confirming the implementation of all features requested as part of work packages
	\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{figure}
	\includegraphics{pictures/POQUITO_at_work.jpg}
	\caption[A Hardware-In-the-Loop testing platform]{A Hardware-In-the-Loop testing platform can consist of the system under test and an external programmer.}
\end{figure}


While verification ensures that the built system satisfies all agreed-on specifications and expectations, \textbf{validation} ensures that the correct system was built \autocite{ECSS-S-ST-00-01C}.

\begin{margindefinition}[Performance]
	\textbf{Performance} is the set of quantifiable characteristics of a system \emph{(adapted from \cite{ECSS-S-ST-00-01C})}.
\end{margindefinition}

\acs{ECSS} defines four verification methods that are typically used in space missions: \emph{review-of-design, analysis, inspection and testing}, which provide different levels of fidelity and reliability \autocite{ECSS-E-ST-10-02C}. It also distinguishes between \emph{acceptance} and \emph{qualification} levels for design parameters, the former of which are used to verify workmanship and functionality, and the latter used to demonstrate design margins, usually at a level that may be destructive for the \ac{DUT} \autocite{ECSS-E-ST-10-03C}.
Verification is said to be made in ``ambient'' conditions if no attempt is made to simulate the space environment,\footnote{Mainly temperature, pressure, radiation, and electromagnetic environment.} or are classified as ``environmental'' otherwise.

Traditionally, verification is done during the dedicated Phase D. Launch providers usually require some form of analysis and testing, in order to ensure that any failures on the hosted systems do not propagate to the host or other clients. \marginnote{For example, SpaceX requires random vibration, inhibit functional, and (when applicable) leak testing for CubeSats \autocite{SpacexRideshareGuide2024}.} Validation is typically said to be performed during the operational Phase E, where the usefulness and correctness of a system can be asserted after its actual use. When the V-model is used, verification can be performed in a tiered fashion, starting from subsystems to the integrated system \autocite{bundesrepublikdeutschlandVModellXT2006}. On the other hand, smaller systems like CubeSats regularly perform prototyping as part of their early phases, as part of risk-reduction or development activities \autocite{sousaCubeSatDevelopmentFramework2021,karvinenUsingHobbyPrototyping}, where functional or environmental verification takes place before a system-level verification phase.

In the context of \ac{SCS}, verification and validation present an worthwhile target for optimisation.
The low development cost of \ac{SCS} means that the cost and time overhead to create a \textbf{prototype} are low and can be part of the regular cycles of an Agile lifecycle process, which routinely include verification.
While CubeSats traditionally experiment with subsystem prototypes or reduced system mockups, in the case of \ac{SCS}, the \textbf{entire system} can be verified as part of these short cycles.

The most critical physical aspects of a design\footnote{Such as the verification of the functionality of deployables and actuators, masses and envelopes, or thermal and electromagnetic behaviour.} have traditionally involved both detailed analysis and testing for verification. In particular, environmental testing of subsystems and systems requires costly dedicated facilities; developers are therefore typically incentivised to create and execute domain-specific models and analyses of their systems before committing to a testing campaign.

The fidelity of these models and analyses can vary. For example, a power consumption analysis might simply creating a spreadsheet with the nominal consumption values of all components and an appropriate margin, or a detailed time-domain simulation of component behaviour during different mission phases, and how they affect electrical converter efficiency. Different fidelities can be also sought in thermal and structural analyses, where the spatial/temporal resolution can be configured and different models can be used for the representation of nodes and interfaces.

Similarly, testing processes can involve different types of equipment and fidelity. Functional testing can take place in an ambient environment, usually in a so-called ``FlatSat'' configuration, where all subsystems are laid out in a flat surface\marginnote{In the case that \aclp{EM} are not available for all subsystems, different methods can be used to perform a reduced, yet complete satellite functional test. \acl{HIL} relies on software components that test specific hardware, or alternatively hardware/software-based subsystem mocks can be used that emulate missing parts \autocite{kiesbyeHardwareInTheLoopSoftwareInTheLoopTesting2019}. Complete software emulation of external devices can be also used to verify functional requirements.} and full functional or mission  tests are executed manually or (semi)automatically.
The required frequency of functional tests for complex mission means that it is often impossible to test the complete functionality of a system at once (especially in cases where payload runs need multiple days to complete). Different test coverage models are therefore typically defined in missions.
\marginnote{Typical test coverage models can include: \autocite{ECSS-E-ST-10-03C}
\begin{itemize}
	\item (Very) Reduced Functional Test
	\item Full Functional Test
	\item ``Day-in-life`` Test
	\item Mission Test
	\item End-To-End Test
	\item Failure recovery Test 
\end{itemize}
}


On the other hand, environmental verification usually involves specialised equipment\footnote{Such as shakers, shock benches, \ac{TVAC} chambers, or anechoic chambers} that is used to simulate space conditions for the \ac{DUT}.
The emulation of these conditions is necessary in order to examine their expected and unexpected effects on electronics, structures and mechanisms.\footnote{These conditions can also affect component behaviour that is difficult or impractical to analyse, such as the behaviour of \acs{COTS} fastener materials, or temperature-dependent electronic performance degradation.}

Environmental testing has generally required access to expensive, time-limited remote facilities.
However, the small size of \acs{SCS} and the popularity increase of the nano-satellite industry have started to make the purchase, operation and maintenance of in-house testing equipment more attractive, with \ac{TVAC} chambers, shakers, environmental chambers and Helmholtz cages starting to become more popular in university and industry campuses. %\footnote{This matches the Agile principle of centralising development as described in Tade}
Even if a manufacturing facility cannot support a full environmental testing campaign, it may be able to perform activities that drastically increase confidence in the design.

In addition to this general shift towards in-house environmental testing, \ac{SCS} are usually composed of simple and well-understood structures, such as singular circuit boards or simple subsystem stacks. Past performance of these structures, especially in terms of rigidity and thermal behaviour, is well understood from previous missions. This reduces the benefits of analysis.

As a result, \ac{SCS} teams might find that they can \textbf{forego analyses} as part of their development and verification stages, instead relying only on tests to either generate data about the performance of their system, or verify requirements compliance. While analysis can increase understanding of a system's performance and operational margins as well as reduce testing risk, a trade-off has to be made between the cost of analysis against the risk reduction offered.\marginnote{The low manufacturing cost of spacecraft versions may also encourage ``destructive'' testing, perhaps performed multiple times at qualification levels, and especially when manufacturing runs produce spare units that have no other use.}


\paragraph{Reduced Testing}

When facilities for full-scale testing are not available or are cost-prohibitive, an alternative is to perform reduced testing, when not performing the final acceptance or qualification of a system. In practice, this can mean reducing the testing levels, the fidelity of measurements, the duration of the tests, or performing a parallel process sequentially. While the results from reduced tests may not fully reflect the equivalent results from a traditional testing process, they still retain high value for intermediate iterations, since they often emulate the environmental levels required for defects to be detectable.

Examples can be conceived by components of tests or test platforms with \acs{COTS} alternatives, and can include:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
\item Using a plain vacuum chamber instead of a \acf{TVAC} to emulate reduced pressure
\item Using a household heater or freezer instead of a \acs{TVAC} to emulate extreme temperatures
\item Using a \acs{COTS} laser setup to emulate radiation effects \autocite{zollnerGeneratingSELSEU2016}
\item Using an incandescent or high-\acs{CRI} \acs{LED} light instead of a sun simulator
\end{itemize}

Based on the available literature, vibration tests seem the most difficult to replicate in a reduced fidelity. However, various approaches have been proposed to implement a low-cost shaker, especially in the fields of biology and chemistry \autocite{hollinghamOpenHW3OpensourceLowcost2024,diaz-roncerosDesignImplementationLowcost2023}.

\paragraph{In-Orbit Testing}
In some cases, the resources available to a development team may encourage designating an \textbf{orbital launch} as a verification (and validation) method. Indeed, this is the approach taken by the \emph{SMOG-1} PocketQube team, who launched the \emph{SMOG-P} PocketQube as an early prototype \autocite{SMOG14thHungarian2021}.
This approach may be risky, as the lead time between the production of a \acl{FM} and its operation can be too long to fit within limited iteration cycles. However, it can be appropriate for teams that have a ready-to-use spacecraft bus, already have plans or access to multiple low-cost flight opportunities, or want to replace generally-costly test campaigns, such as radiation testing.
In-Orbit Testing need also not be performed on the entire system, and can follow the slice-based approach, especially when simple low-cost payload flight opportunities are available.

Alternatively, when available, high-altitude testing platforms can be used instead of test flights, such as \textbf{high-altitude balloons}, drones, or sounding rockets \autocite{berthoudHowSetCubeSat2016,WikisatWiki}. However, the cost-benefit ratio of these solutions will need to be carefully examined, as the conditions simulated by them can often also be emulated on the ground.

In general, the verification \& validation process of an \ac{SCS}-methodology team will differ considerably from the equivalent in a CubeSat process.
However, it will still vary between teams, as it depends on variables set by the internal and external environment. I provide further guidelines for methodology tailoring in \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_tailoring}.

\paragraph{Automated Testing}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_automated_testing}

\begin{figure}
	\includegraphics{graphics/AI4Space_Robot.pdf}
	\caption{Example test suite for an end-to-end test, written in the Robot framework}
\end{figure}

There is significant benefit to automating the functional testing processes. The use of \acf{RPA} is finding increased popularity for testing integrated systems in space engineering \autocite{malyshkinaMissionControlSoftware2025}. 

Self-contained and automated test runs can be performed both during and at the end of a Sat-Scrum iteration. A fully automated test-suite, executing both unit and integration tests \autocite{myersArtSoftwareTesting2012}, can be part of the \acs{CI}/\acs{CD} process that can be executed automatically after every software modification, or by manual trigger when there are hardware modifications. When designed correctly, such a test suite should be completed in a limited amount of time, allowing multiple executions per day. Ideally, automated testing should verify the \textbf{combination of hardware and software} which is best done through a \textbf{\acf{HIL} setup} \autocite{kiesbyeHardwareInTheLoopSoftwareInTheLoopTesting2019}.


However, one has to take care with the additional overhead added by implementing automated solutions, when a manual process can suffice. When a test case requires, for example, changing the hardware configuration or cable connections of the \acs{DUT}, the developers have to consider whether it is more beneficial to automate this process, keep working manually, or forego the test for some iterations.%\marginnote{These calculations can be easily made using unit-aware calculators. For example, if a process takes \(t_m\) time to execute manually, \( t_a \) to automate, then it makes sense to automate when a project lasts more than \( \frac{t_a}{t_m} \) iterations.}
This decision can often be made with back-of-the-envelope calculations on unit-aware calculators; however, care has to be taken, when estimating the manual effort required for a process, to consider reruns that may not be part of the original testing plan.
%\footnote{This can often leave to seemingly counter-intuitive results. If, for example, a process takes 30 minutes to execute manually, is executed twice per iteration, and would take 6 hours to automate, it would make sense to automate when a project lasts more than \( \frac{\SI{6}{hours}}{2\cdot \SI{30}{minute}} =  6\) iterations.}.


\subsection{Agile Maintenance \& Operations}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_operations}

While spacecraft operations are not strictly part of their development, and therefore outside the scope of this thesis, it is important to consider the needs of a spacecraft and its operators after launch, to make sure that no additional failures are generated, and that any faults caused by inadequate design are mitigated to the degree possible.

In theory, the main boon for space systems operations is \textbf{the ability to reprogram a spacecraft in orbit}. While hardware can (mostly) not be reconfigured, the use of in-orbit programming can, in essence, allow completely reconfiguring a spacecraft's functions. However, in practice, small-scale development teams do not often plan for this feature, due to its high implementation complexity. When implemented, in-orbit programming should be robust, by allowing a reversal of the process if errors are detected, and rejecting corrupted code.

Additional guidelines for operations planning include \autocite{lillAgileMissionOperations2018}:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item An idea about a potential operating scheme, mainly based on parameters and events, is given by the ECSS-E-ST-70-41C standard \autocite{ECSS-E-ST-70-41C}. The standard defines a modular architecture that allows almost full configuration of a spacecraft's housekeeping and \acs{FDIR} behaviour. This is further supported by publically available software libraries implementing parts of the standard.
	
	\item Security vulnerabilities have been identified in small satellites in the past \autocite{willboldSpaceOdysseyExperimental2023}.
	These vulnerabilities may involve unauthorised access to a satellite's data\footnote{For radio-amateur satellites, most data is required to be unencrypted. In practice, \acs{SCS} developers have few reasons to encrypt transmitted data.} or unauthorised control through unsigned \acs{TC}. Developers should consider the risk of unauthorised telecommanding, and consider implementing cryptographic signatures (e.g.\@ \acs{HMAC}). In many cases, these implementations will be available off-the-shelf for specific communication protocols (e.g.\@ \acs{CCSDS}).

	\item The success of a satellite's mission relies on its successful identification via radar and its unique transmissions. To that end, it is helpful for developers to participate in a ground station network (e.g.\@ SatNOGS/TinyGS). This action is usually mutually beneficial, with satellite teams offering their ground station to others when it is unused, while benefitting from a larger geographical coverage.
	
	\item The concept of reuse can be applied to operational software, with tools like \emph{YAMCS} providing an off-the-shelf interface for \acs{TC} generation, \acs{TM} parsing, alerts and custom functions.

	\item \acf{GS} planning and development is usually an activity separate from spacecraft design, which can be easily postponed after spacecraft delivery. However, to support complete end-to-end testing as imagined in Sat-Scrum, it is beneficial to have a ready-to-use \acl{GS} setup for commanding and telemetry. To that end, teams can reuse off-the-shelf \acs{GS} components and software, such as the ones provided by SatNOGS \autocite{BuildSatNOGSWiki}. Then, teams can build their communication subsystem with the goal of conforming to the requirements of an already working \acs{GS}, following the \ac{TDD} approach.

	\item When the spacecraft architecture allows, a \textbf{common user interface} can be used to operate the spacecraft for ground-level development and in-orbit operation. On the one hand, this limits the number of data interfaces that have to be developed for the spacecraft, and on the other, it encourages developers to implement a flexible telecommanding scheme. \acs{COTS} tools such as Grafana or OpenC3 Cosmos have been traditionally used to this effect \autocite{slaneyEnhancingGroundStation2024}.
	
	\item Depending on the nature of the project, the engineers responsible for developing the spacecraft might not be the ones responsible for operating it. In the case of non-nominal operating situations, this might result in a poor understanding of the function of the system. When the original developers are not available, software source code and test results can serve as sources of information.
\end{itemize}

\pagebreak[4]
\section{Software Development Methodology}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_softwaredevelopment}

While the small size of \acs{SCS} systems limits the number of physical subsystems, components and interfaces, these constraints are less influential for the software itself. In terms of software requirements, as most \acs{SCS} need to be independent and autonomous spacecraft, a minimum set of functionality should be present, such as energy gathering, communication with the earth and failure recovery.

\Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_software_features} will therefore describe, based on available standards and recommendations, the operability requirements or features that should be present in most \acs{SCS} software, along with standard interfaces and ecosystems that are used. \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_software_reliability} will focus on the reliability of on-board software, and factors that can improve it without significant time investment. Finally, \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_debugging_interfaces} will delve into the interface between software and hardware, by presenting what methods developers can use to upload and monitor their software without wasting real estate.

\subsection[SCS Software Features]{\acs{SCS} Software Features}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_software_features}

%vadOK
\marginnote{%
Required software features for a \acs{SCS} based on \Cref{tab:m_software_requirements} can be summarised as:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-0.5ex]
	\item Periodic \acs{TM} reporting of critical values
	\item Storage of parameter statistics or historical values
	\item Ping (``are-you-alive'') tests
	\item Time-keeping
	\item \ac{FDIR}
	\item Scheduled command execution
	\item Spacecraft passivation (permanently disabling \acs{TM})
	\item Reconfigurability
	%\item Bus subsystem autonomy
	\item Payload commanding/data downlinking
\end{itemize}

Examples of features that would be typically present in larger spacecraft but \emph{NOT} recommended for \acs{SCS} are:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-0.5ex]
	\item Position-based scheduling
	\item Memory/file management
	\item Scripting
	\item Redundancy
\end{itemize}
}
\acs{ESA}'s requirements for standard on-board software functionality are provided by the \emph{ECSS-E-ST-70-11C} standard \autocite{ECSS-E-ST-70-11C}, which explains the required functionality for \ac{TMTC}, mission control, configuration management, and autonomy. These functions are further augmented by the \emph{ECSS-E-ST-70-41C} \acf{PUS} standard \autocite{ECSS-E-ST-70-41C}, which defines 20 services (along with their interfaces) for satellite bus control. While the combination of the two standards guarantees a high degree of observability, flexibility and safety, combined they represent \num{3533} requirements and considerable effort to implement. Therefore, a tailoring is recommended to select a proper balance of requirements for a small SCS system.
% ECSS-E-ST-70-41C: 3185
% ECSS-E-ST-70-11C: 348

I provide an initial such tailoring in \Cref{tab:m_software_requirements}. This table can function as a starting point for a list of software features and work packages in terms of software functionality, and further tailored by \acs{SCS} teams. Compared to CubeSats and larger spacecraft, the overall functionality remains similar, but there are fewer requirements for each general feature.\footnote{For example, requirement \emph{5.8.5.f} from \cite{ECSS-E-ST-70-11C} demands that a \acs{TC} schedule be separable into sub-schedules which can be individually commanded, which is not necessary for \acs{SCS} where these schedules are expected to be only a few commands long.} Also note that these requirements only cover the basic needs of the system bus, and not additional payload-specific functionality that is different for each mission.



\begin{table*}[h]
	\caption[SCS main software operability requirements]{\acs{SCS} main software operability requirements}
	\label{tab:m_software_requirements}
	\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}lX@{}}
		\toprule
		ID & Text \\ \midrule
		\multicolumn{2}{c}{Functional Requirements} \\ \midrule
		100 & The OBSW shall generate TM of internal values, such as OBSW resources, power parameters, and sensor measurements. \\
		110 & The OBSW shall provide at least an ``are-you-alive'' \acs{TC} with the corresponding response. \\
		120 & The OBSW shall provide acknowledgement and/or execution status responses for any correct \acs{TC} received. \\
		130 & The OBSW shall report an internal timestamp accurate to within 100 ppm (points per million). \\
		140 & The OBSW shall have the capability to switch between a ``nominal'' and a ``safe'' mode ensuring spacecraft survival.  \\
		150 & The OBSW shall be capable of running autonomously for at least 24 hours, without restarts. \\
		160 & The OBSW shall provide the capability to enable or disable the emission of TM to the ground segment. \\
		170 & The OBSW shall provide the capability of scheduling TC for later execution. \\
		\midrule
		\multicolumn{2}{c}{Reliability Requirements} \\ \midrule
		200 & The OBSW shall include adequate FDIR to detect, report, and recover from failures. \\
		210 & No single TC executed at the wrong time or wrong configuration shall lead to loss of mission. \\
		220 & The OBSW shall verify the integrity of each data stream received. \\
		230 & Repetition of the same TC shall not result in any permanent degradation or loss of on-board functionality. \\
		240 & The maximum execution duration of a TC shall be bounded. \\
		250 & Failure of TC parsing or execution at any stage shall be reported or observable in TM. \\
		260 & Invalid datastreams received from the OBSW shall not preclude its functional requirements. \\
		270 & Nominal operation of the OBSW (i.e.~without any radiation effects, but with any potential input) shall not lead to an exception or lock-up. \\ \bottomrule
	\end{tabularx}
\end{table*}

In general, the focus of software developers should be to achieve a balance between delegating functionality and complexity to the spacecraft and the ground segment. On the one hand, performing calculations and finely controlling the spacecraft from the ground makes it easier for operators to handle unexpected issues and improve functionality. On the other, on-the-edge processing is necessary to reduce the downlinked data in the case of restricted data budgets.

\newthought{One} important capability that should be planned from the beginning is \textbf{software reprogramming}, which allows developers to patch the on-board software even after launch. While it is recommended by most standards and guides, it is by far the most complex feature to implement, as it depends on the hardware capabilities of each processor, usually involves handling large data volumes, and requires knowledge of the fundamentals of the firmware architectures used.

A \emph{dual-bank architecture} can support in-orbit programming with relative ease and low risk, by trading off an amount of non-volatile memory.
In this architecture, program memory is split into two halves, one holding the working copy of the software, and another where the new version is progressively uploaded. This architecture provides a permanent space to upload and verify a new software version, and a safe way of performing a switch-over when the upload is complete. It is also supported natively by some microcontroller families.

\begin{figure*}[h]
	\includegraphics{graphics/software/block.pdf}
	\caption{Partitioning of typical satellite firmware}
\end{figure*}

\FloatBarrier
\subsection[Improving SCS Software Reliability]{Improving \acs{SCS} Software Reliability}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_software_reliability}

Owing to the prominence of the software engineering field, significant research has been performed on the factors affecting software reliability. A \citeyear{zhuComparisonAnalysisEnvironmental2015} survey of software practitioners identified 32 environmental factors affecting software reliability, which span specification, testing, programmer skill, standards, and technologies used \autocite{zhuComparisonAnalysisEnvironmental2015}. Specifically for flight software, a \citeyear{prokopSoftwareErrorIncident2024} analysis of 55 aerospace software errors categorised their causes into:%

\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-1ex]
	\item An unexpected combination of states, which may be individually tested, but their combination is not,
	\item Issues that rely on receiving an unexpected combination of environmental inputs (e.g.~unprotected numeric type conversion in the Ariane 5 Maiden Flight),
	\item Issues arising due to incorrect synchronisation between \acl{OS} tasks, which in many systems is inherently non-deterministic (e.g.~deadlocks, priority inversion), and
	\item Aging-related issues (e.g.~clock overflows or float error propagation) \autocite{alonsoNatureTimesFlight2012}.
\end{enumerate}%

\marginnote{%
	The list of design considerations identified by \citeauthor{curboResearchAgendaSpace2023} \autocite{curboResearchAgendaSpace2023} are:
	\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0ex]
		\item Requirements and Quality Assurance
		\item Security Analysis and Testing
		\item Security Aspects of Architectural Design
		\item Security of the \acs{RTOS}
		\item Security of Mission Applications and Hosted Payloads/Instruments
		\item Programming Language Selection
	\end{itemize}
}
Additionally, \citeauthor{curboResearchAgendaSpace2023} identified six design considerations for flight software security, which include analysis, testing and programming language selection \autocite{curboResearchAgendaSpace2023}.

Unfortunately, \acs{SCS} projects do not have control over some of the parameters that are said to increase software reliability, such as the rate of specification changes and developer experience. As such, for this section, I will focus on two technical factors that are related to software engineering only: testing practices and programming language selection.

\marginnote[2ex]{}% %for spacing
\begin{margindefinition}[Test coverage]
	Testing coverage is a measure of how much of the software's source code has been executed during the testing process. It can be expressed in terms of functions, lines, statements or branches.
\end{margindefinition}%

\paragraph{Software Testing}
\citeauthor{zhuComparisonAnalysisEnvironmental2015}'s survey lists the following testing-related factors as affecting software reliability: \autocite{zhuComparisonAnalysisEnvironmental2015}
\begin{multicols}{2}
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item Testing coverage
	\item Testing environment
	\item Testing effort
	\item Testing methodologies
	\item Testing tools
\end{itemize}
\end{multicols}

A \citeyear{tohmaEstimationParametersHypergeometric1991} study of a \num{200000}-line monitoring \& control program found an almost-linear correlation between the time spent testing and the number of faults identified, reaching a plateau after 60 days of testing (\Cref{fig:m_software_testing_ds1}) \autocite{tohmaEstimationParametersHypergeometric1991}. This observation has been modelled, depending on constants such as the initial fault number and the fault removal efficiency \autocite{liTestingcoverageSoftwareReliability2017}.

In typical CubeSat systems engineering documentation, developers are recommended to devote 50\% of a project's time to testing activities \autocite{venturiniImprovingMissionSuccess2017}. Within the context of \acs{SCS}, testing takes place as part of normal development and during each iteration (\Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_satscrum}). In the context of software engineering, verification and testing is performed in both an automated\footnote{referred to as computer-based testing} and manual fashion. \citeauthor{myersArtSoftwareTesting2012} separate software verification into the following categories \autocite{myersArtSoftwareTesting2012}:
\begin{enumerate}
	\item \textbf{Code Inspections, Walkthroughs \& Reviews}: This refers to a review of the static original source code. Code reviews are usually performed after every code change (``commit''), but can also take place independently (usually in the form of audits).  \citeauthor{myersArtSoftwareTesting2012} recommend different techniques for code reviews, which can involve different levels of scrutiny, conversation with the original authors and review checklists. While code reviews are not strictly part of software testing, a longer time spent on reviewing is correlated with an increase in code quality (\Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_reviews_general}). Code inspection can also involve automated static analysis through automated tools.
	
	\item \textbf{Test-case execution}: The most common form of software testing involves the creation of programmatic test-cases that verify individual software modules or their integration. The goal of executing test-cases is usually two-fold:
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-2ex]
		\item To verify compliance with a functional specification, and
		\item To ensure that as many as possible states of the software are covered.
	\end{enumerate}

	Since traditional Agile software engineering does not involve the detailed specification of functional requirements, \textbf{the test suite itself serves as the specification}. This is part of the philosophy of, e.g., \acf{TDD}, where the tests are created \emph{before} the actual software is implemented \autocite{beckTestDrivenDevelopment2002}. This approach is not dissimilar to formal model checking, where a model of a system is analysed to ensure that it meets a certain predefined specification \autocite{berardSystemsSoftwareVerification2001}. However, model checking requires creating an additional model of the system, which needs to be further kept correct and requires symbolic manipulation to avoid the state explosion \cite{berardSystemsSoftwareVerification2001}. 

	As such, in practice, developers create a limited number of test-cases that aim to evaluate the most likely and representative states of the software, with the goal of increasing statement and branch coverage of the source code. Additional techniques used to cover potentially invalid states include edge-case testing and regression testing. For complex software, test-cases evaluate code modules at low (unit) or high (integration) levels \autocite{myersArtSoftwareTesting2012}.

	Nevertheless, various approaches are often used in academia and industry to increase software reliability through different methods of formal verification, with examples being Ada's SPARK \autocite{SPARKReferenceManual}, Frama-C for C \autocite{kirchnerFramaCSoftwareAnalysis2015} and Prusti for Rust \autocite{astrauskasLeveragingRustTypes2019}. However, the complexity and effort required with applying these methods prevents their direct application to entire space systems \autocite{jacklinSurveyVerificationValidation2015}.

	\item \textbf{User Testing}: Usually referred to as ``usability testing'', this involves the actual use and interaction with software by its intended users. Even though \acs{SCS} software systems lack a user interface\footnote{Although one could still evaluate user-facing properties of the software, such as error message fidelity, response time, degree of available functionality etc.}, user testing expresses a need to perform a manual evaluation of the software running on actual hardware after every iteration, to make sure that the intended system is being built.
\end{enumerate}

Additional testing methods that increase confidence in the design include:
\begin{enumerate}
	\item \textbf{Fuzz Testing} (or fuzzing) involves the generation of a vast dataset of random test cases as inputs, which are then fed to the software to evaluate any missed edge-cases \autocite{godefroidAutomatedWhiteboxFuzz2008}.
	\item \textbf{Stress Testing} evaluates the performance of the software when it is put under a heavier load\footnote{``Load'' in this case can refer to data volume, data rate, concurrent connections, number of operations etc.} than expected \autocite{myersArtSoftwareTesting2012}.
	\item \textbf{Model Checking}, which tries to prove that a program is correct by logically exploring its theoretical state space \autocite{beyerSoftwareVerificationTesting2017}.
\end{enumerate}

While the benefits of testing are well understood, software quality assurance has historically met a lack of adequate time and resource allocation, combined with a potential lack of motivation for testing engineers \autocite{hardmanTestingNotBoring2025}. This situation becomes exacerbated in embedded systems development, since the verification of embedded software systems requires some form of hardware access as well.

\newthought{Therefore}, the recommendations for software testing within the context of \acs{SCS} entail finding an optimal point between effort spent testing and reliability improvements. Indicatively, they can involve:
\begin{enumerate}
	\item \textbf{Implementing an embedded testing suite} that lets developers execute and evaluate microcontroller code. This can involve, for example:
	\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-1ex]
		\item A \textbf{\acf{HIL}} system, where all the relevant hardware components\footnote{Development Boards, Engineering Models, or Qualification/Flight Models} are physically tested in tandem. Specifically for implementors of the \acs{SCS} methodology, the existence of a complete system model for every iteration simplifies \acs{HIL} construction.\marginnote{A practical \acs{HIL} setup can involve, for example, an ``instrumentation'' computer that sends \acs{TC} to the \acl{OBC} of the satellite, and evaluates the \acs{TM} received, as part of high-level test cases. Low-level test cases can be implemented by uploading custom testing software to the microcontroller, or by including a test-suite in development versions of the software.}
		\item A simulated version of the satellite, running completely on a desktop computer, and based on microcontroller and peripheral emulation.
	\end{itemize}
	\item \textbf{Implementing static analysis tools}, which can assist the code review process by identifying non-compliances to accepted best practices. Many static analysis tools can be used as ``drop-in'' plugins to standard \acs{CI}/\acs{CD} pipelines.
	\item \textbf{Attempting to reach maximum test coverage}, as coverage is one of the metrics typically correlated with defect rate.\marginnote{While coverage can be simply evaluated in plain \acs{SW} testing, embedded systems are not instrumented to share this information by default. To that end, \citeauthor{tsiodrasCoverMe2016} provides a hardware-agnostic coverage generation method based on the gdb debugger \autocite{tsiodrasCoverMe2016}.}
	\item \textbf{Automating the testing process}: The functional requirements of \acs{SCS} usually involve simple functions, and most can be evaluated by plain protocols and processes, such as nominal \acs{TMTC}. This makes \acs{SCS} functional testing suitable for \acf{RPA} methods, where data transfer, analysis and event 
	triggering are performed by an automated process \autocite{hofmannRoboticProcessAutomation2020}. The availability of ready-to-use automation tools also reduces the developer effort needed to automate this process.
	\item \textbf{Focus on integration testing before unit testing}, as, based on project experience, most errors are usually observed on the boundaries of components and the interactions between them. This includes a special focus on \textbf{external interfaces} that are attached to a spacecraft.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Programming Languages}

An increasing number of programming languages are starting to be used for small space system firmware, which have started moving away from the compiled, statically-type paradigm. Languages such as Python \autocite{hollidayPyCubedOpenSourceRadiationTested2019} and Java \autocite{dorinSuitabilityJavaSatellite2019} %and shell scripts (????)
have been used for CubeSats, along with the compiled options of C, C++, Ada and Rust.
While the aforementioned research notes a weak correlation between programming language and software reliability in typical \acs{SW} engineering contexts, typical \acs{SCS} projects differ from conventional projects in terms of team size, developer experience, and fault tolerance. This difference can potentially increase the effect of programming language selection on fault prevention.

\citeauthor{ashmoreProgrammingLanguageEvaluation2022} have hypothesised that specific programming language features can support the detection or prevent the introduction of errors, providing the example of the \emph{Rust} language which avoids memory allocation errors by design. \citeauthor{rayLargescaleStudyProgramming2017} \autocite{rayLargescaleStudyProgramming2017} analysed 728 GitHub projects, identifying commits that fixed bugs, and concluded that ``there is a small but significant relationship between language class and defects''.
\citeauthor{nanzComparativeStudyProgramming2015} \autocite{nanzComparativeStudyProgramming2015} measured runtime failures in programs of the \emph{Rosetta Code} repository, finding that ``compiled strongly-typed languages are significantly less prone to runtime failures than interpreted or weakly-typed languages, since more errors are caught at compile time''.

Both CubeSat and \acs{SCS} software development teams are usually faced with a choice of programming language when starting development. Based on the above findings, it would be interesting to evaluate if a specific programming language or selection criteria can have a measurable effect on the final software reliability. As many members of the CubeSat community follow an open-source philosophy, there is a multitude of space software projects available online, that can be serve \textbf{case studies to investigate the effect of language on software defect rate}.

The remainder of this section will present this \textbf{small-scale multiple-case study} of four \textbf{on-board software frameworks} to evaluate defects that are detected, and identify whether the use of a different programming language or framework would have prevented them.\marginnote{Due to the limited number of available \acs{OBSW} frameworks conforming to the requirements of \Cref{tab:m_software_requirements}, the current lack of diversity in programming languages in \acs{OBSW}, and the difficulty of identifying defects in third-party software, I did not perform an experimental analysis, relying only on the individual interpretation of case study results.

I also consider CubeSat (instead of just \acs{SCS}) software, because the functional requirements are not different between the two form factors, and no diverse programming languages are publically available for \acs{SCS} spacecraft.}

\subparagraph{Libraries Under Test}
\marginnote{%
The selected frameworks are:

\textbf{AcubeSAT}'s C++ on-board software. AcubeSAT is a 3U CubeSat developed in the Aristotle University of Thessaloniki, Greece, implementing the ECSS-E-ST-70-41C services \autocite{tsouposOnBoardComputerAcubeSAT2025,ECSS-E-ST-70-41C}.

\textbf{Prust}, an open-source implementation of the ECSS-E-ST-70-41C standard in Rust \cite{ozleyenOpenSourceImplementation2020}.

\textbf{CubedOS}, an implementation of a CubeSat \acs{OBC} in native Ada \cite{brandonCubedOSVerifiedCubeSat2017}.

\textbf{AI4Space}, a microservice-based Python platform for a Linux payload, developed for the scope of this thesis.
}
For this research, I selected 4 frameworks written in different languages (C++, Rust, Ada and Python) that implement a similar set of functionality representing the needs of typical \acs{OBC} software (\Cref{tab:m_software_requirements}). In order to extract information about the actual number and cause of defects in each framework, I will compile and execute the \acs{OBC} software, and then perform automated testing using \textbf{fuzzing}. For each identified major defect, I will evaluate the influence of programming language choice, and assess whether a different programming language would prevent the introduction of the fault, or if another quality assurance measure would have prevented it.
