More specifically, the execution and testing plan is elaborated into the following steps (\Cref{fig:m_software_fuzz_activity}):
\begin{itemize}
	\item \textbf{Static Analysis}: Involves extracting common metrics to identify code size (\acs{SLOC}) and complexity (cyclomatic complexity) for each tested framework, in order to estimate the scale and complexity of the codebase. I generated this information using the Lizard static analysis tool Lizard analyser \autocite{yinLizardExtensibleCyclomatic2019}.%
	\footnote{Since Lizard does not offer support for Ada, a custom script was used to generate results for this language only.}
	\item \textbf{Emulation and Execution}: Each framework is designed for execution on different sets of hardware, microcontrollers and architectures. However, to ensure similar testing conditions, it would be ideal to employ a similar hardware platform for all tested frameworks, as it is only software that is being evaluated. At the same time, testing on software only, without any hardware involvement, provides much better observability and controllability for the framework under test.
	
	Therefore, I decided to use the \textbf{Qemu Emulator}. Qemu provided a common machine to emulate all frameworks\footnote{More specifically, \texttt{mps2-an500} with a Cortex-M7 core for real-time frameworks, and \texttt{virt} with a Cortex-A9 core for Linux guests. I used Yocto Linux to create appropriate distributions for emulation.}. Each framework had to also be adapted for the relevant Qemu machine, by removing machine-specific code from the repository, and inserting generic Qemu-compatible code.\footnote{To ensure repeatability, all patches applied to the software are treated as ``treatments'', are tracked and versioned.}

	To provide \ac{IO} to the machines, I connected a custom-built device to the emulator, mocking the communication protocols\footnote{Responsible for \ac{TMTC}, for example via \acs{UART} or a custom datagram-based protocol}, sensors (e.g.~temperature) and an error handling observer.
	\item \textbf{Unit Testing}: To verify the correctness of the framework, the emulator and the communication protocol implementation, I used a common set of unit tests to verify the basic functional requirements, mainly involving \acs{TC} execution and \acs{TM} reception. The results of these functional tests are shown in \Cref{tab:m_software_fuzz_functional}.
	\item \textbf{Fuzz Testing}:
	The main part of this evaluation involved performing a dynamic evaluation of the frameworks under test. Fuzzing was selected as the method most likely to identify missed faults and edge cases that still exist on the actual source code.

	I used the AFL++ (American Fuzzy Loop) fuzzer for this evaluation \autocite{fioraldiAFLCombiningIncremental2020}, as it is a grey-box fuzzer with configurable mutators and native support for execution under the Qemu emulator, and more specifically used the LibAFL framework\footnote{LibAFL is itself written in Rust and provides a fork of Qemu with an augmented \acs{API} to support fuzzing execution.} \autocite{fioraldiLibAFLFrameworkBuild2022}.

	The fuzzing process involved:
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-2ex]
		\item Generating a corpus, based on the valid \acs{TC} packets created for unit testing,
		\item Running the \emph{havoc} and \emph{Grimoire} mutators in AFL to generate new permutations of the inputs,
		\item Feeding these inputs to the framework under test via the emulated communication interface in Qemu,
		\item Evaluating whether the software is in a good state, by checking for processor exceptions, Qemu exceptions, language panics, and by running a unit test (\acs{RFT}) suite, and
		\item Letting the fuzzer run for 6 hours to generate a substantial dataset.
	\end{enumerate}
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure*}[h]
	\includegraphics{graphics/software/activity.pdf}
	\caption[][-2cm]{Activity diagram of the CubeSat framework evaluation process}
	\label{fig:m_software_fuzz_activity}
\end{figure*}

\begin{figure}[p]
	\includegraphics{graphics/fuzzing1_inverted.png}
	\caption{Screenshot of LibAFL execution}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[p]
	\includegraphics{graphics/fuzzing2_inverted.jpg}
	\caption{Screenshot of software-under-test result}
\end{figure}

The use of fuzz testing as a methodology cannot ensure that all possible software defects are identified. Fuzz testing relies on an automated process that can identify when a running program has reached an erroneous state: in this investigation, this is represented by a \textbf{crash} (e.g.\ segmentation fault) or an unhandled exception. This approach also does not fully cover the set of all inputs to the software and its state: priority was given to \acs{TC} fuzzing. Nevertheless, the fuzzer managed to identify some software defects that caused undefined behaviour or an unhandled exception when a specially-crafted \acs{TC} was sent to the spacecraft. \textbf{In most cases, these telecommands would cause a software restart or hang if executed in-orbit}.

The results of the fuzzing tests are shown in \Cref{tab:m_software_fuzz_functional}.
Since the number of faults and frameworks tested do not allow statistical analysis, I have listed and elaborated all detected errors in \Cref{app:sw_errors_detected}.

\subparagraph{Conclusions}

The selected frameworks are characterised by a similar order-of-magnitude in terms of scale and complexity, with the most significant difference found in AcubeSAT's token count being \(2.4\times\) the one of AI4Space. The number of defects found is generally low, and prevents generating statistical insights\footnote{It is important to note, however, that the testing performed cannot guarantee that all possible defects have been identified.}. 
\begin{itemize}
	\item The selection of programming language alone \textbf{cannot guarantee program correctness}. Relatively simple issues were identified in projects in 3 out of 4 evaluated languages.
	Programming languages such as Rust, Ada and Python offer a default process for error handling, where unhandled exceptions lead to program termination. For example, Ada's \texttt{Unchecked\_Conversion} and Rust's \texttt{unwrap} calls can lead to a panic if the state of their input is not well established and reasoned about. Creating a program where such ``unsafe'' calls are avoided or properly handled requires a basic understanding of a language's error handling philosophy, and, where applicable, appropriate static checks. However, in Python, the fuzzer identified no crashes, as the nested exception handling in Python seemed to intercept incorrect \acs{TC}.
	\item In C/C++, unsafe calls (such as null dereferencing or out-of-bounds array access) commonly lead to \textbf{undefined behaviour}, which can propagate as a crash (segmentation/bus fault), incorrect data, incorrect functionality, or an infinite loop. On the contrary, the default data access functions of Rust, Ada and Python perform runtime checks and guarantee either a handled panic on invalid calls, or the propagation of the error to a user-defined mechanism. 
	\item The Rust compiler enforces an \textbf{exhaustiveness check} on enumerations and function results by default. Although this can be bypassed, it serves as an additiona layer of safety against unhandled edge cases and logical paths.
	\item The reliability benefit of a programming language selection has to be considered in relation to the \textbf{effort} associated with writing code in that language, and the various other environmental factors affected by this choice.
	
	The frameworks tested did not present noteworthy differences in terms of size and complexity that can be causally linked to the language. However, \acs{SCS} teams have to consider the knowledge/experience of developers for each language, along with its potential learning curve, as well as the size of the available ecosystem. An indication of this ``popularity'' is given in \Cref{tab:m_software_popularity}, which shows that Python and C/C++ have a larger ecosystem for embedded space applications compared to Rust, while Ada has a more than an order of magnitude more limited ecosystem. In turn, this metric provides an idea on the availability of open-source libraries for reuse, as well as the typical experience of students and young graduates, who are more likely to participate in academic \acs{SCS} projects.

	I also noted that Python, while offering a more robust to errors exception handling mechanism, seemed to be the least performant amongst the languages used. While low performance is a common criticism of Python \autocite{stoicoEmpiricalStudyPerformance2025}, additionally supported by performed interviews, the \acs{OBSW} framework used in this example was built around a microservice-based architecture, which may add additional overhead to performance. Therefore, no conclusion was made regarding Python's performance.
\end{itemize}

\subsection[Miscellaneous Software Topics]{Miscellaneous Software Topics}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_misc_software}

\paragraph{Communication Stack}
Many of \Cref{tab:m_software_requirements}'s requirements embody the communication of data between space and ground. Wireless transmission of data is complex by nature. Typically, this complexity is managed by separating the communication process into different layers, starting from the physical waves to the virtual application. \acs{ECSS} summarises the protocols used in space for each layer in the \emph{ECSS-E-ST-50C} standard \autocite{ECSS-E-ST-50C}. This includes a large variety of purpose-built protocols by the \acf{CCSDS}. As these protocols are optimised for use in space, they are typically not implemented in generally available or commercial software and hardware.
\marginnote{%
	For example, Manchester used an AX.25 packet format with a simple modulation for the \emph{KickSat} CubeSat, and a custom packet under \ac{DSSS} encoded with Gold Codes.%
}
CubeSat developers have therefore traditionally relied on custom (in-house) or generic solutions for communication, especially for the physical layer.

The selection of a protocol stack limits the compatible hardware and data budget of a spacecraft. In the case of \acs{SCS}, system characteristics further limit the available transmission power and effective antenna apertures, making these satellites resemble more \ac{IOT} devices than spacecraft.
The performance of an \acs{SCS} communication system is physically limited by the Shannon--Hartley theorem \autocite{khalighiSurveyFreeSpace2014}, and supported by concepts such as Spread Spectrum and Error-Correcting Codes, which help in trading off data rate for robustness to low receiver \acs{SNR}.

I provide a list of communication protocols in \Cref{tab:m_comms_protocols}. Proprietary protocols may be used instead when supported by a specific \acs{IC}, such as CC430 or nRF24. Reception of telemetry can be further derisked by participating in a ground station network, such as SatNOGS or TinyGS\footnote{for LoRa only}.

\begin{table*}[h]
\caption[][1ex]{Communication protocols typically used in CubeSats and pocket-sized spacecraft}
\label{tab:m_comms_protocols}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.4}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}lp{5cm}X@{}}
	\toprule
	Name & Layers & Description \\ \midrule
	\textbf{AX.25} & Data Link & Framing for radio-amateur stations \\
	\textbf{CubeSat Space Protocol} & Data Link, Network, Transport, Application & A protocol stack designed for CubeSats with support for modular services and inter-subsystem communication \cite{belaliConceptStandardTrajectory2025a} \\
	\textbf{LoRa} & Physical & Spread spectrum modulation built for low-power, long-range communication. The protocol is proprietary and its implementation details are not public. \\
	\textbf{DASH7} & Physical & Open-source LoRa alternative \\
	\textbf{OSDLP} & Data Link, Application & A stack of \acs{CCSDS} protocols for \acs{TMTC} data links that is lightweight enough for CubeSats and PocketQubes \cite{vardakisOpenSpaceData} \\

\end{tabularx}

%\subsection[SCS Debugging Interfaces]{\acs{SCS} Debugging Interfaces}
\paragraph{Debugging Interfaces}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_debugging_interfaces}

One part of the hardware-software interface that is used often during development is the \textbf{programming} or \textbf{debugging interface}, which allows developers to:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item Upload new software versions to a microcontroller
	\item Use a debugger to examine and alter the current state of the software
	\item Receive logs and \acs{TM} in various formats
	\item Inject \acp{TC}
\end{itemize}

This function is usually performed by a native debugging interface provided by the microcontroller\footnote{such as \acs{SWD} or \acs{JTAG}}, which is exposed through a dedicated \acs{PCB} connector. This interface then connects to a dedicated ``programmer'' device that interfaces between this \acs{MCU} signal and the programming computer. Spacecraft with multiple subsystems provide this functionality through a so-called \textbf{``umbilical connection''}, which provides diagnostic access to the satellite's power supply, batteries, and debugging interfaces.

\begin{marginfigure}
	\includegraphics{pictures/P1000717_resized.jpg}
	\caption{POQUITO's 16-pin umbilical connector, shown here above the antenna}
\end{marginfigure}

In CubeSats and PocketQubes, this interface is typically provided by a friction-locked connector, exposing each connection as a separate pin or plug. However, the limited space of femto-scale satellites motivates connection solutions that take up less space, by trading off compatibility with common connectors. Some notable examples are:
\marginnote{Typical considerations that have to be taken into account when selecting a physical umbilical interface are:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-0.5ex]
	\item Physical size
	\item Number of distinct connections
	\item Maximum data rate
	\item Maximum current capacity
	\item Lock type
	\item Maximum number of mating cycles
\end{itemize}
}%
\begin{itemize}
	\item \textbf{\acf{FFC} connectors and cables} that increase the density of connections, but usually require dedicated breakout boards to interface with \acs{COTS} \acs{EGSE}.
	\item \textbf{\acs{USB} connectors}, usually compliant to the USB-C specification, take advantage of the miniaturisation and power delivery capabilities of this common standard and allow directly interfacing a computer with the spacecraft. However, they require compatibility with the \acs{MCU}, additional power management hardware and a firmware bootloader. This approach is used by PyCubed-Mini \autocite{manchesterPyCubedMini}.
	\item \textbf{Other miniaturised connectors} that provide a high density, including industrial and commercial components. For example, the Monarch ChipSat used a 19-pin Micro-HDMI connector with a non-compliant pinout (\Cref{subfig:m_connectors_microhdmi}) \autocite{adamsRSelectedSpacecraft2019}.
	\item \textbf{Unpopulated vias, holes (``test points'') or pads} that can be accessed by probes, by soldering temporary connectors, or by using spring-loaded test pins. This approach is used by the Sprite (\Cref{subfig:m_connectors_pth}) \autocite{manchesterCentimeterScaleSpacecraftDesign2015} and SpinorSat \autocite{brashearsSpinorSatStep502018} ChipSats. To further reduce space, these nodes can be placed on \acs{PCB} edges. This kind of breakout pins were also envisioned by the Breakthrough Initiatives StarChip concept \autocite{wordenBreakthroughPrizes2017}. 
	\item A \textbf{snap-off development board that encloses the ChipSat} and provides development-only interfaces. This is also a concept used by the Sprite ChipSats (\Cref{subfig:m_connectors_detachable}) \autocite{manchesterCentimeterScaleSpacecraftDesign2015}. The disadvantage is that, after the development board is separated, it is harder to reprogram the spacecraft (e.g.\space after integration).
	\item \textbf{\acf{OTA} programming through a wireless network}. This network can be the nominal \acs{RF} link used by the satellite, or a purpose-specific link, if supported by the microcontroller, such as Wi-Fi or Bluetooth. % NFC?
	\item \textbf{Programming through another wireless technology}, such as infrared/visible light or sound. This requires dedicated hardware. This concept has been explored for use in Smart Dust devices through active or passive optical transmitters \autocite{warnekeSmartDustCommunicating2001}.
\end{itemize}

It is interesting to note the concept presented by \citeauthor{warnekeSmartDustCommunicating2001} for low-power communication. In the original paper, the authors use the concept of a retroreflector-based passive transmitter, in order to reduce power consumption when transmitting information. The idea of the paper is to use a \acs{MEMS} device to shift a spring-loaded mirror, thus switching between a non-reflective and a reflective state which can be assigned to the two binary states \cite{warnekeSmartDustCommunicating2001}.
\NextCitationOffset{-1.5cm} %vadOK
To reduce reliance on mechanical parts, this concept can be further extended by using an electric instead of mechanical modulator \autocite{rosenkrantzInnovativeModulatingRetroreflector2013}.

Finally, developers should consider solutions to reduce the data volume that needs to be transmitted for in-orbit programming, such as compression or binary diffing. \citeauthor{rosenkrantzInnovativeModulatingRetroreflector2013} note that in centimetre-scale miniaturisation levels it is much easier to \emph{compute} than to communicate, the authors conclude that software-based signal-enhancing measures are beneficial. In the context of the \acs{SCS} methodology, however, the additional complexity and effort required for this additional features has to be compared against the mission's goals.

\paragraph{Use of generative \acs{AI}}
\footnotetext{Credit: \href{https://dl.espressif.com/dl/schematics/pictures/esp32-wroom-32-front-back.jpg}{Espressif}}
Since the mid-2020s, \acs{AI}-assisted software development has become prevalent, through the use of \acfp{LLM} trained on publically available source code repositories.
\acs{AI} tools\footnote{Running on the cloud or locally.} can offer assistance ranging from code completion to generation of code structures and architectures. The training set of coding models usually contains embedded firmware for popular platforms, making them suitable for typical \acs{SCS} software development.

Initially, the use of generative \acs{AI} seems attractive for \acs{SCS} software, as it can easily generate large amounts of base code with less effort from developers. However, the quality and safety of generated code for use in mission-critical systems is still under investigation in research. A \citeyear{liuEmpiricalStudyCode2024} empirical study on three software problems found that ``the current language understanding and generation capabilities of the LLMs are not sufficient to generate complete functional code from highly specialised domain requirements'' \autocite{liuEmpiricalStudyCode2024}. The researchers found that, by default, the generated code from the specification may be incomplete and non-compliant to safety standards\footnote{In this case, the MISRA C standard.}. However, they noted that augmented prompts following a defined procedure can improve code quality.\marginnote{In \cite{liuEmpiricalStudyCode2024}, \citeauthor{liuEmpiricalStudyCode2024} compared \acs{LLM} code generation with model-based code generation, concluding that the latter requires a large investment of developer time and requires a correct model a priori.}

In \citeyear{tihanyiHowSecureAIgenerated2025}, \citeauthor{tihanyiHowSecureAIgenerated2025} compared state-of-the-art \acsp{LLM} and found that 62\% of the generated C programs included at least one security vulnerability, such as a buffer overflow or dereference failure \autocite{tihanyiHowSecureAIgenerated2025}.

On the other hand, a \citeyear{kirchnerGeneratingAutomotiveCode2025} comparison of locally-deployable \acsp{LLM} used an iterative generation (or ``code refinement'') process, where static and dynamic analysis is performed on the generated code, and the (potentially negative) results are fed back to the \acs{LLM}, until the acceptable code is produced \autocite{kirchnerGeneratingAutomotiveCode2025}. The researchers found that this method can produce code that performs the intended functionality and is compliant with the applicable specifications.

\acsp{LLM} are constantly evolving as of 2026, and it is difficult to provide long-lasting recommendations for their use in \acs{SCS} software development. Nevertheless, it seems that \acs{AI}-assisted coding can provide significant utility, as long as developers maintain an understanding of the principles and functions of the generated software, are aware of the potential safety and reliability risks, and perform adequate verification.

In any case, teams should consider adherence to coding standards and best practices when developing. This can be aided by freely available static analysis tools, such as \emph{Cppcheck}, \emph{clang-analyzer} or \emph{NASA IKOS}. Various coding standards\footnote{such as MISRA or the JSF AV C++ standards} are used in the safety-critical software industry, but are often criticised due to their false positive rates, difficulty of static verification, and added ``noise'' \autocite{boogerdAssessingValueCoding2008}. A \citeyear{boogerdAssessingValueCoding2008} empirical study assessed the number of rule violations and actual faults of an industrial project overtime, finding a negative correlation \autocite{boogerdAssessingValueCoding2008}. Instead, they identified a set of 12 rules\footnote{Due to licensing restrictions of the MISRA standard, I cannot reproduce these rules here.} that performed well in predicting faults, commenting however that it is ``possible that adherence to the MISRA standard as a whole would have made the software less reliable''. In practical terms, off-the-shelf static analysis tools provide recommendations of rule subsets that can be provide the required utility to developers.

\section{Methodology Tailoring}
\label{sec:METHODOLOGY_tailoring}

\subsection{Recommended Tools}

\ac{SCS} development is highly tool-based. At the same time, the overhead added by \ac{SYE} has to be minimised, especially to reduce repetitive actions.

In the ideal case, each proposed part of the \ac{SCS} methodology would be supported by dedicated hardware and software tools, and tailored to each specific use case.
However, as this undertaking is impractical and would require large amounts of research, development and testing, I instead recommend using well-established and industry standard tools that offer a degree of tailoring so that they can adequately support the \ac{SCS} methodology.

I consider tool selection as one of the first decisions that have to be taken when starting a new project.
This is suppoorted by case studies found in the literature.
\citeauthor{garrettProjectManagementSystems2022} \autocite{garrettProjectManagementSystems2022} notes that accessibility issues (in this case, difficult installation and no collaboration options) encountered when using \emph{Microsoft Project} led to noticeable delays in project schedule.
\citeauthor{honore-livermoreManagingProductDevelopment2021} \autocite{honore-livermoreManagingProductDevelopment2021} claim that appropriate tools can be helpful by aiding design traceability, cohesiveness and documentation, but require support from dedicated people to be useful.
\citeauthor{eilertsenOpenSpace2025} \autocite{eilertsenOpenSpace2025}, on the other hand, claims that incorrectly designed human-machine interfaces, as often found in software tools for space systems engineering, can adversely impact cognitive load and situational awareness. In turn, this leads to slower work or processes, and an increased chance of mistakes.

\marginnote{It is important to create a distinction between domain-specific tools, such as analysis/testing software, data gathering utilities, programming languages or design software, and generic \ac{SYE} tools.}

It is also hard to identify an ideal, globally-applicable set of tools for \acs{SCS} space missions, as tool selection will depend on the specific project requirements, team expectations and prior experience. However, a high-level list of requirements or needs can be elaborated as follows:
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item New tools should not increase the schedule or cost overhead of a project. If they do, this should be justified by a significant technical risk reduction.
	\item New tools should be easy to use\footnote{Ease-of-use is a subjective term that differs between teams. However, different User Experience definitions can be used to quantify this \cite{internetarchiveHumancomputerInteractionNew2002}.}. In practice, tool design should encourage a preference of using a specific tool against other approaches.
	\item Tools should not fragment information\footnote{For example, if a \emph{requirements management} tool does not support writing a justification for a requirement, developers may be tempted to create a separate document or use a separate tool to store this justification. This leads to data fragmentation, requires keeping more than one database up-to-date, and makes information retrieval harder.}. This can be achieved by using generalisable/tailorable tools.
	\item The overall cognitive load of team members related to tool usage should be reduced, by limiting the amount of different loci that have to be monitored or updated. This can be achieved, for example, by limiting the amount of tools, using tools that combine multiple functions, or using automated notification/update processes \autocite{goodwinWhatSaaSSprawl2025}.
	\item Project Management should actively support the use of tools. This can be achieved through regular training sessions, high support availability, and consistent maintenance of databases.
	\citeauthor{honore-livermoreManagingProductDevelopment2021} \cite{honore-livermoreManagingProductDevelopment2021} recommend assigning ``champions'', i.e.\@ people responsible for the upkeep of these tools.
\end{enumerate}

A list of example recommendations is provided in \Cref{tab:methodology_indicative_tools}.

For software, developers have to choose between proprietary or open-source tools, which can be hosted on-premises or in the cloud.
Recent discourse has identified vendor lock-in as a major risk in long-lasting projects \autocite{lundellPerceivedActualLockin2021}, with additional concerns over the control and security of user data. Anecdotal reports suggest that educational or non-profit discounts may not be perpetually honoured \autocite{kalamSlackExtortingUs2025,elodidthatMaxonStudentLicenses2024}. The European Commission has identified the reliance on external technology suppliers as a ``strategic liability'' and ``supply chain risk'', posing open-source hardware and software as an ideal solution \autocite{europeancommissionCommissionOpensCall2026,pageBrusselsPlotsOpen2026}.

An important consideration is that of \textbf{versioning} software data. The word ``versioning'' encapsulates the need for data to be archivable (with the ability to peek and restore history), traceable (with the ability to identify why/by whom a change was made), and shareable across team members. The use of this terms originates from software Version Control Systems used for collaboration on code. Software utilities sometimes offer these features out-of-the-box. Alternatively, off-the-shelf versioning software can be used on raw data \autocite{broschIntroductionModelVersioning2012}.

Finally, with the emergence of highly-automated ``vibe coding'' as of 2026, where software generation can be performed in large volumes by \acsp{LLM}, rapid changes in the systems engineering software ecosystem are expected to be seen in the short-term. Most notably, \acsp{LLM} can potentially encourage the development of in-house tools, tailored for each mission, and progressively adapted to an organisation's needs \autocite{liscioRIPLowCode2026}.

\subsection{Tailoring Questionnaire}

The methodology presented in \Cref{chap:METHODOLOGY} does not constitute a ``one-size-fits-all'' solution.
Practitioners are encouraged to pick methods suited to their projects (for example, selecting between a system-level or partition-level iteration approach), or internally tailor each method to the degree necessary.

In general, tailoring is not uncommon for Agile methodologies. \citeauthor{campanelliAgileMethodsTailoring2015} analysed 56 studies about Agile method tailoring \autocite{campanelliAgileMethodsTailoring2015}. 
They found that the identification of main environmental criteria, as defined by \citeauthor{kalusCriteriaSoftwareProcess2013} \autocite{kalusCriteriaSoftwareProcess2013}, is a common first step in the tailoring process.
\textbf{Method engineering} is the main tailoring approach used thereafter. \citeauthor{fitzgeraldEmpiricalStudySystem} have defined an approach to method engineering, which suggests that ``methods be represented as discrete predefined and pretested components'', making a method engineer/administrator responsible for selection of the appropriate ones \autocite{fitzgeraldEmpiricalStudySystem}. This is not unlike the approach already proposed in this work.

\citeauthor{xuUsingProcessTailoring2008} have proposed a slightly different process-tailoring strategy, mainly focused for software engineering applications \autocite{xuUsingProcessTailoring2008}.
Their strategy consists of the following steps:
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-1ex]
	\item \textbf{Evaluating the project goals and environment.} This is similar to the elaboration of the project characteristics per \citeauthor{kalusCriteriaSoftwareProcess2013} \cite{kalusCriteriaSoftwareProcess2013}.
	\item \textbf{Assessing challenges.}  This is similar to the previous step, but assesses specific hardships that are encountered by the project in the areas of resources, communication, requirements management, politics, and techical aspects.
	
	Challenge assessment is done through a \emph{challenge assessment questionnaire}. The authors present a 26-question form which evaluates typical project difficulties or strengths for each of the above areas, and assigns a score to each, in order to inform where the tailoring efforts should be focused\footnote{Note that some challenge scores may be countering each other. For example, one evaluation question (resource challenges) asks whether ``the project has enough personnel with relevant skills [\textellipsis]'', while another (communication challenges) asks whether ``the team size is small''.
	For a large project, the project goals might warrant a large team size a priori. In this case, a \emph{large} actual team might encourage focusing the tailoring on \emph{communication}, while a \emph{small} team will call for a focus on \emph{resource} challenges.
	}.
	\item \textbf{Determining tailoring strategies for various process elements}.
	Here, the authors provide specific options for actions that can be taken to modify a process.
	They link \textbf{verbs} (\emph{add, downsize, drop/skip, expand, redefine, replace}) with \textbf{elements} (\emph{task, sequence, artifact/document, role, iteration}).
	For example, tailoring may result in increasing the amount of documentation (\texttt{add + document}) or reducing product scope (\texttt{downsize + task}).
	\item \textbf{Validating, executing and evaluating the tailored process} in an iterative fashion.
\end{enumerate}

\marginnote[2cm]{%
Note that the tailoring suggestions presented here are not intended to replace the case study results and explanations presented in \Cref{chap:CASESTUDIES,chap:DISCUSSION}. Instead, they are given as an initial reference to practitioners.%
}
Since this work proposes a more concrete methodology, it is possible to extend the idea of a \textbf{tailoring questionnaire} based on specific questions and \textbf{recommendations}, influenced either by logical expectations or actual data from the case studies (\Cref{chap:CASESTUDIES}).

\chapter{Case Studies}
\label{chap:CASESTUDIES}

		Beware of bugs in the above code; I have only proved it correct, not tried it.}}{Donald E.\@ Knuth}
\end{fullwidth}

As explained in \Cref{chap:RESEARCH_APPROACH}, the \acf{SCS} \acl{SYE} methodology will be evaluated as part of an initial \emph{Descriptive Study II} research step. To perform this evaluation, I participated in four \acs{SCS} projects as a Systems Engineer\footnote{See \Cref{app:contribution_matrix} for information about team members and my contribution to each mission.}, ranging from a \acs{PCB}-shaped payload to an independent PocketQube satellite, with the goal of successful environmental qualification and, when possible, orbital launch.\marginnote{The maximum duration of each case study was strictly limited by the available launch windows, to a maximum of approx.\@ one year, demonstrating the short turnaround times between development start and launch (\Cref{sec:INTRODUCTION_research_gaps}).} The objective of these Case Studies has been to generate data about the typical \acs{SCS} development environment, iteratively elaborate the \acs{SCS} methodology, and use the generated data to evaluate the validity of this methodology. The case study approach follows a \emph{holistic multiple-case} design based on \citeauthor{yinCaseStudyResearch2014}'s classification \autocite{yinCaseStudyResearch2014}.

The execution of these case studies followed an action research plan as shown in \Cref{fig:c_action_research}, where I observed the interactions between members, project progress and friction points, reflected on them in conversation with each project manager, and proposed changes to the used methodology \autocite{mcniffActionResearchPrinciples2003}. The four projects I was involved in were also developed by different teams, which supports the interpretation of \emph{triangulation} that suggests gathering data from at least three groups of people \autocite{mcniffYouYourAction2010}. The projects also represented a diverse range of system types, including a payload, two ChipSat-style spacecraft, and a PocketQube.

\begin{figure}
	\begin{tikzpicture}[
		>={Stealth[length=6pt]},
		node distance=2cm,
		font=\sffamily\small
	]
		\newcommand{\drawcycle}[1]{
			\begin{scope}[shift={(#1,0)}]
				\node (plan)    at (90:1.5)  {plan};
				\node (act)     at (0:1.5)   {act};
				\node (observe) at (270:1.5) {observe};
				\node (reflect) at (180:1.5) {reflect};
				
				\draw[->] (plan)          to [bend left=35] (act.north);
				\draw[->] (act.south)     to [bend left=35] (observe.east);
				\draw[->] (observe.west)  to [bend left=35] (reflect.south);
				\draw[->] (reflect.north) to [bend left=35] (plan.west);
				
				\draw[->] (plan.east) -- ++(2.5,0);
			\end{scope}
		}
		\drawcycle{0}
		\drawcycle{6}
	\end{tikzpicture}
	\caption{Example of action research action-reflection cycles \cite{mcniffActionResearchPrinciples2003}}
	\label{fig:c_action_research}
\end{figure}

The data from the case studies was collected in the form of personal notes and records on software tools, including spreadsheets, task tracking tools, and Git commits. This process was aided by critical conversations with the project responsibles and team members. These observations included information about team interactions, project \ac{SYE} status, as well as technical details relevant to each project, applicable to the system or subsystems. These observations can be classified as direct participant-observations through my role in the project, combined with the available documentation in the form of the aforementioned records \autocite{yinCaseStudyResearch2014}.

In the following sections, each project is presented in the following order \cite[106]{mcniffYouYourAction2010}:
\begin{enumerate}
	\item \textbf{Project Environment and Requirements}: A description of the fixed external parameters that define the project.
	\item \textbf{Observation and Descriptions}: Enumeration of the directly observed facts and events during the project. This includes documentation or explanation of a system,\footnote{and often justification for specific technical design decisions} or the elaboration of events during a project's timeline.
	\item \textbf{Explanations}: Interpretation of what is happening in the project, how it can be logically connected to the application (or not) of parts of the \acs{SCS} methodology, and technical/organisational lessons learned.\footnote{The explanations are generated through a combination of personal reflection, based on my own claims, and social reflection, based on conversations and retrospectives with other project members \cite[194]{mcniffYouYourAction2010}.
They follow the techniques of \emph{Pattern Matching} and \emph{Explanation Building} \cite[250]{yinCaseStudyResearch2014}.}
	\item \textbf{Counter-factual Analysis}: In order to compare application with non-application of the \acs{SCS} methodology, I perform a counter-factual analysis after each case study \autocite{bennettEventCausationCounterfactual1987}. More specifically, I evaluate the question ``What if the \acs{SCS} methodology were not applied in this project?''.
	
	In order to aid the analysis, I will separate this question into the different \emph{methods} or \emph{practices} proposed by the \acs{SCS} methodology, i.e.\@ the evaluated question will be ``What if the \emph{XYZ} \acs{SCS} method were not applied in this project?''\footnote[][-1cm]{Note that when an \acs{SCS} method is said to \emph{not} be used, one still have to specify \emph{which} method was actually used. In most cases this will be obvious by considering the inverse/complementary of a method (e.g.\@ a fully modular vs.\@ a non-modular design), but I will describe the counter-factual case considered when it is not.}. The evaluated results will be quantitative and expressed in terms of changes in \emph{development time}, \emph{development cost}, and \emph{risk}. When the quantity of this change cannot be inferred directly from a causal relationship, I will use data from the literature, previous missions (CubeSat/\acs{SCS}), or from the current mission. %vadOK

	In terms of terminology, I will use the term ``\textbf{treatment}'' to refer to \textbf{the application of an \acs{SCS} method}, inspired from observational study literature \autocite{zhaoCausalInterpretationsBlackBox2021}.
	\begin{margindefinition}[Treatment]
		A treatment is the application of an \acs{SCS} method of \Cref{chap:METHODOLOGY} in a case study.
	\end{margindefinition}
	For simplicity, I will not consider interactions between the counter-factual (non)-application of treatments, as that would involve exponentially increasing combinations to explore.
\end{enumerate}

\marginnote[1cm]{%
	Regarding the metrics evaluated for the counter-factual analysis:

	\textbf{Cost}: For cost I do not consider labour costs, as their accounting will vary in different organisations. Instead, labour costs should be considered in connection with the development time.
	
	\textbf{Risk}: Because there is no quantitative assessment of ChipSat risk and risk factors, I only perform a qualitative evaluation of risk.
	
	In this case, ``risk'' should be perceived as %
	\emph{the probability that a non-conformance is present in the project at qualification or launch.}
	This definition encompasses the technical aspects of spacecraft functionality. In most cases, a ``non-conformance'' can be understood as a \emph{requirement non-compliance}. Whether optional requirements (\Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_requirements}) are considered in this definition depends on each mission, and for this work, ``must have'' and ``should have'' requirements are considered. In the case of design errors, these non-conformances can appear as ``\acl{DOA}'' or partially successful missions. This definition does not consider programmatic risk of time and budget overruns, as these are considered to be captured by the previous metrics already.
	%\emph{the probability that a rework (an additional iteration) is needed.} This includes technical in-orbit risk, since \emph{an in-orbit failure implies that ground rework is needed.} Finally, risk is considered \textbf{\emph{a-posteriori}}, i.e.\@ considering all available data from the mission. For example, in the general case, more iterations lead to reduced risk. However, given the information that \emph{the system has been validated}, performing an additional iteration will not decrease risk, since the mission data already demonstrates that sufficient technical performance has already been reached. On the other hand, adding an \emph{analysis} would decrease risk even if a test had been performed, since a test might not cover the set of all possible environmental conditions.

	All metrics are considered \textbf{\emph{a-posteriori}}, i.e.\@ considering all available data from each mission. For example, in the general case, more development iterations should lead to reduced risk. However, given the information that \emph{a function has been validated}, performing an additional iteration will not decrease risk, since the validation data already demonstrates that sufficient requirements compliance has already been reached. On the other hand, adding an \emph{analysis} would decrease risk even if a test had been performed, since a test might not cover the set of all possible environmental conditions.
	
	%\textbf{Features}: Based on the \acs{MOSCOW} model, desirable (should/can) features are not requirements, but may be included in the final product.
}
Finally, a cross-case analysis is performed in \Cref{sec:CASESTUDIES_results} in order to assess the external validity of the results \autocite{yinCaseStudyResearch2014}. Apart from a combination of the results of the case studies, this analysis will include \textbf{statistical tests} over the results of the \textbf{counter-factual analyses}, in order to evaluate whether the space missions with no treatments applied (i.e.\@ not following \acs{SCS} methodology) and space missions with all treatments applied (i.e.\@ following \acs{SCS} methodology) have statistically significant differences in development time, cost, and risk.

\begin{figure*}[p]
	\centering
	\includegraphics[height=\textheight]{graphics/CaseStudy.Process.pdf}
	\caption[][-0.5cm]{Activity diagram of the case study research flow}
	\label{fig:c_activity_diagram}
\end{figure*}

\newthought{A detailed} process diagram for the counter-factual analysis research flow is shown in \Cref{fig:c_activity_diagram}. 
The process intends to perform internal and external validation of the \acs{SCS} methodology, following the definitions proposed by \citeauthor{yinCaseStudyResearch2014} \cite[33]{yinCaseStudyResearch2014}. As the purpose of internal validation is to establish whether the \acs{SCS} methodology actually affects the \acs{SYE} metrics of a project from a causal (rather than spurious) relationship, the questions that have to be answered are: \cite{yinCaseStudyResearch2014}
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item Can we infer that the methodology significantly affects a project metric?
	\item Have we considered rival explanations and possibilities?
	\item Does the evidence appear convergent and airtight?
\end{enumerate}
One of the processes that can answer these questions is \emph{explanation building}, which involves the following steps \cite[120]{yinCaseStudyResearch2014}:
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-1ex]
	\item[e1.] Making an initial but tentative explanatory proposition
	\item[e2.] Comparing the data from the case study against this proposition
	\item[e3.] Revising the earlier proposition
	\item[e4.] On a multiple-case study, comparing amongst the set of revised propositions
\end{enumerate}
In order to be able to clearly make logical conclusions and evaluate confouding factors, this process is isolated and tailored for each proposed ``treatment'' of the \acs{SCS} methodology. The end result, linked to each step of the explanation building process, is also shown in \Cref{fig:c_activity_diagram}.

The list of investigated treatments is listed in \Cref{tab:c_list_of_treatments}, as a selection of proposed methodology parts from \Cref{chap:METHODOLOGY}. The internal validity and explanation building is evaluated for each case study, in \Cref{sec:CASESTUDIES_chipsat_evaluation,sec:CASESTUDIES_ai4space_evaluation,sec:CASESTUDIES_poquito_evaluation,sec:CASESTUDIES_chipysat_evaluation}.

The cross-case step of the \emph{explanation building} process is elaborated in \Cref{sec:DISCUSSION_crosscase}. \citeauthor{yinCaseStudyResearch2014} \autocite{yinCaseStudyResearch2014} identifies \textbf{\emph{statistical generalisation}} and \textbf{\emph{analytical generalisation}} as two distinct methods of generalising data from individual case studies to evaluate external validity. Due to the small sample size compared to the information available, analytical generalisation is proposed to extrapolate the results into other missions. However, in cases where there are clear distinctions between the application and non-application of treatments, statistical generalisation can also be used to complement the results of the former approach.

\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.8}
\renewcommand\tabularxcolumn[1]{m{#1}}% for vertical centering text in X column
\begin{adjustbox}{max width=1.1\textwidth,center}
\begin{tabularx}{1.1\textwidth}{@{}l>{\raggedright}p{3.5cm}XX@{}}
\toprule
 & Treatment & Description & Initial theory \\ \midrule
1 & \textbf{Bypassing structural/thermal analysis} & \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_verification} proposes avoiding often-expensive analytical modelling of system behaviour, recommending reliance on more reduced tests instead. Typically, analysis reduces the technical risk of a system, however, its cost-benefit ratio is expected to increase for small \acs{SCS}. & Consistent reduction in development time, but significant increase in cost (due to test campaigns). Increase in risk (due to undetected errors). \\
2 & \textbf{In-house manufacturing} & External manufacturing is one of the main blocking points in a project's development flow (\Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_manufacturing}). This point involves the decision to perform manufacturing processes in house. Generally, this will be considered as equivalent to in-house \emph{soldering}, since this is the only critical manufacturing process that most projects required. & Total development time should decrease. Cost should decrease (assuming no external manufacturing costs). Risk should increase (assuming team not experienced in manufacturing for space). \\
3 & \textbf{Multi-step development} & \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_satscrum} proposes the use of an \emph{iterative/incremental} approach based on iterations, instead of a sequential, single-step approach, where a ``\acf{BDUF}'' step is taken first, and a single manufacturing/testing round is done afterwards. & Total development time should decrease (assuming a long \acs{BDUF} step). Cost should increase (due to more manufacturing runs). Risk should be project-dependent (based on whether a team can predict non-conformances early or late). \\
4 & \textbf{Short iterations} & While iterative design is generally common in small space projects, the \acs{SCS} methodology specifically proposes the use of short, Agile-like iterations (1--2 months), that should make identifying errors faster. Note that no restriction is set on the iterations having a fixed length; instead, their length was flexible (predetermined or not). & Development time should decrease. Cost should increase (due to more manufacturing runs). Risk should be project-dependent (based on project size and whether iteration length \& team size are suitable). \\
5 & \textbf{Iterating over partitions} & An option of the \acs{SCS} methodology is to perform iterations over \emph{partitions} of the system, instead of the entire system (\Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_slicebased}). This allows teams to avoid having to constantly iterate on complex designs. Note that this does not involve iterating over single subsystems only. & Development time should stay the same (assuming constant number of iterations). Cost should decrease (less manufacturing). Risk should increase (less early data about complete system performance). \\
6 & \textbf{Robust software framework} & \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_softwaredevelopment} proposes the use of a software language and/or framework that theoretically increases reliability without an increase in development time. & Development time and cost should stay the same, with reduced risk. \\
7 & \textbf{Robotic Process Automation} & \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_automated_testing} proposes the use of automated testing processes and tools to streamline the system's verification. & Development time should be project-dependent (cost of automation vs.\@ less manual effort). Cost should increase (when infrastructure needed to automate testing). Risk should decrease (general assumption based on increased testing). \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{adjustbox}
\caption{Evaluated methodology treatments and initial statements}
\label{tab:c_list_of_treatments}
\end{table*}





\FloatBarrier
\newpage
\section{ChipSat-1}
\label{sec:CASESTUDIES_chipsat1}
%Seminal papers: \autocite{borgueDevelopingDistributedFractionated2022a}

ChipSat-1 was the first mission I participated in within the scope of this thesis, with the objective of generating data for femto-satellite projects and experimenting on an early iteration of the \ac{SCS} methodology (\Cref{chap:METHODOLOGY}).

The ChipSat-1 mission was motivated and constrained by a launch opportunity provided by a third-party satellite integrator, who offered to mount a ChipSat device as a secondary payload on one of their missions. The ChipSat itself was a technology demonstrator, intending to experiment with the use of \acf{VLC} between components of a distributed system. It consisted of a single \ac{PCB} separated into 3 regions (each emulating one part of a distributed system), measuring \qtyproduct{5 x 5}{\centi\meter} and weighing \SI{18.3}{\g} in total. Power generation and communication were handled by internal solar cells and a transceiver/antenna respectively, with the host providing only a mechanical and electrical inhibition interface.

This \emph{hosted payload} mission was developed in 2022, and environmentally tested and integrated in 2023. However, due to administrative issues with the host's launch provider, ChipSat-1's host satellite, and by extension, ChipSat-1, were never launched.

ChipSat-1 cost \SI{8930}{EUR} in material and testing costs, required a development time of roughly 12 months, and involved seven members of the team who contributed in different degrees. It was developed iteratively, with multiple representative hardware models having been built in the University of Luxembourg's CubeSat Lab. I participated as a Systems, Hardware and Software engineer in the project.

\begin{marginfigure}
	\includegraphics{pictures/chipsat1_1.jpg}\\%
	\includegraphics{pictures/chipsat1_2.jpg}
	\caption{Front and back views of the ChipSat-1 \acl{FM}}
\end{marginfigure}



\subsection{Mission Description}
The main constraint that restricted the selection of possible missions and classes was the \qtyproduct{5 x 5}{\centi\meter} size envelope required by our host for the payload slot. During the beginning of the project,
we hosted brainstorming sessions to decide on a mission concept, based on the criteria of complexity, cost, development time, novelty, risk, available competences, and strategy alignment.

\begin{table*}[]
	\caption{ChipSat-1 initial mission requirements}
	\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}llX@{}}
		\toprule
		& Source  & Requirement                                                                            \\ \midrule
		1 & Host    & ChipSat-1 should cover a maximum area of \qtyproduct{5 x 5 x 1}{\centi\meter}.          \\
		2 & Host    & ChipSat-1 should weigh at most \qty{50}{\gram}.                                         \\
		3 & Host    & ChipSat-1 shall comply to an agreed-upon electrical inhibition mechanism.               \\
		4 & Host    & ChipSat-1 shall conform to an agreed-upon mechanical interface.                         \\
		5 & Host    & ChipSat-1 should be compatible with a \qtyrange{450}{550}{\kilo\meter} \acs{SSO} orbit. \\
		6 & Host    & ChipSat-1 shall be electrically independent of its host.                                \\
		7 & Mission & ChipSat-1 shall demonstrate an inter-satellite communication technology.            \\
		8 & Mission & ChipSat-1 should demonstrate free-space Visible Light Communication in orbit.				\\
		9 & Mission & ChipSat-1 shall validate its operations by transmitting data to the ground.             \\
		10 & Mission & ChipSat-1 should be compatible with the SatNOGS network.             \\
		11 & Mission & ChipSat-1 should survive for at least 1 year in orbit.             \\
		12 & Mission & ChipSat-1 can generate and transmit environmental data from temperature, light, radiation, vibration, cameras, and/or magnetic field sensors. \\
		13 & Mission & ChipSat-1 should not rely on earth-pointing. \\
		14 & System  & ChipSat-1 shall generate on-board energy using solar radiation.             \\
		15 & System & ChipSat-1 should be operational during eclipse.             \\
		16 & System & ChipSat-1 should be separated into one primary and more, electrically independent, secondaries. \\
		
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabularx}
\end{table*}

The objective of a \ac{VLC} demonstrator aligns with the prevalence and research in optical communication for ground-space and inter-satellite links, usually with lasers acting as transmitters \autocite{kaushalOpticalCommunicationSpace2017,khalighiSurveyFreeSpace2014,griffithsHighsensitivityIntersatelliteOptical2021}. Optical communication can offer situational advantages over traditional \ac{RF} links, especially in terms of directivity, bandwidth and power consumption. This comes at a cost of complexity, as laser transmitters and photodiode receivers need to be accurately managed and steered to achieve a reliable link. The use of \acfp{LED} instead of laser emitters trades off this high level of complexity at the cost of directivity. A simple single-\acs{LED} system performing \acf{OOK} can require a less complex implementation than an \ac{RF} transmitter.

\citeauthor{griffithsHighsensitivityIntersatelliteOptical2021} \autocite{griffithsHighsensitivityIntersatelliteOptical2021} have considered the use of \acp{SPAD} in combination with miniature \acp{LED} for an inter-satellite link, and performed a range and data rate simulation for different transmitter lenses. They found that a lens-less system, transmitting with \SI{1}{\milli\watt}, can achieve a \SI{2.87}{\kilo\meter} range at \SI{10}{\kilo\bit/\second}, which can be extended to \SI{141}{\kilo\meter} with a \( \sim \)\SI{2}{\degree} \acf{HPBW} lens.

\acsp{LED} and photodiodes as miniaturised \acs{COTS} components present an attractive use-case for \ac{SCS}, as the cost of reduced functionality that comes with small size can usually be accepted for such systems. The kilometre-scale ranges of \acs{LED}-based systems are for example applicable to satellite swarms that require networking \autocite{timmonsRangeBasedRelativeNavigation2022}. Compared to their \ac{RF} counterparts, these \ac{VLC} systems can be preferable when low power, high bandwidth and high directivity\footnote{For example, \citeauthor{griffithsHighsensitivityIntersatelliteOptical2021} \autocite{griffithsHighsensitivityIntersatelliteOptical2021} calculate that a \SI{2.5}{\centi\meter} diameter ACL25416 lens provides an equivalent of \SI{52.0}{\decibel i} of gain. However, such a directional system will require appropriate steering of the light source. On a centimetre-scale spacecraft this can be achieved, for example, using miniaturised mirrors steered by piezo-electric motors. \qtyproduct{1 x 1 x 0.5}{\centi\meter}-sized steering modules with \SI{0.1}{\degree} accuracy are commercially available \autocite{M3MicroBeamSteeringTechBulletin}.} is required, eliminating the need for a high-gain antenna or high-power transceiver.

ChipSat-1 was therefore decided to act as a technology demonstration experiment for a simple \ac{LED}-based \ac{VLC} system. Due to the limitation of a single flown spacecraft, we decided to place the transmitter (\ac{LED}) and receiver (photodiode) on a single \ac{PCB}, intending to focus on testing only the effects of ambient light, temperature and radiation on the payload and bus components.

In order to create a more representative example and emulate clock drift between the transmitter and the receiver, we split ChipSat-1 into two parts:
\begin{itemize}
	\item The \textbf{``primary''} part, transmitting data to Earth and receiving data from the secondary using photodiodes.
	\item The \textbf{``secondary''} part, generating data through environmental sensors and broadcasting it using an \acs{LED}, but without capability for Earth communication.
\end{itemize}

This configuration essentially emulates a \textbf{``fractionated''} system, where different parts of the intended functionality are separated into smaller independent spacecraft, instead of a larger monolithic one. This concept has been explored before for entire buses \autocite{mathieuAssessingFractionatedSpacecraft2006} or payloads \autocite{alandihallajMultiplepayloadFractionatedSpacecraft2022}.

For ChipSat-1, the available space allowed us to implement two ``secondary'' parts, designed to generate data that would be downlinked through the \ac{VLC} link: One had a gyroscope to measure and report angular velocity, and the other an ambient light intensity sensor.

\begin{figure}[h]
	\includegraphics{renders/chipsat_parts_render.jpg}
	\caption[Render of ChipSat-1, showing its 3 parts]{Render of ChipSat-1, showing its 3 parts. Each part is electrically isolated to the others, sharing only a common ground and the free-space optical link.}
	\label{fig:c_chipsat_parts_render}
\end{figure}

\marginnote{In technical documentation, I refer to the primary as ``\texttt{212}`` and the secondaries as ``\texttt{211A}``, ``\texttt{211B}``, showing a three-part ratio that starts with a common denominator and the horizontal \& vertical sizes.}

The main components comprising the payload are red \acs{COTS} \ac{LED} emitters placed on the secondaries, and two \acs{PIN} photodiodes (one for each secondary) placed on the primary. Information is transmitted (broadcast) by the secondaries unidirectionally, consisting of status information and sensor measurements.

Data is modulated by turning the \acsp{LED} on and off (\acs{OOK} --- \acl{OOK}) to transmit bytes, each bit corresponding to one symbol, with Manchester code applied\footnote[][-2.5cm]{\emph{Manchester code} describes a pattern where one bit is encoded as low, then high, or high, then low. This guarantees that every symbol will have at least one bit transition. While this code reduces bit rate to half, it \begin{enumerate*}
		\item guarantees that no \ac{DC} component remains in the output signal, allowing easy high pass filtering to reduce the influence of ambient light (mainly sunlight);
		\item is self-clocking, allowing easy synchronisation and clock recovery on the receiver side.
	\end{enumerate*} While other techniques such as scrambling or \ac{RLL} could be used to achieve the above, Manchester coding was applied to reduce system complexity. }, at a rate of \SI{50}{baud/\second}.
\marginnote{An alternative plan for the \ac{VLC} system involved using Colour Shift Keying instead of \acs{OOK}, which roughly corresponds to \ac{RF} frequency modulation and could have better noise resistance. \acs{OOK} was however chosen to keep complexity low \autocite{monteiroDesignImplementationColorShift2014}.}

To make the design highly configurable without physical modifications, all processing was done in software, using an \acs{ADC} for reception, with an oversampling factor of \( \times 5 \) to assist in clock recovery.

The signal path of the received pulses was first fed into a band-pass filter to eliminate noise and background light. The synchronisation itself also took place in software. Bit synchronisation to the transmitter's clock happened by detecting the edges of the transmitted Manchester code, while frame synchronisation was performed by waiting for a synchronisation word.

At a system level, the secondaries transmitted data at periodic \qty{1.5}{\second} intervals, while the primary was constantly waiting for data reception.
Once data is received, its integrity was checked and stored on the on-board \ac{NVM}. A statistical aggregation was then broadcast by the primary over the main periodic \acs{RF} beacon.

\begin{figure*}
	\centering
	\begin{tikzpicture}[node distance=1cm,every node/.style={scale=0.85}]

		text centered, draw=black!40, fill=blue!20,text width=2.8cm

		\node (demanch) [block, right=of sync] {De-manchestering};

\subsection{System Description}

\begin{figure}[h]
	%\acuse{IC,LED}
	\acempty{IC}
	\acempty{LED}
	\includegraphics{media/graphics/Block_ChipSat}
	\caption{Internal block diagram of the ChipSat-1 system}
	\label{fig:c_chipsat_block}
\end{figure}

The constraint of the flat \qtyproduct{5 x 5}{\centi\meter} shape of ChipSat-1 was the main driver behind component selection and bus capabilities. Out of the environmental criteria mentioned in \Cref{tab:mission-characteristics}, the following were considered as additionally critical drivers:
\begin{itemize}
	\item \textbf{Developer availability and experience}: Due to the relatively small size of the development team and difficulty in involving external personnel, we tried to limit components and technologies to those that were familiar to the team's engineers.
	\item \textbf{Complexity}\footnote{See \Cref{def:complexity} for the definition of complexity.}: In order to reduce risk, the number of different parts and interfaces between them had to be minimal. For the context of this project, reducing complexity was equivalent to reducing \ac{BOM} size.
\end{itemize}

\begin{margintable}
	\caption{ChipSat-1 \acl{BOM}}
	\begin{tabular}{@{}lr@{}}
		\toprule
		Energy Harvester & BQ25570 \\
		Microcontroller & STM32WL55CCU7 \\
		\acs{FRAM} & FM24V05 \\
		Light Sensor & TSL25853PM \\
		Gyroscope & IAM-20680HP \\
		Photodiodes & VEMD1060X01 \\
		\acs{VLC} \acsp{LED} & 155124RG73200 \\
		Solar cells & KXOB25 \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabular}
\end{margintable}


In terms of system design, the small size of the spacecraft made it impractical to create a ``modular'' structure, where different subsystems are separated.\footnote{See \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_modularity}.} We also decided from the onset to avoid the use of \acs{ADCS}, thermal management and any other structural components, as these subsystems would require unmanageable energy, real estate and mass.

\marginnote{We selected the STM32WL line of ``wireless microcontrollers'' due to my previous experience with this family, although other alternatives will also be suitable.}
The ``brain'' of the satellite is a \acf{MCU} \ac{IC} with an integrated transceiver and power amplifier, essentially consolidating two subsystems in a single package.
\marginnote{We avoided the use of ``radiation-tolerant'' or ``radiation-hardened'' packages due to their usually high cost, size, and supply chain restrictions. Instead, we decided to consider \ac{SEE} damage as an acceptable risk. Also see \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_design_miscellaneous}.}

The \ac{MCU} is responsible for handling spacecraft status, sensor inputs and data outputs. While the primary used a transceiver-equipped \ac{MCU}, the secondaries used a smaller footprint \ac{IC} without wireless capabilities. The primary also included an \acs{FRAM} memory for packet storage. Programming and debugging was done through an integrated ribbon connector, present on all parts.

The resulting block diagram can be seen in \Cref{fig:c_chipsat_block}.

\paragraph{Power Generation}

\begin{marginfigure}
	\includegraphics{pictures/chipsat1_4.jpg}
	\caption{ChipSat-1's solar panels}
\end{marginfigure}

While an unconventional system, such as a ChipSat, can attract the application of unconventional technologies for power generation, such as primary batteries or thermoelectric sources \autocite{SMAD}, we followed an approach similar to past ChipSats, by relying on fixed miniaturised solar cells.

A maximum target power generation budget can be approximated as follows \cite{SMAD}:
%\marginnote{``Design'' degradation refers to incomplete capture of sunlight over a ChipSat's area due to small cells not covering the entirety of the \ac{PCB}}
\marginnote{where:
\begin{itemize}
	\item \( P \): Orbit Average Power
	\item \( A \): Surface Area
	\item \( n \): Solar Cell Efficiency
	\item \( D_{\text{design}} \): ``Design'' degradation
	\item \( D_{\text{temp}}\): Temperature degradation
	\item \( D_{\text{eclipse}}\): Loss due to eclipse
	\item \( D_{\text{pointing}}\): Loss due to no pointing
\end{itemize}

``Design'' degradation refers to incomplete capture of sunlight over a ChipSat's area due to small cells not covering the entirety of the \ac{PCB}.
}
\begin{align}
	P &= G_{SC}
	\cdot A
	\cdot n
	\cdot D_{\text{design}}
	\cdot D_{\text{temp}}
	\cdot D_{\text{eclipse}}
	\cdot D_{\text{pointing}}
	\\ &=
	\qty{1361}{\watt/\meter^2}
	\cdot \qty{25}{\centi\meter^2}
	\cdot 20\%
	\cdot 70\%
	\cdot 85\%
	\cdot 66\%
	\cdot 66\% \\ &=
	% 1361W/m^2×(5cm×5cm)×20%×70%×85%×66%×66% to mW
	\qty{176.4}{\milli\watt}
\end{align}

This theoretical analysis shows the expected power input, which should however be complemented with the relevant component selection. Most commercially available solar cells for space use follow a \qtyproduct{7 x 4}{\centi\meter} footprint.
\citeauthor{manchesterCentimeterScaleSpacecraftDesign2015}'s Sprites \autocite{manchesterCentimeterScaleSpacecraftDesign2015} had cut solar cells to fill the available area. However, due to the complexities of such a cutting process, for ChipSat-1 we instead decided to use low-footprint \acs{COTS} solar cells. Since there was no module that fit the available solar area of the \ac{PCB}, this resulted in slightly less available overall power, due to higher ``design degradation''.

Due to the high number of different commercially available solar cell sizes, I developed a Python optimisation tool based on rectangle-packing to identify the combination of solar cells that could theoretically produce the highest power.

\begin{marginfigure}
	\caption{Example result of the solar cell packing optimisation tool}
	\label{fig:c_panel_packing}
	\includegraphics{graphics/panel_packing.png}
\end{marginfigure}


To perform \ac{MPPT} and convert the panel voltage to an appropriate one for the remaining electronics and energy storage, we used a combined low-footprint power management \ac{IC}, which essentially acts as the \acf{EPS} of each ChipSat part. The commercial availability of such ``power harvesting'' electronics was very beneficial to the available real estate of the ChipSat.\marginnote{With the power management \acs{IC} itself taking up \qtyproduct{4.5 x 5.5}{\milli\meter}, most of the space for power management was used by the 17 passive components required to configure it.}

Each part of the ChipSat was equipped with its set of components for power generation and management, the primary having 4 solar cells in a 2S2P configuration, and the secondaries having 2 in a 2S1P configuration.


\paragraph{Energy Storage}

While secondary\footnote{i.e.~rechargeable \autocite{SMAD}} batteries are the standard choice for energy storage in miniaturised spacecraft, the typical temperature conditions experienced by ChipSats are not suited for use of standard battery chemistries. More specifically, the thin and flat shape of ChipSats combined with their small thermal mass and lack of structure means that they lose their temperature very quickly during eclipse, being able to reach even  \SI{-100}{\degreeCelsius} \autocite{manchesterCentimeterScaleSpacecraftDesign2015}.

As most commercially available batteries do not support charging or discharging on temperatures significantly below \SI{0}{\degreeCelsius}, ChipSats typically forego long-term energy storage \cite{manchesterCentimeterScaleSpacecraftDesign2015}. While alternative solutions can be investigated, such as high-range \acs{LTO} batteries, coin-cell batteries, or heaters, they are associated with further drawbacks, reducing possible power draw or increasing size. ChipSat-1 was therefore decided to operate only with sunlight available.

\marginnote{At \qty{4}{\volt}, the capacitor can support a \qty{300}{\milli\watt} load for
	\( \qty{1}{\milli\farad} \cdot (\qty{4}{\volt})^2 / \qty{300}{\milli\watt} = \qty{53}{\milli\second} \).}
Nevertheless, each ChipSat-1 part included a capacitor to provide short-term energy storage and eliminate energy fluctuations, especially in the case of high-power loads. We avoided the use of liquid electrolyte capacitors due to safety concerns for their use in space \autocite{zednicekPassivesNewSpace2021}, using \qty{1}{\milli\farad} tantalum capacitors instead.

\paragraph{Communications}

Because of the small size of the ChipSat and the lack of pointing, \ac{RF} communication with a fixed non-directive antenna was envisioned.


\begin{marginfigure}
	\includegraphics{pictures/W_CHIPSAT_F.jpg}
	\caption{View of the external side of the ChipSat, with the antenna elements attached}
\end{marginfigure}

The selection of \ac{RF} transmission characteristics was guided primarily by the selected transceiver \ac{IC}, limiting us at \( + \qty{22}{dBm}\) of output power and the \qtyrange{435}{438}{\mega\hertz} radio-amateur band. Different antenna configurations were considered in conversation with our host provider. We settled on a short-wave dipole powered by an impedance matching circuit, with preliminary antenna analysis performed on HFSS software.

The low transmission power and directivity restricted the available data rate of the system. As such, we decided on an \acs{FSK} modulation with a \qty{200}{bit/\second} baud rate. Due to the lack of relevant requirements and need for higher power consumption, ChipSat-1 supported only transmission and not reception of \acfp{TC}.

\subsection{Development Flow}

		\node[slice, below=0.6cm of orb, rounded corners=5pt] (slice) {};

		\draw ([xshift=-2mm,yshift=-2mm]current bounding box.south west) rectangle ([xshift=2mm,yshift=2mm]current bounding box.north east);

In the early planning stages of the ChipSat-1 project, it was decided to follow an iterative lifecycle process, with the first steps consisting of creating an early working prototype (or \acs{MVP}). For this reason, an initial Gantt chart was drafted and updated every month. While 4 iterations were planned in the beginning, the project required 5 iterations in the end.

For project management, the team followed a low-overhead, primarily ``ad-hoc'' approach. We used Microsoft's 365 suite for communication and document sharing, spreadsheets for task tracking, and GitLab for software and hardware versioning.

During the \emph{1\textsuperscript{st} iteration}, we performed initial prototyping and experimentation to confirm the planned design and component selection. More specifically, I experimented on the wireless transmission as well as payload conceptualisation. This involved purchasing electronics and development boards, and setting up a \emph{breadboard model} of the required functionality. I also started iterating on the operational software, developed in C/C++. Finally, we set up purchase orders for critical \acsp{IC} that were identified, in order to maintain enough stock against the chip shortage that was still in effect during 2022 \autocite{mohammadGlobalSemiconductorChip2022}.

This initial iteration provided positive results on the concept feasibility and component suitability, and yielded no significant needs for design changes. It lasted 2 months.

During the \emph{2\textsuperscript{nd} iteration}, enough information was gathered to proceed to full \ac{PCB} production. It was understood by the team that this iteration step was not intended to create a Flight Model, but an Engineering Model, similar in Form, Fit and Function to the \ac{FM}, but lacking the inhibit features required for flight.

\begin{marginfigure}
	\includegraphics{pictures/chipsat1_ADM}
	\caption[Initial concept of a ChipSat-1 deployable antenna]{Initial concept of a ChipSat-1 deployable antenna. Measuring tape is often used for small satellite antennas due to high availability and desirable material properties, mainly elasticity.}
	\label{fig:c_burn_antenna}
\end{marginfigure}

I designed the schematic and layout using the KiCad software. An early technical decision was to use passive components no smaller than 0603\marginnote{0603 (imperial) refers to passive components with dimensions \qtyproduct{0.8 x 1.6}{\milli\meter} \cite{ECIAEIA1981F}}, in order to aid potential future rework. The manufacturing and assembly of the \acs{PCB} was done by an external partner. Between the completion of the design and initial testing, we had a waiting period for manufacturing of approx.\ 30 days for manufacturing, during which software engineering was done.
	
The rest of the team also iterated on a deployable antenna design based on a burn wire release mechanism (\Cref{fig:c_burn_antenna}). However, this design was abandoned in favour of a conventional fixed dipole, as the deployable mechanism would have presented a risk of damage to our host spacecraft.
	
Initial testing revealed the following non-conformances:
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item The \ac{PCB} layout footprints of the solar cells had smaller dimensions than the actual cells, causing a short-circuit between adjacent cells.
	\item An incorrect overvoltage configuration resistor was selected for the power supply of the three parts, causing intermittent voltage output.
	\item The debugging connector was not compatible with standard flexible flat cables, making debugging tedious due to a lack of locking.
\end{enumerate}
These issues were corrected through rework for the initial model.

During the \emph{3\textsuperscript{rd} iteration}, we manufactured a \textbf{structural} model of the ChipSat to verify its interface to the host spacecraft, as well as the dynamic envelope of the antenna design. This required a \textbf{vibration test} at an external facility. We also performed an \textbf{\acf{OTA} \acs{RF} test} to verify the correct operation of the integrated antenna.

For the \emph{4\textsuperscript{th} iteration}, we designed another \ac{PCB} intended as a Qualification or Flight Model, manufactured again by an external supplier. In addition to fixes for the previously found issues, we changed the following:
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item Replaced primary supercapacitor for two tantalum capacitors
	\item Implemented electric inhibit circuit
	\item Added antenna matching circuit
\end{enumerate}

The 4\textsuperscript{th} iteration also had to include qualification testing, which had to be performed in external facilities and took one week, including travel. Based on the host's requirements, vibration testing had to include a resonance search and random vibration testing, while thermal testing had to include 4 thermal cycles under vacuum. The requirement to manufacture a test adaptor added an additional external procurement delay.

While the environmental tests were successful without any issues, functional testing revealed a reversal in the polarity of the inhibition signal\footnote{Meaning that the ChipSat was ON when the activation signal was missing, and OFF when it was present.}. As an acceptable rework was not possible due to the layout of the \ac{PCB}, we instead decided to complete a \emph{5\textsuperscript{th} iteration} of the ChipSat following a minor \ac{PCB} redesign. As such, the ChipSat produced during the 4\textsuperscript{th} iteration was downgraded to an \emph{\acf{EQM}}, while the last iteration would produce an equivalent to a \emph{\acf{PFM}}.

Environmental tests were repeated in an external facility for the 5\textsuperscript{th} iteration, this time functioning as acceptance tests, which were also required by our host provider. Following the successful results, our host integrated ChipSat-1 into their spacecraft.

During the 3\textsuperscript{rd} and 4\textsuperscript{th} iterations, and in conversation with our host, we also designed and manufactured (externally) the aluminium mechanical structure that would serve as the interface between the host spacecraft's exterior and our ChipSat.
% The second iteration was made due to a change in requirements for this interface (??)
