\subsection{Lessons Learned}
\label{sec:CASESTUDIES_chipsat_lessons}

From a technical point of view, we gathered the following points during development of the spacecraft:
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-1ex]
	\item Early versions and renders of the \acs{PCB} were available early in the project. These were useful as they could represent realistic concept art, used in communication with external partners and collaborators of our group.
	\item As the inhibit mechanism was managing a critical electrical interface, it should have been present in the early prototypes and iterations. At least a breadboard model should have been built for the inhibition circuit.
	\item The initial versions of the inhibit interface used wires directly soldered on the board as an physical interface. This is not recommended, as it makes assembly and handling of the \acs{PCB} significantly more difficult. Instead, a secure locking connector should be used, along with the appropriate strain relief.
	\item Due to the small number of engineers participating in the project and their parallel involvement in projects separate to ChipSat-1, it was difficult to perform repeated and accurate design reviews, which would have detected some of the non-conformances before manufacturing. Personnel allocation and work package management are therefore crucial parts of a project like this. Even though a Gantt chart was created and updated monthly, project planning updates should have been more regular and the allocation of tasks clearer, with updates to the expected and completed amount of work for each task. For reviewing with limited domain experts available, various tactics can be employed, such as self-explanation \autocite{chiElicitingSelfexplanationsImproves1994}, checklists, or \acsp{LLM} \autocite{rasheedAIpoweredCodeReview2024}.
	\item While external manufacturing is convenient and results in high workmanship quality, it suffers from high lead times and difficulty of rework. In many cases, especially when design errors are expected, \ac{PCB} rework will be required in any case.
	In the case of ChipSat-1, a lead time of approx.~1 week was required for unpopulated \acs{PCB} procurement, and approx.~1 month for manufacturing. This was often augmented by unpredictable turnaround times for university procurement approval.
	Therefore, teams should consider investing and gaining knowledge regarding in-house manufacturing, especially in electronics and testing equipment.
	\item ChipSat-1 was subject to different changes in external interfaces and requirements during its development period. While internal iterations had the goal of correcting defects before reaching the ``flight-ready'' version of the spacecraft, they would also have been necessary to conform to the design changes. In general, these types of changes and uncertainties should be expected in unexplored payload and spacecraft form factors.
	\item The use of solder on solar cells made assembly \& inspections difficult, and was related to one of the major non-conformances during early iterations. Instead, it is preferable to use conductive glue to mount cells, to the degree possible. The solar cells used proved particularly fragile and sensitive to storage and soldering conditions, making it additionally difficult to determine their state and degradation level at any point in time. Teams are encouraged to maintain spare stock and testing capability to ensure the performance of solar cells.
	\item Test points and \acsp{LED} on \acsp{PCB} are highly beneficial, as they remove doubts about the state of the board during testing. However, labelling has to be clear: on the first iteration of the board, the function of each indicator and test point was not marked correctly, requiring the schematics (or similar documentation) to be viewed before any measurements could be interpreted.
	To prevent confusion, test points and \acsp{LED} should state their function in the \acs{PCB}'s silk screen. On double-sided test-points, both sides of the board should contain the corresponding labels.
	\item For ChipSat-1, the lead time for unpopulated \acs{PCB} manufacturing was close to one week, while the lead time for external \acs{PCB} assembly was close to one month. We identified defects in the design (particularly incorrect footprints or incorrect procured components) after the assembly. These defects can be more easily detected and resolved when the \acs{PCB} designers and manufacturers can communicate directly, so that any dubious design decisions (that are only visible when assembly starts) are investigated. In case another \acs{PCB} version needs to be manufactured, in-house manufacturing would also reduce the cost and time overhead.
	\item Breadboard testing was performed via the use of development boards and individual elements. In the case of ChipSat-1, no significant data was gathered through this approach. From a financial point of view, the cost of purchasing development boards for used \acs{IC} is not always lower than the cost of manufacturing \acsp{PCB}. Unless a specific trade-off experimentation between physical parts needs to be conducted, and if the required expertise is available, I recommend that low-scale projects skip breadboard experimentation and produce \acs{PCB} designs on the early iterations, which can then be progressively improved.
	\item During software development, it was clear that specific functions of the ChipSat-1\footnote{Mainly from \Cref{tab:m_software_requirements}.} could be reused from other open-source spacecraft. The low footprint of available CubeSat software, combined with the substantial performance of miniaturised \acsp{MCU}, make it possible to reuse open-source libraries for real-time operations, \acs{PUS} services, and generic business logic. Nevertheless, due to the simplicity of ChipSat-1's functional requirements, no such large-scale libraries were used. The software was written in C++, based on the FreeRTOS operating system and the STM32 \acs{HAL}.
	\item The \emph{ok\_serialiser} library was used to encode and decode data from binary packets (\Cref{app:ok_serializer}). As (de)serialisation is a task that has to be performed by almost all spacecraft for ground-to-earth communication, internal communication, and storage, developers are encouraged to adopt a robust serialisation library. Doing so will prevent repetition and breaking changes when interface specifications are updated.
	\item While \acs{FRAM} and \acs{MRAM} memories are generally low-density, they provide a very simple and robust interface to allow configuration storage. In contrast to traditional \acs{RAM}, they simplify \acf{FMEA} by eliminating power loss as a failure mode\footnote{However, note that corruption due to incomplete transmissions is still possible.} As such, they are highly recommended for small spacecraft setups.
	\item Decoding the \acs{VLC} signal while rejecting ambient light required a careful selection of sampling parameters and digital filters. Executing this pipeline (for transmission and reception) entirely in software allowed us to freely configure the parameters and data rates for the system, essentially operating as a low-data rate \acf{SDR}, which was made possible because of the low intended data rate for the \acs{VLC} system. If a hardware-backed decoding system was used, physical modifications would be needed for this experimentation. Additionally, I used a software oscilloscope to trace the output of each processing step during signal reception, in order to fine-tune the decoder against ambient light. The combination of these capabilities proved to be highly efficient as an ``in-the-box'' solution, comparable to using a physical signal analyser and corresponding debugging process.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Evaluation}
\label{sec:CASESTUDIES_chipsat_evaluation}
\label{sec:CASESTUDIES_chipsat1_evaluation}


% Be specific about a causal link that led to a specific result that can be identified: By using element of the methodology we had XYZ effect support by XYZ data

During the development of ChipSat-1, we captured numerical data representing the time taken by developers (\Cref{tab:c_chipsat_times}) and associated costs (\Cref{tab:c_chipsat_costs}).
This data can be used to speculatively and retroactively evaluate the different project management decisions made by the team. 
Based on the classification of the \ac{SCS} methodology presented in \Cref{chap:METHODOLOGY}, I will consider the \emph{\acs{SCS} methodology} methods used by the team and evaluate their impact on \textbf{cost, schedule, features} and \textbf{risk}.

\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.8}
%\renewcommand\tabularxcolumn[1]{m{#1}}

Team expertise. & Development time and cost can be accurately predicted based on the times \& cost of a single iteration. \label{tab:c_chipsat_counterfactuals}

The results of the counter-factual evaluation in \Cref{tab:c_chipsat_counterfactuals} are explained as follows:
\begin{enumerate}
	\item \textbf{Bypassing structural/thermal analysis}:
	The decision to not perform any environmental analysis was influenced by the lack of engineers experienced in these disciplines in our team, the pre-existing requirements for testing, and the lack of many tunable design parameters that would be influenced by the tests. These analyses are also sometimes associated with considerable effort. While the actual time taken will depend on the size and complexity of the problem, the resolution of the analysis, and the experience of the engineers, for this calculation I considered the lower end of work hours reported in the literature of CubeSat projects (\Cref{tab:l_cubesat_time_drivers}).

	Nevertheless, these analyses can increase confidence in the design and inform on the non-specific thermal/structural response of the system. They also reduce the necessity of additional vibration/thermal testing. Therefore, in general, I consider that they reduce risk. At the same time, additional environmental testing increases costs due to facility rental costs, which are calculated based on ChipSat-1's actual cost budget (\Cref{tab:c_chipsat_costs}).
	\item \textbf{External manufacturing}:
	One decision that seems to vary between teams concerns whether construction, assembly and manufacturing, especially for \acs{PCB} and electronics, takes place \emph{in-house} or in external facilities. The decision will vary between teams, as different manufacturing requirements will be relevant at different stages, and in-house assembly requires an initial investment in equipment, available space and expertise, and sometimes dedicated lab personnel.

	ChipSat-1 performed all soldering externally, meaning that the recommendation of the \acs{SCS} methodology was \emph{not} used. As such, the counter-factual statement for this point is ``\emph{How would the metrics change if \acs{PCB} soldering was done in-house?}''

	For this counter-factual analysis, I will consider that an unpopulated \acs{PCB} is still manufactured externally,\footnote{This is standard in most cases, as unpopulated \acs{PCB} manufacturing generally requires more specialised equipment, and external procurement can have very low lead times.} but soldering is done in-house with a reflow process, taking up 3 working days, multiplied by the 3 \acs{PCB} runs of the project\footnote{This estimation is based on results from AI4Space (\Cref{tab:_ai4space_times}) and ChiPySat.}. I also consider two risk factors, summing up to no perceptible risk change: \begin{enumerate*}
		\item External manufacturing usually adds a month of lead time, during which no changes can be made, compared to \(\sim \)\qty{1}{week} for an unpopulated \acs{PCB}. This makes iterations longer and prevents the rapid testing required by Agile methodologies.
		\item Experience during the project showed that communicating with an external manufacturer adds an additional layer of error-prone interfacing. Shifting the manufacturing responsibility closer to the designers makes it easier to detect defects early.
	\end{enumerate*}

	In larger space projects, external manufacturing is also associated with higher workmanship quality. However, the low number of components, low overall stresses, and short mission durations of \acs{SCS} generally reduce workmanship requirements, and there are no documented failures because of poor electronics manufacturing (\Cref{sec:LITERATURE_cubesat_reliability}). As such, and considering the above risk factors, I will not consider a risk increase for in-house electronics manufacturing.	
	\item \textbf{Iterative multi-step development}, as opposed to a single iteration with a ``\acl{BDUF}'':
	For ChipSat-1, we followed an Agile mentality, emphasising prototyping and engineering work over iterating on the design and performing reviews.

	While it is difficult to estimate how many of the detected issues would have been resolved early with more time spent in the design, it was clear that, due to evolving external requirements beyond the team's control, \textbf{more than one production run would have been necessary, even in the case of a single-step plan}.
	As such, most of the redesign work that was performed would have still been necessary, even in the case of a \acs{BDUF} approach. For this evaluation, I will consider that a single-step iteration plan adds one person month of design/review work, but that it generally reduces risk due to more detailed evaluations of the design. The cost would be reduced by the manufacturing cost of all incomplete iterations.
	\item \textbf{Short, agile iterations}:
	The \acs{SCS} methodology recommends short (and when possible timeboxed), system-wide iterations. This is in contrast to typical longer iterative/multi-step methods used in CubeSat projects, (\Cref{sec:LITERATURE_cubesat_environment}).

	In the case of ChipSat-1, short iterations allowed us to address externally changing requirements on time, but increased non-conformances due to less time spent in design reviews. Out of the 5 performed iterations, only the 5\textsuperscript{th} iteration was unplanned. Assuming that each iteration would last \(1.5\times\) longer in the longer case, and that each iteration is connected with a \( \frac{75+75}{5} = 30 \) hour prototyping \& integration time, the effect of longer iterations would increase project effort by \qty{120}{hours}. The cost would be reduced by the manufacturing cost of the last iteration.
	\item \textbf{Iterating over partitions} instead of the entire system:
	2 out of 5 iterations for ChipSat-1 were performed on partitions of the system, instead of the entire system. This had the benefit of reducing cost and integration time at the expense of increased risk.
	The cost reduction can be calculated by the \acs{PCB} system cost. The development time reduction can be estimated by considering an additional \emph{integration} duration of \( 2\cdot\frac{75}{3} = \qty{50}{hours} \) 
	\item \textbf{Robust software framework}:
	ChipSat-1 used a standard programming language (\emph{C++}) instead of a safety-oriented one, as well as standard software libraries\footnote{With the exception of \texttt{ok-serialiser}, see \Cref{app:ok_serializer}.}.

	The selected microcontroller did not natively support a recommended language, such as \emph{Rust}. Personal experience shows that creating an appropriate \acs{HAL} for a Rust-based platform would last close to one working week. Based on the analysis of \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_software_reliability}, I consider that the use of Rusk would reduce software risk.
\end{enumerate}

	\includegraphics{analyses/cf_chipsat1_nonconformance}
	\caption{ChipSat-1 cumulative non-conformances}
\end{figure*}

	\label{fig:c_tradeoff_chipsat1}

\section{AI4Space}
%Seminal papers: \autocite{thoemelLeanDemonstrationOnBoard2024} \autocite{thoemelAI4SpaceSnTLaunches}

The AI4Space mission was developed in the University of Luxembourg in the form of a payload to be hosted by a Skykraft Ltd.\@ micro-satellite. I participated in the project as a systems, hardware, and software engineer.

\begin{figure}[h]
	\includegraphics{pictures/ai4space1.jpg}
	\caption{AI4Space \acl{QM}}
	\label{fig:c_ai4space_qm}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Mission Description}

\begin{table*}[h]
	% Source: AI4SpaceLeanProjectPlanAndControl - Copy
	\caption{AI4Space mission requirements}
	\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}llX@{}}
		\toprule
		& Source  & Requirement                                                                            \\ \midrule
		1 & Host    & The AI4Space payload shall be of dimension \qtyproduct{15 x 100 x 100}{\milli\meter} or smaller.          \\
		2 & Host    & The AI4Space payload shall be of a slot card design as required by Skykraft.                                         \\
		3 & Host    & The AI4Space payload shall require less than \qty{5}{\watt} of electrical power.               \\
		4 & Host    & The AI4Space payload shall be enabled with a serial connection for operations and pre-launch checkout.                         \\
		5 & Host    & The AI4Space payload shall conform to the requirements of the Skykraft Interface Control Documentation. \\
		6 & Mission & The AI4Space payload shall demonstrate an in-orbit \acs{ML}-based thermal anomaly detection algorithm. \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabularx}
\end{table*}

AI4Space was hosted as part of Skykraft's payload hosting programme, mainly aimed at educational institutions \autocite{skykraftSkyride2022}. More specifically, Skykraft provided slots accepting \qtyproduct{10 x 10}{\centi\meter} \acsp{PCB} (or ``Skykards''). These Skykards were electrically connected to their host with a \acs{PCI}-like edge connector, responsible for receiving energy and transferring data bidirectionally, through a proprietary serial interface. The requirements and interface details were communicated to users by the host company and updated at regular times.

The selection of a mission for the AI4Space platform was largely limited by the constraints of the payload launch opportunity provided. AI4Space was therefore largely a \emph{constraint-based} mission, restricted primarily by available size, weight, energy, and data budget \autocite{thoemelLeanDemonstrationOnBoard2024}.

\begin{marginfigure}
	\includegraphics{pictures/ai4space2.jpg}
	\caption{AI4Space \acl{QM} (detail)}
\end{marginfigure}
The selected mission involved the detection and classification of \textbf{thermal events} using non-conventional sensors, combined with Machine Learning performed in orbit (``on the edge'').
More specifically, AI4Space intended to demonstrate the use of infrared thermal cameras to generate temperature information about a spacecraft's subsystems and components. This information would then be fed into a \ac{ML} algorithm to classify whether a component exhibits non-nominal (or anomalous) behaviour\footnote{By exhibiting, for example, an out-of-range temperature, or another erratic pattern.}. As this is a simple classification problem, it is greatly supported by established \acs{ML} methods that can reasonably adapt to it.

AI4Space was therefore developed as a technology demonstrator platform for \acs{AI}-based anomaly detection, as well as an educational tool for payload and system development approaches.


\subsection{System Description}

\begin{figure}[h]
	%\acuse{DC}
	\acempty{DC}
	\includegraphics{media/graphics/Block_AI4Space}
	\caption{Internal block diagram of the AI4Space system}
	\label{fig:c_ai4space_block}
\end{figure}


The functional part of the AI4Space payload, responsible for the generation of representative thermal images, consisted of:
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item Four thermal cameras looking towards a fixed point
	\item Four switchable resistors to emulate ``hot points'' 
	\item A dummy microcontroller\footnote{For easy integration, an ATMega Arduino processor was used.} to simulate a representative temperature increase during normal satellite operation
\end{enumerate}

The AI4Space payload contained no Attitude Control, Communications, or Electrical subsystems, as these aspects were covered in their entirety by the host spacecraft.

\begin{figure}
	\centering
	\includegraphics{pictures/ai4space3.jpg}
	\caption{AI4Space ``breadboard'' model}
	\label{fig:c_ai4space_breadboard}
\end{figure}
All other functions were covered by a \emph{Raspberry Pi Zero 2W}, serving as the system's \acf{OBC} and Payload Data Processor. The Raspberry Pi was chosen as a ready-to-use \acs{COTS} platform that essentially acts as a complete \acs{OBC}, and reduces the complexity of hardware design for a microcontroller. An additional benefit was the higher available set of programming languages or tools, seen as positive within the context of multiple software developers for the project. 

The incorporation of a Raspberry Pi implies the implementation of a complete base Linux system. This is usually a disadvantage in pocket-sized spacecraft due to the absence of real-time software and the higher energy consumption. However, given AI4Space's form as a payload and the available power consumption allowance, these disadvantages were not considered severe in favour of the additional convenience, tools, libraries and functions available in a Linux environment, especially considering the need for multiple software developers to be involved in the project. In fact, while most CubeSat projects rely on low-footprint \acp{MCU} for processing needs, Linux boards have found their place in some payload units \autocite{chronasUpsat_msc_thesis2017}.

AI4Space's software was divided into \textbf{microservices} \autocite{thonesMicroservices2015}, each responsible for one part of the payload's operation or mission. These microservices were written in Python and interfaced through the RabbitMQ message broker.

\subsection{Development Flow}
\label{sec:CASESTUDIES_ai4space_flow}

AI4Space followed an iterative lifecycle, similar to ChipSat-1. A Gantt chart was also drafted and kept up-to-date, with 4 planned different iterations. The iteration plan included a fully functional breadboard model, and two identical production models, for qualification and flight. The project management plan also included some semi-formal documentation artifacts, including semi-monthly progress updates, non-conformance tracking, requirements compliance tracking, \ac{CONOPS} and user manuals. The project team consisted of 9 members in total.

\begin{marginfigure}
	\includegraphics{graphics/ai4space_rabbits.png}
	\caption{The AI4Space operator web interface, used for testing}
\end{marginfigure}

The first iterations consisted of experimentation at breadboard-level only, based on a mockup of the final AI4Space board (\Cref{fig:c_ai4space_breadboard}). While some effort was needed to create a similar representative setup, AI4Space's software could be developed nominally, as similar Raspberry Pi boards were used across models. The breadboard model did not result in any decisions to change the design, but was useful purely as a software development platform. Development took place through a \acs{HIL}-only approach, by executing software directly on the target platform. The use of a Raspberry Pi combined with Linux and Python also allowed a remote approach to development: As the target supported Wi-Fi, we could execute scripts without a direct, physical connection to the board.

The qualification model was assembled in-house. No design errors were detected that would require modifications in the design. However, the team struggled to timely find alternatives for out-of-stock \acsp{IC}, influenced by the global chip shortage at the time \autocite{mohammadGlobalSemiconductorChip2022}. The flight model was assembled by an external manufacturer.

Functional testing took the form of Full Functional Tests, verifying nominal and off-nominal situations, and Mission Tests, verifying a day-in-life schedule. Following the ``test-as-you-fly'' approach, these tests used the in-orbit interfaces, instead of the Linux-compatible development interfaces. Full Functional Tests were automated through the use of the Robot Framework (\acs{RPA}) \autocite{robotframeworkfoundationRobotFramework}. In order to verify the data interface with the host spacecraft, the host company delivered hardware and software for a mock host interface which could be connected to AI4Space.

No formal environmental testing was performed for AI4Space, as there were no testing requirements imposed by the host, as part of which AI4Space was expected to be tested regardless. However, the team used an in-house vacuum chamber to emulate the expected thermal conditions for the payload. No thermal control was used, as instead we relied on the natural temperature differences and sunlight to test the behaviour of AI4Space's thermal cameras.

\begin{marginfigure}
	\includegraphics{graphics/ai4space_robot_results.png}
	\caption{Example results from a Robot framework test case}
\end{marginfigure}

The digital interface for \acs{TMTC} was based on Python's \textbf{\texttt{construct}} library \autocite{bulskiConstruct2020}, essentially acting as a \textbf{model-based interface specification}. The Source of Truth for the specification was written in Python, combined with text-based comments. An automatic script then generated additional text-based documentation based on this specification.

\todo{AI4Space Mission Operations section}

\subsection{Lessons Learned}

Based on the development progress and non-conformances detected, the following technical lessons learned were gathered regarding AI4Space:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-1ex]

\item The use of a Raspberry Pi provided an unexpected advantage by allowing \textbf{remote programming and monitoring} through Ethernet and Wi-Fi\footnote{However, as with any \acs{RF} system, care had to be taken to disable transmissions inside resonating structures (e.g.~\acs{TVAC} chambers) and before launch.}. This meant that multiple developers could work simultaneously on the same unit, with no physical connection or wiring requirements. This also significantly simplified the \ac{EGSE} required for testing. 

\item The easy capability for reprogramming, thanks to the file management capability provided by the Linux terminal and interfaces, was made use of after AI4Space was launched and operated in orbit.
After launch, we discovered a major non-conformance in the data interface between the payload and the host: Due to an incorrect understanding of the bidirectional messaging sequence, AI4Space was able to receive (and execute), but not transmit messages. This issue was not discovered during ground testing, as we assumed that our understanding of the provided documentation and the test jig was correct. However, after clarifying the correct sequence, we were able to transmit a software patch (in the form of a Python script) that allowed proper communication. Since the project was split into different scripts, the reprogramming process only required uploading a single file. A notable future recommendation from this experience is the \textbf{recommendation to test and verify electrical interfaces with the actual launch provider}, if the opportunity is available.

\begin{marginfigure}
	\includegraphics{graphics/ai4space_captures.png}
	\caption{Example captures from AI4Space's thermal cameras}
\end{marginfigure}

\item The project suffered from the peak of the electronic component shortage in 2022. One of the shortcomings of the design was the inertia in making considered changes to the design versus performing patches on \acsp{PCB} and waiting for components to be available. Two components were mostly affected by this issue: Namely, the Arduino ATMega328 processor used as the dummy thermal generator, and an I2C multiplexer \ac{IC}. Both components were replaceable with a \acs{PCB} change: The Arduino processor with a similar stocked \acs{MCU}, and the multiplexer with an equivalent chip of a different form factor. Alternatively, earlier preparation would have called for an early stock of components during the \acs{PCB} design process. The component shortage also proved difficult to navigate due to institutional procurement restrictions, combined with the fact that many stock items were only available from one or two days from standard distributors before being depleted.

\item The main obstacles in the critical path were interfaces with external providers and collaborators. The typical procurement times of \acp{PCB} and components were significant and had to be planned around. Any unexpected delays also significantly influenced progress towards the iteration goal. While preparation for such cases might be possible in some contexts, Agile frameworks usually suggest maintaining direct communications with external collaborators, to the extent possible.

\item The breadboard models at the early stages of the project were highly beneficial, as software development would not be possible without access to this kind of representative hardware. However, at the same time, they did not yield considerable insights about the rest of the design. An alternative lifecycle decision would be to procure and assemble \acsp{PCB} as early as possible in the project, swapping the time spent assembling the breadboard model with time spent designing an additional \acs{PCB} iteration, if needed.

\item In contrast to ChipSat-1, the AI4Space \acs{PCB} needed no redesign, and no defects were identified after \ac{QM} production. This can be perhaps attributed to the general simplicity of the \acs{PCB}, as all computations were made on the Raspberry Pi, a ready-to-use board. Developers are therefore encouraged to use such boards or reuse reference designs to increase confidence in their design.

\item  The Raspbian operating system, installed by default on the Raspberry Pi, requires a small amount of configuration before it can be safely used as flight software, namely:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item Make sure that a server version of Raspbian is used, without a \acs{GUI}
	\item Disable Wi-Fi and Bluetooth
	\item Use a service (e.g.\@ \texttt{systemd} or \texttt{supervisord}) to manage services
	\item Install \texttt{nohang} to terminate or restart processes that leak memory
	\item Disable \texttt{logrotate.d}, if the software relies on reading single log files
	\item If \acs{SD} card wear or speed is a concern, then a read-only filesystem, combined with \texttt{tmpfs} or \texttt{OverlayFS}, can be used.
\end{itemize}
Alternatively, a Yocto or Buildroot distribution can be used.


\item While RabbitMQ was a flexible databroker that allowed the effective use of Remote Process Calls and broadcast messages between microservices, it introduced a measurable performance overhead, including boot time, memory consumption, and latency.


\item To generate the on-board \acs{AI} models for thermal image processing, we used the Edge Impulse platform. Edge Impulse generated a binary version of the model for ARM32 systems. The sizes of these binaries were in the order of ~100 MB, making uploading close to impossible. Different binary diff methods were examined with no immediate success. Recommending to use a more standardised format for model weights in the future.

\item AI4Space heavily used Python's \emph{Robot} framework and test-specific Python scripts for automation. These scripts were used to automate:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-3ex]
	\item Execution of \acs{TC} schedules during normal development
	\item Functional testing
	\item Testing for unintended situations/inputs
	\item Repeated procedures (such as log rotation or cache clearing)
\end{itemize}
Prior to automation, we used the command line and a custom-build web interface to manually configure the payload and release the intended \acs{TC}.
The use of automation proved highly beneficial for this project, as it allowed repeated execution of fixed schedules. Especially for AI4Space, these schedules could last up to an hour, which would be impractical for a human to execute and monitor manually.

\item Similarly, the use of a lightweight model-based approach for interface definition greatly simplified the introduction of \acs{TMTC} as needed in the project. However, it is important to maintain a single source of truth, and ensure that any interface changes should affect the least possible number of files. 

The protocol itself did not rely on a ready-to-use implementation (e.g.\@ of the \acs{PUS} services), but rather on a simple, proprietary specification. In each message, the first byte was a ``service'' type, and the second a ``message'' type. Any remaining bytes were message-specific. This option was chosen based on the component reuse guidelines (\Cref{fig:m_reuse}), since we wanted to minimise the overhead added due to extra headers or unused parameters. As the required functionality of AI4Space was simple and mainly payload-specific, only little effort was spent on the implementation of this protocol, rendering it generally successful.

\item For the remaining documentation, AI4Space relied on text-based documentation, based on a combination of Microsoft Office tools (mainly for project management documentation), and a Markdown-centred MkDocs platform (mainly for technical documentation). While this lightweight documentation generally followed the principles described in \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_datamanagement}, it was not beneficial to fragment it into two different formats/platforms. It was not immediately obvious where a piece of information should be, and references between the two could not easily be followed.

\item A measurable portion of development time for AI4Space was spent on necessary \emph{Auxiliary Activities} as defined in \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_satscrum}. This involved procurement activities, liaising with the host spacecraft provider, implementing support tools, and writing documentation.
For documentation, the team produced artifacts such as non-conformance reports (\Cref{sec:CASESTUDIES_ai4space_flow}). While these were not required, they helped in increasing the understanding of the project's current state and served as a log of engineering decisions and justifications. This was especially important as AI4Space participants were not always fully dedicated to the project, in terms of time allocation. Maintaining these artifacts based on spreadsheets in a semi-formal way allowed us to spend only a small percentage of time on documentation in each iteration.

\item The use of Python as a programming language was a double edged sword, mainly because of low-level details regarding the operation of the language. On the one hand, Python provided significant flexibility in terms of reconfigurability and reprogramming. The purported simplicity of the language reduced the learning curve for software developers in the project, while an exception handling hierarchy guaranteed that errors were recoverable. On the other hand, Python's lack of native support for parallelism made it more difficult to schedule concurrent tasks. Although solutions such as \texttt{asyncio} attempt to mitigate this issue, asynchronous Python was not yet fully supported by all used external libraries at the time of development. At the same time, the combination of Python with the microservice-based RabbitMQ architecture introduced delays in message processing, reducing the maximum throughput of the system, and increasing latency. While these issues were acceptable for a payload-level system, more caution has to be taken when developing an independent spacecraft, where requirements for real-time operations are more commonplace.

\item During operations, it was crucial to maintain a second unit, identical to the flight one, in order to test \acs{TC} or software patches before upload. The identical \acl{QM} proved useful for this task. A copy of the final binary flight image was stored and maintained as a common snapshot between space and ground.

\item During operations, as mentioned above, it was necessary to upload a Python software patch against the flight software. Due to constraints related to the available upload data volume, the size of the patch was limited to a few hundred bytes. The size constraints were met by aggressively minimising the Python source code.

Developers should be aware that during the early operations phase of a payload or satellite mission, engineering reasons might prevent the full data rates from being available for uplink and downlink. They should also expect a higher packet loss or bit error rate than usual. As such, when reprogramming is envisioned, robust procedures should be in place, which should include error detection and the ability to switch to the default software image. Simple compression or diffing techniques should also be used.

In the case of AI4Space, the use of an interpreted programming language and separated source files meant that it was easy to patch the affected file individually. However, this might not be the case when using a compiled programming language. While techniques such as binary patching or separating the software into shared libraries can alleviate this shortcoming, they have not yet been widely used in small-scale embedded development.

\item When downlinking images, two images arrived corrupted, due to a high bit error rate. The use of non-error resilient compression formats, such as JPEG or PNG, risks complete data loss when some bytes arrive corrupted. Instead, developers are encouraged to use error-resilient formats, such as JPEG-2000, which can potentially also improve the compression ratio, at the cost of restricted compatibility or performance \autocite{marcellinOverviewJPEG20002000}.

\end{itemize}

		2   & \textbf{PCB Components + Assembly}       & Flight Model (\#4)                            & 2600 \\

\subsection{Evaluation}
\label{sec:CASESTUDIES_ai4space_evaluation}

This counter-factual evaluation of AI4Space follows the same structure as the one of ChipSat-1 (\Cref{sec:CASESTUDIES_chipsat_evaluation}).

\begin{table*}[h]
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.8}
%\renewcommand\tabularxcolumn[1]{m{#1}}
\begin{adjustbox}{width=1.1\linewidth,center}
\begin{tabularx}{1.5\textwidth}{@{}l>{\raggedright}p{3cm}c>{\raggedright}p{2.5cm}>{\raggedright}p{3cm}XX@{}}
\toprule
 & Treatment & ? & Source & External factors & Effects & Revised theory \\ \midrule
1 & \textbf{Bypassing structural/thermal analysis} & \cfyes & \cfactualdata\@ \& \cfliterature & \acs{DUT} complexity affects chance of non-conformance. Manufacturing quality. & The decision to perform no analysis led to a reduction in development time and no additional testing (in this case, due to system simplicity). & Consistent reduction in development time. Test campaigns may increase cost. Increase in risk (due to undetected errors). \\
2 & \textbf{In-house manufacturing} & \cfno & \cfactualdata & \acs{DUT} complexity affects chance of non-conformance. Time, cost \& risk delta depend on external manufacturer. & Fixed processes, leading to almost identical results, can predictably increase/decrease development time and cost. Risk, however, depends on manufacturing capabilities, communication, and added delays. & Actual development time increases due to the added internal effort (blocking time is not modelled in this cumulative model). Cost decreases (no external manufacturing costs). Risk is project-dependent due to confounding factors. \\
3 & \textbf{Multi-step development} & \cfyes & \cfestimateddata\@ \& \cfliterature & System complexity. Team expertise. & A high initial design time would prevail over the actual fast schedule of the mission, influenced by its simplicity. Multiple iterations lead to a cost increase. Risk depends on the quality of the design/iterations. & \cfnorevisions \\
4 & \textbf{Short iterations} & \cfno & None available. &  &  & \\
5 & \textbf{Iterating over partitions} & \cfyes & \cfactualdata & System complexity. Team expertise. & The use of partitions led to a delay in manufacturing and testing a complete system. In turn, this led to later data generation for the system. The use of partitions did not generate significant \& meaningful data to be used for later. & Development time increased due to unnecessary partitioning. Cost remained similar (no more components purchased). Risk unchanged (no significant information affected system design). \\
6 & \textbf{Robust software framework} & \cfyes & \cfactualdata & System complexity. Component compatibility. Team expertise. & System specifications and team expertise allowed for an implementation of a more ``robust'' software framework. We identified acceptable non-conformances related to the software technologies used (mainly latency, unpredictability, low throughput). Other frameworks or architectures are not shown to be prone to these errors. No indications for change in development time/cost. & \cfnorevisions \\
7 & \textbf{Robotic Process Automation} & \cfyes & \cfestimateddata & Degree of ``physicality''. Testing complexity. Team expertise. Available facilities. & Repeated testing identified regressions after software/hardware changes. Added effort for process automation counterbalanced by hypothetical repeated effort for manual testing. No hardware equipment needed for \acs{RPA}, leading to no cost change.  & \cfnorevisions \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{adjustbox}
\caption{Counter-factual evaluation for AI4Space (analytical results)}
\label{tab:c_ai4space_counterfactuals_text}
\end{table*}


\begin{table*}[h]
	\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{lXcS[table-format=5.0,retain-explicit-plus=true]S[retain-explicit-plus=true]l}
	\toprule
\# & Treatment & \multicolumn{1}{l}{Applied?} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{Effect on} \\ \cmidrule(lr){4-6}
 &  & Y/N & \multicolumn{1}{c}{Cost (EUR)} &  \multicolumn{1}{c}{Time} & \multicolumn{1}{c}{Risk} \\ \midrule
1 & Bypassing structural/thermal analysis & \textcolor{nice}{Y} & 0  & \qty{-180}{hours} & Higher \\
2 & Soldering \acsp{PCB} in-house & \textcolor{bad}{N} & -1300 & \qty{+24}{hours} & No change  \\
3 & Multi-step development & \textcolor{nice}{Y} & +1300 & \qty{-265}{hours} & No change  \\
%4 & Short iterations & \textcolor{bad}{N} &  & \qty{}{hours} &   \\
5 & Iterating over partitions & \textcolor{nice}{Y} & 0 & \qty{+27}{hours} & No change \\
6 & Robust software framework & \textcolor{nice}{Y} & 0 & \qty{0}{hours} & Lower \\
7 & \acl{RPA} & \textcolor{nice}{Y} & 0 & \qty{0}{hours} & Lower \\

\end{tabularx}
\caption{Counter-factual evaluation for AI4Space (quantitative results)}
\label{tab:c_ai4space_counterfactuals}
\end{table*}

\begin{figure}
	\includegraphics{analyses/cf_ai4space_trade}
	\caption{AI4Space \acs{SCS} methodology treatment time vs.\@ risk trade-off analysis}
	\label{fig:c_tradeoff_ai4space}
\end{figure}

The results of the counter-factual evaluation in \Cref{tab:c_ai4space_counterfactuals} are explained as follows:
\begin{enumerate}
	\item \textbf{Bypassing structural/thermal analysis}:
	Similar to ChipSat-1 (\Cref{sec:CASESTUDIES_chipsat_evaluation}). Even though AI4Space was a simple \acs{PCB}, the integration of additional mounted components (cameras, Raspberry Pi) still implies that a potential analysis could have reduced risk. No vibration or \acs{TVAC} tests were performed for AI4Space, as these were covered by the host.
	\item \textbf{External manufacturing}:
	For AI4Space, we performed the soldering for the \acl{QM} in-house, while the soldering for the \acl{FM} was performed externally.\footnote{No other parts had to be manufactured.} The in-house soldering process lasted for \emph{3 days}, which I will consider as the added duration for the hypothetical \acs{FM} soldering. Due to the simplicity of the design, I will not consider added risk due to interfacing/delays.
	\item \textbf{Iterative multi-step development}:
	Similar to ChipSat-1, AI4Space also followed a lean, iterative approach for its lifecycle development process, with 4 iterations that considered most of the system. However, no significant design errors were detected in relation to the hardware design\footnote{With the exception of electronic component shortages.}, no significant changes were made between iterations, and there were no significant deviations in external requirements that would require a hardware modification.

	Therefore, a single-step ``\acl{BDUF}'' approach would affect the project as follows:
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-2ex]
		\item A ``design'' step is added. From \Cref{tab:l_cubesat_time_drivers}, considering the ``best'' (fastest) case for one subsystem of a CubeSat project, the design step would last \qty{40}{days}.
		\item Iterations \#2 and \#3 would not have been performed. Assuming \qty{27}{person hours} of prototyping and testing time per iteration (\Cref{tab:c_ai4space_times}), this would result in a reduction of \qty{55}{hours} of the overall project time.
		\item The component purchase cost budget item would not be accounted.
		\item Risk would not change.
	\end{enumerate}

	
	\item \textbf{Iterating over partitions}:
	2 out of 4 iterations for AI4Space were performed on different partitions and representations of the system, instead of on the entire system as a Qualification/Flight Model.

	Since the first AI4Space iterations did not reveal non-conformances that would require a hardware rework, \emph{working on the qualification model during the early iterations would not have a significant adverse effect}. More specifically:
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-2ex]
		\item There would be no need to develop a breadboard model, reducing the development time for iteration \#2 by \(\frac{1}{4}\) of the iteration/prototyping duration.
		\item Due to the correctness of the \acs{QM}/\acs{FM} produced, there would be no need to perform an additional procurement run for iteration \#4. However, the total procurement cost would remain the same, since component prices are guided by a minimum quantity to maintain adequate spare stock.
		\item No changes in risk observed.
	\end{enumerate}

	This case represents an example were a different choice would generally benefit the project. The difference in results between ChipSat-1 and AI4Space can be explained by the higher simplicity of AI4Space's design, which makes a ``one-off'' approach less risky in the general case.

	\item \textbf{Robust software framework}:
	AI4Space used Python as the main programming language within a Linux-based platform. Even though Python is not safety-oriented, its combination with Linux and the easy reprogramming capabilities were a key factor for the success of the mission. As such, I will consider that a robust software framework \emph{was indeed used}, and successfully decreased risk. Since the selected hardware natively supports all possible language choices, I will not consider any added effort or cost for implementing such a framework.

	\item \textbf{Use of \acl{RPA}}:
	If manual testing procedures had been used instead of \acs{RPA}, I will not consider a change in development time, due to the added effort of automation countering the added effort for manual test processes. However, I will consider that risk would increase, due to lower repeatability.

	\item Note on \textbf{short, agile iterations}: Since the average iteration length for AI4Space was 3 months, and a large gap was generally observed during the project to focus on other projects, it is more difficult to evaluate the effect of iteration duration on AI4Space. As such, I will not consider this treatment.
\end{enumerate}

\FloatBarrier
\newpage
\section{POQUITO}
%Seminal papers: Conference \autocite{franzesePOQUITOFirstSatellite2025} Journal \autocite{franzeseSystemDesignPOQUITO2025}

POQUITO (``POcketQUbe for In-orbit Technology Operations'') was a 1p\footnote{That is, \qtyproduct{5 x 5 x 5}{\centi\meter}.} PocketQube developed in the University of Luxembourg, intended to serve as a \acs{LEO} technology demonstration mission. Based on a PocketQube bus that reuses the architecture of Libre Space Foundation's open-source \emph{QUBIK} platform \autocite{daradimosQUBIK1pPocketQube2024}, POQUITO was developed over the course of roughly one year, starting in summer 2023. Its payloads included a ChipSat placed on the top part to demonstrate \acs{VLC} (similar to the one of \Cref{sec:CASESTUDIES_chipsat1}), and an in-house miniaturised \acf{ADCS} system. I participated as a Software and Systems Engineer in the POQUITO project.

\begin{figure}[h]
	\resizebox{\textwidth}{!}{%
        \includegraphics[height=1cm]{media/pictures/P1000717_resized.jpg}%
        \hspace*{0.1cm}%
        \includegraphics[height=1cm]{media/pictures/P1000724_resized.jpg}%
    }
	\caption{Views of the POQUITO \acl{FM}}%
	\label{fig:c_poquito_fm}
\end{figure}

\subsection{System Description}

\begin{marginfigure}
	\includegraphics{pictures/chipsat_on_poquito.jpg}
	\caption{ChipSat v.2 on the top side of POQUITO}
\end{marginfigure}
POQUITO was developed in order to demonstrate the operation of a ChipSat in orbit. A PocketQube form factor was selected due to the availability of PocketQube deployers and accessible launch opportunities, with POQUITO decided to act as a host for a ChipSat. Based on strategic goals, POQUITO was designed to include two main payloads:
\begin{enumerate}
	\item An electrically independent \textbf{ChipSat} placed on the top side of the PocketQube, based on the previous ChipSat-1 design (\Cref{sec:CASESTUDIES_chipsat1}). This ChipSat was intended to demonstrate \acf{VLC} in free-space for, by using an \acs{LED} to transmit data to the rest of the PocketQube bus, similar to the original ChipSat-1 mission. The small \textbf{intra-satellite} communication range was intended to serve as a stepping stone for independent intra or inter-satellite communication missions \autocite{griffithsHighsensitivityIntersatelliteOptical2021,amanorLinkPerformanceImprovement2017}, for example linked with fractionated spacecraft, or ``parent spacecraft''-based missions \autocite{heinAttoSatsChipSatsOther2019}.

	The ChipSat placed on top of POQUITO, referred to as the second version of the university's ChipSat design, was electrically independent, in the sense that it was responsible for power generation, and communication was done wirelessly, through a \ac{VLC} link.
	This version of the ChipSat included no \acs{RF} link for Earth communication.
	With a mechanical interface based on four threads, the ChipSat shared only a common electrical ground with the rest of the PocketQube.

	The design of the ChipSat was largely based on ChipSat-1, reusing miniaturised solar panels linked to a power-harvesting \acs{MPPT}, and managed by a low-resource microprocessor.

	\item A novel \textbf{\acs{ADCS}} system was used to perform active detumbling of the satellite, on account of a need for technology demonstration and an increase in the reliability of the communications link. The development of an \acs{ADCS} system was motivated by the limited number of alternatives for miniaturised PocketQube \acs{ADCS} which are freely available \autocite{albaorbitalBeginnersGuideOpen2025}.
	
	POQUITO's \acs{ADCS} was based on a 9-axis \acf{IMU} and four sun sensors (placed on each side) used for attitude determination. The actuators used were three magnetorquers (one for each axis) in the form of current loops embedded in 4 \acs{PCB} layers each \autocite{anforowiczMagnetorquerCalc2026}. The \acs{ADCS} behaviour was modelled and simulated through a Model-In-The-Loop simulation in MATLAB, with a B-dot controller used for detumbling.
\end{enumerate}

Owing to the availability of open-source PocketQube buses and the small number of engineers on our team, we decided to \textbf{reuse} an free and open-source design to base POQUITO on. The QUBIK platform \autocite{daradimosQUBIK1pPocketQube2024} was chosen, owing to its simplicity, flight heritage, and available documentation. More specifically, POQUITO used the \emph{structure} (including baseplate and antenna deployment mechanism), \emph{communications board}\footnote{More specifically, the \emph{PQ9ISH} communications module.} and \emph{battery management power system} (\acs{EPS}) as-is. The \emph{side solar panels} were modified to accommodate the \acs{ADCS} magnetorquers and light sensors. The \emph{\acs{ADCS} board}, \emph{Z-axis magnetorquer board} and \emph{top-side ChipSat payload} were designed in-house, based on board layouts and schematics from QUBIK. A block diagram of all components is shown in \Cref{fig:c_poquito_block}.

\begin{figure}[h]
	\includegraphics{media/graphics/Block_POQUITO}
	\caption{Internal block diagram of the POQUITO system}
	\label{fig:c_poquito_block}
\end{figure}
\acempty{IMU}
\acempty{SD}
\acempty{POWERAMP}
\acempty{MPPT}
\acempty{IC}
\acempty{BMPS}
%\acuse{IMU,SD,POWERAMP,MPPT,IC,BMPS}

One notable point regarding the selected design is the \emph{lack of a dedicated \acl{OBC}}. Instead, following QUBIK's design, the responsibilities of data handling and control were delegated to the communications board. This is possible due to the low complexity and \emph{technical size} of the PocketQube, which removes the need to separate concerns or have a modular architecture.

In terms of additional components, the \acs{ADCS} contained a photodiode, functioning as a receiver for the ChipSat payload\sidenote[][-2cm]{Even though the \acs{ADCS} should generally not be responsible for payload data handling, in this case this was deemed as the most suitable option against redesigning the communications board as well, or introducing an extra \acs{PCB}.}. The protocol used for communication between the \acs{ADCS} and \acs{COMMS} was \acs{CAN}\footnote{While \acs{CAN} is not used in the original QUBIK, there are provisions for a driverless \acs{CAN} interface between two subsystems in the original QUBIK schematics.} through a stacking PQ9 interface \autocite{PQ91}.

Power storage was done via a camera battery mounted on the \acs{EPS} board. The antenna used QUBIK's original burn-wire mechanism, placed on the baseplate, which also included two electrical cut-off switches (``kill switches'') to detect deployment. The baseplate also included an outwards-facing 16-pin umbilical connector, intended for debugging and charging after integration, and internally harnessed to all subsystems.\footnote{Even though QUBIK originally had one programmable subsystem, the baseplate and harness designs predicted the need for a second programmable subsystem, which we made use of.} To reduce internal cabling, the solar panels included pads compatible with spring loaded ``pogo pin'' connectors.

Structurally, the subsystem stack was kept in place through four screws and two machined plates (top and bottom), each of which had appropriately sized slots to accommodate the solar panels.

		SYS.071 & The system shall be capable of performing a detumbling from a tip-off rate of 20 deg/s about any axes.\\
		SYS.080 & The system shall integrate an on-board data management and handling system.\\
		SYS.090 & The spacecraft modules shall survive to a temperature range of [-20; 45]° C. \\
		SYS.100 & The system shall implement at least the following modes: \begin{itemize*}\item Deployment/Boot \item Nominal\item Communication\item Power Save\end{itemize*}\\
		SYS.110 & The spacecraft shall be compatible with the designated deployer.\\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabularx}
\end{table*}

\FloatBarrier
\subsection{Development Flow}

In terms of lifecycle process, POQUITO followed an iterative approach. Three iterations of the entire system were planned (termed v0, v1 and v2). v0 was intended as a system based on QUBIK but without the POQUITO-specific additions (namely the \acs{ADCS}, ChipSat and custom panels), while the two remaining versions involved iterations of the entire system. In that sense, v0 was a \emph{partition} of a large part of the system, while v1 and v2 were iterations on the entire system. Compared to previous mission, this ``partition-light'' approach was chosen due to the reuse of QUBIK components, and our high confidence in the entire system's operation and correctness.

\begin{figure}[h]
	\centering
	\begin{subfigure}[t]{0.23\textwidth}
		\centering
		\includegraphics[height=3cm]{pictures/MTQ.jpg}
		\caption{v1}
	\end{subfigure}%
	~%
	\begin{subfigure}[t]{0.23\textwidth}
		\centering
		\includegraphics[height=3cm]{pictures/W_MTQ_1.jpg}
		\caption{v2}
	\end{subfigure}%
	~%
	\begin{subfigure}[t]{0.23\textwidth}
		\centering
		\includegraphics[height=3cm]{pictures/W_MTQ_F.jpg}
		\caption{v3}
	\end{subfigure}%
	~%
	\begin{subfigure}[t]{0.23\textwidth}
		\centering
		\includegraphics[height=3cm]{pictures/W_MTQ_2_F.jpg}
		\caption{v4}
	\end{subfigure}%
	\caption{Example of iterative hardware development through the production of different magnetorquer board versions}
\end{figure}

The development of subsystems roughly followed that of the system, with 2 versions of the ChipSat payload being produced, 2 versions of the \acs{ADCS} board, 4 versions of the magnetorquer board, and 3 versions of the side solar panels. Software development followed the development of subsystems, which were initially represented by development boards before the procurement of \acsp{PCB}. Software development sometimes took place remotely, due to the location of various members of the team. Structural elements were replaced by 3D-printed versions while procurement of machined parts was pending.
After each iteration, the entire system was assembled, integrated and functionally tested. Electronic component assembly was done through a combination of external and in-house reflow soldering, depending on the board time.

A simple \acs{CAD} model of the system was built and used for visual representation and renders. Nevertheless, we performed fit checks visually and by estimating using available 3D-printed parts. No structural or thermal analysis was performed due to the high confidence in a flight-proven design. However, we performed an \acs{EMG} analysis to inform and verify the dimensions of the planned antenna elements\footnote{Due to their dimensions being different from the original QUBIK design.}. The team also performed mission and re-entry analysis, to verify compliance to the 5-year space debris rule \autocite{FCCAdoptsNew2022}.

\begin{marginfigure}
	\includegraphics{graphics/POQUITO_Stack_Render}
	\caption{Exploded view of the POQUITO internal stack}
	\label{fig:c_poquito_stack}
\end{marginfigure}

In terms of project management, we used Atlassian Jira for task tracking, Inventree for inventory management, and GitLab for source code and schematic version control. The team produced several textual documents, which were part of the documentation required by the Luxembourg Space Agency. % and were generally not used internally
We furthermore used Excel spreadsheets to track budgets (cost, power, mass, and link), procurement activities, documentation status, and project lifecycle (in the form of a Gantt chart).

Two \textbf{environmental testing campaigns} took place in an external facilities: a qualification campaign for the \emph{v1} iteration, and an acceptance campaign for the \emph{v2} iteration. Both campaigns included vibration and \acs{TVAC} tests, while the second campaign additionally included \acs{EMC} tests, based on the launch provider requirements. Both campaigns ended successfully, with functional tests showing nominal operation after testing.

\begin{marginfigure}
	\includegraphics{pictures/POQUITO_shaker.jpg}
	\caption{POQUITO on a vibration test shaker}
\end{marginfigure}

During POQUITO's development, the team also worked on the ground segment, with the goal of preparing the appropriate GNURadio flowgraphs and transmission hardware. POQUITO planned to use the University of Luxembourg's ground station, which includes a rotator with a high-gain \acs{UHF} Yagi antenna. POQUITO was designed to operate on radio-amateur frequencies, and be compatible with the SatNOGS network.

\subsection{Lessons Learned}
\label{sec:CASESTUDIES_poquito_lessons}

\begin{table*}[h]
	\caption[POQUITO cost budget]{POQUITO cost budget (not including labour, insurance and ground station costs)}
	\label{tab:c_poquito_costs}
	\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lXlS@{}}
		\toprule
		No. & \textbf{Cost Driver}                     & Used In                                       & {Value (EUR)} \\ \midrule

Compared to the previous mission, POQUITO's status as an independent satellite required significantly higher effort, divided amongst a larger team. Despite the use and adaptation of a \acs{COTS} design, the team still experienced challenges related to technical and programmatic aspects. This was further exacerbated by the logistical environment of the university which reduced the availability of key personnel for engineering tasks and roles.

A list of lessons learned that were gathered by the entirety of the team after the end of the project is shown below:
\begin{itemize}
	\item The significant number of \textbf{procurement runs} required increased delays. Lead times were affected both by the university's logistical process\footnote{which is standard across many public institutions}, and the base lead times for custom manufactured components (such as assembled \acsp{PCB} and structural elements). Even though we anticipated 4-month long iterations to counter approximately the standard 1--2-month long procurement ``latency'', this added significant ``lag'' to the Agile iteration lifecycle.
	
	The slowest item in POQUITO's critical path was identified to be the procurement of assembled \acsp{PCB}, which lasted \qtyrange{30}{50}{days}. During the mission, the team started preparing the internal tooling for reflow soldering, and managed to solder some subsystems in-house\footnote{Namely, the solar panels and \acs{MTQ} boards.}, observing a generally smoother process compared to external procurement.
	\item The allocation of \textbf{testing time} to the project was insufficient, as shown in \Cref{tab:c_poquito_times}. Combined with the above point, this meant that some specific functions (e.g.\@ telecommands, antenna deployment) could not be stress tested. Projects are encouraged to allocate approximately 50\% of the development lifecycle to testing, allowing enough margin for additional unexpected development efforts.
	\item While software tooling was implemented to assist development (for task tracking, inventory management, or documentation), it was often not used consistently, leading to a disagreement between the actual and documented status of the project. This was brought about in part because of the external commitments of team members. However, assigning an accountable person for these items seemed to guarantee good upkeep, with members noting a 45-page Mission Control Document as a particularly useful artifact that provided up-to-date information on the system design.
	\item Critical members of the team were unable to work on-location, instead having to contribute remotely, and often with limited time. The combination of the above points shows the need to have at least few dedicated engineers working on the project as a primary occupation, even if the system itself requires little development effort (due to e.g.\@ reuse).
	\item The iterative design approach seemed to perform successfully, as we did not discover any major non-conformances in the developed hardware, and managed to successfully qualify and acceptance-test the system. This was perhaps supported both by the heavy reuse of previous designs (both external and in-house), and the larger experience of the team, based on the two previous missions, and demonstrating a lesser need for a ``\acl{BDUF}''-based approach. The first iteration (``\texttt{v0}''), consisting of a partition of QUBIK parts only, provided experience to the team and allowed them to familiarise themselves with the satellite integration process and connections.
	\item Roughly 30\% of the project time was spent on project management activities, which were mainly devoted to regulatory aspects\footnote{Related to national launch requirements, communication authorisations, insurance, and re-entry analysis.}. These preparations also involved orbital analysis, in order to guarantee a safe re-entry after at most 3 years, and establish that the satellite can be tracked (\Cref{sec:LITERATURE_debris}).
	
	Regulatory requirements can vary by country, but will be significantly easier when support can be afforded by an experienced party. Especially for frequency licensing, the documentation available freely is scarce, and the licensing process is heavily bureaucratic, requiring the coordination between various national and international entities (mainly \acs{ITU}, \acs{IARU}, local radio-amateur societies, and local regulation authorities). Complying with space debris or launch regulations may require a similar process.

	CubeSat literature strongly encourages teams to not consider legal and regulatory aspects as an afterthought, and devote enough time early in the project to handle them \autocite{kinnairdSelectionLessonsLearned2022}. As \ac{LEO} becomes more crowded, the ``failure rate'' of this process starts increasing, as governments are becoming more unwilling to launch objects in space. As a result, future operators should consider regulatory compliance at system level, during early design: For example, acquiring a radio-amateur license will require a radio-amateur payload, while space debris regulation might limit the acceptable launch altitudes.
\end{itemize}

In terms of technical observations:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-1ex]
	\item The original design used two Li-Ion camera batteries. These batteries are interfaced using exposed metal terminals which are suited to a specialised mount, but not to soldering, due to safety and connection reliability concerns. It is therefore recommended to use pre-wired batteries, a bespoke battery holder, or a spot welding-based solution.
	\item Due to the addition of the \acs{ADCS} against the base QUBIK design, POQUITO's \textbf{cable harness} was more complex. This increased difficulty and risk during assembly, and required creating high-quality crimped connections for use with custom, POQUITO-specific cables. On the other hand, the use of spring-loaded pogo pins for the solar panels proved highly reliable and allowed low-effort integration. Teams should therefore invest on reducing the technical size of internal harnesses by using fixed components, to the degree possible.\footnote{This can be supported, for example, by an \acs{RF} or optical intra-satellite link.}
	\item One of the non-conformances detected was the incorrect procurement of \qty{1.6}{\milli\meter}-thick \acsp{PCB} for the solar panels, instead of the required \qty{0.8}{\milli\meter}. This error was detected and resolved before the ``v1'' iteration. However, thanks to access to a university engineering workshop, we were able to enlarge the panel slots in order to perform early fit-checks, without requiring a second procurement run. Teams should try to establish in-house electrical and mechanical rework capabilities to prevent long waiting periods in the case of defects.
	\item In the ``v1'' iteration, it was discovered that there was little space on the PocketQube for the \emph{accelerometers required for vibration testing}. This was resolved by altering the layout for ``v2''.
	\item During ``v0'', we discovered fit and clearance issues between the new solar panels and the subsystem stack. While these were resolved within an iteration, it would have been easily possible to avoid them through simple \acs{CAD} modelling, or by prototyping early versions of the \acs{PCB} (e.g.\@ through 3D printing).
	\item A minor missing element from POQUITO that was later deemed useful were low-power \emph{status-indication \acsp{LED}}, that could be used to increase observability during ground testing by showing the power status of the satellite and its subsystems.\footnote{Compare, for example, with the corresponding Lesson Learned from ChipSat-1 (\Cref{sec:CASESTUDIES_chipsat_lessons}).}
	\item As part of the procurement of structural components, the team also purchased a ``deployer stub'', used to represent the mechanical interface of the PocketQube's deployer during launch.\footnote{We used a part designed by the Libre Space Foundation in FreeCAD for this purpose.} This turned out to be significantly helpful as a fit check device, storage stand, and to keep the PocketQube turned off (thanks to the deployment switches being pressed).
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Evaluation}
\label{sec:CASESTUDIES_poquito_evaluation}

The counter-factual evaluation of POQUITO follows the same structure as the ones of ChipSat-1 and AI4Space (\Cref{sec:CASESTUDIES_chipsat1_evaluation,sec:CASESTUDIES_ai4space_evaluation}).

The results of the counter-factual evaluation in \Cref{tab:c_poquito_counterfactuals} are explained as follows:
\begin{enumerate}
	\item \textbf{Bypassing structural/thermal analysis}:
	Similar to ChipSat-1 and AI4Space (\Cref{sec:CASESTUDIES_chipsat_evaluation,sec:CASESTUDIES_ai4space_evaluation}). The analysis time might be greater compared to the other projects, due to more complicated meshing/contact conditions, however here I consider a best case, based on the examples of \Cref{tab:l_cubesat_time_drivers}. Structural and thermal testing showed a nominal operation of the satellite, and the decision to avoid analysis was also based on the previous heritage of the QUBIK design.
	\item \textbf{External manufacturing}:
	Most \acsp{PCB} were soldered externally. Due to the quantity of \acsp{PCB} with different characteristics\footnote{Mainly layer count.} in POQUITO, \acs{PCB} procurement activities were often a blocking point in the lifecycle of the project. This also includes structural elements (PocketQube frame plates and antenna mounting points). For this point, I consider an assembly time of 2 days per one of 5 \acsp{PCB}, and a reduction to overall project risk due to the clearing of the critical path. Note that in-house reflow soldering of the side solar panel \acsp{PCB} showed successful results during qualification, within the confines of the reduced workmanship standards required for a low-duration \acs{LEO} mission.

	\item \textbf{Iterative multi-step development}:
	
	To compare against a single-step, ``\acl{BDUF}'' approach, I will consider the ``best-case'' 40-person-day metric (\Cref{tab:l_cubesat_time_drivers}), reduced by \( \frac{2}{3} \) of the integration \& verification time. In a single-step case, the development time of the project would be increased by \( 40\cdot 8 - \frac{2}{3} \cdot 108 = \qty{248}{hours} \). This approach would involve, for example, fit-checks using a software-based \acs{CAD} model, a detailed evaluation of assembly and testing requirements to avoid incompatibilities, formal documentation of the proposed design (perhaps compliant to \acs{ECSS} standards \autocite{ECSS-M-ST-10C,ECSS-E-ST-10C}), and significantly more time spent on design review during designated stage-gates. 

	I consider it highly likely that this approach would detect and prevent most or all non-conformances noted in \Cref{sec:CASESTUDIES_poquito_lessons}, thus leading to a successful development of the spacecraft. While both sequential and iterative development would lead to a successfully operating spacecraft, I consider that spending more time on \emph{early design} would lead to a potentially simpler design, e.g.\@ by simplifying POQUITO's harnessing, or by deciding to use a simpler communication protocol\footnote{E.g.\@ \acs{UART} instead of \acs{CAN}.}. Thus, I will consider that multi-step development increases risk. This is also influenced by POQUITO's more complex nature compared to previous missions, leading to a higher potential inertia against later design changes. Finally, I will also consider a significant increase in total manufacturing cost for a multi-step lifecycle process (based on the costs of individual \acsp{PCB} and elements).

	\item \textbf{Short iterations}:
	POQUITO's iterations are not considered short in terms of typical Agile expectations. This was influenced mainly by the long waiting times required. In POQUITO's case, increasing the number of iterations from 3 to e.g.\@ 5 (similar to ChipSat-1) would require a different environment, or the heavier reliance on internal prototyping and/or manufacturing (element \#2). This treatment therefore depends on treatment \#2.\footnote{This assertion does not threaten the validity of the analysis, as I will only consider the cases with 0 treatments, actual treatments, and all treatments applied.}
	
	Nevertheless, in the case of POQUITO, short iterations would increase the total cost (based on the cost of components and unassembled \acsp{PCB}) and development time by \( \frac{2}{3} \cdot 108 = \qty{72}{hours} \). However, shorter, timeboxed iterations would help in the earlier identification of issues, such as incompatible dimensions and bad fits. I will therefore consider a lower risk,  and an additional reduction of time required for machining by \qty{8}{hours}\footnote{Related to resolving the solar panel non-conformance identified}.

	%When comparing with previous projects, where short iterations were considered beneficial, this example further supports that, depending on the environment, external restrictions, and projects, the \acs{SCS} methodology still needs to be tailored to reach a theoretically optimised result in terms of one of its metrics.
	
	\item \textbf{Iterating over partitions}:
	The first iteration of POQUITO did not involve the entire intended system, but only a part of it, consisting of QUBIK's original subsystems.%\footnote{Note that, despite the fact that it was a partition, it was still theoretically flight-ready.}
	In this case, the use of a partition reduced the development costs by not necessitating procurement of payload boards early in the design. In the case of a full first iteration, the team would potentially lack an intuitive understanding of the interfacing requirements for the new subsystems (e.g.\@ position in stack, harnessing/cabling, power requirements), leading to the need for a 3\textsuperscript{rd} payload iteration, or more preliminary analysis. I will therefore consider a {\(\frac{1}{4}\) of the payload \acs{PCB} development time as saved by this treatment}.

	\item \textbf{Use of \acl{RPA}}:
	POQUITO relied on manual testing procedures, instead of \acs{RPA}. Similar to POQUITO, I will not consider a change in development time and cost because of automation. However, I will consider a risk reduction with \acs{RPA} thanks to the repeatability and larger potential generated samples based on automated tests.


	\item Note on using a \textbf{Robust software framework}:
	POQUITO's software mainly involved the reuse of QUBIK's original flight software, written in C, ChipSat-1's firmware, written in C++, and the porting of MATLAB-based \acs{ADCS} models based on available libraries. Due to this amount of reuse, I do not consider the analysis of alternative approaches (such as programming languages or frameworks) as singular and well-grounded.
\end{enumerate}

	\caption{POQUITO \acs{SCS} methodology treatment time vs.\@ risk trade-off analysis}
	\label{fig:c_tradeoff_poquito}
\end{figure}

\section{ChiPySat}

ChiPySat was a ChipSat project I participated in, as part of a research exchange with the Robotic Exploration Lab at Carnegie Mellon University. It involved developing an independent \emph{\acs{PCB}Sat} or \emph{ChipSat}-sized satellite to demonstrate a \textbf{lens-less camera concept} \autocite{liLenslessCameraUnraveling2024}, which can serve as a stepping stone to miniaturise imaging systems in pocket-sized spacecraft.

\begin{figure}
	\includegraphics{pictures/chipysat_keyboard.jpg}
	\caption{ChiPySat Engineering Model}
\end{figure}

Owing to the short (5-month) duration of my research visit, the intention of the project was to perform successful functional testing of a ChipSat capable of capturing, processing and transmitting lens-less images. I was the main engineer in the project, responsible for hardware and software development.

	\label{fig:c_diffusercam_setup}
\end{figure*}
