\subsection{Mission Description}

\begin{figure}[h]
	%\acuse{IMU,MPPT,UHF,USB,SD}%
	\includegraphics{media/graphics/Block_Chipysat}
	\caption{Internal block diagram of the ChiPySat system}
	\label{fig:c_chipysat_block}
\end{figure}
\acempty{IMU}%
\acempty{MPPT}%
\acempty{UHF}%
\acempty{USB}%
\acempty{SD}%

The main payload of the ChiPySat system was a \textbf{lens-less camera} based on the \textbf{DiffuserCam} concept \autocite{kuoDiffuserCamDiffuserBasedLensless2017}. The DiffuserCam setup involves placing a diffuser material at a specific distance in front of an image sensor (\Cref{fig:c_diffusercam_setup}). A point light source aimed towards the diffuser creates a material-based caustic pattern on the sensor, which varies based on the lateral position and depth of the source.\footnote{The dependence of the caustic pattern on depth means that it is possible to reconstruct 3D representations of captured scenes, as depth-dependent data are included on the captured image.} A real scene therefore projects a superposition of these caustic patterns onto the sensor. In the 2D case, this superposition is mathematically calculated as the convolution between the diffuser's \emph{Point Spread Function} (i.e.\@ its impulse response against a point source) and the actual scene. To reconstruct the original scene, a processor has to solve the inverse problem, by calculating a 2D deconvolution operation. Due to the presence of measurement noise and depth effects, the calculation of the deconvolution involves solving an optimisation problem \cite{kuoDiffuserCamDiffuserBasedLensless2017}.

In practice, the DiffuserCam concept shifts the responsibility of focusing a scene \textbf{from the lens to the processor}. On a miniaturised high-performance system, this displays the benefits of reduced mass, size and fragility, and allows implementing features such as \emph{infinite focusing} and \emph{3D reconstruction}.

\begin{marginfigure}
	\includegraphics{external/diffusercam_side_view.png}
	\caption[Side view of DiffuserCam]{Side view of DiffuserCam operation. Image taken from \url{https://waller-lab.github.io/DiffuserCam/quickstart.html}.}
\end{marginfigure}

The question posed by the ChiPySat project was therefore: \textbf{\emph{Is it possible to implement a lens-less camera with on-board processing on a low-footprint ChipSat system?}}

\newthought{The electrical design} of ChiPySat was based on the PyCubed-Mini\footnote{\textellipsis which is also the namesake of the ChiPySat project.} PocketQube platform \autocite{manchesterPyCubedMini,andradePROMETHEUS1SatelliteFramework2025}, which itself is based on the PyCubed CubeSat platform \autocite{hollidayPyCubedOpenSourceRadiationTested2019}. The main differentiator between PyCubed(-Mini) and other open-source satellite buses is the use of \textbf{Python} as an embedded programming language, through the use of CircuitPython or OpenMV\footnote{Both CircuitPython and OpenMV are based on MicroPython \cite{george2021micropython}.}.

Processing responsibilities for ChiPySat were taken over by two microcontrollers running MicroPython: one responsible for \acs{OBDH}, based on PyCubed-Mini software, and one responsible for image processing and camera interfacing, based on OpenMV. Both \acsp{MCU} were programmable over \acs{USB}, and were connected to an \acs{SD} card interface for non-volatile storage. 


The intention of the ChiPySat design was also to build upon Manchester's Sprite design \autocite{manchesterCentimeterScaleSpacecraftDesign2015}, by sacrificing its small size to host batteries and magnetorquer-based attitude control. The design concept selected to support these features was a \textbf{\acs{PCB} ``sandwich'' with the batteries in the middle}. The two boards were connected electrically using a plain board-to-board connector, and mechanically using four corner screws. Solar cells were placed on the outer sides of both \acsp{PCB}, in order to support power generation during tumbling.

For energy storage, we designed the system around \acs{COTS} pouch-cell batteries. To combat low temperatures due to a large radiative area, we embedded heaters as \acs{PCB}. A similar trace-based design was envisioned for 3-axis magnetorquers, intended to manage slow detumbling.

In terms of communications, an off-the-shelf \acs{LORA} module was used, based on PyCubed-Mini's design, and with an interface compatible with the TinyGS ground station network \autocite{kheraPyCubedMiniLowCostOpenSource}. The use of a \acs{COTS} \acs{RF} module simplified the development effort, as high-speed \acs{PCB} layouts often introduce uncertainty in the design.


\begin{figure}[h]
	\includegraphics{pictures/chipysat_v1.jpg}
	\includegraphics{pictures/chipysat_v2.jpg}
	\caption{Renders of the first (top) and second (bottom) ChiPySat iterations}
	\label{fig:c-chipysat_renders}
\end{figure}

\newthought{The development} of ChiPySat also followed an iterative approach, albeit with only two iterations performed due to time constraints. Since the project was based on electronic/hardware elements, the changes between iterations mainly involved schematic changes as follows:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item Magnetorquers were added to the design, as they were not present in the first iteration.
	\item Instead of 2 microcontrollers (one for \acs{CDH} and an ``application'' processor for image processing), the second iterator used a single dual-core microcontroller.
	\item The \acs{USB} power distribution system was simplified by using two \acs{USB} connectors (for power and data).
	\item An external non-volatile memory was added, after experimentation showed that internal system memory could be insufficient for image processing.
\end{itemize}

The first iteration lasted approximately 4 months, including an initial familarisation with the PyCubed-Mini platform, hardware design, software development\footnote{Done on development boards when hardware was not available.}, integration and testing. After completing functional testing and coding for the first iteration, I started working on the second, however, due to a limited available time, the relevant \acsp{PCB} were not manufactured and tested.

\begin{figure}
	\centering
	\subfloat[]{\includegraphics[height=3cm]{pictures/chipysat_experiment.jpg}}%
	\hspace{2cm}%
	\subfloat[]{\includegraphics[height=3cm]{pictures/chipysat_psf.jpg}}%
	\\%
	\subfloat[]{\includegraphics[height=3cm]{pictures/chipysat_input.jpg}}%
	\hspace{3cm}%
	\subfloat[]{\includegraphics[height=3cm]{pictures/chipysat_result.png}}%
	\caption[ChiPySat pipeline example]{ChiPySat pipeline example. (a) Experimental setup. (b) Diffuser Point Spread Function. (c) Sensor output. (d) Reconstructed image (rotated 90°).}
	\label{fig:c_chipysat_results}
\end{figure}

The assembly (soldering) of the boards was performed through an in-house reflow process. Soldering required some initial familiarisation with the equipment, as well as the calibration of the reflow oven against the solder paste that was used. Due to the simplicity of the boards, pick-and-place was done by hand. The combination of common with low-temperature melting point solder paste was used in order to assemble both sides of the \acsp{PCB}. These in-house soldered boards successfully passed initial functional testing. 

\newthought{The results} of an off-board processing experiment are shown in \Cref{fig:c_chipysat_results}, and demonstrate the successful application of the DiffuserCam process in terms of hardware and software. In this experiment, the image capture was performed entirely by the ChiPySat lens-less camera and processor, with the data transferred to a desktop computer via \acs{USB}. However, the processing (deconvolution) of the image took place in the computer, and not the satellite.

For the second iteration, I attempted to move the processing algorithm within the OpenMV application microcontroller.\footnote{The optimisation process for deconvolution revolves around the iterative application of 2D \acs{FFT} and simple matrix operations \cite{kuoDiffuserCamDiffuserBasedLensless2017}.} OpenMV is built around the MicroPython platform and uses Python as the main top-level programming interface. However, Python turned out to be ill-suited for resource-constrained applications. This resulted from the combination of 1.\@ a \textbf{large image size}\footnote{\(320\times 240 = \qty{76.8}{\kilo\byte} \), compare to \qty{1}{\mega\byte} available \acs{MCU} \acs{RAM}.}; 2.\@ the requirement from the optimiser to have multiple images in memory at the same time\footnote{Including, for example, the input image, working copy, \acs{PSF}, function operands, and temporaries.}; and 3.\@  the dynamically allocated nature of Python, leading to inconsistent freeing and significant memory fragmentation. Despite multiple optimisation attempts, the platform as-is could only successfully accommodate a reduced image size of \(32\times 24\) pixels, 100 times smaller than the original.

Resolving the reduced performance issue was one of the key goals of the second ChiPySat iteration, for which we decided to implement the following measures:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item Switch to a statically-typed and statically-allocated programming language, such as C++.
	\item Use OpenMV as a library and adopt its camera drivers, instead of using the entire OpenMV \acs{API}.
	\item Implement an external \acl{RAM} to reduce memory shortage risk.
	\item Switch to a single, dual-core \acs{MCU} instead of a dual-\acs{MCU} design to reduce the real estate required and simplify hardware interfaces.
\end{itemize}

For the second iteration, I implemented the \acs{FFT}-based optimisation algorithm in C, showing the possibility of performing the deconvolution on-board without additional external hardware. I also designed the schematics for the second iteration of the \acs{PCB} with the above changes. However, as I was reaching the end of my research visit, I was not able to complete the integration and testing of this iteration.

\subsection{Evaluation}
\label{sec:CASESTUDIES_chipysat_evaluation}

While ChiPySat was a shorter project than the rest, involving a considerably smaller team and no formal qualification goals, it can still serve as an interesting point for counter-factual analysis. The main differentiating points compared to previous case studies are \begin{enumerate*}
	\item the small, non-standardised form factor of a fully independent spacecraft; and
	\item the implementation of an unknown experimental payload, where there is no guarantee of feasibility.
\end{enumerate*}

For the analysis, I considered a development time of \qty{252}{hours}, divided equally between software and hardware. The counter-factual evaluation of each \acs{SCS} methodology treatment (shown in \Cref{tab:c_chipysat_counterfactuals}) is as follows:
\begin{enumerate}
	\item \textbf{Bypassing structural/thermal analysis}:
	Similar to the previous case studies (\Cref{sec:CASESTUDIES_chipsat_evaluation,sec:CASESTUDIES_ai4space_evaluation,sec:CASESTUDIES_poquito_lessons}), combined with the relatively simple \acs{PCB}-based configuration of the spacecraft.
	\item \textbf{External manufacturing}:
	Early in the mission, we considered the trade-off between in-house and external \acs{PCB} assembly, comparing different available options. We decided to proceed with in-house soldering because of the costs and delays associated with external manufacturing. The availability of calibrated reflow facilities and experienced personnel supported a short \qty{12}{hour} assembly time for each \acs{PCB}.

	\item \textbf{Iterative multi-step development}:
	In ChiPySat, the initial design phase lasted \qty{1.5}{months} and involved design evaluation of different system designs and software development approaches. Nevertheless, the project plan still involved iterating over multiple hardware and software revisions, before reaching a ``flight-ready'' design. On the contrary, a single-step approach would involve more time spent in up-front design over a second iteration. This is linked to the 40-day design time metric found on CubeSats (\Cref{tab:l_cubesat_time_drivers}), here shortened to 20 days to account for the time already spent designing. In this case, however, I consider that the non-conformances identified in the 1\textsuperscript{th} iteration \textbf{would not have been detectable a-priori through design review} of this duration\footnote{And, therefore, a 2\textsuperscript{nd} iteration is needed anyway.}. The reasoning for this argument is provided as follows:
	\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
		\item The team had no access to expert knowledge about the implementation constraints of the selected payload platform (OpenMV), especially when combined with algorithms not used in standard edge image processing.
		\item It is generally difficult to predict software performance without developing the software first, especially in interpreted languages \autocite{kaliberaRigorousBenchmarkingReasonable2013,woodsideFutureSoftwarePerformance2007}. Common approaches such as \acs{WCET} analysis are optimised for \aclp{RTOS}, and not dynamic performance calculations.
		\item The reuse of PyCubed-Mini and OpenMV in their entirety could guarantee the reduction of preliminary and boilerplate work, but not performance. While PyCubed-Mini was understood to be suitable for spacecraft control, with a very smooth learning curve, OpenMV was not suitable for the project in its default configuration.
	\end{itemize}

	\item \textbf{Short iterations}:
	ChiPySat's first iteration, lasting 4 months, cannot be considered short, due to the design effort required beforehand. While the actual duration of the iteration was long, it represented a small amount of person-hours, owing to the small team involved. Nevertheless, it would be possible for ChiPySat to perform even shorter, Agile-like iterations, by moving to manufacturing more ``aggressively''.

	I consider that using shorter (e.g.\@2-month long) iterations would reduce the overall development time by the initial design time, and would increase it by the time required for 1 additional \acs{PCB} revision. The development flow showed that some of the earlier designs considered would not have increased the number of non-conformances or incomplete requirements, showing that risk is not increased by shortening iterations.

	\item \textbf{Robust software framework}:
	As ChiPySat's development experienced issues due to the performance of the frameworks selected, I consider that the use of a more well-understood platform in terms of embedded systems (such as C++ or Rust) would reduce non-conformances, and therefore risk. I also consider a 3-week reduction in development time, related to the optimisation efforts for the Python-based framework.

	\item \textbf{Use of \acl{RPA}}:
	As with previous case studies, the use of automation for bus and payload verification would decrease risk thanks to the guarantee of repeatability. However, because of the heavily hardware-focused nature of the payload, I consider that setting up an automation platform would have taken up more development time. Based on previous experience with AI4Space, I consider this equivalent one week of full-time work.


	\item Note on \textbf{Iterating over partitions}:
	Due to the simplicity and monolithic architecture of ChiPySat, I do not consider that partitioning the system is practical in this project.\footnote{For example, even if one plans to operate on the payload only (lens-less camera), it is still required to implement a microcontroller-based data interface, because of the nature of the camera's interface. Additionally, as the project was based on single \acs{PCB} board, partitioning would have required added effort.}
\end{enumerate}

\FloatBarrier
\section{Cross-Case Analysis}
\label{sec:CASESTUDIES_results}
\label{sec:DISCUSSION_crosscase}

This section presents a cross-case analysis between the four case studies presented before. This analysis depends mainly on the explanations and quantitative counter-factual evaluation performed above. The evaluation goal is defined by the requirements of \Cref{tab:m_methodology_general_rqeuirements}.

The introductory section of \Cref{chap:CASESTUDIES} presented the data gathering process and analysis approach that will also be followed for this chapter.

\begin{figure*}
	\includegraphics{graphics/ResultsCausal.pdf}
	\caption{Observed \acs{SCS} treatment---effect mapping based on case studies}
	\setfloatalignment{b}
	\label{fig:c_causal_mapping}
\end{figure*}



\subsection{Success Evaluation}

\marginnote{%
	The action research-based approach did not allow me to implement a control/treatment group approach, and as such not all elements of the \acs{SCS} methodology were applied at once. However, based on the counter-factual evaluation performed above, there is no significant difference between the \emph{sum of all treatments} and the complete implementation of the methodology. In most cases, 5 out of 6 treatments were applied. Any exceptions are reported in \Cref{chap:CASESTUDIES}.
}
To evaluate the applicability of the \acs{SCS} methodology to internal missions, I consider whether the conditions introduced by the \acs{SCS} methodology, as applied to these case studies, led to successfully achieving the methodology requirements for each mission.

\paragraph{RM1: The SCS Methodology shall enable developing a flight-capable SCS project in 1 year or less, starting from preliminary design to acceptance.}

The development person-hours recorded for each project are listed in the corresponding tables of \Cref{chap:CASESTUDIES}:
\begin{fullwidth}
\begin{equation}
\text{Development Days} = \left[\begin{matrix}
	478 \\ 468 \\ 450 \\ 100
\end{matrix}\right] \text{ person-hours} \div \qty{3}{hours/day} = \left[\begin{matrix}
	159.3 \\ 156.0 \\ 150.0 \\ 33.3
\end{matrix}
\right] \text{ person-days}
\label{eq:totalpersondays}
\end{equation}
\end{fullwidth}
\marginnote[1cm]{Assuming an actual work time of \qty{3}{hours/day}, based on actual observations of daily activities.}

Even in the worst case of 1 person assigned to each project, the development durations in \Cref{eq:totalpersondays} are below 1 year with adequate margin. This is supported by the causal and counter-factual analysis, which suggests that the application of the methodology decreases development time by an average of \(40\%\) (\Cref{fig:c_plot_box_time}).

Furthermore, non-conformance evaluation showed that all completed projects managed to reach a state with no major non-conformances before delivery, confirming that there are no blocking points towards launch.

Therefore, RM1 is considered satisfied for this case.

\paragraph{RM4: The SCS Methodology shall enable equally less risky development of SCS, compared to corresponding ad-hoc or CubeSat-based methodologies.}

While risk is difficult to quantify, counter-factual analyses showed a balanced risk delta between the treated and non-treated cases. In all of the above missions, assigning a numerical value to the categorical risk value showed either a reduction or no overall change in risk. The performed trade-off evaluations for each mission can inform developers on selecting the appropriate treatments. The overall comparison between risk distribution can be seen in \Cref{fig:c_plot_box_risk}.

\FloatBarrier
\subsection{External Validity}

To evaluate the \textbf{external validity} of the proposed methodology \autocite{yinCaseStudyResearch2014}, I will consider whether the results can be generalised to other projects. Since numerical time \& cost data are available, complemented by categorical cost data, a \textbf{statistical analysis} can be performed, based on the following parameters:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item Independent variables: \( \text{Project Number } X_p  \), \( \text{Number of Treatments } X_t \)
	\item Dependent variables: \( \text{Development Time } Y_t \), \( \text{Cost } Y_c \), \( \text{Risk } Y_r \)
	\item Set:
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-2pt]
		\item Actual observations from case studies (\(x_t = x_{t_{\text{actual}}} \))
		\item Counter-factual analysis where \acs{SCS} methodology is not applied (\(x_t = 0\))
		\item Counter-factual analysis where \acs{SCS} methodology is applied (\(x_t = \max X_t\))
	\end{enumerate}
	\item Sample size: \( 3\text{ analyses}\cdot 4\text{ projects} = 12 \) data points (each point associated with \(2 \times 3 \) variables)
	\item Goal: Quantify whether the \emph{dependent variables} have a statistically significant response to the \emph{number of treatments}.
	%\item Null hypothesis: That the distributions between \emph{time, cost or risk} are identical between the \emph{zero-treatment} and \emph{full-treatment} cases.
\end{itemize}

In practice, I am seeking to identify whether the application of more \acs{SCS} treatments leads to a reduction in development time, cost, and risk which can be attributed to the methodology (\(X_t\)), and not to differences between projects (\(X_p \)). After identifying a causal link between a specific treatment and effect, combined with a confirmation by observation, the result can be potentially extrapolated to similar projects.

Before performing a statistical analysis, it must be understood that, due to the nature of the case studies, the sample size is low, and may not guarantee statistical validity\footnote{This also prevents the use of methods that have strict assumptions about their inputs, such as linear regression or \acs{ANOVA}.}. However, strictly causal relationships that, in most cases, guarantee a change in the dependent variables, can be observed studied by statistical tests suitable for smaller sample sizes. These results are considered valid only when such a causal relationship can be established from the case study investigation, as explained in \Cref{fig:c_activity_diagram}.

Consequently, the above problem statement can be answered by the following statistical tests: \autocite{chiccoSimpleGuideUse2025}
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item The \textbf{Student's \emph{t}-test}, which evaluates whether the difference between the response of two groups is statistically significant. The \(t\)-test assumes that the test statistic follows a normal distribution. It tests the null hypothesis that the two groups have equal means.
	\item The \textbf{Mann---Whitney \emph{U} test} which evaluates if two groups have the same distribution. In contrast to the \(t\) test, it is \emph{non-parametric} and makes no assumption about the distribution of the underlying data. It tests the null hypothesis that the distributions of the two groups are identical.
	The test is considered consistent when there is a higher probability that observations from one group are larger than observations the other, and it can be used for low sample sizes \autocite{happOptimalSampleSize2019}.
\end{itemize}
%\marginnote{The Mann---Whitney test can be more or less robust compared to the \(t\)-test, depending on the distribution of the data \cite{zimmermanInvalidationParametricNonparametric1998}. As such, I present both analyses here for completeness.}
\marginnote{Since no assumptions can be made about the underlying distributions of the data, as seen later in this section, but statistically significant results show a clear skew towards a positive or negative difference (as seen below), it is considered that the Mann---Whitney \( U \) test is more robust in this analysis. As such, the \(t\)-test results are mainly provided for reference.}

Both of the above tests assume a comparison between \textbf{two groups}; in this case, considered to be the \emph{group without treatment}, and the \emph{group with all treatments}, i.e.\@ with the complete application of the \acs{SCS} methodology.

The numerical data for these tests is shown in \Cref{tab:c_aggregate_data}. Before applying the above statistical tests, the data is converted to a \textbf{relative form, in relation to the actual observed development time/cost, for each project}. This transformation is performed in order to reduce the impact of the \textbf{inherent scaling} associated with the different technical sizes of each project.

Risk (understood here as \textbf{perceived risk}) is measured only as a categorical variable. Risk values \emph{Low, Base, High} are mapped to numerical values \(-1, 0, +1\) respectively when needed for the analysis. Due to the qualitative nature of risk perception, it is not possible to reliably perform arithmetic operations operations on these values. Nevertheless, the results of this quantification will be presented, in order to assess the \textbf{relative} impact of treatments on risk.

                 & \(t\)-test         & 0.503 & 0.6425 & 3.8741 \\

The results of the analysis are shown across \Cref{fig:c_plot_box_time,fig:c_plot_box_cost,fig:c_plot_box_risk,fig:c_plot_sensitivity_time,fig:c_plot_sensitivity_cost,fig:c_plot_sensitivity_risk}, and aggregated in \Cref{tab:stat_results}. 
These results address the effectiveness of the application of \acs{SCS} on a system as a whole. The effects of the individual treatments (i.e.\@ \acs{SCS} methods) are shown in \Cref{fig:c_plot_specific_sensitivity_time,fig:c_plot_specific_sensitivity_cost,fig:c_plot_specific_sensitivity_risk} and aggregated in \Cref{tab:stat_treatment_results}.

As mentioned above, size of the results prevents generating robust statistical results for all measurements. However, the aggregate results combined from the projects shown above, combined with average treatment effects shown in \Cref{tab:stat_treatment_results}, can reinforce the understanding of the causal effects shown in \Cref{fig:c_causal_mapping}.

Each treatment can be evaluated as follows:
\begin{enumerate}
	\item \textbf{Bypassing structural/thermal analysis}:
	This design choice shows a statistically significant reduction in development time (with project times shrinking to \qty{71}{\percent} of the expected value if these analyses had been performed), low change in cost, and a universal increase in risk. This result is expected, following the reported analysis times from past CubeSat missions (\Cref{tab:l_cubesat_time_drivers}), and the estimation of 23 person-days to complete these analysis (\Cref{sec:CASESTUDIES_chipsat_evaluation}). The small dimensions and generally simple physical interfaces of \acs{SCS} reduce the value of \acs{FEM} modelling, which is typically done for larger spacecraft, but can be replaced by simpler calculations or repeated tests for smaller systems. This observation is supported by the results of the case studies, which passed environmental qualification without any detected non-conformances.
	\item \textbf{In-house manufacturing}:
	The aggregation of the results shows a \qty{10}{\percent} increase in development time, a \qty{33}{\percent} decrease in cost, and a project-specific change in perceived risk, when a team decides to assembly electronic boards in-house.
	These results match the expected increase in manufacturing time combined with the high costs associated with external manufacturing, while risk depends on the specific manufacturing requirements, and whether they can be reliably met in-house. However, this analysis has not modelled the lead times of externally-covered processes, which are seen as blocking points in project tasks, but are not accounted here in terms of development time.
	\item \textbf{Multi-step development}:
	This treatment represents the use of an iterative lifecycle process, as opposed to a sequential single-step lifecycle. All case studies were performed using an iterative approach. The counter-factual evaluation was based on estimating the time required for an initial design (considered as 40 person-days, based on \Cref{tab:l_cubesat_time_drivers}), against the time (and cost budget) saved by not performing the development work related to multiple hardware iterations. Nevertheless, I considered that at least one second hardware iteration would have been necessary in some projects, either due to externally changed requirements, or due to an inherent lack of information before experimentation for novel designs.

	The results indicate that multi-step development reduces development time by \qty{25}{\percent} with high confidence. Low-confidence indicators show that it increases cost by \qty{18}{\percent}, with a project-specific change in risk.
	\item \textbf{Short iterations}:
	Combined with multi-step development, short (1--2 month long) system-level iterations delineate the core of typical Agile practices.
	The impact of this treatment affected development times by \qtyrange{10}{20}{\percent}, but on a direction that was project-specific. On the other hand, short iterations increase cost by \qty{33}{\percent} with high confidence, while generally increasing perceived risk.

	The selection of iteration duration therefore seems both highly impactful and highly project-specific, with ``larger'' projects (in this case, POQUITO) being less suitable for short iterations compared to simpler projects, due to the correlation between technical size and manufacturing cost/integration time. %This factor also depends on the expected lead times and developer availability for the project.
	\item \textbf{Iterating over partitions}:
	The choice of iterating over partitions over the entire system does not seem to have a significant effect in development time or risk, and reduces cost by \qty{8}{\percent} on average, with low confidence.

	While a cost reduction due to partitioning is expected (since ``less of the system'' has to be repeatedly procured), lessons learned from the case studies showed that small-sized systems (in this case, AI4Space) do not significantly benefit from the effort associated with partitioning.\footnote{Partitioning is also less beneficial when there is high early confidence in a design. Compare, for example, the negative impact of partitioning on AI4Space, which depended on an already well-understood hardware architecture, to the positive impact of partitioning on ChipSat-1, where the payload and power system designs were less familiar to the team.}
	\item \textbf{Robust software framework}:
	The selection of a robust software framework has a varied effect on development time based on the project requirements and hardware, without any effects on cost, and a universal reduction in perceived risk.
	\item \textbf{\acf{RPA}}:
	The use of \acl{RPA} can incur an increase in development time for some projects, with no changes in cost, and a universal reduction in perceived risk.
\end{enumerate}

A summary of these results, along with the identified causal links, is shown in \Cref{tab:d_new_statements}.

\begin{table*}[]
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.8}
\renewcommand\tabularxcolumn[1]{m{#1}}% for vertical centering text in X column
\begin{adjustbox}{max width=\textwidth,center}
\begin{tabularx}{1.2\textwidth}{@{}l>{\raggedright}m{3cm}XXX@{}}
\toprule
 &    &  \multicolumn{3}{c}{Revised Theory} \\ \cmidrule(lr){3-5}
 & Treatment & \multicolumn{1}{c}{Time} & \multicolumn{1}{c}{Cost} & \multicolumn{1}{c}{Risk} \\ \midrule
1 & \textbf{Bypassing structural/thermal analysis} & Consistent \textcolor{nice}{reduction}, due to large fixed effort to set up analysis, compared with days-long testing. & Test campaigns may \textcolor{bad}{increase} cost. & Risk \textcolor{bad}{increases} (undetected non-conformances). \\
2 & \textbf{In-house manufacturing} & Consistent \textcolor{bad}{increase} due to in-house effort. However, note that this model does not emulate delays caused due to external lead times. & Consistent \textcolor{nice}{reduction} in costs, under the assumption of ``expensive'' external manufacturing and internal facilities. & Depends on team, facilities, and external suppliers. \\
3 & \textbf{Multi-step development} & Standard iteration durations are consistently \textcolor{nice}{shorter} than an initial \acs{BDUF} step. & Consistent \textcolor{bad}{increase} due to manufacturing costs. & Depends on low-level project details and team expertise. \\
4 & \textbf{Short iterations} & Depends on project complexity and team size. Longer iterations suited for projects with a larger technical size. & Short iterations imply more iterations, more manufacturing runs, and therefore a consistent cost \textcolor{bad}{increase}. & Depends on low-level project details. \\
5 & \textbf{Iterating over partitions} & Depends on project complexity. Partitioning suited for more complex projects. &  Cost may \textcolor{nice}{decrease} (due to reduced manufacturing) & Depends on project complexity. Risk \textcolor{bad}{increases} when partitioning simpler projects, and vice versa. \\
6 & \textbf{Robust software framework} & Depends on low-level factors. & No factors influence cost. & Risk \textcolor{nice}{decreases} due to inherent software framework characteristics. \\
7 & \textbf{Robotic Process Automation} & Depends on project testing complexity (automation vs.\@ manual effort) & \textcolor{bad}{Increases} when hardware infrastructure required. &  Risk \textcolor{nice}{decreases} due to capability for continual, immediate testing after changes. \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{adjustbox}
\caption{Revised methodology treatment statements}
\label{tab:d_new_statements}
\end{table*}

The above results indicate that the ideal or ``optimal'' selection of \acs{SCS} development methods can be project-specific, based on variables both known and unknown at the start of a project, and depending on the desired trade-off between time, cost and risk.

To evaluate the methodology in its entirety, I will consider a ``naive'' application of all the above methods, compared against their complete non-application.\footnote{Alternatively, I could consider the counter-factual project data of applying \emph{only those methods that would lead to the best result}. However, this analysis would have to make assumptions about the possibility to select the appropriate methods a-priori, and the desired optimisation target, making it generally invalid in the general case.}
Based on the aggregate results of \Cref{tab:c_aggregate_data,tab:stat_results}, the following observations can be made:
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-1pt]
	\item The \textbf{development time} of a project shows a clear \textbf{correlation} with the level of the \acs{SCS} methodology applied. One of the clearest drivers of development time reduction is the selection of a multi-step methodology, which foregoes a typically long \acs{BDUF} design step, especially when combined with minor optimisations in the programmatic and technical aspects of development. While not all treatments have a definitively positive effect on development time, a general reduction trend can be observed. The Mann-Whitney \( U \) test shows a statistically significant difference, with an average reduction of \qty{40.7}{\percent}.
	\item The development \textbf{cost}\footnote{This cost does not include labour, insurance, ground segment or asset costs. See \Cref{sec:LITERATURE_cubesat_environment} for the relevant explanation.} of a project is \textbf{not correlated} to the full application or not of the \acs{SCS} methodology. Most iterative methods that are applied on physical systems tend to increase costs. However, this is highly affected by the project context, and especially different allocations on budget categories.
	\item The project \textbf{risk} shows \text{no statistically significant correlation} to the application or not of the \acs{SCS} methodology. While \Cref{fig:c_plot_box_risk} shows a general trend of risk reduction after applying the \acs{SCS} methodology, clearer results are shown in \Cref{fig:c_plot_specific_sensitivity_risk}, where it is shown that \acs{SCS} methods are generally evenly divided between perceived risk increase, decrease, or project-specific behaviour.

	The adoption or not of specific methods can be perceived as a trade-off between time and risk, as shown in \Cref{fig:c_tradeoff_chipsat1,fig:c_tradeoff_ai4space,fig:c_tradeoff_poquito,fig:c_tradeoff_chipysat}.
	A ``naively assumed'' correlation would expect that higher development time reduces perceived risk. While some treatments do follow this trend, this is not universally true. Notable examples of this are treatments \#6 and \#7, which involve decisions related to software and automation.
	While there is no indication that the \acs{SCS} methodology increases risk, a generally-applicable guideline to decrease risk cannot be provided, given the project dependence of this aspect.
\end{enumerate}

\newpage
\section{Answers to Research Questions}
\label{sec:DISCUSSION_rq_answers}

\NewDocumentCommand{\researchquestion}{mm}{%
	\begin{tikzpicture}
		\node [rectangle,fill=MaterialAmber100,draw=MaterialAmber800,minimum height=1.5cm,text width=\linewidth,align=center]
		at (0,0)
		{\textbf{#1}: #2};
	\end{tikzpicture}%
}

\begin{fullwidth}
\researchquestion{RQ1}{What are the current \ac{SYE} approaches used by \ac{SCS} developers?}

A literature review and interviews with \acs{SCS} developers (\Cref{sec:LITERATURE_subcubesat}) do not indicate the existence of a standard \acl{SYE} approach or methodology used for centimetre-scale spacecraft.
Instead, developers tailor existing approaches from the CubeSat ecosystem, ranging from sequential to highly-iterative lifecycles, but usually with lower fidelity or coverage.

\researchquestion{RQ2}{What are the shortcomings of current \ac{SYE} approaches used by \ac{SCS} developers?}

The classification of potential \acs{SCS} systems engineering shortcomings was made in reference to the \emph{six-pointed star model}, which separates the constraints of an engineering project into \emph{scheduling, scope, cost, risk, resources} and \emph{quality}. To answer this question, evidence from the literature and interviews was used (\Cref{sec:LITERATURE_subcubesat}). While cost is often mentioned as a particular benefit for the selection of pocket-sized spacecraft, it is either not referred to as an optimisation target, or already successfully kept low through mainly in-house manufacturing. Instead, developers place significant weight on the improvement of reliability and the reduction of development time (mainly to fit typical academic cycles in educational environments, or to match specific production capabilities). Past mission failures and long average development times indicate a need to focus on reliability and development velocity. \todo{Improve this answer}

\researchquestion{RQ3}{Can a new, tailored \ac{SYE} methodology address a selection of the shortcomings identified in \texttt{RQ2} for \ac{SCS}?}

Due to the existence of previous literature and the difficulty of quantitatively reasoning about reliability and cost, I have selected \emph{development time} as the optimisation target for a new \acs{SYE} methodology, with an additional requirement to not increase technical risk. Previous work from the \acl{SYE}, CubeSat and PocketQube domains has indicated that development time and reliability can be heavily influenced by the systems engineering and systems design approaches used.

\Cref{chap:METHODOLOGY} presents a tailored methodology for \acs{SCS}, based on the principles of Agile software engineering, which covers different aspects of the development of pocket-sized systems. The validity of this methodology, and whether it can address the identified shortcomings, is evaluated in \Cref{sec:CASESTUDIES_results}, through causal and counter-factual analyses, based on data from four case studies. The results show that the appropriate selection of a methodology can reduce total development time by an average of 40\%, even when considering different types of projects, without an upwards change in technical risk.
The development time of the case studies was approximately one year.
These results are supported by the understanding of the causal effects of each part of the methodology. The answer to this question is therefore positive.

\pagebreak[4]
\researchquestion{RQ4}{Is the \ac{SYE} methodology developed in \texttt{RQ3} effective in practical scenarios?}
This research question involves the generalisation of the results of \texttt{RQ3}. Through the four case studies performed, I attempted to cover different mission types (payloads, \acs{PCB}Sats, a PocketQube) that encompass a large set of typical project requirements.  This is supported by the analysis performed in \Cref{sec:CASESTUDIES_results}, showing changes in the measured metrics that are heavily dependent on the methodology used, and not on each specific project. Nevertheless, the number of the case studies is not large enough to take into account all possible parameters and external factors, meaning that more data would be necessary to completely answer \texttt{RQ4}. Therefore, I will consider that the answer to this question is potentially positive.
\end{fullwidth}

\FloatBarrier
\section{Threats to Validity}
\label{sec:DISCUSSION_limitations}
\begin{fullwidth}
	Even though I attempted to use prior knowledge and verifiable claims to inform, synthesise, and validate the proposed \acs{SCS} methodology, each of the performed tasks was limited by the available data and what was practically achievable with the resources allocated to this work.
	This section presents the limitations and threats to validity of this research, following a sequential order, as specified in the original Research Plan (\Cref{chap:METHODOLOGY}). %Most threats relate 
	Most identified threats relate to external influencing factors that can affect case study results by threatening the logical conclusions that can be made regarding the methodology. For each external factor identified as an influence, a specific mitigation is applied, as shown below:


\begin{enumerate}
	% 43 total organisations according to WP
	\item[Threat 1.] \textbf{Initial data}: The answers to \texttt{RQ1} and \texttt{RQ2}, as well as data for the definition and validation of the \acs{SCS} methodology, were composed based on a literature review and discussions with past \acs{SCS} developers. Due to the relative novelty of the domain, the amount of available data sources was limited, and biased towards academic projects, as opposed to commercial ones. While I attempted to gather data from diverse sources, the generation and publication of further \acl{SYE} results from future missions will be able to provide a stronger foundation for my initial claims.

	\item[Threat 2.] \textbf{Evaluation of metrics}: ``Development time'' was chosen as the target optimisation metric for the \acs{SCS} methodology. Even if time is understood as an easily quantifiable parameter, both its passage and its measurement can depend on factors which are uncontrollable or unrelated to the systems engineering discipline (such as available resources or launch delays).
	This affects both the answers to \texttt{RQ2} and the evaluation of the methodology in practical case studies. However, no other salient and comparable parameter could be identified.
	For this work, development time was measured from the start of the design effort until the spacecraft was ready for integration. \todo{Write about other metrics}

	\item[Threat 3.] \textbf{Case study-based validation}: The validation of the \acs{SCS} methodology (\texttt{RQ3} and \texttt{RQ4}) was based on four case studies. In contrast to, e.g.\@, an experimental setup, the case study approach does not require control over all environmental parameters and can be practically performed against complete missions, which are the main unit of analysis of this study. Typical concerns connected to case studies relate to the misuse of data, small sample sizes, and incorrect generalisations \cite[266]{yinCaseStudyResearch2014}. I address these concerns by documenting the gathered qualitative and quantitative data in \Cref{chap:CASESTUDIES}, by using multiple sources of evidence for claims, and by analytic generalisations of findings in \Cref{sec:CASESTUDIES_results}.
	
	In \Cref{chap:CASESTUDIES}, I identified various external/confounding factors that can threaten the logical inferences required to validate the proposed methodology, namely:
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-2ex]
		\item Project technical size, Project complexity
		\item Team expertise, Team size, Available facilities
		\item Launch provider requirements
		\item External supplier/manufacturer conditions (requirements, lead times, cost, quality)
		\item Low-level project specifications
	\end{enumerate}
	The external factors identified as affecting each evaluation point are listed in each individual case study.

	In general, to control for these factors, 
	I identified their potential influence against each treatment, and if it affected the predicted results. In this way, the cross-case analysis pinpointed aspects of the methodology that do or do not depend on project-specific or environment-specific factors (\Cref{tab:d_new_statements}).

	%\citeauthor{yinCaseStudyResearch2014} \cite{yinCaseStudyResearch2014}
	Additionally, I participated in a diverse set of case studies, to the extent possible, in order to cover a sufficiently wide range of external conditions.
	More specifically:
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-2ex]
		\item The case studies covered a large gamut of the \acs{SCS} systems targeted by this methodology, from a \acs{PCB}-based payload to a PocketQube, with different hardware/software designs for each one, as well as different launch configurations.
		\item The case studies were distributed over two institutions.
		\item All projects were performed by different teams, with (predominantly) different participants and different sizes.
		\item While executing the case studies, we performed incremental updates to the universities' lab infrastructure, increasing the amount of local facilities available.
		\item Different external suppliers/manufacturers were used for each project, including \acs{PCB} manufacturers, \acs{PCB} assemblers, machine shops, component suppliers, testing facilities, and launch providers. More specifically, within the first three case studies, we selected between 2--4 different companies for each of the aforementioned tasks.
	\end{enumerate}

	Nevertheless, two invariants remained unchanged across all case studies:
	\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
		\item All case studies were performed within an academic environment. 
		Based on the initial literature survey, academic projects can have small but discernible differences compared to their industrial counterparts.
		More specifically, the former may have different goals, increased bureaucracy,  smaller or less experienced teams, high turnover rates, and low duty cycles of participating members. These effects are more pronounced with student projects (as opposed to e.g.\@ research projects).
		
		To mitigate this invariant, all executed projects were made within a research context, with limited student involvement. Team members were assigned to projects for the duration of the entire mission (with the exception of students invited to take ownership of specific work packages) and based on their expertise and prior familiarity with the used technologies (especially in terms of software/hardware development and satellite integration). This combination of factors can be considered a suitable middle-ground between fully student-led and fully commercial projects.

		Nevertheless, follow-up studies can be performed to confidently evaluate the impact of the different environmental context.
		\item I participated as a systems engineer in all case studies. To mitigate the influencing effect of a constant participant with a single responsibility, I was assigned to different work packages for each project, or shared my responsibilities with other core team members. This involved, for example, switching between the technical tasks of system design, \acs{PCB} design, software development, and testing amongst all projects, while I shared the \emph{systems engineer} role with other participants.
	\end{itemize}
	
	%\item[Threat 4.] \textbf{Case study diversity}: Three of the case studies performed took place within the same institution. Even though the teams involved were mostly comprised of different developers (see \Cref{app:contribution_matrix}), the institutional context, resources available, facilities and environmental constraints were similar. Additionally, none of the case studies performed included an industrial manufacturer. While the evaluated set of environmental characteristics is not uncommon, follow-up studies would be required to confidently evaluate the impact of undetected yet critical environmental factors.
	
	%A particular similarity amongst all case studies was that they were performed within 

	\item[Threat 4.] \textbf{Action research}: The execution of the four case studies (where I participated as an engineer) took the form of action research. While this provided ``insider's knowledge'' into the projects, participatory action research presents a number of limitations on what data can be gathered, and how it is analysed. \citeauthor{kockThreeThreatsAction2004} identifies three main threats of action research: \cite{kockThreeThreatsAction2004}
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-1pt]
		\item \textbf{Uncontrollability}: The researcher \emph{does not have full control over the environment being studied}.
		This was particularly true in the evaluated case studies, as it was not possible to fully control the application and implementation of the \acs{SCS} methodology, allocate resources, or recruit the desired personnel for technical tasks.

		A critical result of this threat, combined with the iterative nature of action research, is that no case study was performed with the complete methodology applied (or not applied). Instead, all case studies involved the application of \emph{most} aspects of the methodology on them, with the complete application being evaluated based on the counter-factual inference.
		\item \textbf{Contingency}: The researcher \emph{gets access to a shallow body of knowledge, that rarely provides evidence for a particular interaction}. It is difficult, for example, to reason about the impact of each specific methodology treatment against the project's flow, as any causal links for a project's result are not evident, 
		\item \textbf{Subjectivity}: It is impossible for the researcher to be in a detached position from the subject being studied, rendering it possible to \emph{introduce biases into their conclusions}.
	\end{enumerate}

	To mitigate these threats, mitigations generally focus on increasing the amount of independent data and perspectives that are collected and interpreted in the final results \cite{kockThreeThreatsAction2004,yinCaseStudyResearch2014,creswellResearchDesignQualitative2023}. In this study, the following approaches were used \cite{kockThreeThreatsAction2004}:
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-1pt]
		\item \emph{Unit of Analysis}: This method supports that research data collection and analysis should be performed on individual and generally independent \emph{units of analysis} identified in the beginning of the research study. The higher number of units of analysis that can support a trend, the higher the validity of these findings. In this study, the unit of analysis represents the combination of \emph{treatments} for the methodology, and target \emph{metrics} for the projects.
		\item \emph{Multiple iterations}: This method involves repeating the action research cycle across different contexts, in order to reduce the effect of confounding variables on single iterations. In this study, apart from iterations within the projects, I participated in different case studies across different contexts to make sure that the expanded research scope can cover diverse practical cases of \acs{SCS} engineering, as described in \emph{Threat 3}.
	\end{enumerate}

	% In general, these threats are mitigated by the \emph{unit of analysis} (prescribing certain target metrics and subjects to study prior to the beginning of the research) and \emph{multiple iterations} (of te action research cycle) methods proposed by \citeauthor{kockThreeThreatsAction2004} \cite{kockThreeThreatsAction2004}.

	\item [Threat 5.] %counterfactual
	\textbf{Counter-factual evaluation}:
	The comparison between the application and non-application of \acs{SCS} methodology treatments in \Cref{chap:CASESTUDIES} was performed through the evaluation of hypothetical scenarios.
%	It is impossible to accurately model the potential interactions between different approaches on the same project.
	As it is impossible to accurately predict the results of emulated interactions in a project, these results may not represent reality.
	However, through the participation and evaluation of different \acs{SCS} treatments across four projects, it was possible to generate high-confidence predictions about the effects of each treatment.
	The data used to inform these predictions was gathered from the missions themselves (e.g.\@ in the case that an alternative option was evaluated but abandoned during the mission), from results gathered from other missions, from past \acs{SCS}/CubeSat literature, or based on a causal evaluation. In the case of disagreements of these previous estimates, I chose the most adverse values, i.e.\@ the values that would lead to the least favourable evaluation of the \acs{SCS} methodology.
	
	\item [Threat 6.] % validation
	\textbf{Lack of mission results}:
	For this research, I gathered in-orbit data from one mission only (AI4Space), which demonstrated a performance within the expected envelope. Two other missions (ChipSat, ChiPySat) were never launched, while POQUITO was launched but not detected by ground stations.

	An obstacle specifically linked to space missions is that no definitive data can be generated about their success until they manage to transmit data from orbit.
	The launch itself requires finding appropriate funding and a compatible launch slot.
	The validation of a mission requires that it is qualified, launched, deployed, and successful.	In the case of a mission failure (either early or late), it is very likely that, due to reduced observability, no information can be gathered about the cause of the failure, and whether the cause is linked to the \acl{SYE} methodology or not.

	To mitigate against the uncertainty of mission events, and to discount their effect on the evaluation of the \acs{SCS} methodology, I defined the success of environmental qualification as the main goal of the methodology. Qualification is a valuable stage-gate, which can provide timely results, and locate critical system faults that are not obvious by inspection. This is represented by a reduced ``\acl{TRL}'' of 8 in NASA's \acs{TRL} scale, which demonstrates that a system is flight-qualified, but not flight-proven \cite{sheaNASASystemsEngineering2020}.
	All three tested case studies passed the qualification step successfully.
	In-orbit demonstration missions are then a typical next step to increase this rating to the maximum 9.


	% statistical generalisation (actually not a threat)
\end{enumerate}

\end{fullwidth}

\chapter{Conclusion}
\label{chap:CONCLUSION}

\begin{fullwidth}

\section{Summary}

This thesis presented a systems engineering methodology for the development of \acf{SCS}, with a focus on the reduction of development time without a negative impact on technical risk.
The methodology focuses on the systems engineering fields of \emph{lifecycle processes}, \emph{requirements management}, \emph{modelling \& knowledge management}, \emph{design solution definition}, and \emph{verification}. Its foundation mainly lies on Agile methodologies found in software development, which have been tailored for use in \acl{SCS} due to these spacecraft's unique size, mass and cost characteristics.

\begin{table*}[h]
\def\arraystretch{1.2}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{lXX}
	\toprule
	& \textbf{Thesis Objective} & \textbf{Addressed by} \\
	\midrule
	1 & Investigate the current \ac{SYE} approaches used by \ac{SCS} developers and gather data about the environment and typical \ac{SCS} missions. & Initial literature review of small space systems environments and open problems (\Cref{sec:INTRODUCTION_defining_systems_engineering,sec:INTRODUCTION_research_gaps}), detailed literature review of \acs{SCS} development environment and constraints (\Cref{sec:LITERATURE_subcubesat}), interviews with \acs{SCS} developers (\Cref{sec:LITERATURE_interviews}). \\
	2 & Identify quantifiable parameters that can describe shortcomings in current \ac{SYE} approaches. & Definition of \acs{SYE} metrics based on available literature and \acs{SCS} needs (\Cref{sec:LITERATURE_metrics}). \\
	3 & Synthesise a set of methods for technical management and system realisation that describe a complete \ac{SYE} methodology for \ac{SCS}. & Investigation of current systems engineering \acs{SOTA} (\Cref{sec:LITERATURE_systems_engineering}), elaboration of a \acs{SCS} methodology (\Cref{chap:METHODOLOGY}). \\
	4 & Validate the new methodology on at least one space mission that will succeed in environmental qualification. & Four case studies performed in iterative fashion based on action-research principles and counter-factual evaluation (\Cref{chap:CASESTUDIES}). \\
	\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\caption{Thesis objectives and how they were addressed}
\end{table*}

\vspace{1.5cm}
In \Cref{sec:INTRODUCTION_research_gaps,sec:LITERATURE_subcubesat}, I investigated the ecosystem and typical development environment of Sub-CubeSat missions, and identified the main characteristics and constraints of \acs{SCS}. Past \acs{SCS} projects have generally involved small teams of up to approx.\@10 people, and development times are driven by launch agreements, which typically involve 1-year long commitments. The small technical size and complexity of these spacecraft match the requirements of lightweight Agile methodologies. 

In \Cref{sec:LITERATURE_metrics}, I identified development \textbf{time}, development \textbf{cost}, and \textbf{technical risk} as metrics related to the performance of Systems Engineering frameworks when applied to \acl{SCS}.
Results from the literature and past missions have shown that developers place significant weight on reducing development time and increasing spacecraft reliability. This is driven by the low success rates of past small satellite missions, combined with the desire to reduce resource utilisation and programmatic risk, by ensuring that projects do not experience cost overruns. Past literature has linked both of these factors to inadequate systems engineering practices followed by small satellite developers and to an unfamiliarity with past lessons learned.

Based on the above, \textbf{development time and technical risk} are identified as optimisation goals for a tailored \acs{SCS} methodology. Informed by findings from topic-specific literature survey and practical mission implementation, this methodology is synthesised in \Cref{chap:METHODOLOGY}, as a combination of:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-3pt]
	\item A \textbf{requirements management} method based on different degrees of necessity and reduced requirement formalism;
	\item Two \textbf{lifecycle process models} based on Scrum, and involving short, predefined system-level iterations;
	\item Guidelines for tool-based technical planning and work package management;
	\item A tool-based \textbf{modelling \& knowledge management} method based on lightweight \acl{MBSE};
	\item A \textbf{hardware} design method and guidelines, specifically for pocket-sized systems, including interface management, with the purpose of being compatible with external safety requirements;
	\item A lightweight test-based \textbf{verification \& validation} method, informed from software engineering and \acl{RPA} approaches; and
	%\item Guidelines for \textbf{maintenance \& operations} based on spacecraft agility; and
	\item A set of methods for \textbf{software development}, with a focus on technology choice for reliability increase.
\end{itemize}

In \Cref{chap:CASESTUDIES}, four case studies, developed within the timeline of this thesis, were used to generate data and retroactively validate the proposed ``\acs{SCS} methodology''.
Two of the studies were designed as small satellite payloads, while the other two were complete \acs{SCS} missions. 
Three case studies aimed to achieve successful qualification, while one aimed at successful functional testing.
One mission was launched and operated successfully.
Different elements of the \acs{SCS} methodology were applied in each case study.
Based on data from the administrative and technical decisions taken during the mission, as well as available project management literature from other projects, a counter-factual analysis was performed to generate quantitative data on the estimated impact of the \acs{SCS} methodology on the metrics identified above.
The analysis showed that the \acs{SCS} methodology can reduce development duration by an average of \textbf{\qty{40}{\percent}} compared to the contrary case, combined with a limited effect in production cost and risk, and thus achieving the main objective of reducing development time without an adverse effect on risk.
Additionally, the significance and magnitude of specific parts of the methodology on the selected metrics was evaluated. 

\section{Key Findings and Contributions}

This thesis has the goal of introducing and validating a systems engineering methodology for the efficient development of satellites less than \SI{1}{\kilo\gram}. ``Efficiency'' is understood as the reduction of development time without a negative impact on technical risk, and with a target of \qty{1}{year} from conception to a flight-ready spacecraft. In this section, I will present the conclusions and limitations of the findings.

Regarding the understanding of the relationship between systems engineering and \acl{SCS}, I report the following findings:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2pt]
	\item The success rate of CubeSat and \acs{SCS} missions has been identified as a concern amongst developers. Current understanding ties this low success rate to inadequate system-level testing, non-following of established procedures, inadequate knowledge transfer, and design decisions that are not considering reliability as a factor.
	\item Reduction of development time is identified as another main target in \acs{SCS} projects, mainly influenced by logistical requirements placed on project owners, and fixed product delivery deadlines. However, a large variation in development duration of past missions has been identified, ranging from \qtyrange{10}{62}{months}, which cannot be tied to project requirements \& environment alone.
	\item 
	In contrast to traditional space systems engineering and CubeSat systems engineering, smaller systems have a disproportionately smaller need for preliminary ``architecting and documenting'' work.
	\acl{SCS} systems present characteristics, such as small technical size and mass manufacturing, testing \& launch capabilities, that specifically match the environment of lightweight Agile software and systems engineering frameworks.
\end{itemize}

Based on the above findings, I have contributed the definition of a tailored systems engineering methodology, which is comprised of:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2pt]
	\item A set of \textbf{methods} covering the systems engineering disciplines deemed most impactful to \acs{SCS} \acl{SYE}, in terms of \emph{Technical Management}, \emph{System Description}, and \emph{System Realisation};
	\item A set of methods and guidelines for the \textbf{hardware and software design definition} of \acs{SCS} spacecraft based on mission lessons learned and past literature;
	\item A list of proposed tools or, when not available, tool requirements, to support the aforementioned design activities;
	\item A tailoring guide to assist developers in selecting the optimal set of methods for their project.
\end{itemize}

The following findings are reported from the application and counter-factual evaluation of the methodology on four \acs{SCS} case studies:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2pt]
	\item Across \acs{SCS} projects, a typical workload was identified, averaging \qty{500}{person-hours} for ChipSats and \qty{800}{person-hours} for a PocketQube.
	\item It is possible to shorten \acl{SCS} development duration to approximately 1 year without adverse effects in technical risk or requirements compliance, given that at least the typical workload is performed.
	\item Compared to a single-step sequential approach, a naive application of the complete \acs{SCS} methodology can reduce \textbf{development duration} by \qty{40}{\percent}. The statistical significance of this result is supported by the reduction of time-consuming processes, which did not prove to provide significant added value in the performed case studies.
	\item The naive application of the methodology was not found to have a statistically significant effect on cost and risk.
	\item The most significant contributing factors to development time were found to be \textbf{frequent testing} (as opposed to detailed analyses) and \textbf{iterative} (as opposed to sequential) development, reducing it by \qty{29}{\percent} and \qty{25}{\percent} respectively.
	The optimal iteration duration is dependent on the specific project context, with larger projects seemingly being suited for longer iterations.
	\item In terms of production cost, the most significant contributors were found to be \textbf{in-house manufacturing} (\qty{33}{\percent} reduction) and \textbf{short iteration duration} (\qty{33}{\percent} increase), which are not associated with a significant effect on development time.
	\item The choice of \textbf{programming language} (and, consequently, software framework), as well as the use of automated software/hardware testing procedures were found to decrease technical risk, without a significant effect on development time or cost.
	\item The option to \textbf{iterate over partitions} of the system, instead of the entire system, was not found to have predictable results on any of the target metrics, being highly environment-specific.
\end{itemize}

One limitation in the validity of the above findings stems from the use of statistical generalisation for the numerical results, as only four case studies were used for the analysis. While an analytical generalisation was performed to demonstrate validity, it is not sufficient to generate a quantitative understanding of the effects of the \acs{SCS} methodology.

It is concluded that specific systems engineering decisions can have an effect on the development time, cost and technical risk on \acs{SCS} missions. No optimal ``one-size-fits-all'' solution could be identified, with different decisions being more suitable to different goals or projects, with a high dependence on project technical size. However, impactful decisions can generally be taken in an informed manner based on the known environment and desired trade-off.

\section{Future Work}

A major challenge in the definition and evaluation of \acs{SCS} methods is the lack of substantial published data for systems engineering practices, success rates and lessons learned from PocketQube and ChipSat missions. The collection of data would inform decisions taken for the application or verification of the methodology, allowing potential quantitative evaluation of risk and other metrics.

Based on the definition of the \acl{DRM}, this thesis performed only an \emph{initial evaluation} of the \emph{Descriptive Study II} stage, i.e.\@ the investigation of the impact and performance of the development methodology.
To that end, one future direction for this research is the further application and empirical evaluation of the methodology on new \acs{SCS} projects. This effort can also be supported by schedule, cost and risk modelling approaches from \acl{SYE} research %, such as COSYSMO,
to provide a formal framework for guiding decisions during methodology tailoring.

Even though the methodology has not been designed for CubeSats or larger systems, it may be possible to apply it in part or in its entirety on these systems, based on the environmental context. What are the factors that allow Agile systems engineering for CubeSats, and which parts of the \acs{SCS} methodology can be transferred to them? As CubeSat manufacturing is predominantly moving to local facilities, it becomes easier to follow approaches (such as 1-month long iterations) that were previously seen as utopian, while still conforming to the mostly stricter safety requirements for CubeSats.

Another notable point of refinement for this work is the set of hardware and software development guidelines, which are mainly sourced from lessons learned during the case studies, but will fundamentally benefit from further experience gained during development and flight, with an end goal of creating a best-practices knowledge base for \acs{SCS} developers, similar to existing attempts for CubeSats.

As software has been identified as a major time allocation and risk driver for \acs{SCS} missions, it would be beneficial to perform more research on the improvement of the time---risk trade-off. Lightweight formal verification techniques for software have been explored, but it is still unknown whether their added benefits overweigh the additional effort needed to instrument the entire or parts of the software, especially for teams without prior experience with these processes.

%\Cref{sec:DISCUSSION_limitations}

Finally, this thesis identified tooling as a substantial area of interest for small spacecraft development. Apart from the actual development of software tools, future research can focus on identifying the set of functions and suitable designs to cover the requirements for work package management and modelling, without the currently understood overhead associated with formal tools.

\chapter{A Reflection-Based C++ Serialisation Library}
\label{app:ok_serializer}\label{app:ok_serialiser}

In the pursuit to build reliable and simple systems, software developers endeavour to reduce the \acl{SLOC} and repetition required to implement a single feature (\Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_software_reliability}).
One common feature that needs to be implemented by almost all spacecraft is the \textbf{handling of telemetry and telecommands}. In terms of software development, this feature can be separated into:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=-2ex]
	\item Parsing incoming messages
	\item Formatting outgoing messages
	\item Creating a data structure to store these messages
	\item Documenting the format of these messages and structures in a human-readable way
\end{itemize}
\marginnote{More sub-features can be potentially identified, such as providing a debugging interface to explain messages, implementing similar features for the ground segment, or sharing this functionality amongst different subsystems.}

In a naive implementation, each of these features would be completed and implemented separately. This approach would increase program size (and therefore complexity) and introduce failure points, in case of mismatches between implementations. Potential changes in message structure would have to be implemented across different parts of the codebase, further increasing development time and risk. However, in an ideal situation, a description of the functionality that describes \acs{TMTC} handling will exist in a single place.

This is generally easy to perform in dynamically-typed languages, such as Python or Julia, which allow changing the behaviour of a program based on its own structure. This is referred to as \emph{runtime reflection} \autocite{smithProceduralReflectionProgramming1982}, but can be implemented using various methods that rely on dynamic memory allocation. Python's \emph{construct} library, for example, allows defining a structure, parser, formatter, and documentation for arbitrary binary messages in a single declaration \autocite{bulskiConstruct2020}.

% \begin{lstlisting}[language=Python, caption=Example telecommand definition from AI4Space using the \emph{construct} library]
% large_packet_request = \
% """Prepare to receive a large packet TM. This places the response in a large packet with a specific
%    name so that it can later be fetched, even if smaller packages are intertwined in between
% """ * \
% Struct(
%     "service" / Const(bytes([common.service.large_packet.intvalue])),
%     "message" / Const(b"r"),
%     "name" / Bytes(3) * "Name of the large packet to be used for storage and fetching",
%     "command" / GreedyBytes,
% )
% \end{lstlisting}

However, as seen in \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_softwaredevelopment}, developers may not always desire to use interpreted programming languages, instead relying on a restricted subset of compiled, statically allocated languages, while avoiding dynamic and runtime-based features. In this case, \emph{autocoding} has historically been one of the approaches used, by generating the required functions from a single, language-agnostic specification. This is, for example, the approach followed by CubedOS, whose team has developed a tool written in \emph{Scala} that generates \emph{Ada} encoding/decoding subprograms from an \emph{External Data Representation (XDR)} \autocite{brandonCubedOSVerifiedCubeSat2017}. However, this approach adds additional steps and complexity to the software's build cycle, increasing the learning curve and difficulty of maintenance, and adding additional abstractions.

Some compiled programming languages, such as \emph{D} or \emph{Rust}, support \emph{metaprogramming} in various degrees, and are allowed to treat code as data. This can be done through the use of compile-time execution, compile-time reflection, or macros. A combination of these features can allow emulating the autocoding functionality mentioned before, or implementing self-describing encoder/decoder data structures. This is, for example, the approach followed by Rust's \emph{serde} library, which can generate binary (de)serialisers through procedural macros \autocite{tolnaySerdeRust2025}. \todo{code with serde}

C++ in particular allows metaprogramming through \emph{templated} classes and functions. On its own, this mechanism does not allow a single compile-time definition of a data structure and relevant parsers. However, multiple extensions to the C++ standard have been proposed to introduce compile-time reflection. As of the writing of this thesis, the most recent example was revised in \citedate{childersReflectionC++262025}, with a proposed inclusion in C++26.
One of the notable compiler implementations of previous reflection specification was that of Matus Chochlik\footnote{Available at \url{https://github.com/matus-chochlik/llvm-project}.}, which implements a \citeyear{sankelExtensionsReflection2020} reflection proposal \autocite{sankelExtensionsReflection2020}.

\marginnote[1cm]{This library, named \textbf{\emph{ok\_serialiser}}, is available at \url{https://github.com/kongr45gpen/ok-serializer}.}
Within the scope of my thesis, \textbf{I developed a (de-)serialisation library that takes advantage of the C++ reflection features of this experimental C++ compiler, with the intention of implementing the guidelines of \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_softwaredevelopment} for software development and \Cref{sec:METHODOLOGY_datamanagement} for documentation, targeting decreased development time and risk.}

The core (de-)serialisation functionality of this library was built using Python's \emph{construct} as a reference.
The library uses internal data types that describe the binary representation (or ``view'') of the data.\footnote{This includes information not embedded in native C++ types, such as endianness or string representation.} Templated \texttt{serialize} and \texttt{deserialize} functions can be used to convert between C++ types and their binary representation.
A ``bundle'' is used to define a sequence of \texttt{okser} types. \acs{IO} types are used to interface with strings or streams.

The simplest example of serialisation, without a need for reflection, can be performed as follows:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++,]
using namespace okser;

std::string result;
auto output = out::stdstring{result};

// 1-byte signed int and 4-byte float
using MyBundle = bundle<sint<1>, floatp>;

serialize<MyBundle>(output, 63, 3.14f);
\end{lstlisting}
Through the use of template metaprogramming, available in stock C++, most of the above expressions can be evaluated at compile-time.

Reflection becomes useful when working with user-provided data structures:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++,]
struct Message {
    int8_t a = 104;
    uint16_t b = 26913;
};
\end{lstlisting}
In the general case,\footnote{The example provided here can actually be simply analysed, e.g.\@ using structured bindings, as it is an aggregate type.} C++ cannot enumerate public class members at compile-time. In this case, it would be necessary to call an additional function to specify the existence and order of members \texttt{a} and \texttt{b}. However, using reflection, it is possible for the compiler to extract this information:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++,]
// Serialisation
auto data = serialize_struct_to_string(Message{});
// data = "hi!"

// Deserialisation
auto result = deserialize_struct(data);
\end{lstlisting}

The implementation of a reflective function is provided below as an example:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++,basicstyle=\small]
template<class T, Output Out, auto config = configuration()>
constexpr void serialize_struct(Out &&output, const T &object) {
    auto mirrored_struct = mirror(T);
    for_each(get_data_members(mirrored_struct), [&](auto member) {
        const auto &value = get_value(member, object);
        using type = std::remove_cvref_t<decltype(value)>;

        using serializer = decltype(config)::template default_serializers<type>::ser;

        auto result = serialize<serializer>(std::forward<Out>(output), value);
    });
}
\end{lstlisting}

The \texttt{ok-serializer} library demonstrates how modern programming language features can help developer efficiency and reliability without a drop in software performance and resource utilisation. By using message-representing structs, it is possible to maintain a single point of reference for \acs{TMTC} of simple systems. With the use of automatic documentation generators, such as \emph{Doxygen}, it is also possible to create in-place documentation of the selected \acs{TMTC} format. However, the customisability of the library is still limited, as it is not possible, for example, to annotate types with additional information, such as validity checkers, ranges, or more complex conditions.
